- **[WorldRuler: Interactive World Modeling under Configurable Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24869)**  `arXiv:2611.24869`  
  _Mingliang Zhai, Chuanhao Li, Jiaming Tan, Zhen Li, Yuwei Wu, Yunde Jia, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.
  </details>

- **[Choosing What to Observe: Budget-Paired Counterfactual Audits for Credible Evaluation of Visual Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24867)**  `arXiv:2611.24867`  
  _Ruiqi Liu, Jinming Li, Mo Wang, Yang Lu, Yanlan Kang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents that read dense documents must ration a finite observation budget across candidate regions, yet answer accuracy conflates where an agent looks, how it adapts to the reads that remain, and how it an?swers once observation stops. We introduce budget-paired counterfactual audits, an evaluation protocol that holds public evidence and region bindings fixed while perturbing only the remaining budget or de?clared task parameters, and credits every acquired history with exact continuation values. The resulting accounting decomposes lost expected accuracy into stepwise acquisition loss and terminal decision loss, separating planning from fixed-role heuristics. Across forty paired routing anchors, five open-weight
  </details>

- **[F$^3$: What Makes Good Experience in World-Action Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24708)**  `arXiv:2611.24708`  
  _Zihe Huang, Sirui Zhou, Haozhi Han, Ruge Zhang, Teng Ma, Tao Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed world-action models (WAMs) can keep improving only if they make good use of their own experience. Existing methods supply retrieved rollouts or demonstrations to the policy as context, yet such experience often turns previously solved tasks into failures. What, then, makes good experience for a WAM? We show that experience is valuable only in use: its value depends not on whether the source rollout succeeded but on what is stored, when it is used, and where it enters the policy. We therefore propose F$^3$, which makes these three decisions with Form, Fit, and Fusion. Form compresses successful and failed rollouts alike into short, verified operation records; Fit teaches the policy to read a record and to predict when it helps; Fusion injects the record's images, text and actions into the policy as a prefix and an action prior. Trained once on only 200 examples and deployed with a growing experience library, the model achieves state-of-the-art results on MolmoSpaces and RoboLab without any training on RoboLab: over native Cosmos 3 Nano, it raises success by 12.0 pp and 10.8 pp, reduces steps to success by 12.2% and 11.3%, and cuts regressions by 57%.
  </details>

- **[Depth Before Breadth: Reverse-to-Forward Curriculum with Advantage Decomposition for LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24697)**  `arXiv:2611.24697`  
  _Siyuan Du, Yuxin Liu, Meng Tang, Haoran Li, Yechao Bai, Feiyang Pan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a common approach for training large language model (LLM) agents on long-horizon tasks. However, learning from sparse terminal rewards makes credit assignment and exploration difficult. We approach these challenges through a policy-induced capability region, the set of states from which the current policy can reliably complete a task. Near its boundary, an agent may need only a few actions to reach a more solvable state, even when the goal remains distant. This reduces long-horizon learning to two coupled subproblems of reaching that state and completing the task from there. We address them with paired advantages and a progressive reverse-to-forward curriculum. The advantages provide separate signals for reaching more solvable states and completing the task from them, while the curriculum expands the capability region backward along successful reference trajectories and then into off-reference states through on-policy exploration. On ALFWorld and WebShop, our method outperforms competitive agentic RL methods at both model scales, achieving success rates of 90.1%/96.1% and 80.2%/82.6% with Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct, respectively.
  </details>

- **[GateWatch: Low-Overhead LLM Monitoring with Boolean Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24619)**  `arXiv:2611.24619`  
  _Firas Spanioli, Yaniv David_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed, monitoring their behavior becomes important for detecting unsafe or unintended actions. Activation probes offer an efficient approach, but existing methods face trade-offs between accuracy, interpretability, and computational overhead. Probes on raw activations lack interpretable inputs, whereas sparse autoencoder (SAE) probes use interpretable features but often rely on additive classifiers and large SAE encoders, limiting interpretability and increasing memory overhead. We introduce GateWatch, a compact, interpretable classifier that expresses its entire decision rule as an explicit Boolean logic over a small number of binarized SAE features. We explore three approaches to constructing these circuits under a specified gate budget. For efficient deployment, GateWatchServe computes only selected SAE features, enables streaming of their binary values across layers during inference, and evaluates multiple GateWatch classifiers in parallel using compact lookup tables. Across 24 tasks on Gemma-2-9B, GateWatch achieves 80.49% mean test accuracy using only 3.8 SAE features and 3.5 logic gates per probe on average, just 1.17 percentage points below full 16K-feature SAE probes, while matching or outperforming them on 10 tasks and reducing probe memory by 99.98%. Inside vLLM, 1,000 concurrent multi-layer, multi-token GateWatch classifiers retain 97-98% of baseline throughput and 99.8% of KV-cache capacity.
  </details>

- **[Think Offline, Act in Real Time: Self-Improving Visual Game Agents through Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24590)**  `arXiv:2611.24590`  
  _Zipeng Qiu, Shusheng Xu, Jiale Li, Yukun Zhou, Chenyue Li, Wenshuang Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained models can read screens and reason about games, yet on an unfamiliar game, an agent still has to learn to play. We ask how an agent can self-improve on such a game from a few of its own attempts, in real-time visual games where the agent sees only the screen and the game keeps running while the agent thinks. We study two kinds of policy that can carry this improvement. In model as policy, a model selects actions during play; we adapt real-time chunking so that committed actions keep executing while the model reasons, and train a 2B controller for this interface. In code as policy, a model writes, tests, and revises a program between attempts, and the program acts during play. Across 170 GameWorld tasks, with at most eight one-minute executions per run, code as policy reaches 47.80% mean progress, against 30.90% for our trained controller, and leads in all five game categories. Improving the controller's weights on a single game, by contrast, yields only modest gains. These results favor letting a model think offline and storing what it learns in a program that acts in real time. Project repository:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AGAMOTTO: An AI-Enabled Framework for Time-Critical Tactical Course-of-Action Evaluation and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24568)**  `arXiv:2611.24568`  
  _Matthew L. Corbett_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current command and control (C2) platforms lack integrated simulation capabilities that would allow commanders to dynamically explore courses of action (COAs) during live operations. This paper presents ongoing research and contributions to the development of AGAMOTTO (Advanced Guidance and Analytics for Military Operations, Tactical Threats, and Outcomes) as part of the overarching NGC2 program, which advances the Army's efforts to modernize C2 systems, such as the Tactical Assault Kit (TAK). The paper outlines the current design, preliminary findings, and the remaining research tasks, with an emphasis on how the completed work will support tactical decision-making in future time-constrained environments. While the full system is still under development, initial development and results demonstrate the feasibility of AGAMOTTO. Early experiments highlight both the potential benefits and practical constraints of deploying AI-enabled tools in operational settings, and introduce lessons learned and a viable training strategy to make AI-generated COAs feasible in tactical scenarios.
  </details>

- **[NaviGATE: Noise-Aware Dual-View Generation for Multimodal Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24552)**  `arXiv:2611.24552`  
  _Jie Peng, Yongxue Shan, Yongfu Zha, Cui Miao, Xiaodong Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal knowledge graph reasoning (MKGR) aims to infer missing facts by jointly exploiting relational structures, textual descriptions, and visual evidence. Existing embedding-based methods effectively capture graph topology, but their fixed multimodal representations are less effective for query-specific fine-grained semantic reasoning, while generation-based methods are vulnerable to noisy retrieval and may produce invalid entities. To address these challenges, we propose NaviGATE, a noise-aware generative framework for MKGR. NaviGATE first aligns graph-derived structural representations with textual semantics, then performs structure-aware internal knowledge activation by fine-tuning the multimodal language model with multimodal entity summaries and injected structural tokens. In the noise-aware dual-view instruction tuning stage, NaviGATE jointly optimizes retrieval-free and retrieval-aware views: reliable retrieved candidates are reinforced through contrastive logit sharpening, while misleading candidates are suppressed through parametric consistency distillation. In inference, structure-constrained decoding restricts generation to valid entities in the target knowledge graph. Experiments on standard benchmarks show that NaviGATE consistently outperforms strong unimodal, multimodal, and generation-based baselines, and remains robust under noisy retrieval scenarios.
  </details>

- **[ABSClaw: From Invented Worlds to Reality-Grounded Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24517)**  `arXiv:2611.24517`  
  _Shaobin He, Yan Gao, Quan Zheng, Zhenyang Lin, Zhen Liu, Jiyan He, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based artificial societies exhibit rich behavior but often lack scientific grounding: personas and environments are assumed, interaction networks are synthetic, and simulators are generated as free-form code, making results difficult to tie to real populations and sensitive to the backbone model. We argue that simulated worlds should be sampled from observed data, while simulation logic should be implemented by composing operations from a shared, task-independent vocabulary. In this paper, we introduce ABSClaw, a zero-code agent that compiles natural-language requests into executable, reality-grounded social simulations. ABSClaw grounds who acts, where they act, and with whom they interact in survey and census data, geospatial sources, and real networks. It expresses state transitions through 26 atomic operations. To support an open and extensible ecosystem, ABSClaw further allows capabilities to be added as lifecycle-scoped skills without modifying its core. Furthermore, we propose ABSBench, a benchmark of 193 real-world social-simulation tasks. On ABSBench, ABSClaw achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated social simulators. Three real-world case studies show that grounding personas, environments, and interaction networks each improves agreement with observed outcomes. With one runtime skill, ABSClaw executes 13 population-scale simulations, collectively involving 25 million agents. Together, these results demonstrate ABSClaw's real-world fidelity, extensibility, and scalability.
  </details>

- **[The Largest Tractable Subclass of Multi-Scale Allen Temporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24498)**  `arXiv:2611.24498`  
  _Quentin Cohen-Solal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a novel model for temporal reasoning over multi-scale intervals. We show that this model yields a complete reasoning framework and exhibits other particularly interesting properties. Furthermore, we identify the maximal tractable subalgebra for this model, as well as for the other complete model, in the qualitative scales context, which exhibits weaker properties but whose results can be derived by transfer from our new model.
  </details>

- **[Study and Improvement of Search Algorithms in Stochastic Perfect-Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24486)**  `arXiv:2611.24486`  
  _Quentin Cohen-Solal, Tristan Cazenave_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this article, we generalize Unbounded Minimax, the state- of-the-art search algorithm for deterministic zero-sum two- player games with perfect information to the framework of stochastic zero-sum two-player games with perfect informa- tion. We experimentally show that this generalized algorithm achieves better performance than the main stochastic search algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Resolution as Attention for Efficient Agent Memory Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24422)**  `arXiv:2611.24422`  
  _Siqi Xiang, Zhipeng Xu, Shengrui Liu, Zulong Chen, Chunyan Miao, Shijian Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents accumulate extensive interaction histories when solving complex, long-horizon tasks, making efficient memory management critical to their performance and scalability. While existing memory management methods primarily focus on what to retain or retrieve, they often overlook how much perceptual detail should be allocated to individual past interactions. We posit that effective agent memory depends not only on the information preserved, but also on the granularity at which each past interaction is perceived. Motivated by this insight, we propose Resolution as Attention (RAA), a memory perception mechanism that uses visual resolution as a means of attention allocation across interaction histories. RAA renders past interactions as images and dynamically adjusts their resolution at every step according to their relevance to the agent's upcoming decision, preserving fine-grained information for critical interactions while reducing visual-token consumption for less relevant ones. To adaptively determine the perceptual fidelity of each interaction, we train a lightweight relevance estimator with teacher-model guidance and further optimize it to preserve the agent's decision consistency at a reduced visual-token cost. Experiments across multiple tasks and backbones show that RAA reduces token consumption by over 50% without compromising task performance. Moreover, RAA can be integrated as a plug-in perception layer with existing memory management methods, yielding further token savings and revealing adaptive perception as a complementary dimension of agent memory.
  </details>

- **[Risk-Triggered Search: Behavior-Preserving Safe Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24409)**  `arXiv:2611.24409`  
  _Xinyu Deng, Bohao Lan, Kunpeng Li, Mingkai Yuan, Weihang Pan, Binbin Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained visual world models generate multi-step action plans from current and goal observations, yet terminal-proximity objectives fail to prevent forbidden-zone traversal or rigid-body collision sliding. Purely visual interfaces may lack analytical dynamics, structured states, or explicit collision geometry, requiring internal prediction of action-conditioned process risk. We propose Risk-Triggered Search (RTS) for frozen Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models. Rather than relying on static state features that cannot distinguish candidate plans sharing an observation, RTS unrolls action-conditioned future latent prefixes in the frozen world model and trains a lightweight risk head with process-violation supervision. To intervene selectively while preserving safe nominal behavior, RTS uses the same predicted prefix-risk criterion as both the search trigger gate and hard feasibility gate: safe nominal plans execute directly, whereas alarms trigger local Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) search to minimize nominal deviation within the feasible set. To prevent isotropic Euclidean minimal modification from shrinking obstacle-directed actions into zero-amplitude stalling, we introduce an amplitude-preserving metric $d_{\mathrm{AP}}$ that retains tangential progression while maintaining a unique zero at the nominal plan. Under unified gating and this unique-zero condition, bypassing safe-state search yields closed-loop trajectories strictly identical to every-step same-kernel search. Evaluated under a process-safety benchmark and Safe Arrival Rate (SAR) metric across five tasks, RTS improves SAR over Direct by 12.67--41.67 percentage points on navigation and articulated control and +2.08/+4.17 pp on Cube/PushT boundary cohorts, maintaining 100% action identity with every-step search while cutting controller time by 17.71%--70.41% across 1,968 paired trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Context Handoff with On-Demand Evidence for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24357)**  `arXiv:2611.24357`  
  _Guolong Zheng, Chen Hou, Xu Yang, Jianshan Zhang, Tao Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional long-horizon LLM agents often hand off context through full-history prompting, retaining all previous action--observation interactions. As execution proceeds, this history keeps growing, increasing inference cost and potentially degrading accuracy. Existing methods control this growth by preserving compact conclusions while removing earlier observations, but doing so can discard evidence that later becomes relevant. We introduce Semantic Context Handoff (SCH), whose central abstraction is state--evidence separation: a compact semantic state carries the agent's current interpretation, while supporting action--observation evidence remains separately addressable and can re-enter context when later execution makes it relevant, without separate summarization or retrieval-model calls.Empirically, SCH improves long-horizon agent performance while substantially reducing context cost. In a controlled 150-task SWE-bench study, SCH's evidence recovery improves Pass@1 from $80.0\%$ to $86.9\%$ at essentially unchanged token use and execution time. In the complete-system comparison, SCH achieves 86.9% Pass@1 while using 43% fewer tokens and 41% less wall-clock time than the strongest baseline by task performance. Across models and benchmarks, SCH consistently reduces token use relative to full-history prompting while remaining competitive with or improving upon the evaluated baselines. Trajectory analysis confirms that SCH recovers evidence from substantially earlier parts of the interaction history rather than merely preserving a recent context window.
  </details>

- **[When Figures Disagree with Text: Evaluating Autonomous Visual Evidence Verification in Scientific Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24353)**  `arXiv:2611.24353`  
  _Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Jiajun Zha, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Huihao Jing, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figures carry evidence for the claims that cite them, so reviewing a paper means checking that a figure agrees with the text, and not only describing what the figure shows. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Deciding what to verify is thus a large part of the difficulty, and the selection rule we tested does not resolve it. Multimodal document understanding should be evaluated on whether a model can propose what to verify, not only answer questions about a figure it is handed.
  </details>

- **[KnowledgeClaw: Towards Agentic Knowledge Engineering via Omnimodal Hypergraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24294)**  `arXiv:2611.24294`  
  _Haoran Luo, Jiapu Wang, Linhao Luo, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge engineering (KE) turns raw files into a reasoning-ready knowledge base, a task now within reach of LLM agents. KE has progressed from chunk and graph retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to memory agents, agentic KE and multimodal KE. However, a live knowledge base needs omnimodality, a closed-loop lifecycle and multi-granular application at once, yet existing systems meet at most two and no benchmark measures all three. We formalize Omnimodal Knowledge Engineering (OmniKE) and release OmniKE-Bench, six tasks with 1,829 model-free items over 3,960 real files in five modalities. We further propose KnowledgeClaw, an agentic system that builds an omnimodal typed hypergraph, keeps it current with a closed-loop lifecycle agent and routes each query to the granularity it needs. Against 30 baselines, it ranks first on all six tasks with an overall OKE of 55.9, +14.6 over the strongest baseline. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Coordinate: LLM-Guided Workflow Evolution for Token-Efficient Red Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24221)**  `arXiv:2611.24221`  
  _Xiaoyang Xing, Wenyu Dai, Xiaoliang Gong, Asoke K Nandi, Anthony Cohn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated red teaming is essential for identifying safety failures in large language models (LLMs), but its practical scalability depends on the resources required to discover those failures. Multi-agent systems can combine complementary capabilities, yet repeated planning, redundant exchanges, and ineffective information routing can make their coordination expensive. Moreover, a target model's response indicates the outcome of a test without identifying which collaboration decision should change. We present Learning to Coordinate (L2C), an LLM-guided framework that evolves an executable multi-agent workflow for each red-teaming case. L2C represents agent responsibilities, execution dependencies, and input bindings in a versioned graph, and associates each graph with records of the information actually delivered and produced. A controller trained through supervised fine-tuning integrates these records with target responses, evaluator verdicts, and remaining budget to retain the workflow, refine instructions, restructure collaboration, or lock further editing. This design connects feedback to explicit changes in subsequent execution while allowing a useful organization to continue without repeated controller intervention. The evaluation protocol covers HarmBench with three execution backbones and two target models, together with component ablations, dataset transfer, and evaluator checks. Across the six executor-target configurations, L2C improves ASR over EvoSynth by 1.4 to 4.5 percentage points while reducing mean online tokens by 35.6% to 37.3%. L2C connects adaptive collaboration to the resource requirements of automated security evaluation.
  </details>

- **[GALA: Gallery-Level Alignment for Real-Life Vision Decoding from EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24206)**  `arXiv:2611.24206`  
  _Philippe Forest, Giulia Lioi, Mai Pham Quyen, Alix Lamouroux, Javen Shi, Adrien Merlini_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural decoding of visual experience from electroencephalography (EEG) has achieved strong performance in intra-subject settings, but current methods fail to generalize across individuals: on the standard 200-way benchmark THINGS-EEG-2, top-1 accuracy drops from above 80% within subjects to about 32% for an unseen subject. Adapting a pre-trained encoder to new subjects and sessions typically requires labeled data, a bottleneck whose collection time severely limits practical device deployment. In a practical Brain-Computer Interface (BCI), however, the possible outcomes are known in advance and form a gallery of candidates, and the unlabeled EEG of the user accumulates as soon as the device is used. We propose a new setting, incremental transductive calibration, in which the decoder is calibrated regularly on all the unlabeled EEG received so far and on the known gallery, without any labels. This setting is closer to the conditions of a deployed BCI than the zero-shot and batch evaluations in current use. We evaluate it with a protocol in which queries arrive one at a time, with unknown and possibly unequal frequencies, and each query is decoded before it is used for calibration. In this setting, we introduce GALA : GAllery-Level Alignment, a label-free calibration method which registers the unlabeled EEG embeddings onto the gallery through shrinkage whitening, hubness correction and a Sinkhorn--Procrustes alignment with query frequencies estimated from the buffer, updated after every query. On THINGS-EEG-2, with a gallery of 200 images, it raises the top-1 accuracy on a new subject from 28% to 67% over 53 minutes of use. Across 60 evaluation settings that vary the gallery size, the query frequencies and the number of repetitions averaged per query, GALA is the most accurate label-free method in every setting, 6.6 points above the best competing method on average at the end of the stream.
  </details>

- **[Embodied Graph: A Relational World Model for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24115)**  `arXiv:2611.24115`  
  _Zongyuan Wu, Xin Wang, Tongtong Feng, Yuan Meng, Zeyang Zhang, Ling Feng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon tasks in embodied and web environments involve extended decision sequences and delayed feedback, making accurate planning crucial for success. World models tackle this by learning latent dynamics for imagination-based planning. However, existing approaches merely rely on dense pixel-level observations, which forces the model to absorb redundant information while overlooking relations among task-relevant entities. Consequently, small errors may accumulate over long imagined rollouts and gradually corrupt the predictions. In this paper, we explore world models that imagine over sparse state abstractions, which poses two key challenges: 1) how to remove redundant information and form a compact state abstraction; 2) how to incorporate the relational structure to learn reliable long-horizon dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose Embodied Graph, a relational state abstraction that supports structured dynamics modeling and long-horizon imagination, and the Relational World Model (RWM), which performs imagination directly over graph states. RWM consists of (i) a Task-Aware Embodied Graph Constructor that filters redundant details and maintains graph memory through online updates, and (ii) an Embodied Graph Recurrent State Space Model that learns node-wise latent dynamics while capturing inter-node dependencies. The imagined graph states provide predictions of action consequences to guide decision-making. Experiments on Minecraft and Crafter demonstrate the effectiveness of RWM in embodied environments. Notably, in Minecraft, RWM is the only method that obtains diamonds within the given budget. Experiments on web interaction further illustrate the applicability of relational state prediction to the decision making process of web agents.
  </details>

- **[3D GameDev Arena: Benchmarking Coding Agents That Plan, Build, and Playtest extreme Long-Horizon Browser Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24073)**  `arXiv:2611.24073`  
  _Bizhe Bai, Jiliang Jian, Yuqing Yan, Tao Chen, Ying Wen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents now resolve repository issues and generate compact playable games, but whether they can build a large 3D game from scratch remains open. Such games tightly couple mechanics, scenes, assets, and interaction logic, and their quality rests on player enjoyment, which existing benchmarks rarely assess. Thus, we introduce 3D GameDev Arena, a first extreme long-horizon benchmark of 108 from-scratch game-development tasks across 14 genres. Given only a text specification and UI/UX reference images, a fully automated plan--implement--test--repair pipeline builds each task into a self-contained browser game. Runs average $\sim$1,000 LLM calls ; the longest uses 14,407 calls, 2.13B tokens, and 608 assets. Evaluation spans (1) objective code-level functional acceptance against the game specification, (2) agent-based QA and playtesting of 3D presentation and playability, and (3) blind pairwise Bradley-Terry human preference in a public arena. The preliminary findings indicate that long-horizon 3D game development remains far from solved, and code-level correctness alone does not dictate player preference. The public arena is hosted online.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Symmetrization: Scaling Laws for Model-Agnostic Learning under Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24032)**  `arXiv:2611.24032`  
  _Behrooz Tahmasebi, Melanie Weber_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time compute has emerged as a powerful way to improve the accuracy of language models at inference time, with scaling laws quantifying how accuracy improves with additional forward passes. Existing scaling laws, however, focus on repeated sampling from a fixed prompt, even though many tasks admit multiple equivalent representations of the same underlying problem. A graph does not depend on how its nodes are numbered, and a program does not depend on the names of its variables, yet language models can change their answers under such rewritings. In this paper, we study test-time symmetrization, which uses forward passes on rewritings of the input and aggregates the resulting answers over the group $G$ of rewritings. We prove that no amount of sampling and repeated decoding from a single representation can replace rewriting, while only $O(\log |G|)$ random rewritings suffice to recover the information provided by all $|G|$ rewritings. The same set of $O(\log |G|)$ rewritings provides simultaneous approximation across models, decoding rules, and sampled reasoning traces, and the logarithmic scaling is optimal in general. We validate the theory with experiments on Qwen2.5-7B, Phi-4-mini, Llama-3.1-8B, and Qwen2.5-14B, with chain-of-thought reasoning, on graph reasoning, logical deduction, and multiple-choice question answering. At the same number of forward passes, spending them on rewritings of the input is substantially more accurate than spending them on repeated sampling from the original prompt whenever the model's answer depends on how the input is written, and the number of rewritings needed grows only logarithmically with the number of possible rewritings, as the theory predicts. Our results provide a principled way to enforce symmetries at inference time when they cannot be built into the model, which we call model-agnostic symmetrization.
  </details>

- **[Space-X: Self-Evolving Spatial Agents Weaving Clues into Evidence Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24004)**  `arXiv:2611.24004`  
  _Zijian Zhang, Wenqing Tian, Qiang Liu, Liang Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning connects visual observations, geometric estimates, and linguistic decisions into a coherent derivation. Yet tool-augmented agents often acquire evidence without clear targets, combine outputs under inconsistent object or reference-frame semantics, and misjudge evidence sufficiency. Thus, more tools or calls do not reliably improve reasoning. We introduce Space-X, a spatial-reasoning framework that self-evolves toward complete, continuous evidence-chain construction. An Execution Agent acquires, associates, and judges evidence per problem using role-specific memory. A Control Agent diagnoses evidence-chain breaks from execution traces and jointly revises tools, interfaces, skills, memory organization, and decision rules. This loop co-evolves capability and evidence use: evidence needs guide capability development; usable results guide actions. Across multiple benchmarks, Space-X yields gains with four base models. Relative to baselines, GPT-6-sol improves answer accuracy on ViewSpatial and MMSI by 50.39% and 23.07%, respectively; Gemini-3.8-Flash gains 47.50% and 25.22% on these benchmarks. This provides a unified route from fragmented spatial clues to traceable, derivable answers.
  </details>

- **[GLIDE: Scalable and Event-Aware Inductive Forecasting over Evolving Logistics Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23942)**  `arXiv:2611.23942`  
  _Zhi Sheng, Chuyue Wang, Jiahao Ji, Yile Liang, Xuetao Ding, Jinghua Hao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate forecasting of order volume at scale-effect hotspots (SEHs), spatially coherent groups of logistics flows that serve as distributed optimization units for real-time many-to-one dispatch, is a prerequisite for efficient order pooling in on-demand food delivery (OFD) platforms such as Meituan. However, real-world logistics networks present unique challenges that render standard spatiotemporal prediction model ineffective. (i) Dynamic Topology: Unlike static road networks, logistics graphs are ephemeral, with hotspot emerging and vanishing continuously; (ii) Scale--Volume Heterogeneity: Graph sizes vary from $10^2$ to $10^4$, while zero-inflated, long-tailed volumes create computational bottlenecks and bias predictions toward inactivity; and (iii) Event-Driven Disruption: Exogenous events (holidays, weather, academic calendars) induce systematic demand shifts that purely data-driven backbones cannot anticipate. In this paper, we present GLIDE, a production-ready framework that models evolving logistics graphs in a scalable latent space while adapting to heterogeneous demand regimes and event-driven shifts. GLIDE introduces a Graph-Guided Latent Aggregation mechanism, which dynamically projects varying physical subgraphs into a fixed-size, semantically stable latent space via a differentiable sparse assignment...
  </details>

- **[Can you make models believe false facts in the prompt?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23938)**  `arXiv:2611.23938`  
  _Ananya Sutradhar, Fabien Roger_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Making LLMs believe false facts is useful for alignment evaluations and for building model organisms. Previous work explored doing so with Synthetic Document Finetuning (SDF). But SDF requires fine-tuning one new model for each set of injected false beliefs. To enable larger scale SDF-powered alignment evaluations, we attempt to produce a single model that believes any fact placed in a <facts> tag in the prompt. To do so, we explore doing prompt-conditioned SDF: fine-tuning a model on synthetic documents covering thousands of facts, with each document conditioned on a fact presented in the prompt. We evaluate how deeply the model believes the facts provided in the prompt using evaluations from prior work and a new Jacobian lens evaluation. We find that the belief depth on unseen facts scales roughly log-linearly with the number of training facts, making this a cheap, promising avenue for making models believe false facts, though preliminary experiments on applying it to alignment audits on the small model we studied showed negative results due to these small models often already acting as if they believed facts provided in regular honeypot system prompts. Using RL to reduce the rate at which models verbalize following a false fact in the prompt, on top of prompt-conditioned SDF, generalizes to held-out evals and almost matches SDF on some evaluations. But training against one of our metrics for belief depth makes it harder to trust those evaluations.
  </details>

- **[Vanguard: Knowing When to Trust the Draft in Speculative Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23917)**  `arXiv:2611.23917`  
  _Hanqi Mao, Jiajun Fan, Ruihan Guo, Shuaike Shen, Ziwen Wang, Hui Ren, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language agents that browse websites, operate phones and act in homes reason before every action, so every step waits for a large model, a latency and cost that stand in the way of deployment. Step-level speculation lets a small model draft each step and calls the large model only on rejected drafts, but its outcome rests on the judge, and a judge that reads only a step's text cannot tell whether the step fits the screen it acts on. We find that a few samples of a small model can contain a correct step about as often as the large model gets the step right, and not always on the same steps: checked against the screen, small drafts can be not only cheaper but better. We present Vanguard, a training-free judge that leaves the draft and target models unchanged: a general decision model, with the screenshot in view, gives each candidate step a probability of being right in one forward pass; the best candidate is kept if it clears a threshold, and otherwise the large model writes the step. On web, mobile and embodied agents, a 2B draft judged by Vanguard is at least as accurate as its 8B target and significantly more accurate on Mind2Web and EB-ALFRED, while the target writes only a minority of the steps; it completes far more closed-loop episodes, and with a 27B target from another family it is both faster and more accurate. Vanguard is the most accurate judge we compare on the agent tasks; it relies on the screenshot on web and mobile tasks, its calibrated threshold transfers to unseen websites, and across model families its gain over the target grows with the draft's pass@4 relative to the target's accuracy. Vanguard composes with token-level speculative decoding and matches trained process reward models on mathematics, turning speculation from a way to save the large model's work into one that can improve on it.
  </details>

- **[MLE Agents: Autonomous ML Engineering in Large-scale Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23910)**  `arXiv:2611.23910`  
  _Matthew Li, Albert Liang, Dilip Raj_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The typical workflow for improving large-scale ranking and recommendation models is slow and tedious: an engineer forms a hypothesis, implements it as a code change, trains a model, and evaluates it both offline and online, often relying on their own intuition and experience along the way. Autonomous LLM agents promise to speed this up by running the loop on their own, but it is still unclear whether they can do this in industry-scale systems rather than on benchmarks or small proxy tasks. We build human-on-the-loop machine learning engineer (MLE) agents that propose, implement, train, and evaluate model changes on large-scale search rankers. Engineers retain control over objective design, search direction, and final-model review. We identify three obstacles that make this industry setting challenging: feedback takes hours per experiment, offline metrics do not always predict online impact, and improvements saturate after the early wins. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Its best model increases search volume by 0.23\% in an A/B test of large scale. Several improvements were architectural changes the team had never tried, drawn from the broader industry and literature, suggesting that MLE agents can explore regions of the design space that human intuition overlooks. We find that the design of the harness around the model matters: intuition suggests that a stronger underlying model is enough, but in our experience, the design of the loop around it is just as critical. More broadly, our work shows that autonomous agents can do real machine-learning engineering in industry-scale systems.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating invariance training with certified answer-changing controls in neural graph algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23907)**  `arXiv:2611.23907`  
  _Zhe Dong, Fang Qin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Invariance training aims to make neural algorithmic reasoners robust to answer-preserving input changes and is judged by accuracy and by how rarely such edits change predictions. We ask whether this evaluation detects lost sensitivity to answer-changing edits on minimum spanning trees of synthetic graphs, where both directions can be certified. Attaching a subgraph through one bridge provably preserves every original label. Attaching it through two edges, oracle-selected to change a label, and lowering one weight inside the graph serve as answer-changing controls; a response is a changed prediction on an edge whose label changes. A reproduced graph transformer (GDT) within its published accuracy range changes a provably fixed label on 53.3-65.9% of the 64-vertex graphs it solves exactly after a 64-vertex bridge attachment; removing the connecting edge changes little. With 16-vertex graphs and attachments, three invariance objectives lower GDT violations from 2.91% to 0.52-2.02% of pairs at essentially unchanged accuracy. Training also lowers the response to two-edge attachments from 97.6% to 66.0-79.0%, while the response to in-base weight edits stays near 99.5%. At 64 vertices accuracy also falls, but on 16-vertex bases only the attachment control reveals the loss. A message-passing network loses some attachment response at small penalty weights but gains little invariance. Running the conventionally trained GDT on each biconnected block separately removes all observed violations and keeps accuracy and both responses, so the task does not force the trade-off. Sensitivity checks for trained invariance should include certified answer-changing edits that attach material as the training edits do.
  </details>

- **[EVA-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement of Video Evaluators for Judging Temporal Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23890)**  `arXiv:2611.23890`  
  _Yu Yuan, Ruizhou Ding, Shaoheng Fang, Stanley H. Chan, Zhuo Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video evaluators play an important role in video generation, yet they miss motion defects, limiting the quality of generated videos. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) has made strong progress in mathematics and coding, where explicit verifiers provide reliable feedback on a single goal. Video evaluation poses two difficulties for RSI: it has no verifier, and it must judge many dimensions at once, such as physics, smoothness and dynamics, where a gain on one can cost another. We introduce EVA-RSI, a multi-agent framework for exploring RSI under these two constraints. We build a fixed proxy exam with construction-defined targets by applying controlled changes to videos, such as temporal reversal and frame dropping. These edits supply supervision without human annotation. A small set of human-labeled real videos anchors the exam to human judgment. Each dimension has an LLM agent that proposes improvements. A leader agent coordinates the proposals, and a Nash bargaining rule selects updates that balance progress across dimensions. Humans design the exam, review candidate changes and revise the curriculum between stages. Starting from Qwen3-VL-8B, EVA-RSI improves motion evaluation, with the largest gain on smoothness, and the gains carry over to defect types never seen during the run. On the dynamics subsets of public benchmarks, our evaluator achieves the highest observed scores among 21 tested models. As an RL reward, it increases generated motion and earns human preference. A data-selection pipeline incorporating it also increases motion.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-Based Credit Assignment for Search-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23824)**  `arXiv:2611.23824`  
  _Wen Wang, Jindong Wang, Jiahua Bao, Tu Yongsiqi, Yihao Liu, Junwei He, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from terminal answer rewards enables search-augmented language models to interleave reasoning and retrieval without step-level annotations, yet outcome supervision assigns identical credit to every search in a trajectory regardless of whether it retrieved useful information. This limitation is especially severe when all sampled rollouts for a question fail, collapsing group-centered outcome advantages to zero even when trajectories gather markedly different evidence. We present Evidence-based Credit Assignment (ECA), which augments terminal outcome rewards with a training-only auxiliary signal measuring how retrieved documents improve the likelihood of a reference answer. Rather than scoring the full dialogue prefix, ECA queries a frozen snapshot of the current policy under an evidence-only prompt containing solely the question and the deduplicated documents observed so far, isolating the utility of retrieved passages from surface variations in generated thoughts or search queries. Within this framework, we compare two return formulations, ECA-Step (which accumulates step-clipped potential increments) and ECA-Horizon (which preserves the telescoping horizon gain in potential), paired with cross-fold group normalization, novelty gating, and search-local token assignment. Across seven Search QA benchmarks, ECA-Horizon improves strict macro exact match over an outcome-only GRPO baseline by 1.03 and 4.80 percentage points on Qwen2.5-3B- and 7B-Instruct, respectively, outperforming both RLOO and GiGPO. Zero-shot evaluation on MultiHop-RAG further demonstrates strong transfer to multi-hop inference queries.
  </details>

- **[Metrics-in-the-Loop: Complexity Feedback Reshapes Agent-Written Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23817)**  `arXiv:2611.23817`  
  _Nicolas Chapados, Jean-François Gagné, Nouha Dziri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents now write whole codebases in long autonomous runs, guided mainly by whether their tests pass. Yet passing tests alone does not reveal accumulating complexity or gaps in test coverage, which then persist as agents extend their own code. We introduce metrics-in-the-loop, an inference-time intervention that gives agents in-flight feedback: cheap, automatically computed code metrics injected into the agent's context during the trajectory. After each code-changing agent turn, we insert short feedback messages naming changed functions whose cyclomatic complexity exceeds a limit (complexity feedback) or changed lines that are skipped by tests (coverage feedback), giving the agent the opportunity to refactor code or strengthen tests before continuing. We run extensive experiments and ablations comparing several interventions across three long-horizon coding benchmarks and four strong models. We find that complexity feedback significantly reduces erosion, a measure of complexity concentration of code, without affecting held-out correctness of the code. Broadly, our experiments investigate a practical form of agent "re-railing", where timely, actionable measurements redirect agents before weaknesses become the foundation they keep building on. As agents scale to codebases with millions of lines, such interventions become critical to sustaining effective development and long-term code maintainability.
  </details>

- **[Continual Tool-Space Extension Learning for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23810)**  `arXiv:2611.23810`  
  _Jinbae Seo, Jungin Park, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jiyoung Lee, Kwanghoon Sohn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents largely rely on generic tools such as cropping, search, and code execution, leaving specialist vision models outside their tool space. However, bringing such a model into an already trained agent is not straightforward. The challenge is to learn each new tool efficiently while retaining the ability to use existing ones. This work proposes COTEX, continual tool-space extension learning, that adds new tools at efficient adapter-level cost. COTEX locates two sets of parameter directions; one triggers a tool call and thereby opens a multi-turn reasoning trajectory, while the other determines which tool is chosen. The adapter of a new tool is restricted to the calling directions that leave earlier tool choices unaffected by orthogonal constraint. We further introduce a protocol that converts specialist models' existing task-specific annotations into verified tool-use trajectories. Experiments on two representative agents and three specialist tools show gains on ten vision-language benchmarks, with at most a 0.3-point drop on existing tools.
  </details>

- **[Modeling LLM Reasoning Dynamics with Structural Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23749)**  `arXiv:2611.23749`  
  _Ilia Karpov, Andrey Atamanyuk, Dmitry Ilvovsky, Aleksandr Anokhin, Alex Elizov, Dmitry Zagorulkin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model reasoning alternates between sustained activities and local operations. We study how this temporal structure predicts continuation and the computation needed to analyze it. T/E/S combines text, local events, and spans of activity. Across reasoning and information-seeking tasks, events and spans improve next-outcome prediction in 70 of 76 evaluation groups for a Transformer and 74 of 76 for Qwen with LoRA. Span duration and preceding events are associated with transitions, and retaining event history across span boundaries improves prediction. We formulate budget-allocation counting and action-conditional continuation queries, prove counting hardness under explicit encodings, and derive conditions for affordable decision certification. In finite-process experiments, bounds that cancel shared continuation mass recover the same optimal actions as full dynamic programming while reducing mean inference CPU time by 77.7%. On 71 held-out HotpotQA and MuSiQue questions, a compact structural observer improves prediction of whether a fixed inference procedure finishes within a CPU allowance: Brier loss falls from 0.0836 to 0.0756. An annotation audit identifies substantial errors in that observer, separating this computational result from semantic validation of its features.
  </details>

- **[Auditable Clinical Support with Decision Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23700)**  `arXiv:2611.23700`  
  _Tiancheng Bao, Riga Wu, Jan Wienhold, Sebastian Billig, Patrick Winnersbach, Hsuan-Chih Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinicians reviewing a diagnostic work-up will ask what was considered at each step and why the assessment changed. Agents powered by free text can reach physician-level diagnostic accuracy, but their written reasoning does not always reflect the factors that went into the decision. Here, we present Decision2Care, a decision agent that works up the acutely ill patient without producing any text. A decision model assigns a probability to each permitted EHR action, and fixed rules carry out the most probable one. We evaluated Decision2Care on the records of 546 emergency admissions across 12 diseases. Decision2Care reached a diagnostic accuracy of 86.5%, compared with 86.1% for the MIRA-v2 agent, and required a median of 2.2 s per work-up, compared with 92-100 s. Each work-up tracked which options Decision2Care was considering at each step. In 10 of the 12 diseases, the differential diagnosis changed most at the test on which the guideline bases the diagnosis. Where Decision2Care was most confident after a single work-up, all but four of its diagnoses were correct (98.5%). We traced each of these four errors back to a single imaging report. Three of them were corrected when that report was removed. The same kind of decision was used to select the treatments, and Decision2Care's drug orders were judged guideline-adherent more often than MIRA-v2's. In emergency patients, its prediction of death was comparable to that of three intensivists on the same cases. In five of the eight intensive care cohorts, it exceeded the best of the three 24-h severity scores within 2-6 h of records. Our results show that decision agents can carry out clinical work-ups while recording the options, probabilities, and execution rules behind each action for audit.
  </details>

- **[Receiver-Conditioned Clarification for Multi-Agent Food Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23572)**  `arXiv:2611.23572`  
  _Kaiwen Shi, Zheyuan Zhang, Changzhi Liu, Han Bao, Yanfang Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Food insecurity creates a pressing need for guidance that helps households find usable food resources and assess food choices in light of their circumstances. Such guidance must connect eligibility requirements, practical access constraints, and dietary needs rather than return isolated listings or generic nutritional advice. Multi-agent food assistants can coordinate these tasks, with uncertainty indicating when clarification or verification is needed before a recommendation. However, uncertainty can be lost across agent handoffs, allowing tentative interpretations to propagate as facts and obscuring opportunities for correction. Moreover, confidence that a service requirement has been interpreted correctly is not confidence that a recommendation satisfies the user's needs; local confidence cannot simply be reused as downstream reliability. We introduce an inference-time, receiver-conditioned clarification framework that preserves shared premise identities and replays consistent interpretations across dependent agents. Each receiver exposes admissible decisions and remaining evidence obligations, so question relevance reflects both decision changes and missing support. Information questions and user-confirmable fallback adjustments are evaluated together with their anticipated follow-up interactions under shared budgets. This structure connects clarification to jointly supported plans for resource navigation, nutritional reasoning, and supply-side planning.
  </details>

- **[LProp: Propagating Latent Uncertainty through Evidence-Conditioned Belief Revision in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23564)**  `arXiv:2611.23564`  
  _Kaiwen Shi, Zheyuan Zhang, Weixiang Sun, Han Bao, Colby Nelson, Yanfang Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When LLM agents pass intermediate judgments downstream without uncertainty, later decisions may treat tentative assumptions as facts. Effective uncertainty propagation must preserve information for correction and update inherited beliefs as new evidence arrives. Yet scalar scores omit information needed for revision, while detailed descriptions and reflection incur additional costs. We introduce Latent Uncertainty, a latent-space estimator that preserves uncertainty-relevant information without lengthy descriptions. Building on this, we propose LProp (Latent Uncertainty Propagation), which revises inherited answer distributions using latent representations and new evidence without additional trajectory sampling. We evaluate both methods across multiple-choice, yes/no, open-ended question answering, and mathematical reasoning tasks. Our approach outperforms baselines on most metrics, while LProp achieves the lowest mean inference-token usage in all eight propagation settings. Matched feature ablations further demonstrate that inherited latent content improves error recovery beyond answer probabilities or model capacity alone.
  </details>

- **[FACET - Facet Aware Contrastive Embeddings for Traits- Big Five Estimation from Life Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23560)**  `arXiv:2611.23560`  
  _Balakrishnan Arumugam, Priyen Parekh, Mehak Gupta, Joshua Oltmanns_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personality is only weakly and inconsistently signaled by written language, and existing text-based methods typically combine language-model and psycholinguistic representations at the input or decision level. It remains open whether a more deliberate combination of the two views, and a decomposition of the prediction target, can recover more of the signal. We present a three-phase framework for estimating Big Five personality domain scores from life-narrative interview text. It encodes text and psycholinguistic features separately and combines them by concatenation or by a learned weighting, decomposes each domain into contrastively trained facet embeddings instead of predicting the domain directly, and pools the facet embeddings by attention into a domain-level prediction. We compare end-to-end with staged training and ablate the input modalities, their combination, and the facet-pooling strategy. On 1,332 participants from the St. Louis Personality and Aging Network (SPAN) study, facet embeddings reach AUC up to 0.740, and domain-level R2 reaches 0.328, highest for Openness. End-to-end training gives lower error than staged training in every domain, and attention pooling gives the lowest error in every domain. Combining the two views lowers error relative to text alone for Conscientiousness and Agreeableness, whereas a learned weighting of the views shows no consistent advantage over concatenation. The framework provides facet- level predictions that a domain-only model cannot, and it improves modestly on a regression from the raw features in four of the five domains.
  </details>

- **[Diagnostic Accuracy and Navigation Behavior of Vision-Language Model Agents Reading Emergency CT and MRI Examinations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23480)**  `arXiv:2611.23480`  
  _Jonathan Lichtle, Aymen Meddeb, Antonin Birot, Ibrahima Niang, Lisa Gastaud, Riyad Hanafi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose. To evaluate the diagnostic accuracy and image navigation behavior of vision-language model (VLM) agents under varying exploration strategies. Materials and Methods. In this retrospective study, six VLMs read 100 emergency CT and MRI examinations published in a public archive (March 2025-August 2026) under four conditions (free navigation, navigation from a key slice, and all slices or key slices in a single request), twice each (4800 runs). Nine radiologists read 30 of those cases. A large language model judge validated against two radiologists graded the answers. Conditions were compared with generalized estimating equations clustered by case. Results. Pooled accuracy was lowest under free navigation (32.6%; 391/1200), followed by navigation from a key slice (37.9%; 455/1200; P = .003), and highest with all slices (42.2%; 506/1200) or key slices (42.5%; 510/1200) in one request (both P < .001). On the 30 cases read by radiologists, freely navigating models were correct in 30.0% of runs (108/360) versus 77.0% for radiologists (208/270; P < .001). Models formed a first hypothesis after a median of 10.8% of the slices and kept it in 63.1% of runs (755/1196); final answers were correct in 12.4% of runs after a wrong first hypothesis (110/885) versus 90.4% after a right one (281/311; P < .001). Median stated confidence in the final answers was 90%, whereas 38.9% of them were correct (1861/4778). Conclusion. Across all exploration strategies, VLM agents remained far less accurate than radiologists. They adopted an early hypothesis and usually kept it, seldom correcting a wrong one.
  </details>

- **[GUARD: Stabilizing Recursive Self-Improvement with Regret Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23476)**  `arXiv:2611.23476`  
  _Minghe Zhang, Xuetao Kang, Yifei Ming, Yanfei Chen, Han Yu, Yufan Zhuang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous AI agents pair a frozen base model with an editable harness of prompts, skills, tool rules, and memory. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) adapts this harness from experience without weight updates, yet self-editing without external graders often backfires: reflection and scalar scores conflate what an edit newly broke with what remains unsolved, causing local regressions, skill forgetting, and memory bloat. We introduce GUARD (Grader-free Unsupervised Environment Design [UED] with Adaptive Rule Distillation), a closed-loop framework for reference-free continual harness evolution. Our key insight is to decompose each task into pre-execution self-checks and guide harness updates via directional regret. Each round grades trajectories against these checks, pinning mastered rules while prioritizing newly broken behaviors, and selects among parallel candidate edits by critic-free group-relative fitness. A Soft-HURT gate then promotes an edit only when gains outweigh regressions, scoping specialized fixes to prevent over-triggering and guaranteeing non-regression on its gated buffer. At test time, one frozen harness runs in a single pass. Across benchmarks on memory, skill evolution, continual task streams, and domain reasoning, GUARD consistently improves performance without catastrophic forgetting or memory pollution while substantially cutting deployment token usage.
  </details>

- **[VeriMath: Human-in-the-Loop Generation and Verification of Curriculum-Aligned Mathematics Assessment Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23456)**  `arXiv:2611.23456`  
  _Quy P. Nguyen, Duong Q. Nguyen, Dinh P. T. Nguyen, Truong-Giang T. Le, Truong A. Bui, Vi H. Vo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic generation of mathematics assessment items is a growing area in educational artificial intelligence, yet classroom deployment remains challenging. Generated questions must satisfy multiple constraints: mathematical correctness, curriculum alignment, appropriate cognitive difficulty, and educator reviewability. While prior work has advanced text generation, reliable assessment authoring requires granular control over localized examination formats and expert educational judgment. To address this challenge, we present VeriMath, a generalized, human-in-the-loop framework for generating rigorous, curriculum-constrained mathematics assessment drafts. Using annotated reference items, the framework anchors generation to the requested examination section and cognitive reasoning level, then applies sequential automated verification and guided repair before submitting drafts for expert review. We evaluate VeriMath through a national curriculum case study using 4,555 mathematics items from the Vietnamese Grade 12 curriculum and 50 distinct generation requirements covering multiple-choice, true/false, and short-answer formats. Across two model backbones, the framework produces verifier-accepted drafts for 94% (47/50) of requirements per run. Blind expert evaluations confirm high mathematical accuracy and curriculum alignment, with strict teacher-readiness assessments accepting up to 56.8% of generated items. Analysis of rejected drafts reveals persistent domain-agnostic challenges involving formatting, statement validity, and solution-step completeness. These findings demonstrate that VeriMath can bridge generative AI and dependable educational applications by producing highly reviewable assessment drafts within tightly constrained national curriculum frameworks.
  </details>

- **[Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23445)**  `arXiv:2611.23445`  
  _Michael Schroeder_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.
  </details>

- **[RFBench: A Simulator-Grounded Multimodal Benchmark for Passive RF Layout Design and Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23410)**  `arXiv:2611.23410`  
  _Jiarui Cui, Boxun Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal language models can interpret diagrams and generate structured outputs, their capacity to connect passive radio-frequency (RF) design intent, layout geometry, and electromagnetic behavior remains largely untested. To address this gap, we introduce RFBench, a simulator-grounded dataset and benchmark for passive RF layout design and understanding. The dataset contains 10,000 two-port planar layouts simulated using ADS Momentum, with each layout represented by editable geometric primitives, a raster image, a 16x16 occupancy grid, complex S-parameters at 201 frequency points, a structural category, and an RF response label. Building on these aligned representations, we define seven tasks spanning specification-conditioned layout generation, representation translation, response prediction, simulator-based validation, design diagnosis and improvement, structural understanding, and response classification. Extensive baselines across seven multimodal language models reveal systematic weaknesses in generating structurally valid layouts and predicting electromagnetically faithful responses. We show that RF-specific fine-tuning increases T1a structural validity from 0.0% to 83.2% and the T3 primitive-input within-2-dB rate from 17.7% to 62.8%, demonstrating the value of domain adaptation while leaving considerable room for further progress. RFBench establishes a common testbed for evaluating multimodal RF capabilities and guiding the development of models that connect design intent and layout representations with physical behavior.
  </details>

- **[Training Language Models for Introspective Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23365)**  `arXiv:2611.23365`  
  _Alex Lin Wang, Linus Yifeng Tang, Dami Choi, Neil Chowdhury_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models increasingly exhibit behaviors that are difficult to detect in transcripts, such as reward hacking, deception, and sycophancy. Asking the assistant follow-up questions about its actions is unreliable, since models are generally not trained to report their own misbehavior. As a result, approaches to oversight typically involve querying external models. These face limitations: external transcript monitoring operates only on visible model outputs, and activation decoders lack full context and are prone to confabulation. We introduce introspective oversight, which trains a new role in the model to answer arbitrary questions about the assistant, rewarding answers that are verified against the assistant's actual behavior. We implement this by adding a separate, third-person observer role that answers these queries, which we can train solely for accuracy and separately from the assistant. On five held-out oversight evaluations, an observer trained on Qwen3.6-27B obtains a mean AUROC of 0.907, outperforming honesty training (0.864), transcript monitoring (0.828), and activation oracles (0.796). We further find that training an observer makes the assistant itself easier to monitor, with every baseline method improving on average when applied to the fine-tuned assistant. These results suggest that training models to answer verifiable questions about themselves is a tractable approach to overseeing unintended behavior.
  </details>

- **[MillBill: A Machining Agent with Geometric Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22565)**  `arXiv:2611.22565`  
  _Gennadii Savrasov, Artem Slavin, Soslan Kabisov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Anton Konushin, Dmitrii Zhemchuzhnikov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Turning a CAD model into a machining program requires both process knowledge and geometric precision. Language models can generate such workflows by writing geometry and toolpath code for each part, but this makes numerical execution part of the model's task. We introduce MillBill, an agent that separates machining decisions from their geometric execution and verification. Given a boundary-representation part, stock, and material, a locally hosted, mid-sized open model proposes operations and cutting parameters for indexed three-axis machining. A geometric harness generates toolpaths, simulates material removal, and returns feedback for plan refinement. Setup planning accounts for what the available cutters can physically reach, rather than visibility alone. We evaluate the resulting programs by the fraction of the target surface area within the specified tolerance after simulated machining. With Qwen3.8-27B, MillBill outperforms frontier-model approaches that construct their own geometry and toolpath workflows on the evaluated machining tasks. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CADMAGEDDON: High-Fidelity CAD Reconstruction with Local Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22563)**  `arXiv:2611.22563`  
  _Oleg Pavlov, Nikita Gavrilov, Soslan Kabisov, Gennadii Savrasov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Anton Konushin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CAD practitioners often work with proprietary parts that they are unwilling to share with external services. For individuals and small companies, bringing this work in-house also requires computational resources they can afford. We introduce CADMAGEDDON, an open-source agent that reconstructs editable CAD from meshes and can run locally on single-GPU systems and current-generation laptops. The method decomposes this complex task into a chain of simple visual decisions about what to construct and what to correct. Geometric tools support precise construction, allowing the agent to focus on visual reasoning. Using the mid-sized Qwen3.8-27B model, CADMAGEDDON outperforms prior approaches, including systems based on frontier models, in reconstruction quality on all the main benchmarks. CADMAGEDDON integrates with FreeCAD, bringing its reconstructions into practitioners' existing CAD workflows. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Think Once, Act Fast: Cost-Effective Recursive Self-Improvement for Decision Model Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22432)**  `arXiv:2611.22432`  
  _Siqi Lai, Haitao Li, Hao Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast System-1 models enable responsive actions but struggle with complex or unfamiliar tasks. Large language model (LLM)-based System-2 provides strong reasoning capabilities, yet repeated invocation incurs substantial latency and computational costs. To bridge this gap, we propose JevRSI, a System-1/System-2 collaboration framework that transforms LLM reasoning into transferable decision support through recursive harness improvement. A Jev-style System-1 model enables low-latency action selection, while an asynchronous System-2 LLM develops a modular harness. The harness integrates procedures, state memory, and world models to provide decision contexts spanning the past, present, and future. Utility-driven retrieval selects modules based on both contextual relevance and their utility on previous tasks. Environmental feedback guides targeted revisions and enables recursive self-improvement. Experiments across three datasets show that JevRSI consistently improves decision quality, matching or surpassing LLM agents in several settings. It preserves low-latency execution with favorable performance-cost trade-offs. Moreover, System-2 development interventions decrease as the harness evolves, amortizing System-2 costs through continuous improvement. Our project is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HealthCLBench:Evaluating Context Learning in Health Conversations through Controlled Case Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22362)**  `arXiv:2611.22362`  
  _Ting Yang, Zijing Hong, Chengjie Wang, Peng Wei, Chunxiao Guo, Yun Yue, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex health conversations require answers grounded in individual information and currently valid evidence. We evaluate health context learning: acquiring, integrating, and applying relevant context during inference with fixed model parameters to answer health questions and respond appropriately to changes in facts, evidence, or task goals. To our knowledge, Chinese-language medical benchmarks have yet to systematically combine explicit contextual difficulties, controlled case variations, and member-specific medical criteria for open-ended answers. We introduce HealthCLBench, comprising 328 controlled clusters, 1,103 cases, and 4,931 rubric criteria across medical consultation and everyday health management. Members share backgrounds while evidence, patient states, or task goals change; each has its own medical scoring requirements. A 70-person medical team annotates and reviews the simulated cases and their rubrics. Human review of 700 full-context responses from the original evaluation yields 90.5% pooled agreement with the original automated judgments at the rubric-item level. We report full-context and query-only results for seven general-purpose models and Ant Afu. Across the eight systems, scores with full context are 11.83 to 31.76 case points and 10.87 to 38.97 geometric cluster points higher than those with the current question alone. GPT-6 Astra leads both full-context metrics at 75.03 and 60.42, followed by Claude Opus 5.5 and Ant Afu. Cross-condition differences describe score changes under the reported evaluation settings. Response comparisons reveal failures in checking historical information, expressing uncertainty when warranted, and ensuring recommendations respect required conditions. The resource supports model comparison and targeted follow-up evaluation.
  </details>

- **[A Hybrid Exact-Heuristic Solver for the Integrated Passenger and Parcel Transportation Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22043)**  `arXiv:2611.22043`  
  _Dung Bui, Ha Thu Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We describe the solver we submitted to the second round of the SOICT 2026 contest on the Integrated Passenger and Parcel Transportation problem, a selective pickup-and-delivery routing problem with time windows, open routes and heterogeneous taxis. The solver is a single-file, standard-library Python program that combines (i) a constant-time insertion test based on latest-arrival slack, (ii) greedy insertion, local search and an Adaptive Large Neighborhood Search (ALNS) with simulated-annealing acceptance, and (iii) an exact route-selection engine that enumerates, for every taxi, the best feasible route for each request set and then solves the resulting set-packing problem by branch-and-bound strengthened with route-based large neighborhood search, Lagrangian bounds and reduced-cost fixing. The engine is chosen automatically from the instance size, and an independent verifier and a watchdog guarantee that a valid answer is always printed. On the official evaluation (38 scored instances, 5-minute limit) the solver obtained a total benefit of 487,237, finished every run within 277 s and used at most 279 MB; 28 of the 38 instances terminated well before the limit.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21885)**  `arXiv:2611.21885`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Changyi Yang, Andy Mao, Xi Zhu, Chaoran Cheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model is trained once and frozen. Making it better takes another training run, or a prompt, a harness or extra computation at inference that is gone once the task ends.
