  _Chenghao Fan, Zhenyi Lu, Sichen Liu, Chengfeng Gu, Xiaoye Qu, Wei Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables parameter-efficient fine-tuning for Large Language Models (LLMs), its performance often falls short of Full Fine-Tuning (Full FT). Current methods optimize LoRA by initializing with static singular value decomposition (SVD) subsets, leading to suboptimal leveraging of pre-trained knowledge. Another path for improving LoRA is incorporating a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. However, weight misalignment and complex gradient dynamics make it challenging to adopt SVD prior to the LoRA MoE architecture. To mitigate these issues, we propose \underline{G}reat L\underline{o}R\underline{A} Mixture-of-Exper\underline{t} (GOAT), a framework that (1) adaptively integrates relevant priors using an SVD-structured MoE, and (2) aligns optimization with full fine-tuned MoE by deriving a theoretical scaling factor. We demonstrate that proper scaling, without modifying the architecture or training algorithms, boosts LoRA MoE's efficiency and performance. Experiments across 25 datasets, including natural language understanding, commonsense reasoning, image classification, and natural language generation, demonstrate GOAT's state-of-the-art performance, closing the gap with Full FT.
  </details>

- **[Hallucination, Monofacts, and Miscalibration: An Empirical Investigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08666)**  `arXiv:2502.08666`  
  _Miranda Muqing Miao, Michael Kearns_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucinated facts in large language models (LLMs) have recently been shown to obey a statistical lower bound determined by the monofact rate (related to the classical Good-Turing missing mass estimator) minus model miscalibration (Kalai & Vempala, 2024). We present the first empirical investigation of this three-way relationship in classical n-gram models and fine-tuned encoder-decoder Transformers. By generating training data from Pareto distributions with varying shape parameters, we systematically control the monofact rates and establish its positive relationship with hallucination. To bridge theory and practice, we derive an empirical analog of the hallucination bound by replacing the population miscalibration term (Section 2.1) with an empirical bin-wise KL divergence and confirm its practical viability. We then introduce selective upweighting -- a simple yet effective technique that strategically repeats as little as 5% of training examples -- to deliberately inject miscalibration into the model. This intervention reduces hallucination by up to 40%, challenging universal deduplication policies. Our experiments reveal a critical trade-off: selective upweighting maintains pre-injection levels of accuracy while substantially reducing hallucination, whereas standard training gradually improves accuracy but fails to address persistently high hallucination, indicating an inherent tension in optimization objectives.
  </details>

- **[A Survey of Query Optimization in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.17558)**  `arXiv:2412.17558`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query Optimization (QO) has become essential for enhancing Large Language Model (LLM) effectiveness, particularly in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems where query quality directly determines retrieval and response performance. This survey provides a systematic and comprehensive analysis of query optimization techniques with three principal contributions. \textit{First}, we introduce the \textbf{Query Optimization Lifecycle (QOL) Framework}, a five-phase pipeline covering Intent Recognition, Query Transformation, Retrieval Execution, Evidence Integration, and Response Synthesis, providing a unified lens for understanding the optimization process. \textit{Second}, we propose a \textbf{Query Complexity Taxonomy} that classifies queries along two dimensions, namely evidence type (explicit vs.\ implicit) and evidence quantity (single vs.\ multiple), establishing principled mappings between query characteristics and optimization strategies. \textit{Third}, we conduct an in-depth analysis of four atomic operations, namely \textbf{Query Expansion}, \textbf{Query Decomposition}, \textbf{Query Disambiguation}, and \textbf{Query Abstraction}, synthesizing a broad spectrum of representative methods from premier venues. We further examine evaluation methodologies, identify critical gaps in existing benchmarks, and discuss open challenges including process reward models, efficiency optimization, and multi-modal query handling. This survey offers both a structured foundation for research and actionable guidance for practitioners.
  </details>

- **[Diverging Preferences: When do Annotators Disagree and do Models Know?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.14632)**  `arXiv:2410.14632`  
  _Michael JQ Zhang, Zhilin Wang, Jena D. Hwang, Yi Dong, Olivier Delalleau, Yejin Choi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We examine diverging preferences in human-labeled preference datasets. We develop a taxonomy of disagreement sources spanning ten categories across four high-level classes and find that the majority of disagreements are due to factors such as task underspecification or response style. Our findings challenge a standard assumption in reward modeling methods that annotator disagreements can be attributed to simple noise. We then explore how these findings impact two areas of LLM development: reward modeling training and evaluation. In our experiments, we demonstrate how standard reward modeling (e.g., Bradley-Terry) and LLM-as-Judge evaluation methods fail to account for divergence between annotators. These findings highlight challenges in LLM evaluations, which are greatly influenced by divisive features like response style, and in developing pluralistically aligned LLMs. To address these issues, we develop methods for identifying diverging preferences to mitigate their influence in evaluations and during LLM training.
  </details>

- **[Reproduction and Replication of an Adversarial Stylometry Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.07395)**  `arXiv:2208.07395`  
  _Haining Wang, Patrick Juola, Allen Riddell_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Maintaining anonymity in natural language communication remains a challenging task. Even when the number of candidate authors is large, standard authorship attribution techniques that analyze writing style predict the original author with uncomfortably high accuracy. Adversarial stylometry provides a defense against authorship attribution, helping users avoid unwanted deanonymization. This paper reproduces and replicates experiments from a seminal study of defenses against authorship attribution (Brennan et al., 2012). After reproducing the experiment using the original data, we then replicate the experiment by repeating the online field experiment using the procedures described in the original paper. Although we reach the same conclusion as the original paper, our results suggest that the defenses studied may be overstated in their effectiveness. This is largely due to the absence of a control group in the original study. In our replication, we find evidence suggesting that an entirely automatic method, round-trip translation, warrants re-examination because it appears to reduce the effectiveness of established authorship attribution methods.
  </details>

- **[UltraTex: Aligning Physical Knowledge for High-Fidelity PBR Texture Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24941)**  `arXiv:2611.24941`  
  _Jingzhi Bao, Xiaohe Ma, Hong Li, Yuanming Hu, Xiaoguang Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating physically based rendering (PBR) texture maps for a 3D mesh from a single reference image is highly ambiguous, as the observed appearance entangles material identity, surface reflectance, and illumination. While current generative methods produce realistic albedo maps, they still struggle to infer accurate metallic and roughness attributes for relighting. We present UltraTex, a cascaded multi-view framework that moves beyond appearance matching by integrating physical knowledge into multi-view diffusion-based PBR generation. UltraTex first jointly predicts albedo and world-space shading normals to generate more detailed albedo maps, and then predicts metallic-roughness (MR) maps conditioned on the generated albedo, reference image, and material semantic priors. To inject material semantics, a lightweight adapter transforms hidden-layer features of a vision-language model (VLM) into intrinsic material representation tokens, providing part-level knowledge anchors for MR estimation. To model material-light interaction, photometric representation alignment guides intermediate diffusion features toward ground-truth material renderings under controlled lighting. This makes metallic and roughness variations observable through highlight intensity, specular sharpness, and local reflectance contrast, providing stronger guidance for fine-grained material prediction without explicit lighting supervision. Extensive experiments show that UltraTex generates high-quality textures and outperforms existing open-source and commercial methods in visual fidelity, material accuracy, and physical plausibility.
  </details>

- **[Unified Law for Visual Tasks (The Four Laws of Computer Vision)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24917)**  `arXiv:2611.24917`  
  _Sen Fang, Yalin Feng, Yancheng Zhang, Yanxin Zhang, Zonglin Lyu, Hongbin Zhong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a Unified Law for Visual Tasks, which could be further divided into Four Laws of Computer Vision, with the goal of enabling a single framework or method to uniformly train on and perform nearly all visual tasks. Our starting point is an inspiration and hypothesis: if the world information is encoded into image, which we call World Snapshots, then image processing is world processing, and image generation is World Snapshot generation. Furthermore, if all visual and visualization tasks can be reformulated as generation tasks, then a unified method, World Snapshot Models (WSMs), can perform the vast majority of visual tasks, much as large language models have largely unified natural language processing. This paper validates these laws across more than 1,000 trained and evaluated visual tasks (defined by distinct input-output mappings) and presents a unified training framework for future visual-task research and applications.
  </details>

- **[PrismRestore: A Compact Restoration Toolbox for Agentic Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24915)**  `arXiv:2611.24915`  
  _Zhaocheng Yu, Kui Jiang, Jing Cao, Yiang Chen, Xiaocheng Feng, Junjun Jiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. We propose PrismRestore, which constructs a compact bank of callable restoration actions from a single frozen all-in-one model. Our approach builds on task-dependent yet overlapping channel responses in the shared model. An offline task-prior search uses activation and gradient statistics to group channels with similar response profiles, then searches task-specific modulation coefficients over non-representative channels while leaving representative channels unmodulated. The resulting prior bank enables explicit action selection and composition without additional restoration backbones or task-specific adapter modules. A state-conditioned policy learns to compose these actions using Group Relative Policy Optimization. Its objective separately normalizes fidelity and perceptual feedback within groups of trajectories sampled for the same input, and combines terminal evaluation with return-to-go derived from stepwise quality changes. Experiments on synthetic composite-degradation and real-world multi-task benchmarks show improvements over the evaluated all-in-one baselines and restoration quality competitive with multi-model agents. PrismRestore achieves these results with a single restoration backbone and faster inference than the evaluated agentic baselines.
  </details>

- **[ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24904)**  `arXiv:2611.24904`  
  _Shagun Mittal, Raymond A. Yeh, Satish Ukkusuri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.
  </details>

- **[Gaspard: Expressive unified speech and gesture synthesis with continuous autoregressive flow-matching and decoupled classifier-free guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24862)**  `arXiv:2611.24862`  
  _Téo Guichoux, Théodor Lemerle, Catherine Pelachaud, Nicolas Obin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human communication involves highly coordinated multimodal signals such as speech and co-speech gestures. Existing approaches for generating both modalities commonly follow a cascaded pipeline, where speech is generated first and subsequently used to drive gesture generation; speech generation is therefore independent of gestures. We introduce Gaspard, a unified speech--gesture generation model based on continuous autoregressive generation with next-step flow-matching predictions, and trained on Seamless Interaction, a large-scale multimodal dataset. The heterogeneous temporal properties of speech and gesture motivate modality-specific prediction heads and decoupled classifier-free guidance over the two generation streams. This guidance rebalances the influence of speech and gesture at inference time, improving expressivity while mitigating dynamics collapse in continuous autoregressive gesture generation. Objective and perceptual evaluations show that Gaspard is a strong gesture generation model against unimodal baselines and that it produces highly synchronous speech and gestures compared to a matched cascaded system.
  </details>

- **[ReCoCo: From Patches to Compositions for Visual Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24806)**  `arXiv:2611.24806`  
  _Juan Gutiérrez, Saman Zadtootaghaj, José Luis Blanco-Murillo, Sara Atito_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning has produced powerful visual representations, yet learning strong dense features remains challenging. While consistency between augmented views provides strong image-level supervision, extending this principle to local visual content is less straightforward. Existing approaches often match individual patches across views or identify coherent regions, introducing uncertainties in correspondence estimation and assumptions about how visual content should be grouped. We introduce ReCoCo (Regional Composition Consistency), a principled approach that extends cross-view consistency to the regional level without requiring such correspondences or semantic grouping. ReCoCo represents the content shared between augmented views through compositions of their patch-level predictions over learned visual prototypes. By aligning these compositions, the model learns from regions containing multiple visual concepts without requiring them to be separated beforehand. ReCoCo integrates naturally with teacher-student self-distillation and introduces no additional learned parameters. Extensive experiments demonstrate substantial improvements in frozen-feature segmentation, clustering, and regional retrieval while maintaining competitive image-level recognition, alleviating the trade-off between dense and global representation quality.
  </details>

- **[Edge-Efficient Joint RAW Image Compression and Restoration for Smart Wearable Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24805)**  `arXiv:2611.24805`  
  _Yunong Sun, Sicheng Chen, Fei Xia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Smart wearable devices, such as augmented reality glasses, increasingly rely on cameras to understand and interact with their surroundings. However, their compact sensors often capture noisy RAW images, while limited compute and power make high-quality on-device restoration difficult. This creates an emerging need to reduce both local computation and transmission cost without sacrificing image quality. Two common solutions are to offload restoration or to compress the raw data before transmission. The first makes RAW transmission costly; the second reduces the payload but can spend bitrate preserving sensor noise. We address this fundamental mismatch with an asymmetric framework for joint RAW compression and restoration that directly optimizes the transmitted representation for clean image reconstruction. A lightweight on-device encoder compresses noisy RAW measurements into a bitstream, while a higher-capacity backend decoder jointly denoises and demosaics the encoded representation. We use knowledge distillation to train a smaller encoder for tighter compute budgets. Experiments on real and synthetic data show improved rate-restoration performance over strong cascaded compression-restoration pipelines and learned compression baselines. On SIDD, our method improves PSNR by more than 1.5 dB over cascaded baselines at approximately 0.25 bits per pixel. We further validate the method on real 12 MP RAW images captured with commercial smart glasses and measure sender-side latency on both smartphone and smart-glasses hardware. The distilled encoder achieves a 27.6$\times$ NPU inference speedup over on-device NAFNet for 256$\times$256 output patches. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Ultimate3D: Teaching Multimodal LLMs 3D Camera-Object Relations by Large-Scale Visual Instruction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24795)**  `arXiv:2611.24795`  
  _Liu He, Yizhi Song, Ruiqi Xu, Baris Sarper Tezcan, Xiao Zeng, Albert Y.C. Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with accurately capturing camera-object relations, especially for object orientation, camera viewpoint, and camera shots. This stems from the fact that existing MLLMs are trained on images with limited diverse camera-object relations and corresponding textual descriptions. To address this, we propose a synthetic generation pipeline to create large-scale 3D visual instruction datasets. Our framework takes 3D assets as input and uses rendering and diffusion-based image generation models to create photorealistic images preserving precise camera-object relations. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) are used to generate text prompts for guiding visual instruction tuning and controlling image generation. We create Ultimate3D, a large-scale 3D-grounded 2D visual instruction dataset. Ultimate3D consists of 240K VQAs with precise camera-object annotations, and corresponding benchmark. MLLMs fine-tuned on our proposed dataset outperform commercial models by a large margin, achieving an average accuracy improvement of 29.7% on camera-object relation recognition tasks. Our code, dataset, and benchmark will contribute to broad MLLM applications.
  </details>

- **[Training-free motion statistics match self-supervised behavior encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24792)**  `arXiv:2611.24792`  
  _Ziwen Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised encoders for animal behavior are often trained on, or fed, hand-built movement features. We ask whether they beat those ingredients when compared fairly. A training-free representation, made of generic per-frame measurements summarized over fixed time windows and reduced with PCA, is compared with retrained and released encoders (BAMS, hBehaveMAE) under a preregistered protocol: frozen representations of equal size and one shared classifier. On CalMS21 it reaches 85.8 macro F1 against 82.4 for the best encoder, ties with four labelled videos, and leads when the annotator changes or new behaviors must be learned from a few videos; the reading holds for encoders retrained with three seeds. On the MABe22 mouse triplets it scores 83.7 against 80.3 for the released hBehaveMAE (gap +3.4, 95% interval +2.1 to +4.7). On a second species, the MABe22 fly groups, the same recipe scores 51.6 mean F1 over 50 tasks against 48.2 for BAMS, the best published method. On Shot7M2, the synthetic basketball benchmark built by hBehaveMAE's authors, it scores 68.8 against 64.7 for hBehaveMAE, the best method in that paper, though hBehaveMAE remains better on the shortest movements. Short time windows carry most of the result. Raw keypoints in each body's own frame, summarized over the same windows, already match or beat the encoders on both mouse datasets and on Shot7M2; on the fly groups they fall short, and the measurements add 5.3 points. On these benchmarks, current behavior encoders have yet to beat the hand-built features they learn from, and such baselines should be reported alongside them.
  </details>

- **[Read Together, Answer Apart: How Earlier Questions Help Vision-Language Models Look](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24785)**  `arXiv:2611.24785`  
  _Xinyi Guo, Kaile Huang, Xitong Ling, Mingyi He, Chende Zheng, Jianyu Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are often asked several questions about the same image in a single prompt (co-asking), yet existing evaluations report conflicting accuracy gains and hallucinations at the prompt level without tracking how individual answers change. By pairing each question's single-question and co-asked answers across 22 open-weight VLMs (1B-35B), we find that erratic net shifts reflect two opposing effects: on Densely Captioned Images (DCI), every model repairs 15.4%-29.8% of its single-question errors under co-asking, while the share of correct answers turned wrong falls from 30.8% on weaker models to 4.7% on stronger ones. Across all 14 models supporting custom attention masks, we show through attention readouts and layer-window interventions that earlier same-image questions read visual information into their question tokens in the early-to-middle layers during prefill, helping later questions concentrate image attention onto their queried targets. This visual help accumulates across the scene even across non-overlapping regions, whereas blocking earlier questions from attending to the image lowers both target localization and accuracy in all 14 models. While cross-question help is established during shared question reading, part of the co-asking damage enters later during answer decoding when the decoder attends to other question stems. We therefore introduce Read Together, Answer Apart, a training-free decoding mask that keeps shared prefill intact and isolates each answer from other stems, improving co-asking accuracy on 12 of 14 models on DCI (up to +4.71 percentage points, closing 70% of the pooled gap to asking alone) and on 10, 8, and 5 of the 14 models on WebUI, FinTabNet, and GQA (up to +11.4, +5.3, and +1.1 points).
  </details>

- **[A$^3$Bench: Compositional Reasoning over Aggressive Interactions in Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24762)**  `arXiv:2611.24762`  
  _Austin C Baker, Raiyaan Abdullah, Daniel Zeev Yoffe, Shruti Vyas, Yogesh S Rawat_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on general video understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to reason about participant roles in aggressive interactions remains largely unexplored. Existing violence and crime datasets primarily evaluate whether aggression occurs, without testing whether models can identify who performed the action, who received it, and how participants are related within the scene. To address this gap, we introduce A$^3$Bench, a benchmark to study aggressive action and association in videos. The benchmark evaluates whether models can jointly recognize aggressive actions, distinguish aggressors from victims and bystanders, and correctly bind actions to participants under challenging distractor settings. We benchmark 17 multimodal models and find that they struggle substantially on fine-grained aggressive-scene understanding. Performance degrades consistently as reasoning complexity increases, with models frequently confusing aggressors with victims or bystanders, revealing a systematic failure in role-action association rather than coarse aggression recognition alone. We further observe that increasing model scale or generic reasoning models experience limited improvement. Finally, we explore a simple role-graph prompting strategy that encourages structured reasoning over participant interactions, leading to improvements in compositional reasoning. Our findings highlight that reliable aggressive-scene understanding remains a major challenge for current MLLMs and motivate future research on role-aware video reasoning beyond coarse violence detection. Code and dataset:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jeveration: System One Models Now Generate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24738)**  `arXiv:2611.24738`  
  _Yuhe Liu, Qi Li, Shihua Zhang, Xinchao Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One models such as Jev return decisions through fast, parallel prediction. In this paper, we introduce Jeveration, showing that the same decision paradigm can also generate high-quality images in a single forward pass. Jeveration builds on a frozen vision-language model, repurposing its visual-semantic knowledge for holistic image generation. The key challenge is to maintain global consistency. A single prompt admits many valid images, yet parallel predictions must converge on a coherent whole. We therefore condition all positions on a shared random state to coordinate parallel predictions, so that each draw yields a different but coherent image. Since the model tends to ignore this state under direct image supervision, we first learn a distribution over discrete image codes to guide single-pass generation. Beyond text-to-image generation, we further unify image-to-image generation and dense prediction within the same architecture, so that one checkpoint supports all three tasks, each in one forward pass. Experiments show that Jeveration generates a 512x512 image in 78 ms, up to 122x faster than existing unified models, while maintaining competitive image quality and dense prediction performance.
  </details>

- **[HandWorldBench: A Unified Benchmark for Hand-Centric World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24731)**  `arXiv:2611.24731`  
  _Zhaolu Wang, Jiacheng Xu, Lihong Wang, Shuqing Wang, Jianjie Fang, Ziyou Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in world models are opening new possibilities for fine-grained embodied tasks, with hand-object interaction offering a particularly demanding setting. Modeling these interactions involves capturing how hand actions change objects and how the resulting states persist over time. Yet systematic evaluation tailored to these hand-centric capabilities remains underdeveloped. To address this gap, we introduce HandWorldBench, a unified benchmark that systematically evaluates hand-centric world models across human hands, simulated dexterous hands, and real dexterous hands along six dimensions: visual quality, hand fidelity, appearance consistency, instruction following, physical consistency, and temporal coherence. To construct the benchmark, we develop a data curation pipeline that extracts hand-object interaction clips and screens them for quality, yielding 20,500 high-quality egocentric clips from over 600 hours of footage. We then manually select 500 clips covering seven task families for evaluation. Benchmarking eleven models under a unified protocol reveals that stronger hand-pose control does not consistently translate into coherent object-state evolution and persistence or robust cross-embodiment performance, with several specialized hand-control models underperforming general-purpose text/image-to-video generators on dexterous-hand tasks.
  </details>

- **[Generalization of Text Line Segmentation for HTR in Historical Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24726)**  `arXiv:2611.24726`  
  _Gayan H. Pathirage, Stephan M. Unter, Simon Corbillé, Elisa H. Barney Smith_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper we investigate the generalization capability of state-of-the-art object detection models (Hi-SAM, YOLO, and RT-DETR) for the automatic segmentation of text lines in historical documents. Generalization is needed because it is not practical for museums and archives to produce large amounts of labeled samples for each new collection. We also evaluate the few-shot setting by fine-tuning the networks with only a small number of training samples per target dataset. To this end, we use nine different datasets that differ significantly in visual layout and language. Additionally, we discuss why mean average precision (mAP), a classic evaluation metric for object detection, is insufficient for assessing the quality of a text line segmentation model. Depending on the dataset, our CRNN models achieve character error rates (CER) ranging from 2.48% to 7.07% per page for the segmented text lines. This is sufficient for reading comprehension and simple text searches. Finally, we quantify the resource and time requirements of training and inference in different use case settings. This forms the basis for more informed decisions about model and hardware choices based on available resources.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Band Galaxy Novel-View Synthesis with Radiative Gaussian Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24723)**  `arXiv:2611.24723`  
  _Yuzhe Wu, Jialin Zhu, Doğa Yılmaz, Daisuke Kawata, He Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a novel view synthesis tool for galaxies based on Gaussian Splatting (GS). Unlike RGB data, where GS has achieved success, galaxy observations consist of multi-band signals from a massive number of bodies, contain complex patterns of cross-band interactions, and capture light propagation in highly heterogeneous media. As a result, the underlying physical process poses great challenges in model representation and optimization, which have not been investigated in existing research. To this end, we propose a physically inspired Gaussian Splatting method, GalaxyGS, which is equipped with physically meaningful components that capture both the energy emission mechanism of different astronomical bodies and the light propagation between them. In the absence of directly comparable baseline methods, we adopt a wide range of possible alternative approaches. Comparisons conclusively demonstrate that GalaxyGS is better in accuracy and physical fidelity across datasets and metrics.
  </details>

- **[Can Omni-modal LLMs Understand Spoken Intentions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24706)**  `arXiv:2611.24706`  
  _Zijie Xin, Tianyi Wang, Daoze Zhang, Jie Yang, Changli Tang, Qijie Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (omni-modal LLMs) can respond to spoken requests directly through voice and video calls. Such requests rely on the scene shared by the user and the assistant, so the same utterance may call for different help or for no response. Most audio-visual benchmarks provide questions as separate text input, and interactive benchmarks mostly score a response as a whole. It thus remains unclear whether a model recognizes when the user is asking for help, and whether its reply meets the user's need once the request is recognized. We propose O-IN-Bench to evaluate how well omni-modal LLMs understand spoken intentions. A model watches a complete video with native speech and outputs one text response, or [SILENCE] when no response is needed. O-IN-Bench contains 1,000 Chinese and English samples in five categories and 16 subcategories. Contrastive pairs combine the same request with different scene conditions to test whether the response changes with the context. A sample-specific rubric judges language, intent, and content separately, and O-IN-Engine verifies each rubric against the video. Across 30 omni-modal model configurations, the best overall score is 59.6, achieved by Gemini3.7-Flash. Gemini3.7-Flash passes 74.4% of the intent judgments but only 42.6% of the content judgments. When the speech is a conversation between peers rather than a request to the assistant, even strong models often keep responding. When a reply must satisfy several people, the best score is only 25.9. These results indicate that recognizing a request and meeting its requirements are separate abilities, and O-IN-Bench measures each of them.
  </details>

- **[Improving Zero-shot 3D Semantic Mapping in Offroad with Geometric Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24696)**  `arXiv:2611.24696`  
  _Eerik Alamikkotervo, Risto Ojala_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic 3D mapping is a powerful representation that enables platform agnostic mobile robot navigation in challenging offroad conditions. Current vision foundation models can accurately predict semantics zero-shot on structured scenes but performance is compromised in natural environments. On the other hand, 3D point clouds encode valuable information for discriminating semantics in scenarios where vision foundation models fail. In this work, we propose fitting a sphericity-elevation distribution for each semantic class to act as geometric anchor. Only the points with the most confident vision foundation model predictions are considered when fitting to produce high quality geometric prior distributions. The geometric prior is fused with the vision foundation model prediction to produce the final semantic mapping. The proposed prior improves SAM3 baseline by +6.3 in mIoU and +8.9 in accuracy on the RELLIS-3D dataset, and by +1.7 in mIoU and +2.1 in accuracy on the WildScenes dataset. The source code will be made public upon paper acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Species Splits: A Challenging Fine-Grained Benchmark for Category Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24672)**  `arXiv:2611.24672`  
  _Christian Lange, Joakim Bruslund Haurum, Bingchen Zhao, Oisin Mac Aodha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Widely used category discovery benchmarks have become increasingly saturated and often fail to reflect real-world discovery settings, making long-term evaluation of new methods difficult. We introduce PS-SPLIT, a large-scale fine-grained benchmark for category discovery, motivated by taxonomic splitting: the real-world process by which what was once considered a single species is found to comprise multiple distinct ones. PS-SPLIT contains 79K globally distributed, high-quality, community-collected bird images spanning 771 species, derived from 257 recent real expert-verified taxonomic splits, with associated geographic metadata and full taxonomic hierarchy. The visual differences between categories arising from real taxonomic splits can be very subtle, often requiring significant human expertise to tell apart, making PS-SPLIT a challenging benchmark which we hope will assist in advancing fine-grained category discovery research. Based on taxonomic splitting, we introduce the task of discovery by category splitting, where the goal is to determine which known categories should be subdivided into new finer-grained subcategories. We adapt four generalized category discovery methods to this setting, evaluate existing methods, and show that there is substantial room for future progress.
  </details>

- **[AggCLIP: Aggregating CLIP for Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24643)**  `arXiv:2611.24643`  
  _Zhi Li, Hanyu Wang, Fei Shen, Zhengtao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-Shot Multimodal Anomaly Detection (ZSMAD) aims to identify both appearance and geometric defects in novel categories without relying on target-specific training data, making it especially valuable in real-world scenarios constrained by data scarcity, privacy concerns, or high annotation costs. While recent approaches successfully adapt vision-language models like CLIP to achieve this open-vocabulary generalization, they still face significant challenges: (i) severe geometric information loss during 3D-to-2D point cloud rendering; (ii) spatially noisy 2D feature maps produced by raw CLIP patch embeddings, compounded by shallow late-stage modality fusion; and (iii) severe cross-modal semantic misalignment due to decoupled processing streams. To address these limitations, we propose AggCLIP, a novel framework that adapts frozen CLIP representations for ZSMAD by explicitly incorporating local geometric structure and visual spatial context. Specifically, to accurately localize fine-grained defects, we design a Geometry-Aware Spatial Aggregation (GSA) module that refines back-projected 3D point features via density-adaptive neighborhood weighting, alongside a Context-Aware Spatial Aggregation (CSA) module that smooths multi-scale 2D patch embeddings using a lightweight trainable adapter. Furthermore, to prevent semantic drift between modalities, we introduce a Dual-Stream Prompt Contrastive (DSPC) loss, which aligns the abstract concepts of normality and abnormality across the point cloud and RGB streams. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed AggCLIP framework achieves state-of-the-art performance on the MVTec 3D-AD and Eyecandies benchmarks under both one-vs-rest and cross-dataset zero-shot settings, notably surpassing the strongest competitive methods by up to 2.9% in pixel-level AUPRO.
  </details>

- **[H3Remover: Reinforcing Video Object Removal without Paired Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24633)**  `arXiv:2611.24633`  
  _Kangjie Chen, Xiangyu Li, Jinhao Deng, Dongbin Zhang, Chaoda Zheng, Shijia Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained video editors possess rich removal and completion priors, yet may retain targets or alter unrelated content. We present H3Remover, which reinforces MiniMax-H3 Base Ref2VA for video object removal without paired object-free training targets. To discourage superficial edits such as recoloring, a target-removal reward favors calibrated depth changes consistent with revealing farther content. To limit unintended scene changes, a background-geometry reward encourages preservation of relative depth ordering. To preserve background appearance without retaining unwanted shadows or reflections, an appearance reward constrains RGB changes in source-derived protected regions while exempting targets and estimated effects. Frozen HPSv3 complements these constraints with perceptual-quality feedback for synthesized content. Evaluation regions remain fixed across candidates, and DiffusionART optimizes LoRA adapters with the backbone frozen. Experiments show substantial reconstruction gains over original H3, with qualitative comparisons demonstrating more effective object removal and better preservation of surrounding scene content.
  </details>

- **[EgoJev: Finding Evidence in Egocentric Video by Reading VLM Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24616)**  `arXiv:2611.24616`  
  _Chunggi Lee, Hanspeter Pfister_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Questions about long egocentric video often depend on one brief moment, such as the second a hand puts an object down. A vision-language model (VLM) that sees a few evenly spaced frames usually misses it. Training-free methods rank frames with a separate image-text model or ask the VLM to write a time. In a controlled test, the VLM itself locates the moment better when it chooses among labeled frames, and we read this choice from its output distribution. With the same candidates, this choice lands on the evidence more often than image-text scores or a written time span, and it compares all candidates in one decision. Based on this finding, we propose EgoJev, a training-free frame selector for wearable assistants. EgoJev reads where the evidence is from labeled thumbnails and looks again at the windows it points to. It places its few answer frames at the quantiles of the resulting distribution. One frozen VLM selects, answers and returns the evidence time, with no second model. A wearable device can encode its coarse thumbnails while it records. With Qwen3.5-4B and 8 answer frames, EgoJev raises QAEgo4D accuracy from 51.2% with uniform sampling to 62.0%, and it outperforms recent training-free frame selectors on QAEgo4D and MyEgo. A single-level version runs on a Mac and an iPhone.
  </details>

- **[DAL: Dynamic Angular Loss for Imbalanced Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24597)**  `arXiv:2611.24597`  
  _Salman Mohammad, Furkan Kasım, Manuel Günther_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class imbalance remains a major obstacle in medical image classification, where rare but clinically important classes are often overshadowed by majority categories. Although current strategies for reducing bias, including angle-based softmax (ABS) losses, offer strong discriminative features, their behavior in settings with a small number of severely imbalanced classes remains insufficiently understood. Previous approaches that estimate the intrinsic parameters of ABS rely on the assumption of many classes, which does not hold in medical settings. We derive data-driven upper and lower bounds for the scale parameter and an upper bound for the angular margin using negative-angle statistics, and use these to drive a batch-adaptive angular margin (BAAM), which we combine with dynamically-weighted cross entropy (DWCE) to define our dynamic angular loss (DAL). Across four highly imbalanced medical image classification benchmarks spanning dermatology, ophthalmology, and gastrointestinal endoscopy, our method consistently achieves superior balanced accuracy and the lowest coefficient of variation of per-class recall. Although this entails a small performance reduction on majority classes, the gains on minority categories are substantial, yielding a reliable and equitable overall classifier. Our source code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[BaseRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement of a Base Vision-Language Model via Training-Free Harness Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24578)**  `arXiv:2611.24578`  
  _Mingda Zhang, Yuanhao Dong, Qiang Huang, Rui Qian, Yanjin Li, Zhenyu Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is moving from the harness of prompts and tools around a base model toward the model itself. However, existing RSI still faces key challenges, including harness-bound gains that stay outside the base model, black-box supervision that reads only the model's outputs, and imprecise edits whose site and strength are fixed in advance. To address these challenges, we propose BaseRSI, a new paradigm of Recursive Harness Internalization: the base model improves its harness, then writes what the harness adds into its own weights through training-free edits, round after round on its own responses. To support this paradigm, we introduce ReGap, a mechanism that supervises every transformer block with the high-dimensional activation difference that the harness makes at that block alone, and updates the block's weights along this signal in a low-dimensional subspace. Building on this mechanism, we further propose ReKey, an algorithm that writes this signal by regression into a few key-value neurons of every block, and measures the site and strength of each edit on the model's own responses. Moreover, the base model fills every role of this recursion with forward passes alone, and its edits, written at different sites, compose in one checkpoint without refitting. Experimental results on ten visual reasoning benchmarks show that BaseRSI consistently improves a base vision-language model both in distribution and on held-out benchmarks. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Appearance: Evidence-Guided Programmatic 3D Scene Reconstruction with Agentic Lighting Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24565)**  `arXiv:2611.24565`  
  _Zeting Luo, Yifan Li, Shuai Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-to-code methods for 3D scene reconstruction produce executable code that generates editable digital content and supports visual simulation. However, existing methods primarily rely on appearance similarity, which provides limited guidance for distinguishing errors in geometry, materials, and illumination. Light shapes scene appearance through its interactions with geometry and materials, influencing shading, cast shadows, and reflections. We therefore present LightHarness, a VLM-based agentic framework that incorporates explicit lighting reasoning into iterative scene reconstruction. To be specific, we leverage complementary geometric, intrinsic, and illumination cues to jointly refine scene structure and appearance, and propose Lighting Reasoning Memory to maintain light-source hypotheses, which supports evidence, and revision history throughout iterations. Together, they support reasoning about partially visible and unseen sources, with evidence-guided verification directing subsequent revisions. Our framework reconstructs explicit scenes through coherent reasoning about geometry, materials, and illumination without additional training or differentiable rendering. Extensive experiments demonstrate superior image-to-code reconstruction accuracy under challenging shadows and complex illumination, further supporting downstream scene editing, object insertion, and versatile relighting.
  </details>

- **[Fuse Before Reconstruct: A Novel Reconstruction Paradigm for Multimodal Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24541)**  `arXiv:2611.24541`  
  _Zhi Li, Hanyu Wang, Qiyu Chen, Fei Shen, Zhengtao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial anomaly detection plays a pivotal role in precision manufacturing, where joint analysis of multimodal data emerges as a critical approach to enhance detection accuracy. Feature reconstruction, a dominant paradigm for such joint analysis, is primarily implemented through intra-modal and cross-modal methods, both of which are plagued by significant limitations. Intra-modal reconstruction neglects complementary cross-modal information, while its cross-modal counterpart is prone to projection errors and ambiguous one-to-many mappings (e.g., same textures mapping to multiple geometries), leading to detection failures. To address these limitations, we propose a novel feature reconstruction paradigm, Fuse Before Reconstruct (FBR), which first fuses multimodal features and then performs reconstruction for anomaly detection. Firstly, we propose a bidirectional cross-modal attention mechanism to achieve deep feature complementarity between image and point cloud features, mitigating projection bias through channel alignment and dynamic spatial prior integration. Besides, a learnable pixel-wise gated fusion strategy is adopted to preserve multi-modal structural consistency while optimizing local feature combination. Finally, dual-path collaborative reconstruction is implemented to recover original modal features from the fused representations, where cross-modal consistency constraints effectively eliminate mapping ambiguities. Extensive experiments show that FBR achieves 98.3% I-AUROC on MVTec 3D-AD and 92.6% I-AUROC on Eyecandies, surpassing previous state-of-the-art methods by +2.9% and +4.1%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[SCRiD: Spatial and Contextual References for Generalizable AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24524)**  `arXiv:2611.24524`  
  _Hao Tan, Zekai Zuo, Boyang Chen, Jinghui Qin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizable AI-generated video detection remains challenging because natural motion and content variations can resemble synthesis artifacts, making isolated video discrepancies unreliable indicators of authenticity. To address this challenge, we propose Spatial and Contextual Reference-informed Detection (SCRiD), a framework that interprets video variations through complementary spatial and contextual references. Specifically, we introduce Matched-Coordinate Residual Response (MCR) to characterize temporal residual associations under motion and coordinate references with matched subpixel sampling conditions. We further develop Real-Conditioned Score Adaptation (RCSA), which learns a content-conditioned denoising reference from authentic videos and probes its responses across multiple noise scales and quality views to guide feature adaptation. To integrate these complementary sources of evidence, we design Reversible Evidence Exchange (REE) to enable information exchange through reversible symplectic updates. We train SCRiD using synthetic videos from only 4 generators and evaluate it across 88 generator or task subsets spanning 6 benchmarks. Our method achieves average ACC and AP of 72.72% and 74.23%, respectively, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.24 and 0.81 percentagethis http URLand robustness experiments further validate the effectiveness of the proposed components and the stability of SCRiD under common post-processing operations.
  </details>

- **[RAID: Representation Autoencoder for Intrinsic Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24521)**  `arXiv:2611.24521`  
  _Claudia Cuttano, Tamy Boubekeur, Elena Garces, Giuseppe Vecchio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-image intrinsic decomposition is an inherently ill-posed problem, requiring the disentanglement of strongly coupled appearance factors from a single RGB observation. Recent methods address this ambiguity with diffusion priors, at the cost of large, iterative, and often stochastic models. We show that accurate decomposition does not require a large generative model. Our key idea is to decouple reconstruction from decomposition on top of a strong pretrained representation. First, an $X \rightarrow X$ teacher learns to reconstruct each intrinsic map from DINOv3 features using a shared decoder, without paired RGB-to-intrinsic supervision. Then, a DINOv3-based student predicts the corresponding teacher representations from RGB through lightweight, modality-specific projections, enabling training on partially annotated datasets and predicting all intrinsics in a single forward pass. \textbf{RAID}, predicts intrinsic properties in the structured latent space, enabling a compact 165M-parameter model, 100x faster than diffusion-based methods. Despite its compact size, it achieves competitive or superior decomposition quality, with improved robustness to input resolution. Additionally, our learned projections yield interpretable, modality-specific feature spaces.
  </details>

- **[Comparing Each Generation with Independent Siblings Gives Video World Model Post-Training a Proper Objective for Outcome Frequencies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24512)**  `arXiv:2611.24512`  
  _Xin Li, Yiwen Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models are used as simulators, so when a first frame leaves several physical outcomes open, repeated generations should produce each outcome as often as it occurs in the data. Physics post-training scores every generation against the single recorded future of its history, which makes single videos more faithful, but the expected score is linear in the model distribution and is maximized by always producing the most likely outcome. The log-likelihood of the recorded outcome is maximized by the data distribution, and its gradient weights agreement with the record by the inverse of how often the model produces the generated outcome, a frequency that other generations of the same history with independent noise, its siblings, can estimate. To train with this weight from a finite group, we propose SibPO, which performs Sibling-paired Policy Optimization: siblings estimate a truncated inverse-frequency weight for agreement with the record, and a second term subtracts the same weight times the agreement with the siblings, which makes the expected objective a strictly proper divergence over outcome cells at every group size. On PisaBench, SibPO lowers the KS distance to simulated landing times by 9.1-10.2% relative to the strongest method outside its reward family on three of four video models without degrading single videos, and it lowers the error of estimated robot policy success rates by 13%.
  </details>

- **[Every Query Routes Itself: Accelerating Video Diffusion Transformers with Per-Query Routed Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24479)**  `arXiv:2611.24479`  
  _Zhen Huang, Shuwei Li, Ruizhe Yao, Xinrui Chen, Danyi Liu, Haoyuan Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-attention over long token sequences dominates the inference cost of video diffusion transformers. Clustering-based sparse attention clusters both queries and keys and lets all queries in a cluster share one selection of key clusters. We show that this sharing limits accuracy: with key clusters and density fixed, attention grows more accurate as the query clusters are refined, and is most accurate when each query selects on its own, which needs no query clustering at all. We therefore propose Per-Query Routed Sparse Attention (PQRS), a training-free method that clusters only keys, lets each query select its own key clusters, and compensates for the unselected ones with their centroids. Per-query selections leave no query tile with a shared key list, so PQRS traverses key clusters instead of queries, buckets queries with identical selections into dense tasks, and merges their partial results in one pass under a fixed softmax offset carried from the previous denoising step. On four text-to-video and image-to-video models from the Wan and HunyuanVideo families, PQRS is the fastest of all the sparse attention methods we evaluate and the closest to the dense output, reaching $28.9$ dB PSNR on Wan2.1-T2V and $35.4$ dB on HunyuanVideo at $1.66$--$2.10\times$ end-to-end speedup.
  </details>

- **[Anchoring with Visual Evidence: Explicit Semantic Anchors for Joint Change Detection and Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24462)**  `arXiv:2611.24462`  
  _Kai Zheng, Hang-Cheng Dong, Puyi Fang, Junjia Pan, Zhenkai Wu, Wei Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint change detection and captioning seeks to localize changes and describe them from bi-temporal remote sensing images. A key challenge is translating dense visual evidence into the semantic conditions that guide description generation. We propose Explicit Semantic Anchors (ESA), a supervised interface that exposes entity presence, temporal appearance variation, and spatial extent. A prediction-guided readout combines visual features and predicted change probabilities, and three factor heads estimate the corresponding anchor activations. These activations guide generation through two complementary routes. The context route maps the three factor activations to three separate memory tokens, one per factor, and appends them to the dense visual memory, while the lexicon route produces an additive vocabulary bias at each decoding step. Dense visual memory remains available for spatial detail and information beyond the explicit factors. Anchor targets are derived from existing images and change annotations, requiring no additional anchor-level language annotation. At inference, all conditions are computed from the input image pair and the model's own predictions. Experiments on LEVIR-MCI and WHU-CDC show strong joint detection and captioning performance, supporting the use of supervised visual attributes as explicit conditions for change description generation.
  </details>

- **[ShoWorld: Shape-Faithful Multi-Hypothesis Occupancy World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24457)**  `arXiv:2611.24457`  
  _Zhiqiang Yan, Guangfa Wang, Yuan Wu, Jian Yang, Gim Hee Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Occupancy world models struggle to forecast moving objects due to motion uncertainty and shape distortion. A single trajectory can average plausible futures into an implausible path, and repeated re-voxelization can erode or inflate object shape even under correct motion. We introduce ShoWorld to address these challenges through separate motion and shape representations. The motion forecaster predicts probabilistic trajectories conditioned on scene layout, nearby agents and the future ego trajectory. Each hypothesis is rendered into occupancy and fused according to its probability to retain alternative futures. The shape component predicts sub-voxel points with occupancy probabilities and offsets from local geometry. These points move rigidly with the object, and voxel occupancy follows the probability that at least one occupied point falls within it. This representation reduces shape distortion under motion. ShoWorld achieves state-of-the-art results on Occ3D-nuScenes. With ground-truth occupancy and ego trajectory, it improves mIoU and IoU by 6.56 and 9.16 points overall and by 6.06 and 5.53 points on dynamic classes.
  </details>

- **[EviScope: Evidence-Conditioned Sampling for Long-Range 3D Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24451)**  `arXiv:2611.24451`  
  _Mingyang Lyu, Yinqian Sun, Gongce Cao, Chujie Tang, Yuhao Zhang, Lu Jia, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-based camera-radar detectors choose where and over which frames each object query samples from the query's position or feature. We test whether radar evidence around the query should also set these choices at the 150 m range of MAN TruckScenes, where recall differs across object-level radar-return counts within every range band. EviScope is a minimal test: the current frame's returns form a non-learned bird's-eye evidence field, which each query reads at its decoded centre to scale its radial sampling extent and to weight eight frames. It adds 5,450 parameters (0.009%) to RaCFormer, with no latency increase in our timing. Our module-free recipe exceeds the published TruckScenes mAP (25.9 over seven runs against 24.1), so we measure the module only against its own baseline at matched seeds. The evidence a query reads rises with its object's return count (Spearman 0.51) but captures zero-return objects only in part, and the learned radius tracks it ($\rho\approx-0.91$ in four runs). Over seven four-epoch pairs the module changes NDS by $+0.31\pm0.36$ and mAP by $-0.27\pm0.22$ (mean $\pm$ SE). Against a geometry-only conditioner with the same parameter count, trained at three matched seeds, it is higher by $+1.25\pm0.72$ NDS and $+0.87\pm0.21$ mAP, on mAP in every pair, and the control's mean NDS is near the baseline's. An exploratory far-range gain failed an internally pre-specified held-out test, and large inference-time substitution penalties also occur without query-local evidence: neither learned allocation nor inference-time dependence substitutes for matched end-to-end comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Retroactive Concept Bottleneck Models for Exemplar-Free Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24445)**  `arXiv:2611.24445`  
  _Yang Ji, Ying Sun, Yuanxin Zhuang, Yuting Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exemplar-free class-incremental learning (EFCIL) requires models to learn new classes sequentially without retaining training samples from previous tasks. Continual concept bottleneck models (CBMs) make their predictions interpretable by identifying visual concepts in an image and using them to score each class. However, these methods typically focus on preserving learned concepts or approximating past classes with generated features, which can leave new concepts underused in old-class predictions. We propose RetroCBM, which retains a small set of vocabulary-independent statistics relating frozen visual features to the embedding space of a pretrained vision-language model. Even after earlier training images have been discarded, we can use these statistics to supervise new concepts. To do so, we develop a retroactive regression rule that yields the same predictor for each new concept as class-balanced ridge regression on all images seen so far. We then reproject the stored statistics to recover the full-history squared classification loss and thus learn how much each new concept should contribute to old-class predictions. Since the classifier may use systematic concept errors to distinguish nearby classes, we further introduce a graph-weighted penalty on the margin shifts caused by those errors. Across six datasets, RetroCBM outperforms state-of-the-art interpretable methods in all class-incremental settings, with an 18.1\% relative gain in mean average incremental accuracy over the strongest exemplar-free baseline.
  </details>

- **[NativeSeg: Native Vision Language Segmentation of 3D Computed Tomography via Spatial Visual Tokens and Verified Local Zoom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24443)**  `arXiv:2611.24443`  
  _Chengqian Ma, Gaole Dai, Zijie Meng, Weixuan Jin, Yijie Du, Yuexiao Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text prompted 3D computed tomography (CT) segmentation requires both full slice context and fine boundary precision across volumes where over $80\%$ of organ prompted slices lack the queried target. Existing medical vision language models (VLMs) are largely non native segmenters: they delegate pixel delineation outside the VLM by compressing reasoning into a 1D [SEG] vector for an external Segment Anything Model (SAM) decoder, quantizing masks into lossy discrete codebooks, or issuing box prompts to stateless external SAM tools without empty slice screening or spatial state memory. We introduce NativeSeg, a native vision language segmentation framework on Low Rank Adaptation (LoRA) adapted Qwen3.8-27B that performs dense mask decoding, volumetric triage, spatial error self diagnosis, and multiround local zoom directly over the VLM's 2D spatial visual tokens without external SAM decoders. NativeSeg decodes confidence maps from upper layer 2D spatial tokens fused with a five slice U-Net pathway, halts at zero zooms on $>91\%$ of standalone evaluations via a native presence gate (<pres>), localizes uncertain boundaries via a 20 cell multiscale error gate (<err>), and accepts zoomed updates only when predicted error decreases. Trained solely on adult CT, NativeSeg improves Round $0$ DSC by $+19.81$ and $+7.04$ points over discrete codebook and 1D [SEG] readouts on the identical backbone, reaches $86.81\%$ adult and $87.04\%$ zero shot pediatric DSC, and safely refines 2D nnU-Net ($89.52\%$ adult; $86.86\%$ pediatric), 3D SegVol ($86.81\%$), and 3D VoxTell ($89.85\%$).
  </details>

- **[Explainable and CPU-Efficient Brain Tumor Classification from MRI Using Two-Phase EfficientNetB3 Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24440)**  `arXiv:2611.24440`  
  _Deepro Das, Saad Kabir Uddin, Mohammad Alauddin, Bornali Sarker, Thasnim Akhter_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Brain tumors are among the most lethal neurological disorders, with over 308,000 new diagnoses annually worldwide. Accurate classification of tumor type from Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans is critical for treatment planning; however, manual radiological interpretation is time-consuming and prone to inter-observer variability. This paper proposes an automated deep learning pipeline for four-class brain MRI classification (glioma, meningioma, no tumor, and pituitary tumor) using EfficientNetB3 transfer learning with a custom classification head. A two-phase training strategy is adopted: Phase 1 trains the head with a frozen backbone (best validation accuracy: 92.77%), and Phase 2 selectively fine-tunes the top 37 layers at a 100x reduced learning rate (best validation accuracy: 94.91%). On an independent balanced test set of 1,600 MRI images, the system achieves 90.62% overall accuracy and a macro-average AUC of 0.9854. Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM) is integrated for class-discriminative visual explainability. The proposed system simultaneously addresses three dimensions absent in prior work: high accuracy, CPU-only computational efficiency, and clinical interpretability.
  </details>

- **[MLIE-CoT: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via Multi-Level Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24433)**  `arXiv:2611.24433`  
  _Baichuan Yang, Dongyuan Xu, Zhao Yang, Yong Liu, Bing Su_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has proven effective in improving the reasoning abilities of large language models by breaking complex tasks into step-by-step solutions. In recent years, researchers have attempted to extend CoT reasoning to visual generative models to improve their ability to handle complex tasks. However, existing CoT methods for text-to-image generation still rely mainly on textual reasoning and therefore cannot fully use and preserve the correct semantic information already present in an image, as even the most detailed language cannot capture all visual information. With advances in image generation, models such as FLUX and BAGEL can generate images conditioned on visual inputs. Recent work has attempted to carry images forward as context in the chain of thought, but this introduces another limitation: unsuitable earlier images can strongly interfere with the model, while repeated whole-image regeneration introduces new hallucinations at each round and causes them to accumulate. To address these issues, we propose MLIE-CoT, a visual chain-of-thought framework based on multi-level image editing. MLIE-CoT learns to select the repair granularity from three levels, preserving correct semantics whenever possible during reasoning while reducing the effect of incorrect content. To improve the ability of multimodal models to route errors of different severity to appropriate repair levels, we construct an image-repair dataset covering different error cases and use it to train a repair-level router. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MLIE-CoT significantly outperforms both its base models and methods using fixed repair strategies, producing clear improvements across multiple benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Let a Small Model Read First: Faster Prefill for Long Interleaved Image-Text Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24431)**  `arXiv:2611.24431`  
  _Xiaolin Lin, Kai-Chiang Wu, Bing Li, Grace Li Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) can answer questions about long interleaved inputs, such as documents of many pages and collections of hundreds of images, but they are slow to start: on inputs of about 100K tokens, the prefill takes most of the wait for the first output token. A common approach prunes the input by scoring its tokens with the target model itself or with a small model of the same family that first writes an answer. However, this scoring takes much of the time that pruning saves. We find that the evidence for a question lies in a few whole units (images or paragraphs), and that a small hybrid model finds them with one attention head of an early layer, even at half image resolution. Based on these findings, we propose SkimPrefill, which lets the small hybrid model read the whole input first, up to that early layer, with images at half resolution. Most layers of the small hybrid model (Qwen3.5-0.8B) run in linear time, so this pass is short. SkimPrefill then ranks every unit with that head and gives the target the top units as an ordinary shorter prompt. One untrained selector serves targets of different model families. Extensive experiments on MMLongBench at 128K tokens, with 19 datasets of five task types, show the effectiveness of SkimPrefill. With 10% of the input tokens kept, SkimPrefill speeds up the prefill of Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-12B and Qwen3-VL-32B by 3.7 times, 11.6 time and 8.2 times while maintaining their average accuracy.
  </details>

- **[PhysResponse: Probing the Physics of Video Generation with Controlled Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24419)**  `arXiv:2611.24419`  
  _Yuhan Li, Shuran Zhang, Haixin Tian, Baonuo Rao, Weipeng Li, Yujia Hou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models are increasingly viewed as world simulators, which requires not only plausible motion but dynamics that respond correctly to specified physical parameters. Existing models, datasets and benchmarks, however, largely target physical plausibility, leaving this physical parameter responsiveness insufficiently explored. We introduce PhysResponse, a benchmark that measures it at two levels: scene-conditioned simulator inversion recovers the effective gravity, friction and initial speed of generated motion, and event evaluation tests outcomes near and across critical transitions. Evaluating ten image-to-video models, we find that even the lowest parameter errors exceed those of physically correct videos by over an order of magnitude, that models rarely reproduce both outcomes of a transition pair, and that the two levels expose complementary failures that visual metrics such as PSNR miss. To supply the missing training signal, we construct 72,000 simulated videos of 20 multi-object interaction types with controlled parameters; fine-tuning on them lowers parameter errors, even on held-out interaction types, and transfers to real videos and an external physics benchmark. Continued training with a numerical encoder further lowers all three errors on held-out types. PhysResponse provides a foundation for world simulators that follow physical conditions, not just appearances.
  </details>

- **[StrideWorld: Benchmarking Scene Rigidity and Action Consistency in Interactive Video World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24408)**  `arXiv:2611.24408`  
  _Chuang Wang, Rui Nie, Jie Song, Junyuan Gao, Jiahe Song, Haitao Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive video world models turn user commands into the exploration of a generated scene, which requires the scene to keep its 3D structure as the camera moves and each action to keep a stable effect. Existing benchmarks mostly evaluate these properties against references fitted to the video under test, either a scene reconstructed from its own frames or a camera trajectory aligned to the command path with a free scale. Such references can absorb gradual scene deformation and changes in pace and route length, so these errors can pass undetected. We therefore introduce StrideWorld, a benchmark whose references are fixed before the motion they judge. It evaluates scene rigidity against the ground-truth geometry of the start view, and action consistency against the stride of each action, the camera motion of one command. The stride is measured on the model's first steps and then used to test the steps that follow, so each model is judged only against its own steps. Its measurements are validated on rendered videos with known camera motion, and its scores agree with human judgments. Across \nvideos{} videos generated by \nmodels{} open models from $140$ start views, all models but one deform their scenes as the camera moves, with offsets up to $7.3$ times those of a rigid rendering along the same camera path. Most models keep a steady pace when a single action is held. Along composed routes, several models often stall, and most completed routes end away from the positions predicted by each model's own strides. Only the two models that scale commands by the estimated scene depth bring most routes close to these positions. Fine-tuning two models on a few rendered clips removes most of their mid-route stalls. Code, data, and a leaderboard are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RAPID: Reconstruction Accelerated by Polarization-Informed Depth for 2D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24404)**  `arXiv:2611.24404`  
  _Daisong Ouyang, Linlin Xia, Liangkun Yan, Song Gu, Shedong Zhao, Xin Dai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient and accurate 3D surface reconstruction from multi-view images remains a fundamental challenge in Gaussian splatting. Many existing methods rely on sparse geometric initialization and iterative densification, resulting in substantial optimization overhead and difficulties in balancing rendering quality and geometric accuracy, particularly in reflective and weakly textured regions. To address these limitations, we propose RAPID, a geometry-first framework that uses multiview polarization cues to recover dense surface priors. We integrate surface normals into depth maps using sparse geometric anchors. These depth maps are then fused across views. Curvature-adaptive sampling and oriented Gaussian initialization then support efficient optimization. Experiments on the PISR dataset demonstrate an approximately \(5\times\) speedup over PhyGaP, which is a state-of-the-art Gaussian-based reconstruction method. RAPID reduces the average optimization time from approximately 21 minutes (PhyGaP) to 4 minutes while maintaining competitive novel-view synthesis quality and mesh reconstruction accuracy. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of RAPID in accelerating Gaussian optimization from polarimetric multi-view inputs.
  </details>

- **[Reliable Tree Recruitment from Scarce Annotations for Forest Inventory Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24401)**  `arXiv:2611.24401`  
  _Luiz Fernando Souza Luz, Pedro J. Soto Vega, Matheus Pinheiro Ferreira, Laura Elena Cué la Rosa, Hudson Franklin Pessoa Veras, Ernandes Macedo da Cunha Neto, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Species-level inventories of individual tree crowns (ITCs) from UAV imagery are limited by annotation cost: in tropical forests, many species are represented by only a handful of field-validated crowns. We propose a reliable tree recruitment framework that expands a small annotated inventory by iteratively self-training a multi-task DeepLabV3 network, combining semantic segmentation with a crown-distance regression head, on RGB UAV orthomosaics. At each iteration, candidate crowns enter the training inventory only if they pass a confidence filter on class probability and distance map and a morphology filter on crown area and solidity, with at most five new crowns per species, keeping recruited samples consistent with the reference inventory. We evaluate the framework at two Brazilian sites: an Amazonian rainforest in Acre (17 species, 355 annotated crowns, 4 cm GSD) and a forest site in the Cerrado of Maranhão (4 species, 51 annotated crowns, 1.8 cm GSD). Over 20 iterations, the number of retained crowns grew from about 500 to 960 in Acre and from about 700 to over 3100 in Maranhão, while crown-level F1 exceeded single- and multi-task DeepLabV3 baselines (53.5% vs. 50.0% and 45.2%; 92.2% vs. 79.7% and 83.6%). The framework offers a low-cost route to expanding species inventories with consumer-grade UAVs and limited fieldwork.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Active Learning Performance with Initial Data Selection Based on Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24384)**  `arXiv:2611.24384`  
  _Mohammad Mirzaee, Mehrshad Fallah Estalkhzir, Mohammad Reza Mohammadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In active object detection, selecting the initial labeled pool is essential, especially in complex datasets with expensive labeling and low resource budget. This study presents an active learning framework designed to optimize the initial training cycle of active learning for the object detection task. Our approach diverges from traditional methods by utilizing feature-based clustering from the YOLO-World model and refining images based on objectness scores rather than relying on uncertainty sampling in the initial cycle. This approach focuses on finding samples with various features and emphasizes training in areas where the foundation model is most confident. Doing so helps the model better understand different types of objects. For subsequent cycles, we employ a consistency-based sampling technique to maintain the robustness of the training process. To evaluate our method, we use two complex datasets called Aquarium and NWPU VHR-10, which are well known for their diverse object sizes and challenging conditions. In the NWPU dataset, our method increases mAP50 by an average of 6.6% in image-based evaluation. Moreover, the image refinement method shows an improvement of approximately 11% in object-based evaluation. The results show improvements in model performance and labeling time, demonstrating the advantages of using foundation models in early cycles of active learning. An implementation of the algorithm is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Where Should a Diffusion Transformer Meet Its Teacher?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24380)**  `arXiv:2611.24380`  
  _Zhuoqi Hu, Shiwei Li, Haozhao Wang, Xiandi Luo, Yihao Ouyang, Wenting Shen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Representation Alignment (REPA) significantly accelerates Diffusion Transformer training by guiding intermediate student features with a pretrained visual teacher, yet existing methods typically attach supervision to a single fixed layer. As student representations evolve, however, we find that the most effective layer for alignment varies across training stages. To track this evolving layer suitability online without costly search, we measure gradient compatibility between the flow-matching and alignment objectives, and propose AdaREPA, an online routing framework that adaptively selects the supervision layer as training progresses. AdaREPA uses a softmax distribution over smoothed layer compatibility scores to sample a layer for alignment at each training step. This probabilistic routing adapts teacher supervision to the student's changing learning needs, with minimal training overhead and no additional inference cost. On ImageNet-256, AdaREPA accelerates convergence and improves generation quality across model sizes, while revealing how the most suitable layers for supervision shift over training.
