To this end, we introduce SAVGE, a large-scale semantically aligned AIGV detection dataset comprising 2,369,822 videos, including 424,115 real videos and 1,945,707 generated videos. SAVGE organizes samples into real-fake semantic groups by using captions paired with real videos as prompts for text-to-video generation, thereby providing both group-level correspondence and textual semantic anchors. Based on SAVGE, we further propose Explicit Disentangling and Semantic-aware Contrasting (EDSC), a model-agnostic training framework for learning artifact-focused representations with reduced semantic interference. EDSC first extracts and aligns visual semantic features with textual representations, and then explicitly suppresses semantic information in the artifact branch while contrasting real and fake samples within each semantic group. By integrating caption-guided semantic supervision with intra-group contrastive learning in a two-phase optimization strategy, EDSC encourages detectors to capture generation-related cues under matched semantic conditions. Extensive experiments on SAVGE and GenVideo demonstrate that EDSC improves most evaluated backbone-metric combinations, enhances robustness under challenging evaluation settings, and strengthens cross-dataset generalization. These results highlight the importance of semantic alignment in AIGV detection and validate explicit semantic-artifact disentanglement as an effective strategy for robust detector learning.
  </details>

- **[STAR++: A Physical-Aware Benchmark and Framework for Astronomical Super-Resolution across Multiple Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24023)**  `arXiv:2611.24023`  
  _Guohang Zhuang, Jiaze Li, Kuo-cheng Wu, Hao Du, Jinyang Huang, Xiang Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Astronomical super-resolution (ASR) aims to reconstruct high-resolution star fields from low-resolution telescope observations across multiple filters for astrophysical analysis. However, developing a physics-aware ASR framework faces two challenges: 1) Data view: no large-scale benchmark supports field-level ASR, especially on unseen filters. 2) Method view: Existing ASR methods often fail to preserve stellar flux across spatial resolutions. Moreover, ASR requires reconstructing high-frequency components, including accurate star locations and profiles, making it more ill-posed than natural-image SR. To tackle these problems, we introduce STAR++, a large-scale benchmark comprising approximately 1.7 million flux-consistent image pairs across 36 filters, derived from Hubble Space Telescope (HST) observations. STAR++ uses a flux-preserving data generation pipeline to ensure photometric consistency across resolutions and provides the Flux Error (FE) metric to evaluate ASR models from a physical perspective. To address method-level challenges, we propose a Flux-Invariant Super-Resolution Plus (FISR++) model that generalizes to unseen filters while accurately reconstructing flux-consistent high-resolution images. Beyond the flux-preserving modules, FISR++ introduces two novel components. Specifically, a Filter-Aware Adapter (FAA) incorporates filter metadata to adapt image representations to varying spectral and optical conditions. In addition, a Spatial Ambiguity Disentangle Loss (SADL) models spatial ambiguity as pixel-shift uncertainty, mitigating the smoothing of learned conditional mean reconstructions caused by this ambiguity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FISR++ consistently outperforms existing ASR methods in reconstruction quality and flux accuracy, while generalizing to unseen filters, highlighting its effectiveness for photometrically faithful ASR.
  </details>

- **[Semi-Supervised GAN-Based Segmentation of CTPA Scans for Pulmonary Emboli Boundary Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24018)**  `arXiv:2611.24018`  
  _Maria Thoma, Michalis Savelonas, Foteini Malli, Dimitris Iakovidis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pulmonary embolism (PE) is a pulmonary vascular disease associated with high morbidity and mortality, where delayed diagnosis can be fatal. While computed tomography pulmonary angiography (CTPA) is the clinical gold standard for PE detection, interpreting these scans manually is time-consuming and error-prone. In this work, we propose a semi-supervised segmentation method, employing generative adversarial networks (GANs) to leverage large pools of unlabeled CTPA scans. The proposed method addresses more effectively the challenges of CTPA image segmentation than previous methods by employing the dice loss and the cross-entropy loss to tackle the highly imbalanced nature of CTPA scans, the feature matching loss to encourage the generator to produce anatomically plausible segmentation maps that are indistinguishable from ground truth, as well as the self-training loss to reinforce learning from highly-confident pseudo-labeled samples. The experimental evaluation on a publicly available dataset shows that the proposed method maintains accurate segmentation, despite a limited number of labeled CTPA scans. with a Dice coefficient of 88.75% and a 95th percentile Hausdorff distance (HD95) of 1.74, outperforming fully supervised baselines.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Contrastive Targets in Radiology Language-Image Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24016)**  `arXiv:2611.24016`  
  _Yunhe Gao, Ashwin Kumar, Jiaming Liu, Chong Wang, Maya Varma, Fuying Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CLIP-style contrastive image-text pretraining is widely used in radiology data, where each image is paired with one report and every other report in the batch is treated as a negative. Under this recipe, two failures appear, neither of which is systematically addressed by existing contrastive learning theory: zero-shot evaluation peaks then degrades while training loss keeps decreasing, and training fails to learn from large batches. We hypothesize the cause is structural rather than radiology-specific: many unpaired reports share clinical findings with a given image, so CLIP's paired-identity target is systematically biased, not merely noisy. To test this, we construct CLEVR-Radiology, a synthetic testbed that preserves only this overlapping-fact structure and reproduces both failures. With evidence that the structure is sufficient to reproduce the failures, we develop a target-misspecification theory that explains both failures from a single deterministic gradient bias amplified by batch size, and use it to motivate a minimal compatibility-based correction, CorLIP: text is treated as positive whenever its clinical assertions agree with the image, with no architecture changes. Beyond benchmark improvements, CorLIP largely removes the post-peak degradation, makes large-batch training succeed where it previously collapsed, substantially amplifies the gains from more data, and is the only configuration that remains stable up to B=2048. The correction transfers from chest X-ray to 3D CT modalities.
  </details>

- **[FlyVQA: Seeing Video Frame Interpolation Quality through Fly Eyes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24012)**  `arXiv:2611.24012`  
  _Baoliang Chen, Lingyu Zhu, Zhiwei Zhong, Shiqi Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ultimate ambition of visual quality assessment is to reproduce the neural computations of human vision and thereby achieve full alignment with human perception. However, the immense complexity of human visual circuitry makes this goal difficult to realize. Recently released fly visual connectomes provide a tractable biological starting point. Despite differences between fly and human vision, the fly's well-characterized motion pathways motivate us to investigate their potential for assessing the perceptual impact of temporal distortions. In this paper, we use a shared, frozen fly visual circuit to perform full-reference quality assessment of frame-interpolated videos, whose artifacts can disrupt temporal consistency. Specifically, compound-eye sampling maps reference and distorted sequences into the circuit, while a connectome-aware readout quantifies discrepancies in cell-type-specific activity and excitatory--inhibitory synaptic dynamics. Reference-guided temporal pooling and a lightweight regression head integrate these differences into a quality score. Experiments across multiple interpolation methods demonstrate better agreement with human ratings than CNN, RNN, and Transformer baselines with comparable parameters, supporting a biologically grounded approach to perceptual quality modeling.
  </details>

- **[HHW-Ego: DSLR-Quality Enhancement for Multi-Source Wearable Ego Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24010)**  `arXiv:2611.24010`  
  _Yunfei Tong, Qianqian Sun, Jixiang Luo, Dell Zhang, Hao Sun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearable ego-centric images are now in high demand for scenarios ranging from daily smart glass usage to embodied intelligence. But the image quality is far behind smart phone due to the lacking paired high-quality reference images and the dedicated enhancement systems. To solve this, a customized degradation pipeline is designed to generate paired samples matching wearable camera images, boosting the upper limit of enhancement performance. Besides a two-stage enhancement framework is further built: first, tuning an efficient model on the paired dataset to enhance real wearable images; second, using hyperspectral data to refine color temperature for better quality. Moreover we present HHW-Ego, a multi-source paired dataset integrating with Hyperspectral, High-dynamic range and Wearable Ego-centric data, which includes 5,000 image groups spanning indoor/outdoor and day/night scenes. On held-out paired data, Stage-1 fine-tuning improves PSNR, SSIM, Lab distance, and LPIPS for Real-ESRGAN and PFT-SR. On real wearable images, no-reference metrics, human preference, gamut analysis, and visual comparisons characterize the effects of Stage 2.
  </details>

- **[Freeforward4d: Annotation-free feed-forward 4d reconstruction of driving scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24007)**  `arXiv:2611.24007`  
  _Luosong Guo, Xiang Yang, Chaoyang Li, Ao Gao, Jiangming Shi, Yanyun Qu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 4D reconstruction enables efficient creation of renderable driving scenes for autonomous driving simulation. However, most existing methods rely heavily on dataset-provided cameras, LiDAR depth, or 3D box annotations, limiting their applicability to unannotated driving videos. Pretrained visual models can predict cameras, depth, and object tracks from RGB alone, but their geometry is mutually inconsistent: multi-view predictions come in short windows with independent scales and frames, while monocular depth is metric only per image. Our key insight is that, once reconciled into a single metric frame, these priors can replace dataset annotations for feed-forward 4D reconstruction. We present \textbf{FreeForward4D}, which calibrates overlapping prediction windows against monocular metric depth and registers them into one frame across time. On this geometry, 2D tracks are associated across cameras in 3D, and objects move through hierarchical motion that combines a shared object velocity with local Gaussian corrections, so that both training and inference use only RGB videos. On Waymo, FreeForward4D achieves 27.66 dB PSNR and 0.836 SSIM while recovering metric-scale cameras and depth without scale alignment. It surpasses STORM, SLARM-F, UFO, DGGT, and StreetForward, which rely on dataset annotations, in full-image PSNR and attains the best RGB fidelity on 8-second windows. RGB-only fine-tuning further adapts the framework to nuScenes and Argoverse 2. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[Choosing Supervised Post-Processing for Frozen Person Detectors: Information, Object Changes, and Complete Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24003)**  `arXiv:2611.24003`  
  _Tianyi Zhou, Zijun Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A frozen detector computes hypotheses that can support better final outputs through supervised post-processing. Choosing a readout requires knowing which available information improves detection and what its processing costs. We compare candidate scores, class distributions, geometry, native-set summaries and adapted neighborhood meta classification under common candidate, supervision and operating-point conditions. On 320 held-out CrowdHuman images, adding native-set context to a 97-dimensional readout yields 275 more matches and 96 more unmatched predictions. On a separate 319-image cohort, Full100 matches 6004 objects,versus 5848 for source-selected raw WBF and 6002 for a MetaDetect-style adaptation. Its gain over raw contains 300 added and 144 lost discoveries. After execution-equivalent optimization, Full100 and the meta-statistics readout both take approximately 95 ms per complete warm request on a fixed 64-image workload. Source-frozen transfer gives positive point results on 223 MOT17Det frames from seven sequences, while the main MIAP result shows no improvement. The study supports using sufficiently informative output readouts while retaining optimized meta classification as a competitive option. It also shows why feature dimension and net detection gain alone are unreliable guides to adoption.
  </details>

- **[ProtoBridge-CLIP: Observation-Masked Finding Prototypes for Medical Image--Report Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24002)**  `arXiv:2611.24002`  
  _Jinrui Wu, Yun Peng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiology reports distinguish affirmed findings, explicit negations, and unmentioned concepts, but conventional global image--report contrastive learning often compresses these different evidence states into a single study-level target. We introduce \model{}, which combines symmetric global image--report alignment with observation-masked finding supervision and a soft relation objective over affirmed report concepts. Text-derived finding prototypes are aligned with local visual tokens, while uncertain and unmentioned concepts are excluded from direct finding labels. We evaluate the framework on three CT benchmarks and five chest-radiograph benchmarks, together with component ablations, paired and clinical retrieval, localization, perturbation robustness, calibration, and per-finding analyses. \model{} achieves macro-AUROC values of 73.6%, 66.5\%, and 68.8% on CT-RATE, RAD-ChestCT, and INSPECT, and 80.2%, 91.4%, 78.5%, 86.5%, and 90.8% on ChestXray14, CheXpert, PadChest, RSNA, and SIIM-ACR, respectively. Relative to the matched global-only controls, the findings and relation objectives yield consistent gains across the evaluated datasets. These results demonstrate the practical value of treating observed and unobserved report concepts differently during medical image--text representation learning.
  </details>

- **[ReSynth: Constructing Synthetic Supervision for Multi-Image Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23996)**  `arXiv:2611.23996`  
  _Yilin Wang, Xin Yu, Xinyi Guo, Xiangxi Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Linjie Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  People often understand the world by comparing different views of it. Vision-language models (VLMs), however, receive these views as separate images and still struggle to relate them. One problem is data. Training on these tasks requires groups of images with a known relationship: the same object from different viewpoints, or the same scene before and after a change. Such examples are hard to collect at scale from existing photo collections. We introduce ReSynth, which turns single-image perception and controllable image generation into cross-image relational supervision. Localized editing produces before-and-after pairs, viewpoint generation produces multiple views of the same instance, and verified scene contents support comparisons of counts and attributes. ReSynth combines Qwen3-VL-8B's captioning, grounding, and counting capabilities with Qwen-Image-2.1's generation and editing capabilities. It verifies single-image facts such as object counts and attributes, then combines these facts to construct multi-image QA. Using GRPO on approximately 440K ReSynth questions, with no real images during training, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B improve by 17.73 and 6.10 average points on BLINK, MUIRBench, and VLM2-Bench, while single-image performance is broadly preserved on Qwen2.5-VL-7B and improves across all four evaluated benchmarks on Qwen3-VL-8B. Notably, this supervision enables the 8B perception model itself to outperform Qwen3-VL-235B on BLINK and MUIRBench.
  </details>

- **[WaveView: The Novel View Is in the Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23994)**  `arXiv:2611.23994`  
  _Kaiwen Wang, Jan-Hendrik Pauls, Yinzhe Shen, Frank Bieder, Christoph Stiller_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative novel view synthesis (NVS) employs a diffusion model to render a static or dynamic scene from a target camera view. Explicit warp methods complete a reprojection of the source with video diffusion models, and implicit methods encode the camera parameters or 3D cues. Both add inference overhead, and the generated layout often drifts from the ground truth. We present WaveView, which injects the warped source into the phase of the initial noise of a pretrained generator. The Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform (DT-CWPT) allows local and anisotropic phase injection. The generator is thus constrained less in disoccluded regions and along the epipolar line. WaveView keeps the architecture and adds negligible overhead. On the DL3DV benchmark, it outperforms all baselines in every overlap bin. In the hardest bin, it gains 0.84 dB PSNR on the covisible region over MetaView, which shares its generator, while running 2.1 times faster. For dynamic NVS, fine-tuning AnyView with the same noise raises its zero-shot PSNR on AnyViewBench from 11.58 to 12.35 dB.
  </details>

- **[SIRAZ: Sparse Inter-Layer Residual Aggregation for Zero-Shot Visual Neural Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23991)**  `arXiv:2611.23991`  
  _Neha, Mudasir Ganaie_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Brain-computer interfaces aim to establish direct communication between neural activity and external systems, making interpretation of brain signals an important problem. In this context, neural visual decoding seeks to infer visual information from neural activity by learning a mapping between brain responses and the corresponding visual stimuli. It investigates how visual information emerges from neural signals and relates to perceptual processing while enabling the reconstruction or retrieval of visual stimuli from neural signals. Our brain primarily follows a hierarchical pathway to process visual information captured by the eye. However, neural representations learned by existing methods differ fundamentally from those produced by the biological visual system. To bridge this gap and mimic the human vision hierarchy, we introduce Sparse Inter-layer Residual Aggregation for Zero-shot (SIRAZ). SIRAZ is a framework that aligns neural visual representations by jointly modeling early visual processes via fovea- and magnocellular-pathway-inspired Difference-of-Gaussians transformations. To align the semantic information with neural information, it extracts multiple intermediate representations of the image at multiple depths and passes them through the Residual Gated Adapter Mechanism. To study the temporal dynamics of neural signals, the Direction-aware Multi-Scale Temporal Attention Encoder is introduced to align the learned neural representation with the final visual representation. The proposed framework achieves 92.5% top-1 and 99.27% top-5 accuracy in the intra-subject setting on the THINGS-EEG dataset, and 50.25% and 75% top-1 and top-5 accuracy in the THINGS-MEG dataset. The results indicate that combining biologically motivated visual representations with the structured modalities of neural dynamics improves cross-modal alignment and zero-shot image retrieval.
  </details>

- **[Agreement Is Not Accuracy: LiDAR Elevation Errors Hidden in LiDAR-Camera Extrinsics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23972)**  `arXiv:2611.23972`  
  _Yeon Kun Lee, Dongseok Yang, Deok-Kyeong Jang, Choongho Chung, Junyoung Lee, Hyeongseok So_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LiDAR and cameras provide complementary measurements for 3D vision, yet agreement between their observations does not establish the accuracy of either sensor's internal geometry. We show that systematic LiDAR elevation biases can be absorbed into the estimated extrinsics. Using synchronized LiDAR intensities and camera-estimated checkerboard poses, we jointly estimate a sensor-specific elevation correction shared across recordings and extrinsics for each recording. Ray-plane intersections locate the checker pattern without using reported ranges, preventing range errors from directly moving the lookup. Under matched intensity and correction models, this lookup gives smaller camera-consistency residuals than measured-point lookup. Across six identically designed rigs using the same sensor models, recording-excluded corrections reduce the mean translation-reference distance from 7.57 to 3.04 mm. On separate structures, they reduce inter-plane angle errors against references from independent dense camera scans and improve image-edge alignment. Rolling the rigs shifts the plane pairs exhibiting the largest angular improvements, as predicted for an elevation-only correction. Within the ray-plane model, calibration-target projections and loop-consistency residuals change little despite these geometric improvements. These findings show why agreement after extrinsic calibration need not establish correct internal LiDAR geometry. Synchronized observations of a familiar checkerboard provide a practical route to estimating and testing elevation corrections without a dedicated large calibration field. We plan to release code and data for reproducing the main results.
  </details>

- **[TOMAS: Topology-Aware Operation Modeling and Adaptive Staging for Egocentric Hand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23970)**  `arXiv:2611.23970`  
  _Guanzhong Sun, Junyi Ma, Yuxuan Wu, Erhang Zhang, Chenyang Xu, Yanzi Miao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting future hand movements from egocentric videos plays an important role in understanding human intentions and hand-object interactions. However, different manipulation intentions can lead to substantially different hand trajectories even when interacting with the same object. Existing methods have not sufficiently explored the intrinsic relationships between articulated hand movements and object semantics to capture such operation-dependent motion patterns. Therefore, we propose TOMAS, a topology-aware operation modeling and adaptive staging framework for egocentric hand forecasting. Specifically, we develop a topology-aware hand-object reasoning mechanism that associates hand skeletal structures and articulation dynamics with object semantics and spatial relationships to jointly infer potential interaction targets and latent manipulation operations. The resulting object-operation representations provide interaction conditions for future hand trajectory prediction, with supervision adapted to the annotations available in each dataset. Furthermore, we introduce an adaptive staging mechanism that organizes future hand movements into ordered latent stages with learnable temporal boundaries. Stage-specific geometric subgoals are then incorporated to guide trajectory refinement according to the underlying manipulation patterns. Experimental results on multiple publicly available benchmarks demonstrate that TOMAS achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-dimensional and multi-target hand motion forecasting.
  </details>

- **[Dual-Manifold Variational Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23964)**  `arXiv:2611.23964`  
  _Hongxu Chen, Chun Wang, Yanghao Wang, Hongxiang Li, Zhen Wang, Zhixiang Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In latent diffusion models, expanding the latent dimension of variational autoencoders (VAEs) is essential to push the upper bound of reconstruction quality. However, without explicit structural constraints, such unregularized expansion easily yields a rugged and ill-conditioned latent manifold, causing generated latents to drift off the manifold during generation (i.e., off-manifold problem). Recent works smooth this manifold by using external foundation models (e.g., DINOv2) as a topological regularizer. However, we argue that tying the latent space to frozen external priors inherently restricts generalization: bound by their pre-training distributions and semantic biases, they may fail to capture faithful visual details on out-of-distribution domains. Observing that low-dimensional latents naturally form a smoother, generation-friendly manifold, we propose dual-manifold VAE (DM-VAE) to leverage internal representations as a topological regularizer. Specifically, DM-VAE pairs a high-dimensional reconstructive branch with a low-dimensional branch that extracts a clean topological skeleton, anchoring the high-dimensional manifold to this smooth subspace. Building upon this framework, we further introduce topological representation alignment (TRA) to supervise intermediate generator features during training, and internal guidance with topological skeleton (IG_TS) to guide structural components while preserving high-frequency details at inference. On ImageNet-1K (256x256), our foundation-model-free DM-VAE achieves a state-of-the-art rFID of 0.12 and a generation FID50K of 1.28 (w/o CFG) and 1.20 (w/CFG), and further reaches 1.11 when orthogonally combined with DINOv2.
  </details>

- **[SEE: Unlocking the Hidden Perception of Frozen VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23962)**  `arXiv:2611.23962`  
  _Alin-Ionut Popa, Tuo Zhang, Dimitrios Dimitriadis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) grasp the overall content of an image but often miss the fine details a question hinges on, such as a small object in a large photo or a number in a dense infographic. Yet even when their answer is wrong, the region they point to often contains the evidence. We introduce Self-Elicited Evidence (SEE), a training-free method that lets a frozen VLM look again where it points. When the initial answer has low confidence, SEE prompts the model to propose regions where the details may appear, answers the question on these enlarged crops, and aggregates the answers with the original prediction through confidence-weighted voting. The model also answers with its own transcript, which helps on text-rich images. SEE needs no detector or OCR engine. Across five backbones, SEE improves every small-object benchmark by 2.3-13.1 points and outperforms five training-free baselines on small objects. With SEE, Qwen3.5-4B surpasses the direct answer of Qwen3.5-9B and matches the 6 times larger Qwen3.5-27B.
  </details>

- **[DORMS: Image Segmentation via Depth-Ordered Region Merge Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23958)**  `arXiv:2611.23958`  
  _Furkan Mumcu, Yasin Yilmaz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce DORMS (Depth-Ordered Region Merging for Segmentation), a purely algorithmic approach to image segmentation from monocular depth maps, requiring no neural networks or learned parameters in the segmentation process. An off-the-shelf monocular depth estimator provides the input depth maps. Our method constructs depth-ordered merge trees through a deterministic union-find sweep, combining component area, depth persistence, and spatial contact constraints to guide region merging. A threshold search determines the segmentation granularity according to a specified target region count, while global depth detrending improves the separation of local geometric structures. Extensive experiments on COCO Panoptic, Cityscapes, and ADE20K demonstrate consistent improvements over classical segmentation baselines and competitive performance against zero-shot neural segmentation models. Notably, our method outperforms all evaluated zero-shot models on Cityscapes, while remaining competitive with several approaches on COCO and ADE20K. These results highlight the potential of purely algorithmic geometric reasoning for image segmentation without learned segmentation models.
  </details>

- **[SimplePro: A Simple Baseline for Proactive Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23954)**  `arXiv:2611.23954`  
  _Yilin Wang, Xiangxi Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Linjie Li, Zhengyuan Yang, Alex Jinpeng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive video understanding requires vision-language models (VLMs) to decide not only what to answer as a video unfolds, but also when newly arrived evidence warrants a response and when to remain silent. Although modern VLMs excel at offline long-video understanding, they struggle in proactive streaming settings, leading prior work to introduce task-specific reinforcement learning or auxiliary triggering modules. We find that explicit reporting rules and structured streaming context substantially improve a frozen VLM's proactive response performance. We exploit this observation to build SIMPLEPRO, a training-free baseline for proactive video understanding. At each decision step, the model combines recent high-resolution frames, bounded historical observations, and prior answer records to determine whether new information warrants a response and, when needed, generate a concise answer. The system then filters consecutive duplicate answers while retaining the raw outputs as context for subsequent decisions. Without any parameter updates or auxiliary triggers, SIMPLEPRO with a 4B backbone consistently outperforms specialized proactive systems across OVO-Bench, StreamingBench-PO, and ProactiveVideoQA. SIMPLEPRO shows overall performance gains as backbone size increases from 2B to 32B. We hope this simple yet effective method will serve as a strong baseline for proactive video understanding, and we will release our code and inference pipeline to support future research.
  </details>

- **[VOL-Agent: Programming Your Film via Video Orchestration Language Agentic System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23950)**  `arXiv:2611.23950`  
  _Hongji Yang, Yucheng Zhou, Lingting Zhu, Shengju Qian, Xinye Li, Huan Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent video foundation models generate high-quality and limited-length clips, but orchestrating them into minute-level, story-coherent films remains an open problem. Existing video agents highly rely on natural-language planning or predefined workflows, which make their production hard to program, customize, and extend. Therefore, we introduce VOL-Agent, a programmable video agent built on a Video Orchestration Language (VOL). With static compilation and verification, followed by runtime dynamic loading, VOL-Agent decouples structural reliability from execution flexibility. Through the skill-driven executor, VOL-Agent dynamically resolves tools and skills from an open-world repository and supports user-defined skills in natural language. Moreover, VOL-Agent is capable of crafting new director skills from learned knowledge and improves itself through feedback to accumulate filmmaking knowledge. To overcome the disconnect between different segments, we apply a structural memory mechanism to record and recall world and entity states, sustaining entity and scene consistency across different shots. Critic feedback supports local revision, while reusable filmmaking knowledge and experiences can be retained for subsequent tasks. Experiments on three benchmarks show that VOL-Agent generates minute-scale videos with strong overall quality and limited cost.
  </details>

- **[How Complete Must an Environment Be to Train a Capable GUI Agent?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23946)**  `arXiv:2611.23946`  
  _Jingxing Wang, Teng Wang, Xingyu Lou, Zihan Guo, Jun Wang, Weiwen Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graphical user interface (GUI) agents are widely trained in executable GUI environments. To lower cost, build environments in batches, and reduce manual effort, each work trades away part of the environment through its construction choices. We describe these choices along three dimensions: reward coverage, the goal-reaching trajectories the verifier rewards; data coverage, the application data the agent is exposed to beyond what the task reads or writes; and feature coverage, the application features beyond what the task uses. Existing works set each dimension on engineering grounds, but they do not measure its effect on training. How complete must an environment be to train a capable agent, and along which dimensions? In MobileGym, we adjust each dimension separately, train Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct, and evaluate every policy in the original full environment. A verifier that rewards only one reference path lowers success by 10.53 points, and removing unneeded records also lowers success. Pruning unneeded features instead raises success from 51.61% to 56.13%, entirely on tasks whose environments it left unchanged. The gain reverses when each episode is weighted equally and vanishes when only episodes on the pruned tasks are balanced. Without pruning, length weighting there reinforces long detour successes that erode skills on other tasks. Following these findings, we build MobileGym-Lite, which keeps reward and data coverage complete and prunes unneeded features. It outperforms Full in the full environment (55.48% versus 51.61% success) with lower construction cost.
  </details>

- **[SIGNTOK: A Text-Aligned Continuous Pose Tokenizer for Sign Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23944)**  `arXiv:2611.23944`  
  _Nabeela Khan, Gaurav Rai, Bowen Wu, Sihan Tan, Benjamin Yen, Takeshi Ashizawa, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-conditioned sign language generation (SLG) depends not only on the generator but also on the space in which signing is generated. Existing approaches generate directly in pose space, in quantized code spaces, or in learned latent spaces trained for reconstruction. We find that these spaces are organized largely by signer identity rather than linguistic content: on CSL-Daily, a test clip's nearest neighbor in raw pose space is the same sentence signed by another person in only 2% of cases. To address this, we propose SIGNTOK, a continuous pose tokenizer whose latent space is shaped by three training-only heads: a kinematics head that predicts motion dynamics, a sentence-level head that aligns signing with the overall text, and a word-level head that aligns short motion windows with content words. Together, these objectives raise the same-sentence cross-signer nearest-neighbor rate to 82%. On three sign language benchmarks, the same generator trained in the SIGNTOK latent instead of pose space raises back-translation BLEU-4 in five of six benchmark-scorer settings at comparable joint accuracy, and word-level alignment gives higher back-translation scores than sentence-level alignment on every benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Angular Conditional Distillation: Overcoming Classifier Geometry Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23941)**  `arXiv:2611.23941`  
  _Xuefei Zhang, Ruoqi Wang, Lei Cui_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classifier outputs provide a fundamental and univer- sally available knowledge interface for knowledge distil- lation (KD). However, standard logit objectives entangle transferable semantic relations with architecture-dependent parameterization artifacts, causing a classifier geometry mismatch that makes direct knowledge transfer unreliable. To address this, we propose Angular Conditional Distilla- tion (ACD). First, the Angular Coordinate Interface (ACI) reconstructs the student classifier using angular coordi- nates, suppressing magnitude and bias effects to provide a geometry-compatible space. Then, Scale-Invariant Condi- tional Distillation (SICD) standardizes outputs and exclu- sively transfers conditional non-target relations, filtering out untransferable absolute confidence. Together, ACI de- fines where to transfer, while SICD defines what to transfer. Extensive experiments show that ACD achieves state-of-the- art performance over existing KD methods. Moreover, as a universal drop-in replacement, it consistently improves existing distillation pipelines while requiring no feature adapters or additional inference-time cost. Code is avail- able atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TRoPE: Topology-aware Relative Positional Encoding for 4D Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23933)**  `arXiv:2611.23933`  
  _Chengzhuo Gui, Zhenglin Zhou, Fan Ma, Zongxin Yang, Yi Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video-to-4D mesh generation has made substantial progress in recovering dynamic 3D geometry and motion from monocular videos. However, commonly used feed-forward approaches remain prone to sticking artifacts, where geodesically distant vertices move together incorrectly when they become spatially close. We attribute this failure to cross-part motion leakage: spatially nearby yet structurally distant vertices may retrieve similar motion information when motion decoding fails to distinguish spatial proximity from mesh connectivity. To address this issue, we introduce TRoPE, a topology-aware relative positional encoding that makes mesh connectivity explicit in the interaction between vertex queries and mesh-supported motion sources. TRoPE derives spectral features from the graph Laplacian and constructs pairwise topological relations from distances between multi-scale diffusion features, using these relations to modulate vertex--anchor attention through rotary phases. We further introduce a vertex-wise tail-risk objective that emphasizes persistently high-error vertices to explicitly suppress sticking artifacts. To better characterize and quantify these failures, we introduce MeshSticking-4D, a dedicated benchmark for this failure mode. Experiments on MeshSticking-4D show that TRoPE suppresses sticking artifacts while improving overall motion accuracy. Compared with the baseline, it reduces the worst-vertex error by 39.4% and lowers the separation failure rate from 49.35% to 34.90%. Additional evaluations on Motion-80 and Motion324-Subset show lower tail errors on diverse objects, while transfer to Mesh4D demonstrates applicability across backbones.
  </details>

- **[ProxySearch: Decoupled Inference-Time Scaling for Diffusion Models via Asymmetric Noise-Rank Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23929)**  `arXiv:2611.23929`  
  _Seungwook Kim, Jongmin Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Noise search at inference time improves text-to-image diffusion but requires many multi-step teacher rollouts per query. Distilled few-step models are now routinely released, yet their potential as proxies inside noise search is uncharacterized. We measure proxy-teacher rank agreement across three distillation regimes and three backbones (DiT, MMDiT, UNet) and find a sharp asymmetry. On our main pool, closed-form image statistics transfer from the proxy to the teacher (Spearman $\rho$ up to 0.80), while learned semantic scorers do not ($\rho \le$ 0.12). Paired null controls trace this rank to the first denoising step for coarse statistics and to distillation for finer ones. We use the asymmetry for noise search under a joint constraint: a semantic reward specifies content, and a numeric target specifies image statistics. In PROXYSEARCH, the proxy filters candidates by statistical match in 1 step, and the teacher returns the survivor with the highest semantic reward. No weight trades one objective against the other. On FLUX-1024, PROXYSEARCH tightens the statistical target by 21-23% at the same number of teacher trajectories, with no detected loss on the optimized reward.
  </details>

- **[SixthView: A Benchmark for Hidden-View Reconstruction and 3D Assembly Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23891)**  `arXiv:2611.23891`  
  _Pooya Khosravi, Jen-Feng Chang, Fnu Pramono_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inferring the unseen side of a 3D assembly requires reasoning about part placement and occlusion. Different assemblies can match the observed views while producing different hidden views, leaving the target ambiguous. We introduce SixthView, a benchmark in which models predict a sixth orthographic view from five observed views and information about the component shapes. The benchmark contains 602 synthetic polycube assemblies with 2--10 parts and requires a dense grid of part labels for the missing view, allowing both exact-view and cell-level evaluation. For each item, we exhaustively verify that all legal assemblies consistent with the exact component geometry and observed label grids yield the same target view. With these inputs, the task tests symbolic assembly and coordinate reasoning. The image-only variants additionally require shape recovery from rendered views, where occlusion can make different 3D shapes look identical. We evaluate three vision-language models under four input conditions. Across 552 items with complete evaluations, exact-grid accuracy with images plus exact text ranges from 33.2% to 85.4%. Adding exact text to separate images improves accuracy by 19.7--63.0 percentage points, whereas adding images to exact text changes it by only $-0.5$ to $+2.1$ points. Separate image panels outperform a merged sheet by 6.7--11.1 points. The results show that performance depends strongly on access to exact symbolic information, with little further improvement from images once the geometry is specified. GitHub:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Additive Composition: Interventional Modeling of State-Dependent Cross-Degradation Responses for Composite Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23889)**  `arXiv:2611.23889`  
  _Zongda Tang, Jie Feng, Wenda Zhao, Mingtao Feng, Yuping Liang, Ronghua Shang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Composite image restoration aims to recover images corrupted by multiple coexisting degradations. Existing methods predominantly decompose composite corruptions into individual factors, relying on these isolated priors to guide restoration. However, coexisting degradations are inherently interdependent: the manifestation of one degradation dynamically shifts with the states of others, inducing non-additive interactions that factor-wise paradigms fail to capture. To bridge this gap, we formulate state-dependent cross-degradation responses and propose PROBE, an interventional framework that explicitly models how coexisting degradations influence one another. Specifically, we first construct unified degradation axes by aligning visual and textual degraded-clean offsets. Along these axes, a calibrated coordinate head estimates current degradation states, while bidirectional feature perturbations are injected to probe cross-coordinate responses under the current states. Contrasting these responses with corresponding intensity-matched single-degradation references quantifies intrinsic non-additive deviations, allowing the resulting response descriptors and state coordinates to jointly guide restoration. For comprehensive evaluation, we introduce SCD-15, a new benchmark covering all 15 nonempty combinations of four degradation factors at three severity levels. Experiments demonstrate that PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance on the CDD-11, AWMM-100K, and SCD-15 composite restoration datasets using a compact model with modest GPU memory requirements.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Understand Indonesian Children's Book Illustrations? Compliance, Safety, and the Cultural Alignment Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23887)**  `arXiv:2611.23887`  
  _Rian Adam Rajagede, Rochana Prih Hastuti, Fitri Hasanah Amhar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Children's book illustrations carry developmental, pedagogical, and cultural responsibilities that are often formally codified in guidelines. As AI systems begin entering children's publishing pipelines, both as evaluators and as generators, a natural question arises: can they reliably meet the structural, safety, and cultural standards required? We investigate this across three criteria: guideline compliance, safety, and cultural alignment, using Indonesia's Book Leveling Guideline as a government-issued evaluation framework with explicit visual criteria for four reading levels. To understand how well VLMs can identify and apply these guideline criteria, we first evaluate them on real published children's book pages and compare their assessments against a human baseline. To then examine whether AI-generated illustrations meet the same standards, we apply the VLM judge to images produced by two generation models and cross-check them against a subset of human illustrator raters. We find that VLMs are reliable on explicit criteria such as compliance and safety, but fall short on cultural alignment, systematically missing content that human raters flag as culturally inappropriate. AI-generated illustrations show the same blind spot: they satisfy structural and safety criteria but fail to capture implicit cultural details that human raters consistently identify.
  </details>

- **[NullTok: empirical-null token selection for training-free long video understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23881)**  `arXiv:2611.23881`  
  _Zhang Ziyue, Juwei Jian, Baiyang Song, Yunshan Zhong, Jun Peng, Yiyi Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding remains a daunting challenge for existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), mainly due to the large number of visual tokens accumulated in long videos. Currently, frame selection and token selection are two main paradigms for addressing this issue, both aiming to select relevant visual information to form a compact input for MLLMs. A natural approach combines these two paradigms by selecting frames before pruning tokens, but noisy frame-level relevance estimates can cause irreversible evidence loss. In this paper, we study this problem from the perspective of assessing the spatiotemporal distribution of query-specific evidence and propose a novel and training-free approach termed NULLTOK. For every patch across all frames, we construct an empirical-null distribution from 64 group-matched irrelevant control queries, capturing the scores expected in the absence of target-specific evidence. Target affinities are calibrated against these same-position references into right-tail Monte Carlo p-values, which are discrete super-uniform under a conditional exchangeability assumption. Calibrated screening and a high-recall semantic guard produce candidate tokens for deterministic visual deduplication and joint spatiotemporal budget allocation. To validate NullTok, we apply it to two representative MLLMs and conduct extensive experiments on a set of video benchmarks. The experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness under a bounded visual token budget. For example, it achieves 65.81% and 68.85% on Video-MME using frozen LLaVA-Video-7B and Qwen3-VL-8B backbones, respectively, at a cap of 64 full-frame equivalents (FFE), without subtitles or audio. These results outperform all evaluated baselines on Video-MME under the same budget for both backbones. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Keep the Image in Mind: A Visual Board for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23872)**  `arXiv:2611.23872`  
  _Haiming Zhu, Zhiwei Chen, Ding Cao, Tianxiang Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-step visual reasoning suffers when early visual misinterpretations persist as incorrect premises in subsequent reasoning. We argue that how visual evidence is represented during reasoning matters as much as how much of it is gathered. Rather than appending observations and inferences to an ever-growing dialogue, we introduce a Visual Board-centered harness that separates visual observations, uncertain interpretations, and derived reasoning within a structured, capacity-bounded, and revisable workspace. This separation makes each premise explicit and auditable, so that conflicts between what is seen and what is derived become detectable even by a text-only critic and can trigger targeted reinspection of the original image. Without task-specific training, our harness consistently outperforms standalone models across 4B, 8B, Qwen3-VL-Flash, and 32B configurations on Euclid30K, SolidGeo, and ChartQA bar charts, with gains of up to 11.68, 18.42, and 24.82 percentage points, respectively. Under identical tools, critic, and interaction limits, the structured Board outperforms shared free-form notes by 6.67 to 13.00 points and no-Board retries by 7.35 to 18.80 points while using 7.9\% to 38.2\% fewer tokens. Supervised fine-tuning on harness trajectories further improves direct answering and critic-free tool use, with positive transfer to chart reasoning.
  </details>

- **[HOForce: Motion and Force Reconstruction of Hand-Object Interaction Using a Single-View RGB-D Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23871)**  `arXiv:2611.23871`  
  _Jiahui Wang, Wanqing Du, Yuanbin Zou, Zhefeng Xu, Feng Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing hand-object interactions is an important task, and achieving it with a single camera makes large-scale capture practical but challenging due to heavy occlusions. We introduce HOForce to track hands and objects, and further recover interaction forces from single-view RGB-D video and known object properties. For two-hand manipulation, we jointly model fingertip forces of both hands and the contact forces between objects when two objects are presented. The reconstructed forces also improve motion tracking by giving pose constraints in the occluded regions. HOForce recovers hand and object motion more accurately on DexYCB and TACO and produces less hand-object penetration than interaction baselines on DexYCB. On our dataset of fingertip forces recorded with tactile sensors, HOForce reduces normal-force MAE by 24.5% relative to the strongest baseline. The recovered forces reproduce object motion in MuJoCo and transfer to other engines after adaptation.
  </details>

- **[On Demand, Not Uniformity: Adaptive Expert Orchestration for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23865)**  `arXiv:2611.23865`  
  _Mengxin Qin, Kun Wei, Weitao An, Jiahua Li, Xu Yang, Cheng Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) enables Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to adapt to sequential instruction data while retaining previously acquired knowledge. While recent Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)-based methods mitigate forgetting by freezing historical experts and introducing new ones, they largely overlook the varying adaptation demands across tasks. Probing incoming tasks reveals pronounced heterogeneity in layer-wise gradient pressure and token-wise prediction difficulty. This observation highlights two unresolved decisions: where additional capacity is needed and how strongly new experts should contribute to individual tokens. To address both questions, we propose the Coverage-Calibrated Expert Injection and Residual Arbitration framework (CIRA-MoE), which derives expert acceptance margins via learnable threshold routing to serve as a unified coverage signal. Specifically, historical acceptance evidence guides the selective injection of new experts into insufficiently covered layers. Meanwhile, relative evidence between historical and current experts modulates token-wise residual contributions, coupled with arbitration-consistent regularization to promote expert specialization. Comprehensive experiments on UCIT and CoIN demonstrate that CIRA-MoE outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in MCIT.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Radiology Reports in 3D Chest CT: Results of the ReXGrounding Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23862)**  `arXiv:2611.23862`  
  _Mohammed Baharoon, Mahmoud Hussain Alabbad, Sung Eun Kim, Xiaoman Zhang, Lucas Bijnens, Zirong Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Segmenting each reported finding in a radiology scan would allow findings to be measured and tracked over time and let AI systems generate reports that are verifiable, but so far AI models have performed poorly at this task. To advance this task, we organized the ReXGrounding Challenge at MICCAI 2026, the first challenge dedicated to it. Given a non-contrast chest CT scan and the set of findings from its report, participants trained models to produce the corresponding set of 3D segmentations. The challenge used 2,992 training and 200 validation scans from the ReXGroundingCT dataset, and evaluated methods on a held-out test set of 300 scans with 582 findings, exhaustively annotated by board-certified radiologists. In total, 48 teams from 15 countries made 760 submissions, and the best method reached a Dice of 0.375 and a hit rate of 0.77, matching agreement between two independent radiologist groups (Dice 0.32, hit rate 0.76, on a 50-scan subset). On the 100 scans of the pre-challenge benchmark, the winner reached a Dice of 0.390, against 0.285 for VoxTell, the best model before the challenge, which five of the six best teams fine-tuned. Non-focal findings remained far harder to segment than focal ones, instance F1 stayed below inter-reader agreement (0.34 vs. 0.47), and models sometimes segmented the right abnormality in the wrong location. Top methods thus match radiologist agreement in Dice, but non-focal findings, findings with multiple instances, and adherence to the anatomical location described in the text remain open problems.
  </details>

- **[MobileWeave: Program-First Synthesis of Dependency-Linked Tasks for Training Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23860)**  `arXiv:2611.23860`  
  _Ziyi He, Xian Wu, Qiyuan Chen, Haitao Li, Cehao Yang, Yinghao Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Native mobile GUI agents require large-scale training trajectories covering both individual app functions and multi-goal workflows within and across apps, which makes automatic task synthesis a promising way to scale training data. Existing synthesis pipelines, however, produce each task as free-form text, leaving implicit how its subgoals depend on one another. This matters because composition is a skill in its own right: a policy may complete each subtask of a workflow on its own, yet fail to chain these subtasks into the complete workflow. We present MobileWeave, which instead synthesizes every task as an executable program derived from the app implementations of a controllable simulator. MobileWeave abstracts each app into a typed capability graph with semantic types shared across apps, composes atomic programs into single- and cross-app composite programs through explicit value and object dependencies, and verifies each program by native execution, deriving both its instruction and its completion check from the same program. Trained on teacher trajectories collected for these tasks and on recovery supervision at the policy's own stalls, MobileWeave-8B improves its base model by 26.7 points on MobileGym and by 8.6, 21.3, and 17.6 points on AndroidWorld, AndroidLab, and MobileWorld, with comparable gains at 4B. Beyond these gains, we find that composition is learnable from task structure: explicit dependencies between subgoals act as a training signal that teaches a policy to compose skills it already masters. We release data, model, and code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Image Representations for Mouse Dynamics Authentication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23859)**  `arXiv:2611.23859`  
  _Junyi Li, Charles Devlen, Daqing Hou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mouse dynamics authentication has traditionally relied on handcrafted features or time-series representations that capture both spatial and temporal characteristics of mouse movements. More recent approaches have modeled mouse data as images, enabling the use of powerful pretrained computer vision models. However, prior work primarily focuses on spatial-only representations where the cursor coordinates are plotted as a trajectory image. These representations discard the temporal characteristics which can help discriminate users. In this work, we propose image representations that incorporate speed and velocity information for mouse dynamics authentication. We investigate two mouse data representations, a XYPlot representation that intuitively shows the path of movement, and a Pairwise Distance (PWD) representation that utilizes the entire image canvas to encode relationships between mouse events. Both representations are evaluated under both location-aware and location-free settings using pretrained 2D-CNN and ViT architectures. We found that the incorporation of temporal information improved performance over spatial representations alone, and that the location-aware variants generally outperformed the location-free. We achieve state-of-the-art performance on two datasets, Balabit and TWOS, and extend our evaluation to two other datasets. We found that in general, location-aware PWD with temporal encoding combined with the 2D-CNN architecture provided the best results, with mean EERs of 5.21% and 12.70% on the Balabit Test-1 and Test-2 sets, respectively, 4.94% on TWOS, 6.16% on ChaoShen, and 0.59% on DFL.
  </details>

- **[ReflectScene:MLLM-Guided Closed-Loop Diffusion for 3D Indoor Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23845)**  `arXiv:2611.23845`  
  _Jiawei Shen, Jie Feng, Junpeng Zhang, Guanbin Li, Wanyi Chen, Weisheng Dong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-conditioned 3D indoor scene generation requires layouts satisfying geometric, functional, and semantic constraints. However, existing diffusion methods rely primarily on static textual conditions, limiting their ability to assess evolving scenes and translate semantic discrepancies into executable object-level corrections. MLLM-based post-hoc refinement provides semantic feedback but remains decoupled from denoising, potentially disrupting scenethis http URLpropose ReflectScene, an MLLM-guided closed-loop diffusion framework. First, we introduce Hierarchical Prior-Grounded Scene Understanding that aligns structured layouts with multi-view visual evidence, to interpret object-specific context and scene global semantics via a frozen multi-modal large language model (MLLM). Second, we propose MLLM-Guided Semantic Reward Evaluation to distill MLLM's diagnoses and assessments on possible candidate corrections into a lightweight Reward Predictor, enabling correction decision making with reduced computation burden. Finally, Decision-Conditioned Trajectory-Consistent Diffusion is proposed to integrate selected corrections through transient conditioning and persistent clean-estimate projection, correcting object layout in the generated scene while preserving non-target arrangements throughoutthis http URLon 3D-FRONT demonstrate improved functional consistency and semantic alignment while maintaining competitive generation quality and diversity.
  </details>

- **[TOGBench: Benchmarking and Improving Tiny-Object Generation Fidelity in Aerial Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23840)**  `arXiv:2611.23840`  
  _Chanhwi Jeong, Dongmin You, Seungwook Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in deep generative models now enable the synthesis of realistic imagery. However, aerial imagery is dominated by tiny objects, such as vehicles, ships, and aircraft, which occupy only a few pixels owing to the overhead, wide-area nature of aerial imaging. Existing generative models struggle substantially more with tiny objects than with larger ones. This limitation has been overlooked, as prior work on generative evaluation emphasizes prompt following or human preference rather than whether small objects are rendered faithfully. To address this gap, we introduce TOGBench, a quantitative, object-level benchmark for evaluating tiny-object generation fidelity in aerial imagery. We further propose two simple train-/inference-time techniques that improve the ability of layout-to-image generators to render small objects, raising their performance on TOGBench.
  </details>

- **[Mapping the Design Space of Feature Sampling for Optical Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23822)**  `arXiv:2611.23822`  
  _Liping Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The computation of correlation features is crucial for optical flow estimation. Existing methods (e.g., RAFT) perform fixed-radius lookups on a cost volume, yielding a set of correlation features for each position; alternatively, WAFT performs single-point feature indexing, yielding one correlation feature vector per position. None of them designs a suitable correlation feature sampling scheme for each position according to regional characteristics. Our insight is that not every position requires the same sampling scheme. Applying an identical sampling scheme to every position is either redundant or insufficient and ignores the specific characteristics of the corresponding region. This paper rethinks the design of feature sampling and investigates the following question: should the support of feature sampling---i.e., the extent and directions over which evidence is aggregated---be learned separately for each query position? To study this question, we propose SAGE (Sampling with Anisotropic Gaussian Expectation). This method replaces single-point sampling with a per-pixel anisotropic Gaussian expectation on a fixed candidate grid centered on the current optical flow estimate. A positive-definite precision matrix controls the scale, anisotropy, and orientation of the sampling support, with single-point sampling included as its limiting case. Consequently, the sampling support becomes an explicit and analyzable design variable and provides a principled way to analyze how directional feature aggregation affects correspondence learning. Extensive experiments show that SAGE, while adding only 1.5% multiply-accumulate operations (MACs), effectively improves zero-shot cross-dataset generalization. When trained only on FlyingChairs and FlyingThings, SAGE reduces the end-point error (EPE) by 24.2% and 16.4% on Sintel clean and final, and reduces KITTI F1-epe and F1-all by 7.2% and 11.6%.
  </details>

- **[SWIFT: Slow World, Instant Freshness, and Timely Action for Asynchronous Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23813)**  `arXiv:2611.23813`  
  _Jiazhe Wang, Tianyi Yang, Ruihang Li, Fan Lu, Xianming Zeng, Jianyun Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving scenes keep changing during model computation, making both observation freshness and action-delivery time central to world-action planning. To retain rich generative world priors in autonomous driving without stalling real-time control, we present SWIFT (Slow World, Instant Freshness, and Timely action), a deployment-oriented asynchronous world-action system that moves world updates off the observation-to-action critical path. On a background GPU, a slow Video DiT streams surround-view history into read-only predictive world context, trained with a predictive span that covers both the update interval and background world computation. On a foreground GPU, a lightweight freshness carrier encodes current surround-view images into a gated key/value bank at every step, letting a fast Action DiT combine live visual feedback with the latest completed world context without waiting. On open-loop NAVSIM, SWIFT reaches 94.2 PDMS at 198.4 ms per decision with a trajectory scorer; on closed-loop Bench2Drive, it achieves an 80.30 Driving Score at 109.9 ms foreground latency. SWIFT further transfers to the Waymo E2E hidden test (8.0050 Rater Feedback Score) and nuScenes (0.54 m mean endpoint error, 0.03% mean collision rate). Across all four benchmarks, SWIFT achieves the best results among compared world-action methods, establishing a favorable accuracy-latency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[PReMind: When Personal Memory Meets Visual Hazards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23808)**  `arXiv:2611.23808`  
  _Yeongtak Oh, Donghyeok Lee, Joonhee Oh, Jaewon Jeong, Sungroh Yoon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized vision-language assistants use visual observations and personal memories to provide advice tailored to the user. When the observed scene contains a physical hazard, personal context, such as allergies, medical conditions, or mobility constraints, can change what help is appropriate. Responding appropriately therefore requires recognizing the visible hazard and applying the relevant person's information. We introduce PReMind-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that evaluates this combination across diverse hazards and personal-memory conditions. It separately measures memory access, grounding, abstention, and hazard response, and assesses joint success on interdependency cases. Existing models often satisfy one requirement while failing the other, and training only for safety or personalization leaves a gap in joint success. We therefore propose PReMind, a two-phase reinforcement learning framework that combines on-demand and forced-call interactions (memory always retrieved) to jointly train grounded memory use and hazard response. At 4B, PReMind raises joint success from 12.9% to 28.6% over its base on the interdependency set and improves hazard adequacy and correct abstention on the main test. Joint-success gains persist under forced-call evaluation and under a second LLM judge across three Phase II seeds; averaged over these seeds, joint success also improves on unseen hazard groups. These findings highlight the need to evaluate and train personal assistants to connect visual evidence with the relevant person's context.
  </details>

- **[ROBIN++: Unified Copyright Protection and Tamper Localization for Diffusion Models via Dual-Domain Synergistic Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23793)**  `arXiv:2611.23793`  
  _Huayang Huang, Siqi Zeng, Qian Wang, Bo Du, Yu Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Digital watermarking provides a promising solution for verifying the provenance and integrity of generated content. However, existing methods often suffer from an inherent trade-off between copyright robustness and localization sensitivity due to the entanglement of conflicting signals within a single domain. In this paper, we propose ROBIN++, a paradigm that shifts from single-domain entanglement to a Dual-Domain Synergistic design. Our key insight is to explicitly decouple robustness and fragility during injection by assigning them to distinct domains, while re-coupling them for mutual reinforcement during verification. Specifically, we introduce an Adversarial Optimization Algorithm to inject a frequency-domain copyright watermark during generation, maximizing extraction robustness without visual artifacts. Complementarily, a Content-Aware Perturbation generator is integrated into the latent space to inject the spatial-domain localization watermark, ensuring sensitivity to spatial tampering with minimal interference to the copyright watermark. During verification, we propose a dual-branch synergistic detector that facilitates Frequency-to-Spatial Synergy, where robust spectral priors act as global anchors for local anomaly detection. Conversely, a Spatial-to-Frequency Refinement strategy is employed to rectify corrupted latent features using predicted tamper masks. Extensive experiments show that ROBIN++ significantly outperforms existing approaches in both copyright recovery and tamper localization accuracy, effectively mitigating the long-standing trade-off between the two tasks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When accuracy hides risk: risk-controlled confirmation of the critical view of safety under expert disagreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23781)**  `arXiv:2611.23781`  
  _Bowen Xiang, Xiao Yu, Mingze Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated assessment of the critical view of safety (CVS) in laparoscopic cholecystectomy has advanced rapidly, but a deployed check must decide, before the first clip, whether to confirm CVS. The standard rule calibrates a threshold so that false confirmations are rare on average. We show that this rule concentrates its errors on disputed videos, which one of three expert raters judged sufficient, and that the failure is structural. When reference labels are votes of a panel sampled from a larger expert pool, the rule that maximizes sensitivity at a fixed average false-confirmation rate also maximizes false confirmation of disputed videos, and for recognizers that recover expert judgement with less noise this rate rises with accuracy. We propose certified confirmation, a risk-controlled monitoring rule whose threshold is selected by multiple testing so that, with probability at least 1 - {\delta}, false confirmation is at most {\alpha} overall, among disputed videos, within vendor and country groups, and before CVS is visible, using disputed labels only to calibrate. In a pre-specified, hash-locked evaluation on the 300 test videos (18 countries) of the SAGES CVS Challenge, standard calibration falsely confirmed 8 of 39 disputed videos (0.21; one-sided p = 0.037 against {\alpha} = 0.10), whereas certified confirmation confirmed 2 (one-sided 95% upper bound 0.15). On development data, protection held for vendor and country groups left out of calibration. The cost was confirming 10% rather than 35% of sufficient videos on time. Average accuracy hides this risk; disputed cases must be protected explicitly.
  </details>

- **[Keep the Reasoning, Read the Image: On-Demand Visual KV for Multi-Turn Reasoning VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23769)**  `arXiv:2611.23769`  
  _Ziyi Wang, Xiyue Zhang, Bing Hu, Guangmin Song, Chenyi Zhang, Li Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning vision-language models (VLMs) think for thousands of tokens before they answer, and retention policies keep the KV cache affordable by pruning it as the reasoning grows. We find that this pruning sacrifices the image in multi-turn conversations: the image and the reasoning share one budget, so the growing reasoning crowds the visual entries out. A chart question that loses 8 points to the full cache when asked first loses 24 points when asked after a round of reasoning. Protecting a small visual reserve from eviction recovers most of this loss, while adding the same slots to the shared budget recovers considerably less. The image therefore lacks protection, not capacity. Based on this finding, we propose ShelfKV, a training-free method that works on top of existing retention policies. It keeps every visual entry in CPU memory and, at each decoding step, swaps the visual entries that the current query scores highest into the attention slots of the text entries it scores lowest. The image is never evicted, yet it occupies attention slots only while it is read. We evaluate ShelfKV on four benchmarks, three of them conversational, with two retention policies and two reasoning VLMs. In conversations, it improves the strongest existing policy, DBTrimKV, by up to 20.3 points at matched GPU memory, and still leads by 8.7 points when DBTrimKV uses twice the memory. It also consistently outperforms a fixed visual reserve of matched GPU memory in these settings, without hurting accuracy on standalone questions. These results show that the image should be protected from the reasoning but read only on demand.
  </details>

- **[Mean Curvature Minimizing Foliations Of A Vector Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23765)**  `arXiv:2611.23765`  
  _Tabish A. Syed, Marc Theveneau, Benedikt Wirth, Rustum Choksi, Kaleem Siddiqi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streamline patterns are ubiquitous in nature. Prominent examples include white matter fibre tracts in the mammalian brain and arrangements of elongated cells, such as cardiomyocytes in the heart wall. It has been posited that such organisations foliate into locally smooth surfaces to optimise function. However, the recovery of these foliations from a single vector field aligned with the local orientation of the underlying sheets remains an open problem. We address this challenge by introducing a mathematical model of a sheet and developing an algorithm for sheet inference, from measurements of the oriented structures. Given an input vector field, our model estimates a second vector field that is orthonormal to it, while also requiring the Lie bracket of the two to vanish. To balance sheet likelihood against low surface area, we also reduce the mean curvature of the recovered local surface. We compute the first variation of an associated energy and provide a numerical method which admits an efficient, parallel implementation for sheet estimation. We demonstrate our approach on both synthetic and biological examples, motivated by applications to medical imaging. These include white matter fibre tracts from a brain atlas and confocal microscopy images of cleared heart wall tissue. For the latter we are able to reconstruct sheets aligned with local cardiomyocyte directions at unprecedented (micrometre) scale.
  </details>

- **[Detect What You Need: Chain-of-Causal Reasoning for 3D Intent Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23754)**  `arXiv:2611.23754`  
  _Zihao Zhang, Aming Wu, Yang Li, Yahong Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately matching human intentions in 3D space is an important goal of artificial intelligence. Recently, 3D Intention Grounding (3D-IG) has emerged, aiming to localize target 3D objects given a natural-language intent. Unlike conventional visual grounding with descriptive referring expressions, 3D-IG intents are abstract and non-descriptive, making object localization substantially more challenging. This requires inferring latent functional requirements from non-descriptive intents and aligning them with object-level 3D representations. However, existing methods largely rely on implicit intent-object matching, leading to logical gaps and limited interpretability, robustness, and generalization. To address these challenges, we propose Chain-of-Causal Reasoning (CoCR), a causality-inspired functional dependency reasoning framework that explicitly bridges abstract intentions and candidate objects through intermediate functional requirements. Specifically, CoCR progressively decomposes complex intents into ordered functional requirements, thereby forming an explicit intent-function-object reasoning chain that links abstract intentions to object suitability. Building on this chain, we construct a causality-inspired functional dependency graph to model requirement-attribute relationships and introduce a causal-visual alignment module that aligns function-aware representations with the geometric-semantic features of 3D point clouds, enabling bidirectional verification between structured functional reasoning and visual evidence. Extensive experiments on 3D Intention Grounding and 3D Visual Grounding demonstrate that our method enhances intent-aware object localization. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ACUS: An Angular Calibrated Uncertainty Score for Incomplete-View CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23752)**  `arXiv:2611.23752`  
  _Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Quankeng Huang, Yujie Liu, Zhenzhen Gong, Junjie Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse-view and limited-arc computed tomography (CT) are commonly treated as projection completion: a network fills an unmeasured view, and the result is ranked by similarity to one ground-truth image. That score can be high even when the query angle is far from every acquired measurement, because a learned prior may invent anatomy the scan never constrained. Existing protocols therefore leave a basic question unanswered: how does predictive reliability change with distance from the acquired views? We propose the Angular Calibrated Uncertainty Score (ACUS) to answer that question. ACUS is the Winkler interval score of a nominal 90% predictive interval on held-out projections, binned by distance to the nearest acquired view. It charges interval width and adds a large miss penalty when a held-out pixel falls outside the interval, so a narrow band helps only when coverage holds. We use collapse on acquired angles as a necessary consistency check on the predictor. On chest and dental cone-beam orbits, interval width grows with the angular gap. Empirical coverage ranges from 77% to 92%, and the peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) of the point estimate falls as the width rises. Nearest-neighbor, interpolation, and point completion, each given one residual scale, have zero reported far coverage and incur outside-distance penalties. ACUS exposes gap-dependent overconfidence that a point-estimate PSNR alone does not measure.
  </details>

- **[GaussLearner: 3D Gaussian Primitives as Self-Regulating Continual Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23736)**  `arXiv:2611.23736`  
  _Gaoxiang Linghu, Haonan Zhou, Xuanhe Liu, Weili Xu, Kaiyue Zhou, Jun Xiao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning of 3D Gaussian fields aims to maintain faithful reconstructions of the evolving world. However, directly fine-tuning an existing Gaussian field on new observations can cause catastrophic forgetting of previously reconstructed content. To address this challenge, existing methods employ selective local optimization, replay, or parameter regularization, yet still struggle to balance adaptation to scene changes with preservation of still-valid content. Our key insight is to view 3D Gaussian primitives themselves as self-regulating continual learners. Based on this insight, we introduce GaussLearner, which equips each Gaussian with a closed continual-learning loop connecting observation, self-regulation, and adaptation at the primitive level. At each novel state, GaussLearner first attributes pixel-wise inconsistencies in current observations to their contributing Gaussians. Each Gaussian then self-regulates through a learning weight that balances current observations against cross-state reliability. This weight governs both Gaussian parameter adaptation and optimizer memory refresh. As current inconsistencies are resolved, the adaptation outcome renews each Gaussian's reliability for future states. The resulting field is a globally coupled population of locally self-regulating learners. Across scenes from CL-Splats, WAT, and our CL-3D dataset, GaussLearner improves average PSNR by 3.41 dB over the strongest baseline. On CL-3D, it achieves the best reconstruction quality on both changed and unchanged views.
  </details>

- **[GTA: Generative Tower of Agents for Scalable Holonic Scene Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23727)**  `arXiv:2611.23727`  
  _Zeng Tao, Yunuo Chen, Xiang Feng, Yumeng He, Peihao Li, Nanxiang Jiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scalable world modeling requires coordinating scene-wide goals with fine-grained objects and dynamic characters under limited time and context budgets. We introduce GTA (Generative Tower of Agents), which represents every unit at every level of an adaptive scene hierarchy as a holon, both a self-contained construction task and a composable scene unit. Parallel agents recursively plan the hierarchy top-down, generate assets, and assemble and refine them bottom-up.
