Uniform interfaces carry scene-wide requirements down the hierarchy, guiding local construction so that each holon fits into the larger one. GTA accelerates scalable world modeling through high-throughput parallel execution while preserving well-structured layouts and fine-grained asset details. GTA then populates the assembled environments with dynamic human characters whose appearance and motion are guided by the scene, yielding realized 4D worlds. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Do 3D Point Representations Encode Architectural Functions? Probing Supervised and Pretrained Models on Urban Facades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23720)**  `arXiv:2611.23720`  
  _Abdiaziz Muse, Jiju Poovvancheri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic labels say what a building element is, not what it does: two identical-looking windows on one facade may be operable or fixed, a column may carry load or only decorate. We ask whether 3D point representations encode such architectural function. On 22 mobile-LiDAR buildings with 15 functional attributes per element, we hold out entire buildings and compare hand-crafted descriptors, networks trained from scratch and frozen self-supervised point transformers. Descriptors and frozen features are read by the same linear layer. No network trained from scratch, from PointNet to a 108M-parameter PTv3, clearly exceeds the descriptors (0.610 mAP on functionally ambiguous elements), whereas frozen Utonia reaches 0.732. Random-weight controls of the same architectures (0.649 and 0.652) attribute the gain to pretraining (Utonia: +0.080, 95% building interval [+0.035, +0.124]). Pretrained features improve at every tested number of labelled buildings while descriptors and random features plateau, and, in the configurations we tested, fine-tuning erodes the benefit: at today's data scale, function is better read from pretrained representations than learned from scratch.
  </details>

- **[CoScan: Multi-Scale Content-Adaptive Space-Filling Scans for Causal State-Space Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23717)**  `arXiv:2611.23717`  
  _Wei Dong, Terry Ji, Yan Min, Shahram Shirani, Jun Chen, Han Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selective state-space models (SSMs) have recently shown strong potential for efficient image restoration. A central difficulty, however, is that a 2D feature map typically needs to be linearized into a 1D token sequence for causal state-space updates, making restoration performance highly sensitive to the imposed scan order. Existing deterministic scans follow space-filling principles but remain fixed and content-agnostic, and can yield fragmented or semantically inconsistent contexts under degradations. We propose \textbf{CoScan}, a \textbf{co}ntent-adaptive \textbf{scan}-order design for causal vision SSMs. CoScan learns image-dependent space-filling traversals via differentiable proxy supervision driven by \textbf{scan-quality objectives} that \textit{encourage short-range coherence and causal predictability, while preserving spatial adjacency}. To support hierarchical restoration backbones, we introduce an \textbf{efficient multi-scale scan construction by coarsening a fine-level minimum spanning tree}, producing coherent scans across feature resolutions. In addition, a \textbf{frequency-guided start selection strategy} is designed to \textit{place informative structures early in the sequence prefix to strengthen causal context formation}. Plugged into existing Mamba-based backbones, CoScan yields consistent improvements across several restoration benchmarks with minimal overhead and shows promising results on high-level vision tasks. Code will be released atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[A Hardware-Aware Comprehensive Study of Edge Vision Deployment with A Unified Hardware-Model-Runtime Mapping Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23705)**  `arXiv:2611.23705`  
  _Le Duc An, Le Duc Thanh, Vo Hoai Viet_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid growth of deep learning has driven the deployment of computer vision directly on resource-constrained edge devices. However, the extreme heterogeneity of edge hardware architectures poses major challenges in selecting and designing suitable vision models. This survey bridges the gap between edge hardware constraints, model architectures, and deployment software toolchains. We establish a comprehensive taxonomy classifying edge hardware into three distinct tiers: mid-range devices (GPUs and dedicated NPUs or TPUs), dedicated embedded MPU or SoC processors, and microcontrollers (MCUs). We aggregate and analyze multi-source benchmark results for image classification and object detection across the computing spectrum, spanning from tens of watts down to milliwatt-scale bare-metal MCUs. Based on these empirical findings, we propose a two-tiered hardware-to-model mapping framework that provides concrete model selection matrices across performance priorities (Speed, Balanced, Accuracy) and actionable architectural design principles (peak SRAM budgeting, resolution-first scaling, and algorithm-compiler co-design). Finally, we highlight critical deployment challenges including toolchain fragmentation, operator incompatibility, and Vision Transformer feasibility, and outline promising future research directions in on-device adaptation and sustainable Edge AI.
  </details>

- **[What Do Learned 3D Models Add to Localization from Posed References?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23697)**  `arXiv:2611.23697`  
  _Jaehyun Kim, Wonseok Kang, Jeongmin Lee, Tae-Wan Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Localizing a query from a few posed reference images avoids building a 3D point map. We ask what the camera predictions of learned 3D models add to geometric estimation from the same references, and where failures arise. Across eight learned models and variants and five test sets, we compare reference budgets, swap pose components, and intervene on the references a model sees. At fine pose-error thresholds, geometric estimation with one additional reference is significantly more accurate than every model on two references on Cambridge and NAVER B1 and never significantly worse, alone or as a fallback behind a shared acceptance gate; with the same three references, the more accurate approach depends on the scene. The gain from the learned translation magnitude depends on reference selection: it improves seven of the eight models on 12-Scenes with the spread-out pairs we select, but none of the five we test with the two top-ranked retrieved references. Where retrieval also returns references from other locations, at NAVER Gangnam Station, models fail at different stages. A robust alignment brings Pi3X and MapAnything within 3.3 pp of adding references as needed. In controlled tests, aligning on the nearby references largely restores VGGT, Pi3X and MapAnything but not Depth Anything 3, and pose-conditioned Pi3X recovers only if the far poses are masked and left out of its input-pose normalization.
  </details>

- **[MoMoVL: Modality-Aware Adaptive Compression for On-Device Sparsely-Activated Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23695)**  `arXiv:2611.23695`  
  _Dongkeun Lee, Wonjun Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rise of the agentic web has been accompanied by growing demand for on-device Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that perform diverse cross-modal web tasks with enhanced privacy and reduced latency. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are widely adopted in modern VLMs for compute-efficient scalability, yet their multi-expert architecture poses deployment challenges due to substantial memory demands. Mixed-precision quantization is an effective compression technique that reduces bit-widths while adapting to the heterogeneous hardware budgets of edge devices, particularly well-suited for MoE due to its inherent structural sparsity, which causes imbalanced importance among experts. While prior work has applied quantization for efficient MoE inference at the edge, we point out that in MoE-based VLMs, the modality-specific characteristics of visual and text tokens further impact expert quantization. To address this issue, we present MoMoVL, a modality-aware adaptive compression framework for MoE-based VLMs that brings vision-language inference within reach of on-device resource budgets. Motivated by our empirical findings on modality-induced disparities across experts, MoMoVL jointly exploits the per-expert activation profile and per-modality quantization sensitivity to guide bit-width assignment. Experimental results show that MoMoVL improves the accuracy-compression trade-off relative to state-of-the-art mixed-precision baselines and reduces memory footprint by up to 15.44% with a modest drop in accuracy over its uniform-precision baseline. Our code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Degrade: A Recoverability-Aware Curriculum for Reliable Smart-Glasses Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23674)**  `arXiv:2611.23674`  
  _Yaopeng Wang, Qingliang Wang, Yilun Jin, Xiujin Liu, Han Yue, Chen Xia, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Degradations in smart-glasses images obscure small text and fine-grained structures needed by vision-language models (VLMs) for visual assistance. We introduce an high-resolution (HR)-referenced semantic evaluation protocol that compares VLM outputs on restored images with those on corresponding HR references. Our evaluation reveals two failure modes: generative SR can synthesize misleading details, whereas restoration-oriented SR can leave critical evidence blurred or ambiguous. These findings motivate improving faithful detail recovery in restoration-oriented SR, where a key training challenge is to provide sufficiently difficult inputs while retaining recoverable visual information. We propose ReDiC (Recoverability-Difficulty Curriculum), which combines a model-independent recoverability proxy with model-relative restoration difficulty to adapt training degradation severity. ReDiC increases severity when inputs are recoverable and restoration is easy, maintains challenging but recoverable conditions, and reduces severity when estimated recoverability is low. It modifies only training degradation generation, leaving the SR architecture and reconstruction objective unchanged. Across NAFNet, SwinIR, and MambaIR, ReDiC improves average PSNR by approximately 2.5 dB and HR-referenced semantic scores by about 3 points over the corresponding baselines on synthetic benchmarks, with further gains confirmed by LLM-based and human evaluations on 200 real smart-glasses captures.
  </details>

- **[Image-Condition-Controlled Diffusion Network with Large-Scale CT Pretraining for CT-Free Attenuation Correction in Dynamic Cardiac PET](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23672)**  `arXiv:2611.23672`  
  _Zhejia Zhang, Scott M. Leonard, Jinkui Hao, Ryan J. Avery, Richard L. Weinberg, Bo Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Positron emission tomography (PET) provides quantitative information on myocardial perfusion. In dynamic PET, tracer kinetics are tracked through a series of reconstructed time frames. Reliable attenuation correction is essential for accurate measurements. Clinical attenuation maps ($\mu$-maps) are typically derived from computed tomography (CT), which adds radiation exposure and may introduce PET-CT misregistration due to respiratory and cardiac motion. Moreover, supervised CT-free methods rely on paired non-attenuation-corrected (NAC) PET and CT data, which are scarce, whereas large-scale unpaired CT data are more readily available. To address these challenges, we propose Large-scale Unpaired CT-Informed Diffusion (LUCID), a framework that transfers anatomical priors learned from 10,000 chest CT volumes in the public CT-RATE dataset to $\mu$-map synthesis for dynamic cardiac $^{82}$Rb PET. First, a convolutional neural network (CNN) predicts an initial $\mu$-map from each NAC PET frame. Second, the CT-pretrained diffusion backbone refines a low-noise perturbation of the CNN prediction through a single denoising step, conditioned on the corresponding NAC PET frame via ControlNet. Finally, frame-wise predictions are averaged voxel-wise to obtain the final $\mu$-map. We further introduce the voxel-wise temporal standard deviation (TSD) to quantify inter-frame variability without requiring a reference $\mu$-map. We evaluated LUCID on an in-house dataset of 102 patients and found that it achieved the best quantitative accuracy while maintaining competitive inter-frame consistency. Ablation studies showed that refinement with a diffusion backbone pretrained on in-house CT data from the training patients was less accurate than the CNN prediction alone, highlighting the importance of large-scale CT pretraining.
  </details>

- **[EyeDiT: Anatomy-Grounded Multi-Modal Eye Generation and Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23670)**  `arXiv:2611.23670`  
  _Yonghan Lee, Zachary Willms, Kun Liu, Pushkar Anand, Ali Behrooz, Dmitri Model, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic eye data are critical for rapid development of eye tracking (ET) in AR/VR applications. However, existing synthetic eye generators are largely image-centric and lack a high-quality underlying 3D anatomy of the eye, which limits their control mainly to the gaze and provides no anatomical labels. To address this problem, we propose EyeDiT, an anatomy-grounded, multi-modal 3D eye generation and simulation model trained with optical coherence tomography (OCT), labeled anatomy point clouds and near-eye images. Our pipeline first trains a Multi-Modal Anatomy Variational Autoencoder (VAE), which encodes an anatomy-aware latent representation from all input modalities. Then, our Eye Latent DiT is trained to generate latent samples conditioned on the optical axis, the pupil position and radius, and the subject identity. Together with Gaussian decoders for novel-view simulation, our model enables control over the pupil, subject identity and viewpoint for data synthesis. We show that our model outperforms previous eye image synthesis models in novel-view synthesis (24.9 against 21.8 dB PSNR from a single view), and that a public ET neural network trained on our synthetic data shows a smaller sim-to-real gap than when trained on data from other generators (3.5$^\circ$ vs. 8.4$^\circ$ gaze error), approaches a network trained on real data, and further improves ET performance when combined with real data (2.3$^\circ$ vs. 2.6$^\circ$). More information is available on the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PRISM: Profiling and Representation of Individual Stereotypical Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23646)**  `arXiv:2611.23646`  
  _Austin A. MBaye, Savik Kinger, Matthew S. Goodwin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An anatomical pose provides interpretable joint structure, but no fixed set of physical landmarks can fully specify a person's movement. We introduce PRISM, a complementary representation that characterizes repetitive movement using tracked masks and dense optical flow. Torso-relative flow measures intensity, frame-wise mask variation measures spatial extent, signed-flow recurrence estimates frequency, and persistent sliding-window geometry describes the rhythmicity of the movement. Across 1,890 synthetic conditions, the measurements recover controlled speed, amplitude, and frequency while exposing observation limits. An idealized hand-motion experiment demonstrates variation omitted by identical exact COCO-17 trajectories; controlled corruption experiments separately examine measurement sensitivity. Audits of two pose methods on the same data cohort and 36,930 external skeleton clips document incomplete observation and dependence on processing choices. In 1,404 annotated complex stereotypical motor movement bouts from six autistic participants, total flow converges with pose motion, while matched-coordinate examples and repeated-session profiles show differences in intensity, extent, frequency, and rhythmicity within behavior labels. A separate VLM comparison evaluates quantitative frequency estimation, and a profile-conditioned decoder links the measurements to illustrative skeletal movements. PRISM connects numerical profiles to visible examples, providing an interpretable way to compare how the same behavior is expressed beyond its label or event count.
  </details>

- **[Isthmus: Bridging Supervision Islands Restores Uncertainty and Stability in One-Step Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23616)**  `arXiv:2611.23616`  
  _Luming Yang, Siqing Li, Haoxian Liu, Zhenyu Bu, Jiazheng Zhu, Alper Yilmaz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-step flow maps are increasingly used as posterior samplers, where the point of sampling is the uncertainty that the samples carry. We find that the one-step sampler loses this uncertainty and becomes less stable. Compared with multi-step samples of the same network, its samples lose variance and decorrelate. Supervising the one-step jump directly makes this worse; the samples collapse further toward the conditional mean and break down when training is extended. We trace both failures to the locations where training places its pairs of start and end times. Only pairs on the diagonal of the time triangle receive targets from data, and the pair used for one-step sampling is either left unsupervised or supervised on an island, a set of pairs that the self-consistency identity does not connect to the diagonal, where the objective neither determines the one-step map nor anchors its training. We bridge the island with an isthmus, which places 5% of the training pairs along the whole edge of the triangle that joins the one-step pair to the diagonal. The error of the one-step map is then bounded by the self-consistency residual along this edge. With the network, the loss and the inference code unchanged and at no added cost, the isthmus improves one-step sampling across several flow-map objectives, in MRI reconstruction, face inpainting, and unconditional generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet 32x32. On brain MRI, it restores one-step calibration to 0.96 and the correlation with multi-step samples to 0.93; both vary far less across seeds, and the agreement holds when training is extended to three times its length.
  </details>

- **[Weight Space Programming for Vision Language Model Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23615)**  `arXiv:2611.23615`  
  _Avinash Goutham Aluguvelly_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled rows in the projector and noise in the vision encoder of both models, infers the unknown scale factors, restores most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values, and returns accuracy to near its original level; programs that it writes from such weights repair unseen checkpoints of the same model without feedback. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry. Rounding those bits away leaves each model's answers to our test questions unchanged, and the agent, unaided, then restores no weight exactly: on SmolVLM it no longer finds the damage, and in a single session on LFM2-VL its only gain comes from rescaling answer-token embeddings, which changes how often the model answers yes. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling, in the distribution of stored values and in row norms, and one sentence in the prompt that names such a statistic directs the agent's edits to the damaged rows on both models. Swapped rows or columns, which leave the set of stored values unchanged, are not found unaided on either model within our session budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.
  </details>

- **[GRAFT: Graph-Regularized Prototype Fields for Realistic Test-Time Adaptation of VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23591)**  `arXiv:2611.23591`  
  _Maxx Richard Rahman, Wolfgang Maass_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlabeled test data can improve the zero-shot classifier of a vision-language model, yet transductive and test-time adaptation methods often fall below the zero-shot baseline when only a few vocabulary classes are present in a batch or when streams are correlated. We trace this failure to a shared design: every class owns independent parameters estimated from its own samples, so absent or rare classes are left at biased text anchors or fitted to foreign samples. We propose Graph-Regularized Anchor-displacement Field Transduction (GRAFT), which models the adapted prototypes as text anchors plus a displacement field that is partially pooled over a semantic class graph through a Gaussian Markov random field prior, together with an explicit class-presence model. Inference is closed-form and gradient-free on cached features. Anchor-only shrinkage and a single global shift are the two extreme settings of the prior; absent classes are provably harmonic extensions of the present ones, and the pooling strength can be chosen by empirical Bayes. Under the realistic batch and online protocols on eleven benchmarks and four ImageNet shifts, GRAFT improves over the zero-shot model in every setting and over anchor-based transduction where few classes are present.
  </details>

- **[ExiST: Existence-Aware Self-Training for Semi-Supervised Multi-Organ Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23581)**  `arXiv:2611.23581`  
  _Maxx Richard Rahman, Prakhar Kumar, Wolfgang Maass_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semi-supervised multi-organ segmentation should learn more than a dozen organs from a handful of labeled scans, and the smallest organs occupy less than one percent of the foreground. We identify a failure mode of self-training that is specific to this regime. When a teacher misses a small organ in a scan, every voxel of that organ receives the confident pseudo-label background, and the student is trained toward the organ absence. Repeated over many scans, this loop extinguishes tail organs. In a pilot study, removing the gallbladder from the pseudo-labels reduced its Dice on AMOS from 36.5 to 1.0. We argue that a missing organ should be treated as unresolved rather than absent and propose Existence-aware Self-Training (ExiST). It builds pseudo-labels by leave-one-out consensus of independently trained networks, screens them with a quality gate on validation scans, and infers, per scan and per organ, whether the organ was found. For every unresolved organ, the background voxels of that scan receive the set-valued target, trained with a marginal cross-entropy that accepts either answer and a Dice term that skips uncertain voxels. We show that this loss never pushes a candidate organ toward absence and never favors one member of the set, which leaves the decision to labeled evidence. ExiST outperforms the current state-of-the-art methods and reaches 77.1 mean Dice on Synapse with 20% labeled scans and 75.9 on AMOS with 5% labeled scans.
  </details>

- **[Layer-Matched Sparse Attention for Low-Redundancy 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23554)**  `arXiv:2611.23554`  
  _Sammam Mahdi, Fariha Binta Salim, K. M. Jubair Sami, Rakin Bin Rabbani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  VGGT reconstructs cameras and geometry from unposed images in one pass, but its global attention grows quadratically with the number of views. Training-free accelerators thin this attention and work well on redundant video, but lose pose accuracy when views share little geometry. This paper traces these losses to two kinds of cross-view information that a uniform sparse approximation can fail to preserve together: diffuse layers aggregate distributed context, whereas matching layers depend on query-specific interactions that carry little attention mass. A per-layer statistic measures the log-mass missed by a mean token. Its row-free proxy is the logit variance, and it selects the estimator for each layer. Diffuse layers use shared, importance-sampled hub keys, with log-weights that make the softmax numerator and denominator unbiased. Matching layers stay exact: every aggressive thinning tested cost accuracy. At equal cost, the selected layers outperform the four shifted windows tested. On 13 unseen low-redundancy scenes with 32 views, LMSA stays within 1.4 AUC@5 of dense attention, compared with 10.3 for AVGGT and 4.5 for SAF3R; its difference from contemporaneous GPA-VGGT is not significant, and only its gains over AVGGT pass both scene-level tests. With 100 views, it stays within 1.9 AUC@5 while its aggregator runs $2.0\times$ faster. Used post hoc to choose the layers GPA-VGGT protects, the statistic improves it by 1.2 AUC@5 for 5% more FLOPs. Applied untuned to $\pi^3$, it beats AVGGT but remains further from dense accuracy.
  </details>

- **[ORGANIC3D: Grounding 3D CT Vision Language Models in Organ Level Image Text Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23529)**  `arXiv:2611.23529`  
  _Zelong Liu, Hamza Ahmed, Wynn Wingyi Lee, Mickael Torjman, Alexander Dimitrakakis, Jiahua Lu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in 3D computed tomography (CT) classification, but high accuracy does not reflect whether their predictions rely on the correct anatomical evidence. We introduce ORGANIC3D, a framework for evaluating and improving organ level image text alignment through controlled anatomical interventions. Our benchmark assesses 12 vision encoder checkpoints and 7 generative VLMs using matched patient comparisons, organ erasure, lesion replacement, and laterality perturbations, revealing substantial alignment failures even among top performing models. To learn organ level image text correspondence, ORGANIC3D aligns each finding sentence with the anatomy aware image features and trains on edited scans to anchor this alignment in the organ's image evidence. We further extend this framework to generative VLMs, promoting evidence dependent question answering and report generation. Trained exclusively on public data, ORGANIC3D achieves SOTA classification AUROC on CTRATE, Merlin, and the heldout RADChestCT dataset and reaches 77% laterality accuracy, compared with at most 66% for prior models. Its generative VLM matches or exceeds all evaluated publicly released VLMs on visual question answering, achieves the highest report accuracy, and retracts 96 to 99% of target organ findings after organ removal, versus a maximum of 69% for other models. Together, these results establish ORGANIC3D as a framework for testing whether 3D CT vision language models ground their predictions in anatomical evidence and for improving organ level alignment alongside predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Sink Token: Sink-Guided Key Editing in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23516)**  `arXiv:2611.23516`  
  _Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Xiangyu Zhou, Dongxiao Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In vision-language models (VLMs), a few sink tokens attract a disproportionate share of attention. While previous studies have examined why these tokens attract attention, we investigate how the sink-key direction affects attention to image and question tokens. Across four probed VLMs, boosting the magnitude of positive sink-aligned components increases attention on average, whereas boosting negative components decreases it; these responses persist through later layers. This identifies the native sink key as a reference for controlling attention beyond its own position. Motivated by this finding, we propose SHRINK, a training-free method that partially reduces sink-aligned components across visible keys during generation. On nine benchmarks under sampled reasoning, it improves mean accuracy by 5.1 and 2.7 percentage points on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B, with smaller gains on the other two main models, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Gemma-3-4B. Beyond SHRINK, rescaling sink-aligned key components can replace the attention operations of two existing attention-steering methods, GIFT and ACG, with comparable accuracy on hallucination benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Trust the Ray, Not the Depth: Ray-Lateral Agreement for Multi-View 3D Human Pose Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23513)**  `arXiv:2611.23513`  
  _Farnoosh Koleini, Ahmed Helmy, Pu Wang, Xiwu Cao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Put a monocular mesh estimator behind every camera of a calibrated rig, and the reconstructed bodies land a meter or more apart. The cause is geometric: each estimate sits at the depth implied by a focal length the network assumed, not the one the camera has. Its error therefore points almost entirely along its own viewing ray, and the calibration tells us that direction exactly. To address this, we introduce the Ray-Lateral Agreement (RLA), which follows one rule: trust the ray, not the depth. RLA first slides every estimate along its own rays, which removes the depth bias and leaves the shape untouched. It then fuses the views one point at a time, under a Gaussian error whose precision follows each viewing ray. The maximum-likelihood solution is closed-form, interpolates between averaging and midpoint triangulation, and stays well conditioned where triangulation is singular. Robustness is the harder half, because correct views also disagree along their rays. Any weight built on the full residual therefore punishes good views together with bad ones. Ray-lateral agreement instead scores every view, at every point, by its residual across that view's own ray alone. We evaluated RLA on multi-camera public benchmark datasets. Across two, three, and four cameras and three per-view pose estimators, RLA consistently achieves lower worst-case error than all triangulation baselines.
  </details>

- **[MedHarness: A Unified Budgeted Runtime for Reliable Medical Imaging Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23501)**  `arXiv:2611.23501`  
  _Haofan Wu, Le Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical imaging agents already return answers on heterogeneous imaging surfaces, yet deployment still faces a limited verification budget where each wrong accept can harm clinical trust. When a thin check and a deeper evidence pass disagree on the same case, deciding when to stop, open more evidence, repair, or reject without a shared controller remains ill-posed. Tool-using workflows often lock a surface-specific evidence order per dataset, or a private always-open full-depth verification path; those pipelines stay costly, leave many wrong accepts, and cannot share one controller across modalities. We present MedHarness, a registered multi-surface runtime reliability layer that reformulates post-answer control as shared decision-object attachment after a proposer's initial prediction. Under Shared Decision Object, Minimum-Sufficient Verification, and Intermediate Repair, one shared policy with surface recipe adapters lets a new imaging surface join the same stop / open-evidence / repair / reject controller by recipe registration. On a 512-case live pool, MedHarness cuts wrong accepts versus a full-verification baseline by about 47% and raises accepted accuracy to 83.2% vs. 72.9% at 86.1% coverage versus full release under that baseline. On a failure-enriched multi-surface stress ranking, the same controller cuts estimated remote service calls per case by about 71% versus that baseline under a matched context-load setting. A held-out three-surface check and an alternate language-model transfer keep the same control ordering. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GRID-UAV: Geometry and Ray-depth Integrated Decision-making for Unmanned Aerial Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23491)**  `arXiv:2611.23491`  
  _Anas Anwarul Haq Khan, Amrin Kareem, Muhammad Umer Sheikh, Biplab Banerjee, Muhammad Haris Khan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action models (VLAs) encode images with 2D encoders that capture little 3D structure. A common approach is to feed the policy estimated depth, but a UAV chooses its own altitude, must judge distances tens of meters away, where monocular depth is less reliable, and must relate its forward and downward views. On long aerial routes, adding depth features directly brings no significant gain (42.5% vs. 42.6% success), so we study how 3D information should be given to the policy. Analyzing flight failures, we find that two forms of 3D grounding fail in opposite ways: explicit 3D lifting of depth collides less on the way but more often fails to finish at the target, while implicit alignment with a 3D geometry model finishes more often but collides more. We propose GRID, which combines both: (1) it lifts monocular depth into ray-corrected 3D positions in the UAV's frame for both cameras, and (2) during training only, it aligns its visual features with a frozen multi-view geometry model, at no inference cost. GRID adding explicit lifting to implicit alignment significantly increases how often the policy reaches the target ($61.5\% \rightarrow 69.5\%$, $p<0.001$), and adding implicit alignment to explicit lifting significantly increases how often it completes the task once there ($57.7\% \rightarrow 61.4\%$, $p<0.05$). On TravelUAV (3,005 test episodes), GRID achieves the best success rate on every split, with relative gains over the state of the art of 9.5% (seen), 17.1% (unseen objects) and 25.4% (unseen map).
  </details>

- **[Reference-Guided Data Curation for 3D Medical Vision-Language Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23484)**  `arXiv:2611.23484`  
  _Faidra Patsatzi, Ashwin Kumar, Chong Wang, Curtis P. Langlotz, Reinhard Heckel, Akshay S. Chaudhari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training 3D vision-language models (VLMs) on large medical imaging datasets collected across institutions is constrained by data quality. Vast, uncurated pools of image-report pairs are characterized by redundancy, class imbalance, and cross-institutional heterogeneity. We address this challenge of data curation for improving 3D VLM pretraining by proposing an offline, training-free, reference-guided data curation recipe. Our approach leverages a small, high-quality reference set to independently extract quality signals for each modality of a large, uncurated pool and merges them through a rank-aggregation step into a single ranking that guides sample selection. We validate this method on datasets comprising 35k 3D abdominal CT volume-report pairs from two institutions and 58k 2D chest X-ray image-report pairs. On 3D CT, using our reference-guided data curation strategy, our curated model matches the zero-shot disease classification performance of a state-of-the-art baseline (Merlin) while using $3.3\times$ less training data and a reference set of 5k samples. On 2D chest X-rays, our method yields a curated model that matches or exceeds the zero-shot disease classification performance of a model trained on the full pool while using up to $10\times$ less training data and a reference set of only 74 samples. Ablations show that the selection is stable under resampling of the reference, that smaller reference sets still outperform random sampling, and that label rebalancing does not improve performance. To support reproducibility, we release our code.
  </details>

- **[mmTherm: Absolute 3D Human Pose Estimation from Low-Resolution Thermal Images and mmWave Radar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23477)**  `arXiv:2611.23477`  
  _Zhaoxin Chang, Badii Jouaber, Daqing Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Absolute 3D human pose estimation (HPE) aims to recover both a person's body pose and their global position in 3D space. Privacy-preserving millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar provides distance and direction measurement capabilities, but its sparse point clouds make HPE under-constrained. Low-resolution thermal imaging can provide complementary observations of body direction and structure. However, our evaluation shows that a strong feature-level fusion baseline loses much of its localization and HPE advantage when generalizing to unseen subjects, with radial localization becoming less accurate than a radar-only baseline. Motivated by this finding, we introduce mmTherm, a lightweight framework that explicitly incorporates the complementary geometric properties of thermal imaging and mmWave radar. It combines body structure and joint directions estimated from thermal images with range information from radar to localize the body and reconstruct its 3D pose. Experiments on OctoNet show that mmTherm reduces cross-user absolute joint error by 33% and root localization error by 38% relative to the strongest corresponding baselines, while requiring substantially fewer parameters and less computation at inference.
  </details>

- **[Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23446)**  `arXiv:2611.23446`  
  _Michael Schroeder_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).
  </details>

- **[TransFIRA+: Transfer Learning for Biometric Recognizability Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23442)**  `arXiv:2611.23442`  
  _Allen Tu, Kartik Narayan, Joshua Gleason, Jennifer Xu, Matthew Meyn, Tom Goldstein, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biometric recognition in surveillance, video, and web imagery must contend with extreme variation in pose, blur, illumination, occlusion, and distance, where visual quality metrics fail to predict whether inputs are recognizable to the deployed encoder. Existing face image quality assessment methods rely on visual heuristics, curated annotations, or costly generative pipelines, leaving their predictions detached from the encoder's decision geometry. We introduce TransFIRA, a lightweight framework that grounds recognizability directly in embedding space. TransFIRA derives class-center similarity (CCS) and class-center angular separation (CCAS) from a pretrained encoder's embeddings and learns to predict them from images without quality annotations. Using predicted CCS for template weighting and CCAS>0 as a natural filtering threshold yields state-of-the-art face verification performance on BRIAR, raising TAR at $10^{-6}$ FMR from 0.085 with uniform averaging to 0.598. The predicted scores also reveal how image degradations affect encoder-specific recognizability. TransFIRA+ extends this framework to cross-camera multimodal person identification in untrimmed surveillance video. In this setting, body recognizability scores saturate within a narrow range, making direct weighting effectively uniform. We introduce a post-hoc sigmoid calibration that transforms these saturated scores into informative weights without retraining. After calibration, the BRIAR-trained TransFIRA predictor improves fused face and body identification on two unseen multi-camera datasets, outperforming detector-confidence weighting and uncalibrated CCS-rank weighting. Together, TransFIRA and TransFIRA+ demonstrate that encoder-grounded recognizability generalizes across biometric modalities and recognition settings.
  </details>

- **[Autoregressive View Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23417)**  `arXiv:2611.23417`  
  _Jensen, Zhou, Samuel Rota Bulo, Denis Rozumny, Duncan Zauss, Peter Hedman, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Autoregressive View Model (AVM), which generates a scene as a stream of views without constructing an explicit 3D representation. Through a unified in-context interface, AVM conditions on images or videos to generate short clips along a requested camera path. Visual context grounds reconstruction and interpolation, while a learned prior enables extrapolation into unseen regions. AVM retrieves relevant views from inputs and generated history to form a bounded active context. We fine-tune a pretrained bidirectional video diffusion transformer with flow matching in an N-in-1-out formulation. We then tune a causal model on its own rollouts to improve photorealism, reduce denoising steps, and limit long-horizon drift. We scale training to approximately 8M scenes and video clips. We evaluate generation capacity and scene persistence across benchmarks for camera control, text control, visual quality, dynamics, sparse and 4D reconstruction, and long-horizon consistency. The results demonstrate that AVM adapts to diverse input and output settings through one in-context interface.
  </details>

- **[OCLDA-GS: Object-Centric Local Deformation-Aware Gaussian Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23416)**  `arXiv:2611.23416`  
  _Ashim Dahal, Nikesh Subedi, Rabab Abdelfattah, Nick Rahimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) allows for online reconstruction of dynamic scenes from multi-view videos for Free Viewpoint Video (FVV). Current time and storage efficient methods rely on selecting a subset of anchor Gaussians and neighborhood interpolation for fast streamable 3DGS. However, these methods can overlook object-specific motion and introduce inconsistencies near object boundaries. We propose OCLDA-GS, an object-centric streaming framework that dynamically reallocates object and motion-aware Gaussian anchors to allocate anchor Gaussian support for objects undergoing large motion while at the same time, restricting the gaussian neighborhood within object groups for more precise object boundaries. Furthermore, it estimates object-wise rigid transformations (e.g. a hand moving) and leverages these estimates to learn per-anchor local deformation correction for small deformations (e.g. wrinkles on a shirt). Then, we conduct experiments on three dynamic multi-view video datasets and demonstrate improvements over the previous baselines alongside improved quality between refinement timesteps. Under matched refinement budgets, our method improves PSNR over Instant Gaussian Stream (IGS) by 0.169 dB on N3DV, 0.202 dB on Meeting Room and +0.199 on Google Immersive, while reducing the mean PSNR drop between refinement timesteps by 9.2% and 13.8% in N3DV and Meeting Room, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Any2Building: Unifying Point Clouds and Images for Building Reconstruction via Shared Spatial Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23187)**  `arXiv:2611.23187`  
  _Hongye Hou, Zhuoxiao Li, Liu Xiangyu, Tengxi Wang, Jing Ou, Dongli Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively. This motivates a unified framework that aligns geometric and visual cues in 3D and reconstructs buildings from either or both modalities. Building on this connection, we present Any2Building, a unified model for Level of Detail 2 (LoD2) building reconstruction from partial point clouds, overhead images, or both, with optional coarse geometric priors. At its core is an occupancy-grounded shared spatial queries representation that organizes available observations within a common 3D grid. Supervised by complete building surface voxels, these queries connect observational evidence to target geometry, enabling a shared generator to learn structural priors across tasks and providing spatial guidance for recovering missing structure. A trained voxel conditional surface-generation and low-poly remeshing pipeline then converts the predicted structure into compact LoD2 building models. To support this framework, we introduce BuildingWorld-Image, comprising approximately one million paired samples of partial point clouds, overhead images, and LoD2 building models across 18 cities. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Any2Building outperforms existing methods in geometric reconstruction accuracy across unimodal and multimodal point-cloud completion, single-image building generation, and prior-assisted generation, while maintaining detailed and robust recovery when point cloud input modality is degraded.
  </details>

- **[Wall-to-wall maps of canopy height and aboveground carbon across the Atlantic Forest](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23048)**  `arXiv:2611.23048`  
  _Matheus Pinheiro Ferreira, Thiago Carmuega Rabaçal, Giulio Brossi Santoro, Giovanna de Andrade Ferreira, Rodrigo Costa Pinto, Danilo R. A. de Almeida, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Global canopy height maps are increasingly used to estimate forest carbon, yet their regional accuracy is rarely tested. We present wall-to-wall maps of canopy height (10 m) and aboveground carbon density (30 m) for the Brazilian Atlantic Forest in 2024. Height was predicted by a deep learning network fusing Sentinel-2 reflectance, Sentinel-1 and ALOS PALSAR backscatter and ALOS elevation, trained on 7,179 and tested on 1,793 patches (58,800 km$^2$) from a statewide airborne LiDAR campaign over São Paulo state, Brazil. On held-out test patches, the mean absolute error is 2.26 m at 10 m and 1.90 m at 30 m, and the model was validated at two LiDAR sites outside São Paulo. Over the same pixels, the Meta and World Resources Institute global product has more than twice this error. A power law fitted to 124 inventory plots converted canopy height to above ground carbon density. The remaining forest holds 1,944 TgC over 28.3 Mha. Global products therefore need local validation before use in carbon accounting.
  </details>

- **[M3OPD-Reasoner: An Open Recipe for Post-Training Multimodal Reasoning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23034)**  `arXiv:2611.23034`  
  _Nguyen Quang Trung, Hoang Phan, Shuo Sun, Longyin Zhang, AiTi Aw_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD) enables a single student to absorb the skills of several domain teachers by learning from its own generated responses. We present Multimodal Model Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation Reasoner (M3OPD-Reasoner), a fully open recipe for post-training vision-language models (VLMs) over three reasoning domains: image STEM, image perception, and video. We curate two datasets, M3OPD-SFT-1.35M for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and M3OPD-RL-193K for reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards. On these data, we compare post-training recipes that start from Qwen3.5-9B: training one model on the mixed data of all domains (MixSFT and MixRL), and training one teacher per domain with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), directly or after an SFT warm-up, and distilling the teachers into one student with MOPD. We evaluate on 21 image STEM, image perception, and video reasoning benchmarks after we decontaminate the training data against them. The comparison establishes MOPD of teachers trained with GSPO directly from Qwen3.5-9B as the best recipe: the student closely matches each teacher on its own domain and is on average above the mixed-training baselines on the same prompts. Since MOPD already merges the domain capabilities that MixRL would otherwise have to learn, its student is a stronger starting point for RL, and we ask whether the same data can carry the model further from there: we add MixRL on top of the MOPD student, on the same prompts and with the same settings, which, to our knowledge, no prior VLM recipe has studied. The same prompts still improve the MOPD student, with no collapse, and give our main model, M3OPD-Reasoner-9B. MOPD and MixRL can therefore work as consecutive stages that get more out of the same data. We release all data, the training code, and the checkpoints of every stage.
  </details>

- **[Check Your Blind Spots: Decision-Guided Perception by Probing the Planner with Hypothetical Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23030)**  `arXiv:2611.23030`  
  _Hoang Pham Cong, Long D. Nguyen, Ho Viet Duc Luong, Nguyen Tran Minh Nhat, Binh P. Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Not every perception error changes the planner's action. Yet adaptive-resolution perception chooses where to spend high-resolution computation from image cues, which can neither reliably point to an object that low resolution missed nor tell which objects matter to the planner. We observe that the planner can say where an object would matter before anything is seen: inserting a hypothetical object at a location tests whether its action would change. Decision-Guided Speculative Perception (DGSP) treats the low-resolution scene and its action as a draft and probes the planner in this way to obtain a decision-sensitivity field, the locations where an unseen object would change the action. Only this field and the support, the detections on which the current action rests, are verified at high resolution, repeatedly until the action is stable. For two planner classes, we prove that DGSP returns exactly the action of whole-frame high-resolution perception if crop detections match whole-frame ones and the crops cover the field, conditions that real images meet approximately. On three driving datasets, it reproduces this action on about 98% of frames with about a quarter of the pixels. At the pixel cost of uniform downscaling, it repairs more of the draft's disagreements with this action, brakes falsely less often, and recovers many more of the decision-relevant objects that the draft misses.
  </details>

- **[Code World Model 2.0: Towards Infinite Controllable Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22925)**  `arXiv:2611.22925`  
  _Ruoyu Wang, Hao Zhong, Yanming Yang, Jiahao Tian, Lin Liu, Biao Gong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual proxies decouple explicit world-state simulation from photorealistic video synthesis, offering a controllable interface for both offline clip creation and real-time interactive streaming. Yet a practical renderer must address two challenges. Upstream proxies vary from coarse layouts to fine articulated geometry depending on the interaction, and streaming inference must operate in a few causal steps without accumulating drift across self-generated history. We present Invoker-Render, pairing a bidirectional creation model with a causal four-step streaming model under a shared conditioning interface over structural proxies, an appearance reference, and language. Trained on an aligned approximately 200-hour multi-granularity corpus, Invoker-Render honors fine geometric constraints when supplied while synthesizing plausible local motion under coarse proxies, enabling runtime selection of input detail. To bridge bidirectional creation and real-time streaming without compounding step compression and rollout drift, we introduce Progressive Forcing (ProForcing). ProForcing first trains a causal predictor and distills it into a four-step renderer using histories derived from training videos. Training then shifts to continuous self-generated rollouts. A pipelined serving stack supports efficient streaming inference. Experiments show versatile control across proxy families, granularities, and camera paths, consistent gains over recent streaming baselines, and stable 30-minute racing rollouts that preserve scene and subject appearance.
  </details>

- **[Visual Harness Scales General VLMs to Medical Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22815)**  `arXiv:2611.22815`  
  _Shaowen Wan, Hanqi Jiang, Zihao Wu, Junhao Chen, Weihang You, Ruiyu Yan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We show that an appropriate visual harness can substantially improve the performance of general vision-language models (VLMs) across diverse medical imaging tasks. We introduce VERA, a medical visual harness that equips general VLMs with interactive visual tools and systematic diagnostic evidence management. With VERA, general VLMs interactively explore medical images and progressively acquire diagnostic evidence, which is preserved in a lossless memory for subsequent retrieval and use. After submitting an initial answer, the VLM receives an assessment of potential omissions and the reliability of identified findings. This assessment guides further image inspection and measurement, followed by answer revision in an iterative agentic loop until sufficient evidence is obtained. Across 11 tasks and four imaging modalities, VERA improves performance on all tasks with Qwen3.8-27B, with notable gains in spleen measurement (63.3% to 98.3%), renal search (57.5% to 71.2%), and pulmonary nodule detection (0.0% to 28.2%). High-capability API-based VLMs achieve even higher performance with VERA. For example, GPT-6.1 Sol improves pulmonary nodule detection from 10.8% to 69.2% with VERA, demonstrating the complementary benefits of stronger model capabilities and an effective medical visual harness. Our results highlight the potential of visual harnesses as a new paradigm for scaling general VLMs to medical imaging tasks.
  </details>

- **[TANGO: Real-World Unregistered RGB-Guided Hyperspectral Super-Resolution with Two-Camera Acquisitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22699)**  `arXiv:2611.22699`  
  _Matteo Kolyszko, Marco Buzzelli, Simone Bianco, Raimondo Schettini_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unregistered hyperspectral image super-resolution aims to recover a high-resolution HSI from a low-resolution hyperspectral observation and an unregistered high-resolution RGB reference. Although recent methods have begun to address the geometric misalignment between the two modalities, their evaluation is still constrained by the lack of a large-scale public benchmark based on real two-camera RGB-HSI acquisitions with a high-resolution HSI reference. To address this gap, we introduce DUET, the first large-scale public dataset for unregistered RGB-guided hyperspectral super-resolution, comprising 403 indoor and outdoor scenes captured with two separate RGB and hyperspectral cameras. We further propose TANGO (Two-camera AligNment with Gated Occlusions), which first compensates for the dominant cross-camera displacement through end-to-end global alignment and then refines the remaining inconsistencies with a coarse-to-fine local feature alignment. A learned gating mechanism further attenuates the RGB guidance where the two views cannot be consistently matched. Experimental results show that TANGO achieves state-of-the-art performance on both DUET and REAL across all evaluated scale factors, outperforming the strongest competitor on DUET by up to 2.29 dB in PSNR while requiring 2.5$\times$ fewer FLOPs. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Camera: A Tri-Modal Driving World Model for Future RGB, Thermal, and LiDAR Forecasting in Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22669)**  `arXiv:2611.22669`  
  _Faysal Mehrab Chowdhury, Md. Mahfuzur Rahman, Ahmed Rafi Hasan, Kishor Datta Gupta, Mohd Ariful Haque, Savannah Shannon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving world models forecast what a vehicle's sensors will record next. Most forecast camera video and some add LiDAR, but thermal cameras, which still see in darkness and some adverse weather, are rarely a forecast target, and to our knowledge no world model forecasts all three sensors. We study joint forecasting of RGB, thermal, and LiDAR on real adverse-weather sequences of the DSERT-RoLL dataset. We post-train a pretrained video world model for each sensor and represent LiDAR as a depth video aligned with the RGB camera, with densification and encoding chosen by what the model's video tokenizer can reconstruct. A tri-sensor model sees all three sensors as one video. We score LiDAR in meters against measured returns and compare against persistence baselines passed through the same tokenizer as the model. With this matching, an apparent loss of the thermal model to persistence in fog disappears. On all 58 validation sequences in six weather conditions, post-trained RGB and thermal forecasts beat compression-matched persistence. Joint training lowers the LiDAR error at 2s by 2.1m at a small cost in camera fidelity, and only the tri-sensor model's LiDAR forecasts beat persistence in mean error. Blanking one sensor degrades the forecasts of the others more in snow than in fog.
  </details>

- **[MAVE: Reconstructing Editable 3D Blockouts from Film Shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464)**  `arXiv:2611.22464`  
  _Yifan Chang, Jian Yang, Jiangnan Shao, Xiaofeng Wang, Zhaoxin Fan, Renjing Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Decoding Without a Decoder: Direct Pixel Prediction from Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22287)**  `arXiv:2611.22287`  
  _Hansheng Chen, Shengqu Cai, Jan Ackermann, Rayhan Zirvi, Gordon Wetzstein, Xinchen Yan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent diffusion models rely on a decoder to map denoised latents to pixels, adding complexity and inference cost. We introduce Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), which lets a finetuned diffusion transformer produce final pixels directly through a linear head. Unlike previous methods, where the denoising output leads to final pixels, DWD separates the two roles: the original denoising head continues to drive sampling, then the new DWD head predicts the pixels at the final step. We extend pretrained latent models with DWD efficiently by finetuning only the last few transformer blocks, training the DWD head with pixel quality losses while anchoring the denoising head to the pretrained model to preserve the sample trajectory. Across latent image and video generators, DWD removes the decoder while maintaining or even improving pixel quality. On Wan video generation, it visibly reduces detail distortion and flickering, lowering warping error by 23%. Remarkably, the same separation also improves pixel diffusion models that do not even use a decoder: a final-step DWD head produces substantially better texture detail in text-to-image generation, and improves ImageNet FID from 1.55 to 1.43, a new state of the art among pixel flow models.
  </details>

- **[Exploring the link between predictive uncertainty and the sim-to-real gap in deep learning models utilizing synthetic data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21365)**  `arXiv:2611.21365`  
  _Alexander Moriz, Lukas Krebs, Amon Göppert, Robert H. Schmitt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today, state-of-the-art methods for quality control (QC) are based on Machine Vision, combining deep learning (DL) models with camera-based inspection. Yet, the integration of such solutions in industrial applications is often impeded by data sparsity and trust in model reliability. While synthetic data offers a promising approach to generate the necessary data with perfect annotations, it poses risks related to performance degradation due to the possible occurrence of the sim-to-real gap. This study conducts experiments using the ensemble method to estimate the predictive uncertainty of DL models trained on synthetic data. The results show that predictive uncertainty increases under the sim-to-real domain shift. However, the observed performance drop is dominated by a systematic sim-to-real bias rather than by ensemblethis http URLfindings contribute to the effective utilization of synthetic data in industrial settings while highlighting the importance of risk assessment for the deployment of DL solutions for QC tasks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Family Labels: A Taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Prior for Avian 3D Shape Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21102)**  `arXiv:2611.21102`  
  _Yunchan Jeon, SooGon Kim, Seungwook Kim, CheolWon Lee, Jongmin Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering 3D pose and shape of birds from single images is bottlenecked by the long tail of avian diversity: out of ~10,000 species, only a few dozen have 3D-labeled training data, and in-the-wild test sets routinely contain species never seen at training. Generalizing across clades is therefore a central problem. The current state of the art, AniMer+, mitigates it with a family-level contrastive loss that conveys only a binary same-family indicator per pair and discards the continuous structure of the species tree. We propose a taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck prior on the shape code of the AVES parametric bird mesh model: a multivariate Gaussian whose covariance decays exponentially with Linnaean rank distance, with a learnable selection strength and noise scale. The prior generalizes family contrastive into a continuous, generative form, exposing the graded cross- and within-family similarity that a binary indicator cannot, and brings continuous comparative phylogenetics in as a biology-grounded inductive bias for the long tail. Our method improves over AniMer+ on both metrics of the unseen-species CowBird benchmark and on CtrlAVES3D, while remaining comparable on seen-species CUB. A permutation ablation isolates taxonomic structure from generic Gaussian smoothing, and a rank-granularity ablation identifies cross-order distinctions (the structure beyond a family-vs-not indicator) as the dominant source of the unseen-species gain.
  </details>

- **[BlockAlign: Preference Optimization for Trajectory-Distilled Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20351)**  `arXiv:2611.20351`  
  _Jingyuan Zhu, Biaolong Chen, Yangyang Qin, Shengyang Shen, Yaohua Wang, Weiming Bai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Directly aligning trajectory-distilled video generators slashes rollout costs and optimizes the deployed few-step model without teacher fine-tuning or re-distillation. However, conventional flow-matching preference objectives assume single-head local velocity supervision, mismatching the finite-interval, block-average transition interface of trajectory-distilled generators such as Parallel Decoding Distillation (PDD) and Parametric Trajectory Distillation (PTD). Naively supervising individual unfused sub-interval velocity heads across all sub-steps rather than the fused block-average velocity at block starts (DirectAlign) induces a severe training--inference mismatch that degrades preference below the unaligned baseline. We introduce BlockAlign, an interface-consistent preference optimization framework that constructs states at valid block boundaries, queries the fused block-level velocity predictor, and supervises span-normalized transition residuals---seamlessly supporting DPO-, NFT-, and rollout-style objectives while preserving the native few-step sampler. We further build a unified post-training pipeline integrating offline pairs, reward-free semi-online continuations, and online rollouts with hierarchical preference selection and objective-aware block scheduling. Experiments on multi-reference video generation across MiniMax-H3 Ref2VA and an in-house model RefVid-V4 show that BlockAlign consistently outperforms unaligned distilled models and DirectAlign in structural plausibility, reference consistency, and visual quality. Notably, our approach unlocks PDD's 5x few-step rollout speedup for 3x faster end-to-end online training, achieving statistically significant human preference gains over PDD baselines (54.0% vs. 33.3% wins/losses, or 61.8% non-tie win rate on RefVid-V4; 51.3% vs. 34.7%, or 59.7% non-tie win rate on MiniMax-H3) and up to an 82.7% win rate against DirectAlign.
  </details>

- **[Passable for Whom? Few-Shot Learning for Personal Calibration of Sidewalk Accessibility Perception for Human-Centered Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19839)**  `arXiv:2611.19839`  
  _Wesley Maia, Angel Martinez-Sanchez, Maitrayee Keskar, Ross Greer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a sidewalk is passable depends on who is trying to pass: people who use the same mobility aid disagree about the same image, so one label per scene, or per aid, is wrong for many of them. We introduce few-shot personal accessibility perception: from K answers of a person never seen before, predict their judgment of sidewalk scenes never seen before. PassCal separates a shared description of the scene from the person's interpretation of it. The description comes from the internal state of a frozen vision-language model (VLM) and from barrier type, severity and context properties predicted by a detector trained on 14.9k public audits; the interpretation, a calibration of the person's response style and a weighting of the properties, is fit from their answers. On 8,883 judgments in which each of 135 verified mobility-aid users rated the same 50 sidewalk scenes, with people and scenes both held out, eight answers lower the Brier score from 0.550 to 0.479. The same VLM prompted with those answers reaches 0.562, and the same calibration without the scene 0.521. Conformal risk control turns predictions into passable, blocked or "depends on you" statements with a bounded expected rate of wrong statements over people; at 5%, eight answers raise the share of decisive scenes from 27% to 42%. Audit-supervised properties improve all sixteen vision and VLM encoders we test and outperform attributes from a sidewalk navigation VLM.
  </details>

- **[Pros4D: Modeling Evolving 4D Worlds in Latent Space for Dynamic Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19803)**  `arXiv:2611.19803`  
  _Rongxin Gao, Dongxuan Liu, Chu Li, Chengyang Ji, Jialing Huang, Runyu Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic spatial reasoning from monocular video enables vision-language models (VLMs) to understand how 3D environments evolve over time, supporting physical world understanding and embodied intelligence. Existing efforts primarily address observed-dynamics understanding, whereas agents in dynamic environments also require future-dynamics prediction beyond the observation boundary. Moreover, external geometric processing and textual descriptions can increase inference complexity or lose information when representing continuous dynamics. These limitations motivate a shared internal state that preserves observed dynamics and remains predictive beyond the input video. Accordingly, we present Pros4D, a framework for modeling evolving 4D worlds in latent space that unifies retrospective understanding of observed-dynamics with prospective reasoning for future-dynamics prediction. Specifically, Pros4D-Engine derives geometry-grounded supervision and constructs paired examples for both tasks, while Pros4D-Bench evaluates them across eight spatial task categories spanning dynamic cameras, objects, and scenes. At the model level, Pros4D compresses observed dynamics into latent observed states and autoregressively rolls them forward into latent future states. 4D Latent Alignment (4DLA) grounds these states through complementary visual-semantic and geometric supervision, while joint language supervision connects them to task reasoning. Experiments across benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong baselines in both capabilities.
  </details>

- **[VibePrevisBench: Evaluating Agentic Coding for 4D Scene Generation in Blender](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15789)**  `arXiv:2611.15789`  
  _Yuzhe Chen, Muxi Diao, Lele Yang, Wenbin Xu, Xuyang Liu, Qile Su, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Controllable real-world video generation models can condition on a rendered graybox proxy video, which fixes layout, motion and camera. Coding agents can build such videos by generating a 4D scene in Blender, yet no benchmark checks both these rendered proxy videos and the complex, multi-shot scenes behind them. We introduce VibePrevisBench, a benchmark for 4D scene generation: an agent turns a brief into an executable 4D scene and renders its proxy video. Its 242 tasks span four input kinds, including scripts, photos, continuations, and reference videos. Each delivery is scored along two evaluation dimensions. Rule-based Scene Check re-runs the code and compares the exported 4D scene with hidden ground truth facts in nine dimensions. VLM-based Screen Check asks a VLM judge whether facts that pass the rules are recognizable in the proxy video. We analyze the evaluation results and trajectories of 11 frontier coding agents and identify three key observations. First, multimodal understanding separates the agents, especially in understanding space and camera motion. Second, all agents seem to already master basic camera work and staging, and fail at challenging requests. Third, agents inspect their own renders but fail to correct their errors. Agents thus need better visual understanding of space and camera, explicit checks for challenging requests, and item-by-item verification of renders against the brief.
  </details>

- **[AcrossPlace: Do Vision--Language Models Recognize Places Beyond Their Source Views? A Comprehensive Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15314)**  `arXiv:2611.15314`  
  _Zhenyu Li, Tianyi Shang, Shuhan Dong, Ruotong Xu, Qingyu Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-guided place recognition requires recovering geographic identity across visual observations beyond the image from which a description originates. However, aggregate retrieval scores often entangle observation transfer, gallery competition, and linguistic evidence, obscuring the underlying recognition capability. We introduce AcrossPlace, a provenance-aware benchmark establishing a systematic evaluation standard for bidirectional language--vision place recognition. Built upon GSV-Cities, AcrossPlace comprises 56,726 places across 23 cities, 156,800 automatically generated, rule-filtered descriptions, and 113,404 target images. Each description retains its source-image provenance, while retrieval operates on disjoint target observations. Text-to-image (T2I) and image-to-text (I2T) retrieval share a unified place-identity relevance criterion supporting multiple valid matches. Two geographic protocols evaluate unseen places within represented cities (P0) and generalization to held-out cities (P1). Paired observation comparisons, gallery-controlled evaluations, fixed-query scaling, and blinded annotation auditing systematically characterize retrieval performance. Across three training seeds, place-supervised SigLIP2 T2I Recall@1 decreases from 19.69% on source images to 12.31% on independent targets under P0. For fixed P1 queries, expanding the gallery from 500 to 8,425 places reduces Recall@1 from 14.67% to 2.87%. A 240-description audit further reveals discrepancies between automated acceptance and factual evidence. These findings establish AcrossPlace as a reproducible evaluation standard for assessing transferable place identity across visual observations and geographic environments.
  </details>

- **[2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12805)**  `arXiv:2611.12805`  
  _Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $\Phi$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.
  </details>

- **[Graphene: A Lightweight Transformer with Ising Mean-Field Refinement for Image Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11139)**  `arXiv:2611.11139`  
  _Seungki Hong, Taekwang Jang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper proposes Graphene, a lightweight image super-resolution architecture with two complementary feature refinement mechanisms: Ising-model-inspired local feature refinement using an Ising Refinement Block (IRB) and stage-history-based feature refinement using a History Attention (HistAttn) module. The IRB reuses attention output features and pre-softmax attention logits to construct signed pairwise token couplings within each local window, applies unrolled mean-field updates, and refines features through a gated residual correction. These signed couplings allow alignment and opposition between latent spins to modulate local feature refinement. HistAttn attends over the current stage output and earlier representations at the same token position and adaptively blends the current stage output with a history-weighted feature aggregate without adding spatial pairwise attention. On Urban100 at $\times4$, Graphene and Graphene-light improve over IET and IET-light by 0.24 dB and 0.38 dB, respectively, with 34.3%/13.7% fewer parameters, 44.8%/44.4% fewer FLOPs, 83.9%/95.9% lower latency, and 63.7%/92.0% lower peak memory. Real-world SR comparisons further show that Graphene recovers fine details and preserves textures across diverse degradations. Code and reference checkpoints are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[MotionGraphicsBench: Evaluating Motion Graphics Generation with Programmatic Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10592)**  `arXiv:2611.10592`  
  _Jiaju Ma, Ashvini Kumar Jindal, Chuhua Wang, Sumithra Bhakthavatsalam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, outperforming frontier VLM judges by 4.4%-12.8% while achieving at least 90% recall for both human approvals and rejections. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 5.0% to 68.0%.
