Across 15 models at their highest reasoning effort, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We fine-tune Qwen3.8-27B with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) using our verification programs as rewards, improving pass@1 from 30.31% to 52.19% on held-out instances. Our project page is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FusionCLIP: Multimodal Contrastive Pretraining for Fusion Plasma Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09493)**  `arXiv:2611.09493`  
  _Qiang Chen, Xiao Wang, Qingquan Yang, Hao Si, Zikang Yan, Guosheng Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visible-light videos and multivariate diagnostic signals provide complementary observations of tokamak discharges. However, existing fusion pretraining methods primarily learn representations by reconstructing or predicting observations, leaving the correspondence between these modalities underexplored. Representation learning is further challenged by spatially localized plasma regions in video frames and substantial differences in diagnostic sampling rates. We propose FusionCLIP, a multimodal contrastive pretraining framework. A selective video encoder retains informative visual tokens, while a query-based time-series encoder integrates asynchronous diagnostic signals using their observation timestamps. Contrastive learning aligns representations of the two modalities. We construct the EAST Multimodal Diagnostics (EAST-MMD) dataset, comprising 1307 EAST discharges, and evaluate the model through independent fine-tuning on three tasks: disruption prediction, edge-localized mode (ELM) classification, and H98 regression. FusionCLIP achieves a Joint score of 89.07%, with absolute gains of 2.41% and 2.90% over the best competing baseline, and the in-domain contrastive pretraining baseline CLIP-Base, respectively. Ablations and attention visualizations further support the contributions of its key components.
  </details>

- **[Look Closer, Align Better: Explicit Visual Attributes for Robust EEG-to-Image Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09123)**  `arXiv:2611.09123`  
  _Ke Xiao, Banghua Yang, Wenbin Guo, Fenqi Rong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EEG-to-image retrieval commonly aligns neural responses with representations from pretrained vision encoders. However, these representations may underrepresent fine-grained visual details needed to distinguish semantically similar images. To address this limitation, we first investigate the EEG decodability and complementary predictive value of explicit visual attributes, identifying visual cues that capture EEG-relevant information beyond pretrained representations. Building on these findings, we propose AttrEcho, a framework that enriches pretrained visual representations with complementary, spatially aligned visual attributes. Specifically, Region-Aligned Visual Attribute Fusion (RAVF) integrates EEG-relevant local visual attributes with spatially corresponding pretrained features through a learnable signed residual gate. We further employ Feature Token Aggregation (FTA), which learns dimension-specific weights across regions to aggregate refined visual features for contrastive alignment with EEG representations. Experiments on THINGS-EEG demonstrate that AttrEcho achieves 94.8% Top-1 and 99.7% Top-5 accuracy in intra-subject 200-way retrieval, while maintaining 82.1% Top-1 accuracy as the gallery expands to 1,600 images with same-concept distractors. AttrEcho also achieves 25.3% Top-1 and 53.0% Top-5 accuracy in inter-subject retrieval. These results highlight the importance of EEG-relevant visual attributes and spatially aligned fusion for fine-grained EEG-to-image retrieval.
  </details>

- **[EgoGeneralist: Unifying Egocentric Action-Video Tasks through Video-Native Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08477)**  `arXiv:2611.08477`  
  _Yanlin Jin, Bowen Yang, Cheng Zhang, Yifan Yang, Zhe Li, Jianfei Cai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern video models possess strong visual priors, yet do not natively represent human action in metric 3D space. We present EgoGeneralist, which adapts pretrained video generators to unify action-conditioned video generation, pose estimation, and joint action-video generation for egocentric manipulation. Our role-adaptive action renderer provides video-native representations for multidirectional action-video learning, making metric motion usable as both a spatial control signal and a recoverable generation target. Cross-stream action-video coupling establishes close correspondence between metric motion and visual dynamics through lightweight adaptation while preserving the pretrained visual prior. Together, these designs establish a generalist framework spanning six action-video tasks. With only narrow-domain data, EgoGeneralist generalizes to diverse unseen scenes and interactions with effective action control and visual quality, bridging pretrained visual knowledge and metric 3D human motion.
  </details>

- **[GenRA: Ultra-Low-Bitrate Generative Video Compression via Representation Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08099)**  `arXiv:2611.08099`  
  _Jiancheng Zhao, Jixiang Luo, Haibin Huang, Chi Zhang, Xuelong Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preserving fine visual details in ultra-low-bitrate video compression remains challenging due to the loss of critical information. Recent generative video compression methods exploit generative priors to improve perceptual quality. These pretrained generative representations are generally not optimized for compression. Some approaches therefore use an additional learned codec to map them into a separate latent space for compression. To this end, we propose GenRA, which adapts pretrained video representations for direct quantization and entropy coding without an additional learned codec. Specifically, we use vector quantization to adapt a pretrained video VAE into a discrete video tokenizer. A lightweight categorical autoregressive entropy model combines local and global context to model the probabilities of the resulting token indices. We jointly optimize the tokenizer and entropy model using a differentiable rate surrogate, allowing the encoder to adjust token assignments based on coding cost and reconstruction quality. Experiments on UVG, MCL-JCV, and HEVC Class B show average bitrate savings of 84.1% and 82.3% over GLC-Video in terms of LPIPS and DISTS, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Integrating Learned Priors through Prior-Space Reparameterization for Physically Based Inverse Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07573)**  `arXiv:2611.07573`  
  _Hun Hwangbo, Minjun Park, Yongsung Kim, Wooseok Song, Jungmin Ko, Junghyun Hwang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physically based inverse rendering is an ambiguous problem, allowing independently optimized material variables to absorb appearance variations caused by illumination. Learned material predictions provide useful cues about which surface points have similar materials. Existing approaches often incorporate these cues through auxiliary regularization losses. Instead, we propose a novel prior-guided reparameterization of the material optimization space. We fuse multi-view material predictions onto a mesh and construct a permutohedral lattice in the resulting prior feature space. Surface points with similar predictions share lattice variables, restricting the material field to a prior-defined subspace that is optimized jointly with geometry and illumination using a purely photometric objective. Experiments on three synthetic benchmarks demonstrate improved material decomposition and relighting, with results on par with the strongest baseline on Stanford-ORB. As a complete system, our method improves relighting PSNR by 1.4-1.5\,dB over the strongest evaluated mesh-based baseline on Synthetic4Relight and TensoIR-Synthetic. Analysis of the optimization shows that shared variables pool per-vertex photometric gradients into updates that are more consistent across sampled views. In a controlled comparison within the same pipeline, the restricted subspace, despite its reduced degrees of freedom, yields more accurate material decomposition than both unrestricted per-vertex optimization and a regularization loss built from the same prior.
  </details>

- **[VidDub: Replacement-Aware Supervision for Two-Step Video Dubbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03404)**  `arXiv:2611.03404`  
  _Yexin Liu, Yunlong Lin, Zehan Wang, Guangxin He, Xinli Xu, Jiajun Zha, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video dubbing aims to synthesize lip movements that match target speech while preserving the speaker identity, head pose, and background of a source video. Existing editors are predominantly trained via self-reconstruction under original paired audio, even though the source mouth becomes a conflicting target when driving speech is replaced. We introduce \emph{replacement-aware cross-audio supervision}: paired-audio samples use full-frame reconstruction, whereas unpaired replacement-audio samples mask out source-mouth reconstruction losses and supervise the mouth solely through audio--visual synchronization while preserving unmasked facial regions and background. Because offline dubbing provides the complete source video and target speech beforehand, we present \method, a full-context diffusion editor that uses matched trajectory regression to transfer CFG-guided teacher trajectories to a two-step student and distribution matching to recover visual quality after compression. We will release the curated training dataset, VidDub-Bench, fixed paired- and cross-audio evaluation manifests, preprocessing outputs, and unified evaluation code. Across three training seeds, masking out conflicting source-mouth reconstruction improves cross-audio Sync-C by 0.833. At two NFE, the final student provides the strongest cross-audio synchronization and visual-speech recognition among the evaluated methods, with competitive visual fidelity and source preservation.
  </details>

- **[OctreeSplat: Hierarchical Octree-Guided Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02658)**  `arXiv:2611.02658`  
  _Yibin Zhao, Yihan Pan, Wenli Yang, Jun Nan, Jianjun Yi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has shown considerable potential for novel view synthesis (NVS) and 3D reconstruction. Feed-forward methods avoid costly per-scene optimization, but fixed image or spatial resolutions limit their ability to allocate primitives according to local complexity. We present OctreeSplat, a hierarchical octree-guided feed-forward Gaussian splatting. OctreeSplat extracts multi-view features and geometric priors from a frozen visual foundation model (VFM), constructs a hierarchical octree, and predicts densification scores. The resulting representation allocates Gaussian primitives at spatially varying densities according to local complexity. Stochastic solids volume rendering improves depth rendering, strengthens geometric supervision, and supports surface reconstruction. When camera parameters are available, an optional test-time optimization (TTO) module improves agreement between predicted and ground-truth parameters. Training updates only 76 million additional parameters and requires 35 GPU-hours. Experiments on Replica, TUM, Tanks and Temples, and DTU demonstrate competitive RGB view synthesis, depth synthesis, and surface reconstruction. OctreeSplat reduces the number of Gaussian primitives while maintaining high reconstruction quality.
  </details>

- **[GSExplorer: Interactive World Generation with a Persistent 3DGS World State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01543)**  `arXiv:2611.01543`  
  _Sicheng Yu, Liu Liu, Xinjie Wang, Xinjiang Wang, Taojun Ding, Wenhao Hu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive world generation requires both accurate local control and stable scene consistency over long rollouts. Existing methods improve local generation through camera or geometry conditioning and extend historical context with memory mechanisms. However, long-horizon exploration remains challenging due to incomplete target-view guidance and inconsistencies accumulated during scene updates. We present \textbf{GSExplorer}, a generation--reconstruction framework built around a persistent 3D Gaussian world state. At each step, the current 3DGS scene provides multimodal rendering conditions for a video generator, while camera geometry is injected through a separate Relative Ray Encoding branch. Generated videos are then fed back to a SLAM-based reconstruction backend, where appearance and geometric consistency checks produce a shared support mask for selective scene updates and cross-chunk pose refinement. We further design a data-construction pipeline that reproduces realistic 3DGS degradation during scene extrapolation, yielding 293K training pairs of degraded multimodal renderings and real target videos. GSExplorer achieves the best overall performance among the compared methods on both single-step and three-step WorldScore evaluation, including a Camera Control score of 99.07 in single-step extrapolation. On 481-frame leave-and-return rollouts, it also shows strong revisit consistency.
  </details>

- **[PhoneValue-Bench: A Dual-Ground-Truth Scorecard Benchmark for Smartphone Recommerce Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00791)**  `arXiv:2611.00791`  
  _Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Junjie Huang, Xiaozhen Zhong, Du Tiantian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote smartphone recommerce is scaling quickly, and locking a repurchase offer from seller photos and diagnostic screens before the handset arrives has become a central operational task. Existing multimodal resources mainly target defect localization, catalog attributes, or screen reading. They overlook payout-facing scorecards with dual cosmetic and operational ground truth under a declared input contract. This gap stems from two challenges: (1) the lack of a large weakly labeled trade-in corpus kept separate from a compact audited ranking cut, and (2) architectures that either flatten unordered photo bags into one prompt or collapse evaluation into a single price scalar. To address these challenges, we propose PhoneValue-Bench, a dual-ground-truth recommerce framework with PhoneValue-Corpus for weak training on 21,550 orders and 278,748 images, and PV-Rank as the only leaderboard on 335 quality-gated orders. Systems are ranked by CardScore under LeadGate, with Cosmetic Fill and Operational Fill as diagnostics, while grade and price stay off the board. Notably, ValueAgent is a six-stage perception-fusion reference that canonicalizes views, detects regional defects, reads screenshots, and fuses evidence under warehouse-style rules, so bag-of-images prompting is not the only evaluation unit. On the same PV-Rank cut and frozen scorer, this full-system and input-contract comparison finds ValueAgent at 84.1 CardScore against the strongest Flat Vision-Language Model at 69.9, with the largest reported gap in CosFill, while OpsFill remains limited under the two declared contracts. CardScore measures field-fill completeness, not quote accuracy.
  </details>

- **[TurboWAM: Learning What Matters for Fast World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33941)**  `arXiv:2610.33941`  
  _Guile Wu, Shuangzhi Li, Xingxin Chen, Chengjie Huang, Sam Hosseini, Tongtong Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models have demonstrated promising performance in robot policy learning, yet their high inference latency remains a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. Recent approaches have sought to improve inference efficiency by eliminating test-time future-frame imagination or accelerating action prediction. However, aggregating representations from all video world model layers can introduce redundant computation, while standard backpropagation may distribute action-supervision gradients unevenly across layers, limiting the effectiveness of world representation learning. In this work, we propose TurboWAM, a fast world action model that learns to efficiently aggregate and optimize world representations for action prediction. Specifically, TurboWAM introduces an efficient world representation aggregation mechanism that restricts aggregation to a bounded range of relevant world model layers identified through layer relevance analysis. Within this bounded range, TurboWAM estimates aggregation weights for different world model layers based on the current action denoising state and combines them with learnable layer-wise weights to fuse world representations. Moreover, TurboWAM employs a world model gradient modulation mechanism that decomposes action-supervision gradients into a layer-shared component and layer-specific residual components, enabling more balanced gradient propagation across world model layers. This mechanism operates exclusively during training and introduces no additional inference computation. Together, these designs enable more efficient world representation learning and low-latency action prediction. Extensive experiments on multiple robot manipulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks show that TurboWAM achieves low inference latency while maintaining competitive task success rates compared with state-of-the-art methods.
  </details>

- **[Tempo-Zero: Recursive Self-Play in Executable Worlds for Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32433)**  `arXiv:2610.32433`  
  _Jingmin Zhu, Yanyan Zou, Xiuyu Li, James Bailey, Dinh Phung, Tao Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video reasoning requires understanding how events unfold over time, yet videos with accurate temporal annotations are expensive to collect. Self-play offers an alternative by letting a model generate and solve its own tasks without manual annotation. However, existing video self-play methods train on fixed sets of recorded videos, so they cannot alter the underlying event dynamics and rely on noisy pseudo-labels derived from the model's own outputs, such as majority voting, that risk reinforcing shared model errors. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Tempo-Zero, a self-improvement framework that closes a loop of executable environments, execution-verified self-play, and environment evolution, where executing a program renders a video with the ground-truth option and interval of every question. A single policy plays both Questioner and Solver, proposing questions and answering them with timestamped evidence lines, and is trained on execution-grounded rewards instead of model consensus. The Solver's performance steers a curriculum over tasks and difficulty levels, and the policy selects rule changes that enrich the event dynamics of later rounds. Despite being trained solely on simple programs, without supervised fine-tuning or external video data which prior methods typically require, Tempo-Zero outperforms the base models and recent self-play baselines on average across twelve real-world video benchmarks at different model scales, with particularly strong gains on temporal grounding tasks. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[CAPTOR: a vision-language foundation model for Chest-Abdomen-Pelvis Oncologic CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14226)**  `arXiv:2610.14226`  
  _Séléna Jean-Mactoux, Sophie Loizillon, Ghina Jardali, Amandine Crombé, Nathalie Lassau, Alexandre Bône_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objectives: Radiologists interpreting oncologic chest-abdomen-pelvis (CAP) CT must also detect non-oncologic conditions, yet existing CT foundation models are trained on single anatomical regions or on non-oncologic data. We developed and evaluated CAPTOR, a vision-language foundation model for oncologic CAP CT that uses an anatomically structured preprocessing strategy to learn from whole examinations.Materials & Methods: CAPTOR is a contrastive vision-language model pretrained on 25,512 predominantly oncologic CAP CT-report pairs (single or multiple series) from a retrospective multicenter dataset (North America, Europe, Asia). It was compared with five CT foundation models, a larger-scale variant (CAPTOR-100K) and an ablation without anatomical preprocessing (CAPTOR-NoPre) on classification and retrieval using held-out internal and public test sets, and on survival prediction using TumorImagingBench. Confidence intervals and pairwise comparisons used patient-level bootstrap and permutation tests.Results: On 10,794 test studies, CAPTOR achieved higher AUC than all five models on CAP (0.767 vs. 0.695 for the next-best model; p<0.001) and oncologic subsets (0.784 vs. 0.734; p<0.001), with superior retrieval on both, and ranked first on three of four oncologic survival benchmarks. Despite substantially fewer training examinations, it performed comparably to CAPTOR-100K. Removing the anatomically structured preprocessing reduced classification performance, including on the institutionally held-out test subset (macro-AUC 0.737 vs. 0.691 for CAPTOR-NoPre, p<0.01).Conclusion: Anatomically structured preprocessing enabled whole-examination CAP CT interpretation, including multiple-series acquisitions, and improved downstream performance. CAPTOR outperformed state-of-the-art CT foundation models on CAP and oncologic subsets while matching a substantially larger-scale variant.
  </details>

- **[Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14094)**  `arXiv:2610.14094`  
  _Tongxi Wu, Yuyang Cheng, Jiacheng Xian, Hu Huang, Zhihao Wu, Menglin Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Most prior attacks rely on specially constructed visual inputs to carry or support harmful intent. In this paper, we uncover a different vulnerability: task-relevant scene semantics alone can shift an MLLM from refusal to compliance, even when the visual input introduces no additional attack instructions and the original task intent remains unchanged. We formalize this failure as a scene-grounded visual safety vulnerability and introduce SceneSafetyBench to investigate it across eight risk categories and nine white- and black-box MLLMs. Our analysis reveals that this failure is associated with how task-relevant visual evidence influences downstream safety representations, suggesting that visual grounding alone cannot fully explain the observed safety shift. Building on this finding, we propose Scene-Grounded Attacks (SGA), which preserve the original task while constructing validated scene-grounded queries and combining their independent responses. Across nine target MLLMs, SGA achieves an average attack success rate (ASR) of 86.17%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 8.55 percentage points and achieving the highest ASR on eight models. Our results identify scene semantics as an overlooked attack surface in MLLMs and highlight a growing tension between stronger visual grounding and robust multimodal safety alignment.
  </details>

- **[An Invariant Tangent-Angle Descriptor and a Band U-Net for 2D Fragment Adjacency Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09459)**  `arXiv:2610.09459`  
  _Guillaume Brouillette, Alain Goupil, Pierre-Olivier Parisé, Fadel Touré_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reassembling a broken artefact begins with deciding which fragments are adjacent. This paper addresses adjacency prediction for pairs of 2D fragments by analysing their contours. We propose a two-stage pipeline. In the first stage, each contour point is encoded using a tangent-angle profile, a descriptor that does not depend on rotation, translation or the choice of starting point. Pairs of profiles are then used to build an adjacency matrix, each entry computed either from a log-likelihood ratio or using a small 1D convolutional network. In the second stage, a U-Net classifies this matrix to predict whether the two fragments are adjacent. We compared our pipeline with two approaches from the literature using their own datasets. The results show that our approach outperforms them by reaching a Recall@10 of 0.823 versus 0.564 on the real dataset of PairingNet, and an accuracy of 0.979 versus 0.916 on Beaulac's dataset.
  </details>

- **[No Corners Cut: State-Grounded Transitions for Mid-Stream Prompt Switches in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38691)**  `arXiv:2609.38691`  
  _Zejing Rao, Ketong Ren, Xiaoqiang Liu, Yiping Meng, Guoxin Zhang, Fan Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video generators allow users to dynamically modulate video synthesis via mid-stream prompt switching. Existing streaming methods can respond to the updated instruction while still cutting corners, prematurely realizing goals or taking heuristic shortcuts that bypass necessary intermediate state changes needed for a plausible transition. In this study, we present SEGUE, a novel framework that makes this process explicit and trains the generator to execute these transitions faithfully. At each switch, a training-free planner parses the latest frame and prompts, writes a few segue prompts with roles and durations, and then hands control back to the user's prompt. Furthermore, to address the inherent difficulty of training causal models on short-lived temporal schedules without corrupting preparatory supervision, we introduce SpanDMD, which evaluates each active prompt using the full rollout as temporal context while retaining its DMD residual only within the prompt's assigned span. On OpenTrans-360, a benchmark of 1,800 switches that scores how the old state exits and the new one begins, SEGUE ranks first on all eight transition metrics and raises the overall score over the strongest baseline from 0.866 to 0.887. It leads on four of six StreamAV-Bench instruction-response metrics and achieves the highest CLIP score in all five post-switch segments of VBench-Long evaluation, while the planner transfers to frozen autoregressive generators without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Masked Swingers: Harnessing Data Augmentation to Advance Autoencoders for Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38278)**  `arXiv:2609.38278`  
  _Anthony Fuller, Scott C. Lowe, Daniel G. Kyrollos, Graham W. Taylor, Evan Shelhamer, James R. Green_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning (SSL) trains on inputs alone, allowing us to make models that are more useful in more settings. The autoencoder SSL framework learns by reconstructing its own input after information loss through a bottleneck or noise injection. Masked autoencoders (MAE) are the most successful instantiation of this framework: they encode a random subset of patches, then decode the masked-out patches. In this work, we introduce key modifications to improve MAEs. Our method augments an image in two different ways, then masks and encodes each view separately. It then exchanges the global representations (CLS tokens) between views before decoding the masked patches. By design, our Masked Swingers encourages learning a view-agnostic summary of the image for efficient transfer. We perform extensive experiments, and find Masked Swingers outperforms the official MAE checkpoint by +10.7% averaged over 10 frozen-feature tasks at equal pre-training compute. Gains are highest on fine-grained tasks, e.g., instance retrieval. Swingers also reduces error by 64% relative to MAE on three new state-probing datasets for world modeling, while keeping the fine-tunability of MAE. Post-training by swinging nearest neighbors gains again, e.g., by +5% on IN-1K kNN, opening the door to learning invariances via autoencoding.
  </details>

- **[Reimagine Video Dynamics: A Unified Framework for Video Editing with Editable Spatiotemporal Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36496)**  `arXiv:2609.36496`  
  _Yu Yuan, Yawen Lu, Guoxian Song, Kevin Duarte, Ratheesh Kalarot, Di Chang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most video editing methods can change how a scene looks but offer limited control over how it moves. We introduce Reimagine Video Dynamics (RVD), a unified framework for editing actions, motion pace, and appearance with reusable dynamics tokens and a shared renderer. The tokens capture motion from the source video and retain an explicit temporal axis. A language-guided editor transforms them to change an action or event. Resampling them changes the pace of motion, while keeping them fixed allows the renderer to reproduce the motion with a new appearance. We train the renderer to reconstruct source videos. We pretrain the editor on motion from real videos, then fine-tune it on counterfactual pairs. Across 450 dynamics edits, RVD achieves the highest instruction-following score and an 80.0\% pass rate. The same renderer supports retiming and motion reuse without additional task-specific training.
  </details>

- **[Difficulty-Aware Statistical Matching for Dataset Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34299)**  `arXiv:2609.34299`  
  _Hongxu Ma, Guang Li, Shijie Wang, Dongzhan Zhou, Suorong Yang, Baoli Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dataset distillation (DD) condenses a large original set into a small distilled set with high training utility. Decoupled statistical matching methods substantially reduce distillation time and memory overhead while achieving strong performance. However, they typically supervise all distilled data with running statistics estimated from the entire original set. These statistics mainly capture the average feature distribution while ignoring the difficulty differences among original data, making them insufficient for characterizing the difficulty structure of the original data. To address this issue, we propose Difficulty-aware Statistical Matching (DSM) for Dataset Distillation. After pretraining, DSM uses the Global Difficulty Score (GDS) to evaluate image difficulty, sorts the original data according to the scores, and then partitions it into IPC (Images per Class of distilled data) difficulty groups. During distillation, Statistics Updated Again (SUA) updates the teacher's BN running statistics using original data from each group via forward passes, providing difficulty-specific supervision for each corresponding distilled batch. Meanwhile, Initial Sample Screening (ISS) initializes distilled data with original images from the corresponding group, providing an effective starting point for precise matching. Experiments across multiple datasets and model architectures demonstrate the effectiveness of DSM, showing that it broadens the difficulty range of distilled samples and further improves the final performance. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Reconstruction-Generation Gap in Representation Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31620)**  `arXiv:2609.31620`  
  _Hongyang Du, Yunfei Xie, Junjie Ye, Jiawei Yang, Xiaoyan Cong, Haodong Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fusing shallower encoder layers into a Representation Autoencoder (RAE) sharpens pixel reconstruction but degrades diffusion generation. Prior studies and the research community commonly attribute this trade-off to the latent content itself, assuming detail-rich features are harder to model. We show that this gap stems from geometric mismatch at the encoder-decoder interface. Freezing a foundation encoder preserves rich visual information, but imposes an encoder-native Euclidean metric shaped without pixel decoding, whereas the decoder amplifies sampling errors sharply along sensitive directions. At fixed information, reducing pixel error admits two dual geometric interventions: regularizing the decoder to flatten its sensitivity to latent variations, and aligning diffusion coordinates with the frozen decoder's metric at identical clean reconstruction. On ImageNet-256, aligning diffusion coordinates largely closes the guided generation penalty of multi-layer fusion and reaches state-of-the-art 0.984 gFID and 2.14 FDr6 in 80 epochs. RAEs should take information from the encoder and geometry from the decoder.
  </details>

- **[Distilling a Geospatial Foundation Model into Compact Surface-Water Segmenters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20441)**  `arXiv:2609.20441`  
  _Fabian Schmalstieg, Karsten Mueller, Wojciech Samek_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geospatial foundation models segment surface water accurately, but their size limits deployment on edge hardware. Therefore, we distill a 300-million-parameter Prithvi-EO-2.0 teacher, fine-tuned on the 252 hand-labeled Sen1Floods11 training images, into compact student models. The teacher labels 2,500 Sentinel-2 images around flood alerts and supervises the students via its labels and probability maps, without manual annotation. A 0.7-million-parameter EfficientViT-B0 student achieves the teacher's accuracy in water intersection over union (IoU) on WorldFloods-v2, STURM-Flood and 97 held-back WorldFloods events. Unlike prior work, we evaluate every model on four test sets from three public datasets and on two new dry-scene sets. Controlled comparisons show that teacher labels achieve human-level annotation quality for student training on the same images. The teacher's probability maps account for much of its value, and the full pipeline adds 5.7 IoU points on the held-back events over training on hand labels alone. All students, with parameter counts ranging from about 1,000 to 32.5 million, perform within 2 IoU points of the teacher on WorldFloods-v2 and STURM-Flood. On a Jetson Xavier NX, the main student runs as a 1.6-megabyte mixed 8-bit integer and 16-bit floating-point engine at 4.9 milliseconds per 512-by-512 image, and a 12,230-parameter student runs in full 8-bit integer format within 0.5 IoU points of its 32-bit version. The main student outperforms a fixed water index by 11.5 IoU points on the held-back events and raises false alarms on under 10\% rather than 57.0\% of water-free windows, as unattended use requires.
  </details>

- **[Ischemic Stroke Segmentation and Net Water Uptake Quantification on Multicenter Non-Contrast CT Using Supervised Target-Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20151)**  `arXiv:2609.20151`  
  _Linus Britt, Maximilian Nielsen, Susan Klapproth, Andre Kemmling, Michael H. Lev, Gabriel Broocks, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantitative assessment of infarct hypodensity on non-contrast computed tomography (NCCT), including net water uptake (NWU), requires manual or semi-manual lesion delineation, often guided by CT perfusion or diffusion-weighted MRI, limiting clinical applicability. Automated NCCT segmentation could enable such biomarker extraction but remains challenging across heterogeneous multicenter data. This study developed and externally tested a deep learning framework for ischemic stroke segmentation and NWU quantification on NCCT. In this retrospective multicenter study of 801 patients from four datasets, an nnU-Net-based model with foundation-model initialization, extended by modules comparing local features with the contralateral hemisphere, was trained on NCCT scans from the University Medical Center Hamburg-Eppendorf and the Acute Ischemic Stroke Dataset. The model was fine-tuned on target-domain subsets of the Boston (n=11) and ISLES (n=75) datasets, with evaluation on held-out cases. Segmentations and NWU were compared with expert references. Internally, mean Dice increased from 0.53 (conventional nnU-Net) to 0.57 (proposed approach). After fine-tuning, the proposed approach achieved median Dice of 0.56 for acute lesion segmentation (Boston) and 0.26 for post-treatment final-infarct prediction (ISLES), where small lesions predominated. For lesions >=30 mL, median Dice increased to 0.67 and 0.57, respectively. On the Boston dataset, automated and reference NWU agreed (concordance correlation coefficient 0.93, mean absolute error 1.24 percentage points). Contralateral feature comparison and target-domain adaptation supported NCCT-only infarct segmentation across heterogeneous external cohorts, although performance was lower for post-treatment final-infarct prediction. The approach enabled low-error NWU quantification from baseline NCCT without advanced imaging or manual annotation.
  </details>

- **[Small Object Detection in Drone Aerial Imagery with LAF-YOLOv10](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14560)**  `arXiv:2609.14560`  
  _Quratulain Nayeem, Fahmina Taranum, Mohammed Mudassir Uddin, Sara Rizwan, Samia Areej_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose object detectors lose accuracy on UAV footage, where targets span only a handful of pixels and onboard compute is limited. Prior work composes independently-validated architectural techniques into one detector, assuming gains reported in isolation transfer once combined. We stress-test that assumption directly. LAF-YOLOv10 integrates four techniques into YOLOv10n: a Partial Convolution C2f (PC-C2f) backbone block, an Attention-Guided Feature Pyramid Network (AG-FPN), a P2 detection head replacing the large-object P5 head, and Wise-IoU v3 regression, asking whether their combined effect matches what each contributes alone. We train LAF-YOLOv10 three times (seeds 42, 123, 256) on VisDrone-DET2019, benchmark against unmodified YOLOv10n, and use TIDE error decomposition, per-category breakdown, per-component ablation, attention/loss comparisons, zero-shot transfer to UAVDT, and held-out/test-dev evaluation to localize where the combination succeeds or fails. Composability does not hold here. LAF-YOLOv10 reaches 24.0+/-0.4% mAP@0.5 at 2.14M parameters, 7.8 points below YOLOv10n (31.8%), a deficit that transfers to UAVDT (-10.0 points) and is confirmed by held-out and test-dev evaluation (23.5%, 22.5%). Background false positives, localization error, and duplicate detections move in the direction AG-FPN and Wise-IoU were designed to push. Ablation traces the deficit to a specific source: the P2/-P5 head swap costs 2.5 points independently plus a 2.5-point interaction penalty when layered onto a backbone weakened by PC-C2f, whose own 2.0-point loss is consistent with a partial pretrained-weight transplant (73/150 backbone tensors transfer). The failure is attributable to a specific interaction, not the four components individually. Composability must be verified directly, not assumed. Code/checkpoints:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Synthetic Leprosy Image Generation Using Mask-Conditioned Latent Diffusion and Transfer Learning from Large Chronic Wound Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13226)**  `arXiv:2609.13226`  
  _Yusuf Abdulkadir_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning for neglected tropical diseases is limited by data, not algorithms: public annotated image sets for leprosy (Hansen's disease) number in the hundreds. We ask whether a model trained on abundant chronic wound photography transfers to this low-data regime. We build a three-stage pipeline: a DeepLabV3-ResNet50 segmenter supplies lesion masks for two wound datasets that ship without them; a mask-conditioned latent diffusion model built from Stable Diffusion 1.5 components is trained on 3,280 wound crops; and the model is fine-tuned on leprosy image-mask pairs from CO2Wounds-V2 (764 images of 96 patients). We evaluate on 136 held-out leprosy lesions using LPIPS anchored by a real-versus-real baseline, kernel inception distance (KID), and a paired design that runs both models on the same input. Fine-tuning moved the generated distribution toward real held-out leprosy images: KID fell from 0.167 to 0.089, and LPIPS distance to real images from 0.806 to 0.765, with non-overlapping 95% confidence intervals. Giving the base model the leprosy prompt alone did not reproduce the gain (KID 0.250). For a fixed input, fine-tuning changes the output less than a new random seed does (paired LPIPS 0.544 versus 0.728): it keeps layout and alters tissue color and texture. The generated set shows no mode collapse (internal diversity 0.734 versus 0.667 for real images). A dataset audit shows that a majority of training images carry synthetic flat regions from preprocessing, which the fine-tuned model partly reproduces. Chronic wound photography is a viable donor domain for leprosy lesion synthesis; the remaining barrier is semantic control rather than image quality.
  </details>

- **[Adapting a Foundation Model for Lunar Surface Height Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02448)**  `arXiv:2609.02448`  
  _Patrick Bauer, Marius Schwinning, Melanie Siegel, Andreas Weinmann, Hichem Snoussi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Digital elevation models (DEMs) can provide accurate height information, making it invaluable for analyzing the lunar surface. Due to recent advancements in computer vision, the focus is on generating DEMs using monocular depth estimation via deep learning. Classic lunar crater detection methods rely mainly on 2D image data. Our goal is to address this issue by developing a relative lunar surface height estimator that could provide additional information for crater localization. In this letter, we present a methodology that builds on the zero-shot relative depth estimation model Depth Anything V2 (DAV2). Other works have been using it as a state-of-the-art comparison for their proposed lunar DEM estimation method, but without adaptations to the target domain. Thus, it may underperform. Therefore, we propose a fine-tuning strategy with publicly available SPG-derived DEM data of the lunar surface, and refer to it as DEM-DAV2. Specifically, we introduce new learnable parameters in the Transformer-based encoder with low-rank adaptation (LoRA), and we unfreeze certain layers in the model's decoder. On our test set, the proposed DEM-DAV2 reduces the average MAE and RMSE by $\sim$80\% and $\sim$79\%, respectively, compared to the zero-shot DAV2. This demonstrates a significant improvement in performance compared to the zero-shot model, effectively transforming DAV2 into a reliable relative height estimator of the lunar surface. However, the predictions remain relative and require scale recovery from a low-resolution DEM such as SLDEM.
  </details>

- **[RadMatch: Auditable Radiology Report Evaluation via Finding-Level Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01470)**  `arXiv:2609.01470`  
  _Charles Corbière, Léo Machado, Baptiste Callard, Aubin Charley, Korentin Le Floch, Martin J. Willemink, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI systems are increasingly used to draft radiology reports, reliably evaluating their clinical quality remains a critical challenge. We introduce RadMatch, a multi-stage LLM-based framework that turns report evaluation into a structured, auditable finding-level record. It extracts findings from a reference and a candidate report, matches them by clinical equivalence, grades each match over ten clinical attributes, and gives every match and unmatched finding a verdict and a clinical-significance tier. To validate metrics on CT, we build and release MetricEval-BodyCT, the first public metric-evaluation benchmark of chest and abdomen CT reports annotated by radiologists error by error. On it and three chest X-ray benchmarks, RadMatch with an open 31B judge on consumer GPUs agrees with radiologists more closely than previous published metrics, and even reaches inter-expert agreement on ReXVal and on the significant errors of MetricEval-BodyCT. Radiologists auditing its decisions on 60 studies confirm 98.5% of its matches and 93.6% of the errors it counts. When used to evaluate state-of-the-art radiology report generation models, RadMatch shows that most CT models, unlike several chest X-ray models, actually leave more significant errors than an image-blind normal template, as their added unsupported findings outnumber the reference findings they correctly report. We provide RadMatch's code, its dashboard, the MetricEval-BodyCT dataset and all per-report scores.
  </details>

- **[Visual Token Coding for Video Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28008)**  `arXiv:2608.28008`  
  _Chenxin Fang, Tao Chen, JunChao You, Jun Peng, Yiyi Zhou, Rongrong Ji_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a new token compression paradigm for video Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), termed Visual Token Coding ($VTC$). Inspired by classical video coding principles, e.g., HEVC, VTC performs structured compression by predicting the I/P frames of a video and measuring their frame-wise residuals to estimate token redundancy. Based on this base paradigm, we further extend VTC with a set of dynamic budgeting designs towards more cost-effective token coding, termed $VTC_{dy}$. To validate VTC and $VTC_{dy}$, we apply them to three MLLMs and conduct experiments on a set of video understanding benchmarks. The experimental results show that as a base paradigm, VTC can achieve an average performance retention of 98.4% on Qwen3-VL under a 50% token compression ratio, and the enhanced $VTC_{dy}$ can further improve the retention to 100.3%, outperforming a set of compared methods. Moreover, as a plug-and-play design, VTC requires no additional tuning of MLLMs for token coding. Our code is given in the supplementary materials.
  </details>

- **[Video-OPSD: Exploiting Privileged Visual Evidence for On-Policy Self-Distillation in Video Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27065)**  `arXiv:2608.27065`  
  _Ziyue Wang, Shiqi Huang, Weiwen Xu, Bihan Wen, Xudong Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) has recently emerged as an effective post-training paradigm that provides dense token-level supervision from a privileged self-teacher, yet it remains underexplored for Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). Existing methods typically construct privileged teachers by augmenting their context with additional information while keeping the primary input unchanged for both teacher and student. In video reasoning, however, privileged information can also be found within the primary input itself: videos contain substantial temporal redundancy, and only a few frames carry the evidence needed to answer a question. Building on this observation, we present $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$, an OPSD framework that exploits privileged visual evidence for both self-teacher construction and knowledge transfer. First, our Evidence-Grounded Self-Teacher conditions the teacher exclusively on annotated evidence frames, while the student reasons over a broader set of frames that contains them. This focused visual context enables more targeted supervision without introducing visual content unavailable to the student. Second, our Evidence-Guided Token Optimization adaptively weights token-level distillation according to an estimate of each reasoning token's reliance on privileged visual evidence, thereby emphasizing perceptually grounded reasoning. Across five video benchmarks and three Qwen-VL backbones, $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$ consistently outperforms Standard OPSD by 1.8-2.3 percentage points on average, establishing an effective and efficient post-training approach for Video-LLMs.
  </details>

- **[MVC-Bench: Benchmarking Calibration of Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27004)**  `arXiv:2608.27004`  
  _Ashshak Sharifdeen, Shihab Aaqil Ahamed, Ufaq Khan, Muhammad Akhtar Munir, Sujair Ibrahim, Mohamed Rafeek Mareer Ahamed, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable evaluation of vision-language models (VLMs) and medical vision-language models (Medical-VLMs) requires calibrated confidence, particularly under realistic clinical conditions. However, existing efforts mainly focused on improving accuracy, leaving calibration in the medical domain underexplored. To this end, we propose MVC-Bench, a calibration-centric benchmark for medical image classification with VLMs and Medical-VLMs. MVC-Bench assesses the calibration across three axes: (i) robustness to modality, backbone, and domain shift (ii) effectiveness of calibration strategies and prompt-tuning methods (iii) stability under prompt-template and random-seed variations. The benchmark covers eight different backbones, three medical modalities, including fundus imaging, histopathology, and chest X-ray under in-domain and domain shift settings. It compares post-hoc calibration, train-time calibration, and zero-shot inference methods, together with six prompt-tuning methods. Across more than 1638 controlled experiments, we report accuracy and Expected Calibration Error (ECE) as primary metrics, and further report results with complementary calibration measures, including Maximum Calibration Error (MCE) and Adaptive Calibration Error (ACE). We further investigate the underlying causes of miscalibration in VLMs and Medical-VLMs and propose a simple train-time calibration method, Multi-Class Margin (MCM) regularization, which achieves lowest ECE on 10 out of 12 settings in in-domain and remains competitive under domain shifts. Collectively, MVC-Bench provides a structured evaluation framework and actionable guidance for improving calibration in safety-critical medical workflows.
  </details>

- **[TEA: Text Encoder Alignment for Robust Concept Erasure in Text-to-Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15341)**  `arXiv:2608.15341`  
  _Alireza Dehghanpour Farashah, Zhuan Shi, Negar Rostamzadeh, Golnoosh Farnadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image diffusion models can be misused to generate harmful content through adversarial or paraphrased prompts that bypass built-in safety mechanisms. Existing concept erasure methods often suffer from limited robustness against adversarial prompts, degradation of benign generation quality, or reliance on inference-time interventions that introduce persistent computational overhead. To address these limitations, we formulate concept erasure as a domain alignment problem in the text representation space. We propose a lightweight Text Encoder Alignment framework (TEA) that fine-tunes only the text encoder while keeping the generative backbone fully frozen. Given concept-anchor prompt pairs, our method trains a discriminator to distinguish token-level representations of concept-bearing prompts from those of safe anchor prompts, while updating the text encoder to make these representations indistinguishable. TEA introduces zero inference-time overhead and trains in seconds with only a few fine-tuning steps, making it practical for large-scale deployment. Despite this efficiency, TEA achieves state-of-the-art erasure robustness against black-box and white-box adversarial attacks on Stable Diffusion v1.4, while preserving generation quality on benign prompts. Furthermore, TEA is model-agnostic and achieves the lowest attack success rate on Stable Diffusion v3.5, extending concept erasure to Rectified Flow Transformer architectures with T5 conditioning, which remain largely unexplored by prior methods. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[OmniEdit-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Instruction-based Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05049)**  `arXiv:2608.05049`  
  _Chenxuan Miao, Yutong Feng, Yi Lu, Yunfeng Yan, Donglian Qi, Shiwei Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-based video editing (IVE) offers diverse applications, yet evaluating editing models remains challenging. Existing benchmarks suffer from two key limitations: narrow task coverage inherited from image editing that overlooks video-specific dimensions, and metrics that inadequately capture instruction fidelity, allowing visually plausible but incorrect edits to receive high scores. To address these limitations, we introduce OmniEdit-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark covering spatial, temporal, audio, and reference-based editing. It distinguishes between explicit and implicit instructions and incorporates reasoning-based scenarios to reflect real-world editing requirements. We further propose an evaluation framework that assesses accuracy, preservation, realism, and consistency using both human judges and a state-of-the-art vision-language model (VLM). An accuracy-aware penalty mechanism conditions the other three scores on accuracy, prioritizing instruction fidelity and penalizing plausible but incorrect edits. Experiments on prominent open-source and commercial IVE models reveal substantial room for improvement. OmniEdit-Bench provides a structured testbed for identifying model limitations and advancing instruction-based video editing. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Localize, Don't Beautify: Client-Side Control of Image-Editing APIs for Cosmetic Surgery Previews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02841)**  `arXiv:2608.02841`  
  _Sukhrobbek Ilyosbekov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ask a commercial image editor to preview a cosmetic procedure and it will often change more of the face than the request names: a jawline edit can also smooth the forehead or alter the eyes. Existing methods for confining an edit to one region require access to the model's internals, which a public editing API does not expose. We ask how much control is possible from the client side alone. In a pilot benchmark, five current commercial editing configurations perform facelift-style jaw-neck and rhinoplasty edits on synthetic faces under two levels of client-side control: the prompt alone, and a masked composite that cuts the edited region out of the response and pastes it back onto the original photograph through a landmark-derived mask. Of 162 attempted edits, 151 could be scored. ArcFace cosine measures identity preservation; a CIELAB pixel-change ratio measures how much change lands inside the requested region rather than a protected facial zone. On 15 faces, the masked composite improved localization over the paired prompt-only output by a median of 0.315 (95% face-clustered bootstrap interval 0.279-0.366) while retaining most of the target-region change. Editors differed in edit strength and identity retention, and one returned near-copies for the facelift request. This is a study of control, not clinical accuracy: the faces are synthetic, no surgeons rated the outputs, and each condition was generated once. Within that scope, keeping a surgical preview inside its intended region needs no access to the model; a mask and composite on the client enforce it across every editor tested, at low provider cost.
  </details>

- **[CADENA: Stepwise CAD Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00799)**  `arXiv:2608.00799`  
  _Soslan Kabisov, Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Antonio Rodriguez, Daniil Ignatiev, Nikita Gavrilov, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-Aided Design (CAD) underpins modern engineering, yet converting existing shapes into editable models still demands substantial expert effort. Most AI systems emit the entire CAD program in a single pass, never inspecting the intermediate geometry. In contrast, human engineers build a part feature by feature, checking after each operation what remains to be modeled. We introduce CADENA (Spanish for "chain"), a model that reconstructs a 3D mesh as a parametric CAD program, growing its sequence of operations one at a time and comparing the target with the currently predicted geometry at every step. We also address the lack of benchmarks for evaluating reverse-engineering methods on mechanical parts, introducing CADENA-Bench, a benchmark that measures performance across categories of mechanical parts. CADENA outperforms prior methods on CADENA-Bench and on the DeepCAD, Fusion 360, and MCB datasets. Code is available atthis https URL, model weights atthis https URL, and CADENA-Bench atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VETO: Towards Protecting Images From Frontier AI Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27292)**  `arXiv:2607.27292`  
  _Jonas Grebe, Hossein Shakibania, Tobias Braun, Marcus Rohrbach, Anna Rohrbach_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern image editors blur the boundary between image editing and text-to-image synthesis, extracting people and objects from a single photo and inserting them into new scenes without subject-specific fine-tuning. Existing defenses struggle to disrupt such manipulations in these models without large, visibly damaging perturbations to the images. We introduce VETO, a white-box image-cloaking method for open-weight editors that maximizes the entropy of attention between reference image and generation tokens to disrupt selective retrieval of source details, while requiring neither the future editing instruction nor modifications to the model. We also introduce VetoBench, a benchmark of 300 benign and harmful tasks balanced between within-scene editing and recontextualization. Full Pareto evaluations on FLUX.2 and FIBO-Edit across three benchmarks and five perturbation budgets demonstrate overall better protection-fidelity trade-offs, particularly against recontextualization. Protected images remain effective on fine-tuned and distilled variants of the editor without reoptimization.
  </details>

- **[SciFigQual-Bench: Scientific Figure Quality Assessment with Manuscript-Linked Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27084)**  `arXiv:2607.27084`  
  _Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Huiqi Liang, Xiaozhen Zhong, Weidong Cai, Lequan Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figure quality is bound to the manuscript: a crop can be visually clear and still contradict its caption or the paragraph that cites it. Most image quality assessment and chart-understanding methods target a detached visual surface or a question-answering score, rather than alignment among the figure, its caption, and the citing text. A single end-to-end prompt that sees the image and the text together still mixes what is visible with what the caption and the citing paragraph claim, so fluent wording can raise a visual score and missing text can be treated as low quality. To address this gap, we introduce SciFigQual-Bench, a manuscript-linked benchmark for published CS-conference figures that binds each figure to its caption and to index-resolved citing paragraphs, and scores visual clarity, layout, caption consistency, context consistency, and misleading risk, leaving a dimension unevaluated when its evidence is absent. SFQ-Agent reads the image and the text in separate calls and records modality-specific evidence. A cross-modal judge scores caption and citing-text alignment from those reports, and a deterministic runner keeps the visual scores, sets misleading risk by a fixed rule, and applies the written caps, so each dimension follows the rubric instead of a single end-to-end prompt. Experiments comparing this staged judge with single-pass and OCR-sidecar protocols find the closest point-estimate fit to mean human ratings under staged judging, while the gap between protocols remains small. Caption consistency remains the main gap, and agreement with the rater mean answers a different question from agreement among human raters. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[What Does a Temporal Benchmark Score Measure? Decomposing Channel Use in Video VLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12304)**  `arXiv:2607.12304`  
  _Farrukh Rahman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A score on a temporal video question answering benchmark is meant to measure that a model has temporal understanding, but it conflates two questions. (1)~is the question even temporal, does it need several frames and their order? And (2)~when it does, does the model recover the order from the pixels, or read it off the positional encoding (RoPE)? Most of a temporal score answers neither, since a single frame and answer priors often carry it, and the field's validity checks, frame-shuffle sensitivity and multi-frame gain, speak only to (1). For (2) we score a reversal conflict, frames reversed while RoPE stays forward, two ways: label-free, as the accuracy lost against the forward labels, and against reversed labels wherever labels transform deterministically under time reversal. This separates a model that follows the reversed content from one merely disrupted. The two readings dissociate two similarly scoring models at both model scales. Qwen3-VL answers the reversed event it actually sees, reading the visual sequence. Molmo2 correctly reports a backwards video when positions agree, and flipping only the RoPE indices flips its prediction to the forward events, i.e. it reads positions. We call these regimes \emph{visual-sequence-dominant} and \emph{position-dominant}. Controls show reversed content is processable whenever the channels agree, and that both families share a bias toward describing events as running forward (arrow-of-time prior) that, being similar in strength, cannot by itself account for the dissociation. The distinction matters because the two channels fail on opposite inputs: two models with the same score are not interchangeable.
  </details>

- **[Do Egocentric Video-Language Models Capture Both Hand- and Object-Centric Cues?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08514)**  `arXiv:2607.08514`  
  _Masatoshi Tateno, Alexandros Stergiou, Risa Shinoda, Yoichi Sato, Dima Damen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing hand-object interactions (HOIs) requires capturing both hand dynamics and object transformations, which provide complementary information for distinguishing visually similar manipulations. However, Video-Language Models (VLMs) trained solely on egocentric videos tend to over-rely on hand appearance, struggling to distinguish interactions with similar visual appearances but different dynamics (e.g., taking vs. kneading dough). To better utilize complementary hand- and object-centric cues, we propose a learning paradigm with (i) hand-object masked training to encode both hand- and object-centric cues and learn their relationships through cross-cue reconstruction, and (ii) an HOI-dynamics-aware decoder that grounds each cue through auxiliary location and semantic predictions. To assess how effectively models leverage hand- and object-centric cues for action recognition, we introduce Cue-Isolated HOI (CI-HOI), which independently evaluates recognition performance based on each cue. To enable this evaluation, we curate the DEHOI testbed using video inpainting to selectively remove either hands or manipulated objects, allowing us to evaluate action recognition with each cue isolated. DEHOI reveals that existing VLMs trained only on egocentric video rely more on hand-centric cues than VLMs trained on more non-egocentric video data. We show that our training paradigm mitigates this hand-centric bias, outperforming VLMs trained on the same egocentric data across DEHOI, standard action recognition, and object state recognition benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Aura: Consistent Multi-Subject Video Generation via VLM-Grounded Semantic Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04311)**  `arXiv:2607.04311`  
  _Zixiang Zhou, Zhentao Yu, Yifeng Ma, Hongmei Wang, Wenqing Yu, Cong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Subject-driven and multi-element video generation are central to controllable video synthesis, but existing methods still struggle to preserve identity consistency and model complex relationships among multiple subjects. In this paper, we propose Aura, a unified framework for high-fidelity and identity-consistent video generation. To better capture scene dynamics and subject interactions, we introduce AI director-level captions that provide dense and structured descriptions of video content. We further leverage a vision-language model (VLM) with learnable queries to extract multimodal semantic features from textual and visual references, covering both global semantics and fine-grained visual cues. To bridge the representational gap between the VLM and the Diffusion Transformer (DiT), we design a two-stage alignment strategy that progressively maps VLM features into the DiT feature space. For visual conditioning, we adopt token concatenation to inject reference information directly into the generation process. To distinguish heterogeneous subject types and reduce common copy-paste artifacts, we develop a subject-aware RoPE-Shift mechanism. To further differentiate reference images of different categories, we introduce subject-aware learnable tokens. In addition, we introduce Memory Tokens to balance the training signal across examples with different numbers of reference subjects. During inference, Progressive-APG (Adaptive Prompt Guidance) further alleviates oversaturation and improves semantic alignment with user prompts. Finally, we build a high-quality video-subject image dataset through a dedicated data construction pipeline. Extensive experiments show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance on both single-subject generation and more challenging multi-element scenarios.
  </details>

- **[SPIRAL-360: Stratified Panoramic Instruction Replay and Angular Learning for Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02479)**  `arXiv:2607.02479`  
  _Jingtao Xu, Zizhuo Lin, Jianwen Sun, Yi Yang, Yawei Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Localizing targets in $360^\circ$ environments requires combining global context with fine-grained evidence despite projection distortion and horizontal wrap-around. Uneven directional coverage compounds these challenges, particularly for seam-adjacent and polar targets. We present SPIRAL-360, a geometry-aware framework with separately trained single-turn and multi-turn models for panoramic localization. Spherically Stratified Replay (SSR) broadens directional coverage through stratified sampling and consistent transformations of panoramas, target annotations, and tool trajectories. TapSphere incorporates spherical distance priors into panoramic visual attention, while Spherical Direction Regularization (SDR) complements coordinate-token prediction with auxiliary direction supervision during training. The single-turn model predicts directly from the panorama, while the multi-turn model inspects tool-generated perspective views. We construct SPIRAL-360 Search, a curated dataset of trajectories interleaving reasoning, projection actions, and visual observations. Both models undergo full-parameter supervised fine-tuning followed by policy optimization. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art localization on S360 and competitive cross-dataset transfer, including H$^*$Bench. Systematic ablations support the effectiveness of this geometric learning design.
  </details>

- **[V-Zero: Gating On-Policy Distillation with Contrastive Visual Evidence for Fine-Grained Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25319)**  `arXiv:2606.25319`  
  _Haoxiang Sun, Zhihang Yi, Langxuan Deng, Yuhao Zhou, Peiqi Jia, Jian Zhao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained visual reasoning requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to identify task-relevant visual evidence and ground their reasoning in local image regions. Existing methods typically rely on reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards or supervised fine-tuning on large-scale annotated reasoning traces, resulting in costly exploration, hand-designed verification rules, or heavy dependence on textual supervision. While On-Policy Distillation (OPD) offers a promising alternative via dense teacher supervision, its token-level objective inherently focuses on local token corrections, failing to assess global trajectory quality. In this work, we first reinterpret OPD as a stop-gradient alignment objective, revealing that its potential is limited by the absence of trajectory-level discrimination. Motivated by this observation, we propose V-Zero, which gates on-policy distillation with contrastive visual evidence. Specifically, V-Zero evaluates each trajectory under paired positive and negative visual views; the resulting likelihood contrast forms a trajectory-level gate that selectively amplifies updates from visually well-supported reasoning paths while suppressing ungrounded ones. Experiments on multiple visual reasoning and perception benchmarks show that V-Zero improves the Qwen3.5-4B backbone by 3.9 points on average while preserving strong generalization. Notably, V-Zero achieves more than 3$\times$ and 6$\times$ training speedups over representative supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning baselines, respectively. It requires neither annotated textual reasoning traces nor external reward signals. Code and dataset are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous Video Generation for Self-Evolving World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24152)**  `arXiv:2606.24152`  
  _Xin Wang, Wenxuan Liu, Tongtong Feng, Wenwu Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale video generation models are increasingly described as world models because they can learn rich spatiotemporal regularities from visual data. We argue that an ideal world model should benefit in a self-evolving generative character. As such, a self-evolving world model can be realized via a generative model conditioned by various constraints, whose future predictions are refined through feedback. However, traditional visually plausible predictions alone are not enough to establish whether an imagined future is physically actionable for a particular embodied agent, failing to provide informative feedback from environments for self-evolving improvement. To realize self-evolving world models, this article proposes the concept of autonomous video generation, which is evaluated through counterfactual controllability, i.e., the ability to i) generate intervention-conditioned futures, ii) bind these future frames to embodiment constraints, iii) verify them under distribution shifts, and iv) distil surviving branches into compact variables for decision-making. We formalize a four-stage closed-loop optimization of Generation, Binding, Verification and Distillation, together with four corresponding evaluation metrics: novelty, consistency, out-of-distribution (OOD) and efficiency. We further discuss two examples, i.e., drones and manipulators, as early embodied testbeds where wind, sensing limits, actuation delay, contact dynamics and recovery constraints can be systematically perturbed and verified. The central claim is that the framework of autonomous video generation for self-evolving world models should not be judged by video fidelity alone, but by whether the generated frames improve valid action under counterfactual interventions and various embodiment constraints.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Metrics for Synthetic Object Detection Data: A Proxy for Detector Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19817)**  `arXiv:2606.19817`  
  _Myeongseok Nam, Donghun Yeo, Seungwook Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic images are increasingly used to augment scarce real data for object detection. However, not all synthetic sets help equally, and the only way to know a set's value is to train a detector on it, which is slow and demands dense annotation. We ask whether a training-free metric can instead rank candidate synthetic training sets by their downstream utility. We propose Conditional-Composition Domain Match (CCDM), which converts any feature-space distance into a composition-stratified comparison, matching candidate and target within metadata-defined strata without training a detector. On COCO and VisDrone-DET, the best CCDM variant ranks 20 and 19 candidate training sets in strong agreement with YOLOv8 mAP (Spearman $\rho$ = 0.98 and 0.96), outperforming FID, KID, and MMD. Furthermore, CCDM holds when reference metadata comes from detector pseudo-labels instead of ground truth.
  </details>

- **[DLWM: Diverse Latent World Models for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15160)**  `arXiv:2606.15160`  
  _David Huang, Lianlei Shan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have improved considerably in recent years. Existing approaches typically rely on explicit chain-of-thought or continuous latent-space trajectories to enhance multi-step reasoning. However, these methods generally assume that an input admits a single latent interpretation and unfold reasoning along a fixed path or under a uniform computation budget. In real-world multimodal settings, visual observations are often subject to occlusion, blur, viewpoint variation, or semantic ambiguity, giving rise to multiple plausible interpretations. A uniform reasoning strategy not only limits the model's ability to explore multiple hypotheses but also incurs high memory usage and rollout cost. We present DLWM (Diverse Latent World Models), a multimodal reasoning framework that combines latent-space reasoning with reinforcement learning. First, we construct a set of diverse latent world hypotheses in continuous latent space, each representing a candidate interpretation of the visual input, and unfold latent reasoning independently on each hypothesis. An orthogonality-based diversity regularizer discourages hypothesis collapse. Second, we formulate the latent reasoning process as a resource-constrained sequential decision problem and introduce a resource-aware reinforcement learning policy that adaptively allocates computation across hypotheses, dynamically deciding whether to expand a reasoning path, prune a hypothesis, merge reasoning paths, or produce the final answer, thereby substantially reducing memory footprint and improving rollout efficiency. Experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that DLWM outperforms existing methods by 2-5 points in accuracy while reducing memory usage by 24%.
  </details>

- **[RPC-GS: Gaussian Splatting with native RPC Rendering for Satellite Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06690)**  `arXiv:2606.06690`  
  _Valentin Wagner, Sebastian Bullinger, Christoph Bodensteiner, Michael Arens_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present RPC-GS, the first Gaussian Splatting framework for satellite imagery that operates natively with Rational Polynomial Camera (RPC) models. The RPC model is the de facto standard for representing the complex imaging geometry of modern pushbroom satellite sensors. To simplify rendering, prior satellite Gaussian Splatting methods replace the RPC model with perspective or affine camera approximations, leading to geometric errors during reconstruction. RPC-GS avoids these approximations by projecting Gaussian means and covariances directly through the RPC model during the splatting process. We embed the RPC model in a chain of carefully selected geo-coordinate transformations representing a mapping from splatting-suitable scene coordinates to image coordinates. To map the Gaussian covariance matrices, we derive a numerically robust Jacobian-based covariance projection for the (partially nonlinear) coordinate transformations. Since RPCs lack an explicit notion of camera depth, we integrate a metric ray-based depth formulation. We benchmark RPC, perspective, and affine camera models in a unified framework, with our native RPC renderer achieving the lowest reconstruction error on leading satellite benchmark datasets, improving mean altitude error over perspective and affine approximations by 34.3% and 13.5% on DFC2019, and by 5.0% and 1.2% on IARPA2016. Our implementation is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EVIDENT: Routing MLLM Adaptation through Entity-Grounded Visual Evidence for Cross-Domain Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26104)**  `arXiv:2605.26104`  
  _Geo Ahn, Jiwook Han, Youngrae Kim, Joonseok Lee, Jinwoo Choi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning MLLMs for Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) often improves in-domain performance but degrades sharply under domain shift. In this work, we find that this failure is primarily driven not just by unseen query concepts, but by visual domain shift, which prevents the model from coupling its learned temporal localization knowledge with its inherent entity-attention capability. To address this, we introduce EVIDENT, a parameter-efficient adaptation framework that anchors temporal grounding in the inherent entity-attention of pre-trained MLLMs by routing VTG adaptation through explicit visual entity evidence. EVIDENT consists of three components: (i) an Entity Bottleneck Adapter that transforms dense visual tokens into compact entity-level slots, (ii) an Entity-Binding Distillation loss that instills objectness priors into the semantically unstructured MLLM visual space, guiding each slot to bind to a coherent entity, and (iii) an Entity-to-eVidence gating mechanism that leverages the captured entities as evidence, steering the model to localize moments containing query-relevant entities. Together, these components enable VTG fine-tuning to rely on entity-grounded evidence rather than brittle dataset shortcuts. Experiments on cross-domain VTG benchmarks show that EVIDENT consistently improves out-of-domain robustness while preserving competitive in-domain performance with modest parameter overhead. These results suggest that entity-level grounding is an effective inductive bias for generalizable temporal localization.
  </details>

- **[GRTO: Bootstrapped Group Relative Tool Optimization for Referring Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23500)**  `arXiv:2605.23500`  
  _Mario Markov, Stefan Maria Ailuro, Mohammad Mahdi, Luc Van Gool, Danda Pani Paudel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring segmentation requires reasoning to connect language with visual evidence and identify the intended target in challenging scenarios. This makes GRPO a natural choice for training VLMs together with a segmentation tool. However, existing approaches optimize only the VLM, assuming that the tool is perfect and leaving it untouched. This assumption is particularly limiting when the tool faces domain shift, as segmentation errors can undermine even correct reasoning. We therefore argue that the segmentation tool should be optimized together with the reasoning policy. However, this is challenging because policy optimization and differentiable tool learning rely on fundamentally different objectives. In this work, we introduce group relative tool optimization (GRTO), a mathematically grounded two-stage framework for jointly optimizing a policy and its differentiable tool. The key idea is to first align the tool with the policy expected after joint training, rather than the initial policy. The prealigned tool then leads to a more robust reward signal and superior policy convergence in subsequent policy training. In the first stage, GRTO bootstraps the tool toward the theoretically optimal policy, capturing readily attainable gains without costly on-policy rollouts. This alignment bootstraps the second stage, where GRTO extends GRPO to jointly refine the policy and tool. Across three challenging referring segmentation benchmarks, GRTO achieves substantial improvements over plain GRPO and alternative tool training procedures, matching or surpassing domain-specific state-of-the-art methods. This demonstrates the value of unifying reinforcement learning with differentiable auxiliary objectives for reasoning-intensive segmentation.
  </details>
