- **[Balancing Uncertainty and Diversity of Samples: Leveraging Diversity of Least, High Confidence Samples for Effective Active Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22169)**  `arXiv:2605.22169`  
  _Vipul Arya, S.H. Shabbeer Basha, Srikrishna U N, Sunainha Vijay, Snehasis Mukherjee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models, including Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs), have achieved state-of-the-art performance on various computer vision tasks such as object classification, detection, segmentation, generation, and more. However, these models are data-hungry as they require more training data to learn millions or billions of parameters. Especially for supervised learning tasks, curating a large number of labeled samples for model training is an expensive and time-consuming task. Active Learning (AL) has been used to address this problem for many years. Existing active learning methods aim to choose the samples for annotation from a pool of unlabeled samples that are either diverse or uncertain. Choosing such samples may hinder the model's performance as we pool based on one dimension, i.e., either diverse or uncertain. In this paper, we propose four novel hybrid sampling methods for pooling both easy and hard samples, which are also diverse. To verify the efficacy of the proposed methods, extensive experiments are conducted using high- and low-confidence samples separately. We observe from our experiments that the proposed hybrid sampling method, Least Confident and Diverse (LCD), consistently performs better than state-of-the-art methods. It is observed that selecting uncertain and diverse instances helps the model learn more distinct features. The code related to this study will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Structure and Language: Graph-Based Visual Reasoning for Autonomous Road Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20942)**  `arXiv:2605.20942`  
  _Lena Wild, Katie Z Luo, Marco Pavone_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured road understanding of lane geometry, topology, and traffic element relationships is foundational to safe autonomous driving. While vision-language models (VLMs) offer promising semantic flexibility, they lack the geometric and relational grounding required for precise road reasoning. Conversely, traditional modular systems, e.g., HD maps and topological road graphs, provide structural precision but remain semantically rigid. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Combined Road Substrate (CRS), a graph-grounded framework that makes geometric road structure and open-vocabulary semantics jointly executable in a single representation. CRS enables the automatic generation of compositionally complex and linguistically varied question-answer pairs via recursive graph queries, augmented with a "grounding for free" mechanism that ensures logical traceability to specific map elements, and procedurally extracted chain-of-thought supervision traces. We demonstrate that state-of-the-art VLMs - including large, closed-source models - struggle significantly with structured road reasoning, yet training a small 2- or 4-billion-parameter model with as few as 20 to 80 CRS-enriched scenes yields stable gains in compositional reasoning tasks of varying depth. Analysis of model behavior via verifiable reasoning traces reveals a systematic shift in failure modes: whereas baseline models fail at relational scene understanding, CRS-trained models reduce failures to attribute recognition, suggesting that for structured road reasoning tasks, the lack of structured supervision is a greater limitation than model scale. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PVRF: All-in-one Adverse Weather Removal via Prior-modulated and Velocity-constrained Rectified Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14045)**  `arXiv:2605.14045`  
  _Wei Dong, Han Zhou, Terry Ji, Guanhua Zhao, Shahab Asoodeh, Yulun Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adverse weather removal (AWR) in real-world images remains challenging due to heterogeneous and unseen degradations, while distortion-driven training often yields overly smooth results. We propose PVRF, a unified framework that integrates zero-shot soft weather perceptions with velocity-constrained rectified-flow refinement. PVRF introduces an AWR-specific question answering module (AWR-QA) that uses frozen vision--language models (VLMs) to estimate soft probabilities of weather types and low-level attribute scores. These perceptions condition restoration networks via attribute-modulated normalization (AMN) and weather-weighted adapters (WWA), producing an anchor estimate for refinement. We then learn a terminal-consistent residual rectified flow with perception-adaptive source perturbation and a terminal-consistent velocity parameterization to stabilize learning near the terminal regime. Extensive experiments show that PVRF improves both fidelity and perceptual quality over state-of-the-art baselines, with strong cross-dataset generalization on single and combined degradations. Code will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Direct Cartesian Integration vs. Filtered Back Projection: A Note on Direct Discretization and Variance Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09020)**  `arXiv:2605.09020`  
  _Mikhail G. Mozerov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This note compares two reconstruction operators for the parallel-beam inverse Radon problem, each obtained by the most direct discretization of a continuous inversion formula. The first is Filtered Back Projection (FBP), discretized with a sampled ramp filter and no zero-padding or empirical normalization. The second, termed Direct Cartesian Integration (DCI), evaluates the two-dimensional spectrum of the image point-wise on a Cartesian frequency grid by integrating each projection along the corresponding radial direction, using an identity that follows from the Central Slice Theorem and Hermitian symmetry. The comparison is deliberately restricted to these unmodified discretizations: its purpose is to expose properties of the discrete operators themselves, not to rank DCI against engineered FBP implementations. On a set of ten 512 x 512 test images with 800 projections, DCI preserves the standard deviation of the reference image (mean ratio 0.997, mean absolute deviation from unity 0.003) and produces spatially uniform error maps, whereas the direct FBP discretization changes the standard deviation by -38% to +20% depending on the object and exhibits the radially structured "cupping" error. The observation is consistent with the fact that DCI never discretizes the polar Jacobian |omega|, which is the source of the scale distortion in a direct ramp-filter discretization.
  </details>

- **[Supersampling Stable Diffusion and Beyond: Training-Free Neural Network Scaling via Signal Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08698)**  `arXiv:2605.08698`  
  _Md Abu Obaida Zishan, Jannatun Noor, Annajiat Alim Rasel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents a training-free approach for interpolating neural networks using signal interpolation methods (i.e., linear, bilinear, bicubic). We discovered this phenomenon while attempting to interpolate the convolution layers of Stable-Diffusion-1.5 for beyond-training-resolution (BTR) image generation. As we know, for generating BTR images using DDPMs (Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models), there are mainly two approaches, namely, 1) patch-wise diffusion and 2) convolution-kernel dilation. Patch-wise diffusion cannot solve the object-duplication problem efficiently, while the kernel dilation approach may be harder to fine-tune later. Thus, we proposed convolution-kernel interpolation of the Stable Diffusion 1.5 model for $4\times$ BTR image generation. We discover and show mathematically that signal interpolation methods can correctly scale convolution kernels when multiplied by a flat-attenuation factor, while achieving competitive performance against the state of the art. We then applied the same methodology to non-DDPM deep neural network architectures, adapting them for BTR image classification, achieving a worst-case performance drop of $\sim 7\%$ on the ImageNet-1k validation set. Again, we subsample the penultimate fully connected layer of VGG-16 using our method, reducing up to $\sim 9\%$ parameters with $\sim 5\%$ loss in performance. Finally, we also discuss how we can reduce the memory footprint of training neural networks to $4\times$ or greater.
  </details>

- **[CURVAS-PDACVI: A Multi-Rater CT Dataset and Challenge for Pancreatic Cancer Segmentation and Vascular Involvement Assessme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27582)**  `arXiv:2604.27582`  
  _M. Riera-Marín, O. K. Sikha, J. Rodríguez-Comas, M. S. May, T. Kirscher, X. Coubez, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical resection remains the only potentially curative treatment for pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC), and eligibility depends on accurate assessment of vascular invasion (VI), i.e., tumor extension into adjacent critical vessels. Despite its importance for preoperative staging and surgical planning, computational VI assessment remains underexplored. Two major challenges are the lack of public datasets and the diagnostic ambiguity at the tumor-vessel interface, which leads to substantial inter-rater variability even among expert radiologists. To address these limitations, we introduce the CURVAS-PDACVI Dataset and Challenge, an open benchmark for uncertainty-aware AI in PDAC staging based on a densely annotated dataset with five independent expert annotations per scan. We also propose a multi-metric evaluation framework that extends beyond spatial overlap to include probabilistic calibration and VI assessment. Evaluation of six state-of-the-art methods shows that strong global volumetric overlap does not necessarily translate into reliable performance at clinically critical tumor-vessel interfaces. In particular, methods optimized for binary segmentation perform competitively on average overlap metrics, but often degrade in high-complexity cases with low expert consensus, either collapsing in volume or overextending at uncertain boundaries. In contrast, methods that model inter-rater disagreement produce better calibrated probabilistic maps and show greater robustness in these ambiguous cases. The benchmark highlights the limitations of volumetric accuracy as a proxy for localized surgical utility, motivating uncertainty-aware probabilistic models for preoperative decision-making.
  </details>

- **[Graph-augmented Segmentation for Enhanced In-Situ Geometry Reconstruction of Complex Shapes in Laser Powder Bed Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24234)**  `arXiv:2604.24234`  
  _Stefano Raimondo, Matteo Bugatti, Marco Grasso_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In situ geometric inspection in Laser Powder Bed Fusion of Metals (PBF-LB/M) opens new possibilities for anticipated defect detection and more efficient qualification. It relies on image segmentation to reconstruct layer-wise geometries and identify manufacturing deviations as the part is being built. However, existing approaches face a critical yet largely overlooked challenge: photometric out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, namely the ability to maintain reconstruction accuracy when image appearance differs from the conditions represented during model training. This study represents the first exploration of a graph-augmented U-Net architecture for in situ geometric inspection in PBF-LB/M. The hybrid network integrates a Graph Neural Network (GNN) within the bottleneck to enhance reconstruction robustness under photometric distribution shifts. It is evaluated against conventional U-Net and active contour segmentation methods using two experimental case studies involving different lattice geometries, materials, and manufacturing setups, with X-ray computed tomography providing reference geometries. Generalization is assessed across different training and inference conditions and under controlled image perturbations. Results show that the UNet-GNN achieves higher reconstruction accuracy and consistency than the benchmark methods, with more pronounced improvements when training and inference conditions differ. Its advantages are confirmed across both case studies and under controlled photometric perturbations, while preliminary anomaly detection experiments indicate that improved robustness does not necessarily compromise sensitivity to geometric deviations. These findings highlight graph augmentation as a promising strategy for more reliable in situ geometric inspection under heterogeneous manufacturing conditions.
  </details>

- **[Unsupervised Skeleton-Based Action Segmentation via Hierarchical Spatiotemporal Vector Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15196)**  `arXiv:2604.15196`  
  _Umer Ahmed, Syed Ahmed Mahmood, Fawad Javed Fateh, M. Shaheer Luqman, M. Zeeshan Zia, Quoc-Huy Tran_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a novel hierarchical spatiotemporal vector quantization framework for unsupervised skeleton-based temporal action segmentation. We first introduce a hierarchical approach, which includes two consecutive levels of vector quantization. Specifically, the lower level associates skeletons with fine-grained subactions, while the higher level further aggregates subactions into action-level representations. Our hierarchical approach outperforms the non-hierarchical baseline, while primarily exploiting spatial cues by reconstructing input skeletons. Next, we extend our approach by leveraging both spatial and temporal information, yielding a hierarchical spatiotemporal vector quantization scheme. In particular, our hierarchical spatiotemporal approach performs multi-level clustering, while simultaneously recovering input skeletons and their corresponding timestamps. Lastly, extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks, including HuGaDB, LARa, and BABEL, demonstrate that our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art performance and reduces segment length bias in unsupervised skeleton-based temporal action segmentation.
  </details>

- **[SHAMISA: SHAped Modeling of Implicit Structural Associations for Self-supervised No-Reference Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13669)**  `arXiv:2603.13669`  
  _Mahdi Naseri, Zhou Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  No-Reference Image Quality Assessment (NR-IQA) aims to estimate perceptual quality without access to a pristine reference. Learning an NR-IQA model faces a fundamental bottleneck because it requires many costly human perceptual labels. We propose SHAMISA, a non-contrastive self-supervised framework that learns from unlabeled distorted images through explicitly structured relational supervision. Unlike prior methods that impose rigid binary similarity constraints, SHAMISA shapes soft, controllable implicit structural associations that are both distortion-aware and content-sensitive. A key innovation is its compositional distortion engine, which generates an uncountable family of degradations from continuous parameter spaces and groups the resulting images so that exactly one distortion factor varies at a time. This construction enables fine-grained control over representational similarity during training. Images with shared distortion patterns are pulled together in the embedding space, while severity variations produce structured shifts. Dual-source relation graphs integrate known degradation profiles with emergent structural affinities throughout pre-training. The learned encoder is then frozen and paired with a linear regressor. Extensive experiments on synthetic, authentic, and cross-dataset NR-IQA benchmarks demonstrate strong overall performance, improved transfer in the reported cross-dataset comparisons, and favorable qualitative robustness diagnostics. Self-supervised pre-training requires no IQA opinion-score annotations or contrastive loss.
  </details>

- **[Generating Adversarial Events: A Motion-Aware Point Cloud Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08230)**  `arXiv:2602.08230`  
  _Hongwei Ren, Lansong Yang, Youxin Jiang, Tuopusen Huang, Xiangqian Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event cameras have been widely adopted in safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving, robotics, and human-computer interaction. A pressing challenge arises from the vulnerability of deep neural networks to adversarial examples, which poses a significant threat to the reliability of event-based systems. Nevertheless, research into adversarial attacks on events is scarce. This is primarily due to the non-differentiable nature of mainstream event representations, which hinders the extension of gradient-based attack methods. In this paper, we propose MA-ADV, a novel \textbf{M}otion-\textbf{A}ware \textbf{Adv}ersarial framework. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to generate adversarial events by leveraging point cloud representations. MA-ADV accounts for high-frequency noise in events and employs a diffusion-based approach to smooth perturbations, while fully leveraging the spatial and temporal relationships among events. Finally, MA-ADV identifies the minimal-cost perturbation through a combination of sample-wise Adam optimization, iterative refinement, and binary search. Extensive experimental results validate that MA-ADV ensures a 100\% attack success rate with minimal perturbation cost, and also demonstrate enhanced robustness against defenses, underscoring the critical security challenges facing future event-based perception systems.
  </details>

- **[PANC: Prior-Aware Normalized Cut for Training-Free In-Context Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06912)**  `arXiv:2602.06912`  
  _Juan Gutiérrez, Victor Gutiérrez-García, José Luis Blanco-Murillo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study training-free in-context segmentation with externally annotated context priors: given a target image and a small set of foreground/background tokens extracted from annotated context images, the goal is to segment the corresponding target concept without training, fine-tuning, or target-image clicks. Existing ViT-token spectral methods such as TokenCut are class-agnostic and can select the wrong object in multi-object scenes, or become unstable in low-semantic texture-dominated domains. We propose PANC, a prior-aware spectral segmentation pipeline that injects external labeled tokens into a frozen ViT affinity graph through two foreground/background anchor nodes. The anchors do not clamp target-image tokens; instead, they softly bias the Normalized Cut eigenspace while preserving the target-image affinity structure. PANC further uses prior-aware sign stabilization and prior-driven thresholding to obtain deterministic masks. Across heterogeneous saliency datasets and homogeneous low-semantic domains, PANC improves over architecture-matched no-prior token-graph baselines, with the largest gains in settings where class-agnostic saliency is ambiguous or weak.
  </details>

- **[CausalSpatial: A Benchmark for Object-Centric Causal Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13304)**  `arXiv:2601.13304`  
  _Wenxin Ma, Chenlong Wang, Ruisheng Yuan, Hao Chen, Nanru Dai, S. Kevin Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans can look at a static scene and instantly predict what happens next -- will moving this object cause a collision? We call this ability Causal Spatial Reasoning. However, current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) cannot do this, as they remain largely restricted to static spatial perception, struggling to answer "what-if" questions in a 3D scene. We introduce CausalSpatial, a diagnostic benchmark evaluating whether models can anticipate consequences of object motions across four tasks: Collision, Compatibility, Occlusion, and Trajectory. Results expose a severe gap: humans score 84% while GPT-5 achieves only 54%. Why do MLLMs fail? Our analysis uncovers a fundamental deficiency: models over-rely on textual chain-of-thought reasoning that drifts from visual evidence, producing fluent but spatially ungrounded hallucinations. To address this, we propose the Causal Object World model (COW), a framework that externalizes the simulation process by generating videos of hypothetical dynamics. With explicit visual cues of causality, COW enables models to ground their reasoning in physical reality rather than linguistic priors. We make the dataset and code publicly available here:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EgoControl: Controllable Egocentric Video Generation via 3D Full-Body Poses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18173)**  `arXiv:2511.18173`  
  _Enrico Pallotta, Sina Mokhtarzadeh Azar, Lars Doorenbos, Serdar Ozsoy, Umar Iqbal, Juergen Gall_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Egocentric video generation with fine-grained control through body motion is a key requirement towards embodied AI agents that can simulate, predict, and plan actions. In this work, we propose EgoControl, a pose-controllable video diffusion model trained on egocentric data. We train a video prediction model to condition future frame generation on explicit 3D body pose sequences. To achieve precise motion control, we introduce a novel pose representation that captures both global camera dynamics and articulated body movements, and integrate it through a dedicated control mechanism within the diffusion process. Given a short sequence of observed frames and a sequence of target poses, EgoControl generates temporally coherent and visually realistic future frames that align with the provided pose control. Experimental results demonstrate that EgoControl produces high-quality, pose-consistent egocentric videos, paving the way toward controllable embodied video simulation and understanding.
  </details>

- **[MonitorVLM:A Vision Language Framework for Safety Violation Detection in Mining Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03666)**  `arXiv:2510.03666`  
  _Jiang Wu, Sichao Wu, Yinsong Ma, Guangyuan Yu, Haoyuan Xu, Lifang Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial accidents, particularly in high-risk domains such as surface and underground mining, are frequently caused by unsafe worker behaviors. Traditional manual inspection remains labor-intensive, error-prone, and insufficient for large-scale, dynamic environments, highlighting the urgent need for intelligent and automated safety monitoring. In this paper, we present MonitorVLM, a novel vision--language framework designed to detect safety violations directly from surveillance video streams. MonitorVLM introduces three key innovations: (1) a domain-specific violation dataset comprising 9,000 vision--question--answer (VQA) samples across 40 high-frequency mining regulations, enriched with augmentation and auxiliary detection cues; (2) a clause filter (CF) module that dynamically selects the Top-$K$ most relevant clauses, reducing inference latency by 13.56% while maintaining accuracy; and (3) a behavior magnifier (BM) module that enhances worker regions to improve fine-grained action recognition, yielding additional gains of 3.45% in precision and 8.62% in recall. Experimental results demonstrate that MonitorVLM significantly outperforms baseline vision--language models, achieving improvements of 22.01% in precision, 34.22\% in recall, and 28.37% in F1 score over the 72B unfine-tuned baseline. A lightweight web-based interface further integrates MonitorVLM into practical workflows, enabling automatic violation reporting with video timestamping. This study highlights the potential of multimodal large models to enhance occupational safety monitoring in mining and beyond.
  </details>

- **[FedS2R: One-Shot Federated Domain Generalization for Synthetic-to-Real Semantic Segmentation in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19881)**  `arXiv:2507.19881`  
  _Tao Lian, Jose L. Gómez, Antonio M. López_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated domain generalization has shown promising progress in image classification by enabling collaborative training across multiple clients without sharing raw data. However, its potential in the semantic segmentation of autonomous driving remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose FedS2R, the first one-shot federated domain generalization framework for synthetic-to-real semantic segmentation in autonomous driving. FedS2R comprises two components: an inconsistency-driven data augmentation strategy that generates images for unstable classes, and a multi-client knowledge distillation scheme with feature fusion that distills a global model from multiple client models. Experiments on five real-world datasets, Cityscapes, BDD100K, Mapillary, IDD, and ACDC, show that the global model significantly outperforms individual client models and is only 2 mIoU points behind the model trained with simultaneous access to all client data. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of FedS2R in synthetic-to-real semantic segmentation for autonomous driving under federated learning
  </details>

- **[Retinexformer: One-stage Retinex-based Transformer for Low-light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.06705)**  `arXiv:2303.06705`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Hao Bian, Jing Lin, Haoqian Wang, Radu Timofte, Yulun Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When enhancing low-light images, many deep learning algorithms are based on the Retinex theory. However, the Retinex model does not consider the corruptions hidden in the dark or introduced by the light-up process. Besides, these methods usually require a tedious multi-stage training pipeline and rely on convolutional neural networks, showing limitations in capturing long-range dependencies. In this paper, we formulate a simple yet principled One-stage Retinex-based Framework (ORF). ORF first estimates the illumination information to light up the low-light image and then restores the corruption to produce the enhanced image. We design an Illumination-Guided Transformer (IGT) that utilizes illumination representations to direct the modeling of non-local interactions of regions with different lighting conditions. By plugging IGT into ORF, we obtain our algorithm, Retinexformer. Comprehensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that our Retinexformer significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on thirteen benchmarks. The user study and application on low-light object detection also reveal the latent practical values of our method. Code, models, and results are available atthis https URL. An online Gradio demo is available on Hugging Face atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[MST++: Multi-stage Spectral-wise Transformer for Efficient Spectral Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2204.07908)**  `arXiv:2204.07908`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Jing Lin, Zudi Lin, Haoqian Wang, Yulun Zhang, Hanspeter Pfister, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing leading methods for spectral reconstruction (SR) focus on designing deeper or wider convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to learn the end-to-end mapping from the RGB image to its hyperspectral image (HSI). These CNN-based methods achieve impressive restoration performance while showing limitations in capturing the long-range dependencies and self-similarity prior. To cope with this problem, we propose a novel Transformer-based method, Multi-stage Spectral-wise Transformer (MST++), for efficient spectral reconstruction. In particular, we employ Spectral-wise Multi-head Self-attention (S-MSA) that is based on the HSI spatially sparse while spectrally self-similar nature to compose the basic unit, Spectral-wise Attention Block (SAB). Then SABs build up Single-stage Spectral-wise Transformer (SST) that exploits a U-shaped structure to extract multi-resolution contextual information. Finally, our MST++, cascaded by several SSTs, progressively improves the reconstruction quality from coarse to fine. Comprehensive experiments show that our MST++ significantly outperforms other state-of-the-art methods. In the NTIRE 2022 Spectral Reconstruction Challenge, our approach won the First place. Code and pre-trained models are publicly available atthis https URL. An online Gradio demo is available on HuggingFace atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Coarse-to-Fine Sparse Transformer for Hyperspectral Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.04845)**  `arXiv:2203.04845`  
  _Yuanhao Cai, Jing Lin, Xiaowan Hu, Haoqian Wang, Xin Yuan, Yulun Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many algorithms have been developed to solve the inverse problem of coded aperture snapshot spectral imaging (CASSI), i.e., recovering the 3D hyperspectral images (HSIs) from a 2D compressive measurement. In recent years, learning-based methods have demonstrated promising performance and dominated the mainstream research direction. However, existing CNN-based methods show limitations in capturing long-range dependencies and non-local self-similarity. Previous Transformer-based methods densely sample tokens, some of which are uninformative, and calculate the multi-head self-attention (MSA) between some tokens that are unrelated in content. This does not fit the spatially sparse nature of HSI signals and limits the model scalability. In this paper, we propose a novel Transformer-based method, coarse-to-fine sparse Transformer (CST), firstly embedding HSI sparsity into deep learning for HSI reconstruction. In particular, CST uses our proposed spectra-aware screening mechanism (SASM) for coarse patch selecting. Then the selected patches are fed into our customized spectra-aggregation hashing multi-head self-attention (SAH-MSA) for fine pixel clustering and self-similarity capturing. Comprehensive experiments show that our CST significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods while requiring cheaper computational costs. The code and models will be released atthis https URL. An online Gradio demo is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[On Evaluating User Simulators for Interactive Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24892)**  `arXiv:2611.24892`  
  _Hengguang Zhou, I-Hung Hsu, Li Chen, Priyanshi Shrivastava, Anastasios Angelopoulos, Wei-Lin Chiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User simulators offer a scalable way to evaluate interactive agents, but how to assess their quality remains underexplored. In particular, it is unclear whether existing user simulator evaluation frameworks reliably detect meaningful failures in simulated user behavior. This uncertainty makes it difficult to interpret simulator evaluation. To understand what a high benchmark score tells us about simulator quality, we (meta-)evaluate the metrics themselves. To instantiate this meta-evaluation, we deliberately perturb simulated replays to induce failures such as altered goals, omitted constraints, premature stopping, and acceptance of broken work, then test whether the metrics penalize these failures. Evaluation methods from three prior studies detect only 5-6 of the 10 perturbations; their combined scores even favor populations instructed to accept broken work over unmodified controls. Motivated by these gaps, we propose a new evaluation suite that combines utility of user simulators for evaluating agents and two measures of fidelity to real users: population-level fidelity assesses collective interaction patterns, while trace-level fidelity assesses the preservation of individual users' goals, constraints, and persona. Under the same detection criterion, our evaluation suite detects all 10 perturbations. Evaluating six user simulators on 351 real multi-turn sessions in which 348 distinct users across 23 languages and agents work together to create and refine artifacts such as full-stack web apps, games, design assets, and automated workflows, we find that population-level measures can miss when a simulated user pursues the wrong goal, ignores a constraint, or behaves out of character, highlighting the need to assess fidelity at comprehensive granularities.
  </details>

- **[Cap-and-Track: Optimizing LLM Inference Request Dispatch Across Shifting Capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24891)**  `arXiv:2611.24891`  
  _Benjamin Novotny, Doug Szeto, Chang Wang, Shicheng Chen, George Zhihua Yuan, Bob Kamma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fixed dispatch rate for LLM inference becomes stale as serving fleets resize and request sizes change. Generative output length is unknown at admission, and shared provider pools can lose capacity. We present Cap-and-Track, a design for asynchronous inference across provider capacity and internal GPUs. Meta's traffic shaper implements Cap-and-Track for post and live-video understanding. Refusals signal the backend's current admission ceiling. Durable priority queues hold excess work outside execution, including refused requests for retry. Cap is a fail-fast admission check at the saturated resource that refuses work before a long wait. Using only those refusals, the Track dispatcher probes upward and backs off. It follows the ceiling without output-length prediction or a fleet-level rate to retune. In a concurrent same-traffic production A/B, the shaper ran per-replica Cap plus Track. It served a classifier on 43% fewer H100s than its queued, fixed-rate precursor, though lower-priority work waited longer at backfill peaks. In steady-capacity real-engine replay, Track ran within 1% of the goodput of a fixed rate tuned above measured capacity, with about a quarter of that rate's refusal share. The shaper carries more than 300 million online requests and hundreds of terabytes of media a day. During a backfill, it handled 1.1 billion requests in its busiest 24 hours. Lessons include keeping queues inside inference engines just long enough to fill batches, steering dispatch by capacity refusals alone, keeping dispatch stateful, and sizing fleets by how long low-priority work can wait.
  </details>

- **[Monoculture Robust Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24881)**  `arXiv:2611.24881`  
  _Lee Cohen, Jon Kleinberg, Omer Reingold_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a notion of monoculture robustness in Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learning, as a way to mitigate the problem of algorithmic monoculture (Kleinberg and Raghavan, 2021): the tendency of models sharing the same components (e.g., training data) to fail on the same inputs. Specifically, we ask whether a learner with access to a single dataset can output $k$ individually accurate hypotheses whose joint failure probability is comparable to training on $k$ independent datasets. We say that such a learning procedure achieves monoculture robustness, and we quantify this by bounding the conjunctive error that all $k$ hypotheses misclassify points in an individual sense. We design methods for achieving this type of guarantee by drawing a surprising connection between replicability (Impagliazzo et al., 2022) and monoculture robustness. Specifically, we show that if the base learner is $\rho$-replicable, then running it independently $k$ times on the same sample yields individual monoculture error that shrinks exponentially with $k$, controlled by the single-run error of the base learner at the point and $\rho$. We complement these positive results with a lower bound: for individual monoculture robustness, we prove a worst-case lower bound showing that, for learners, driving the individual conjunctive error down to $\gamma$ can require a sample-size multiplier of $t = \Omega(\log(1/\gamma))$.
  </details>

- **[Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24877)**  `arXiv:2611.24877`  
  _Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.
  </details>

- **[TangentPDE: Learning Local Operator Responses with Sparse Finite-Difference Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24873)**  `arXiv:2611.24873`  
  _Xinjian Xie, Ruxuan Chen, Jiashu Li, Tianyu Gao, Ye Wei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural PDE operators are often optimized through state reconstruction, which largely constrains operator values at observed inputs but does not fully characterize their sensitivity to local perturbations. A more faithful approximation of operator behavior requires not only accurate state predictions, but also correct variations along local perturbation directions. We present TangentPDE, which uses finite differences to approximate directional derivatives and constructs task-relevant finite-step responses from the state outputs of a reference solver at sparse paired inputs. Its objective jointly constrains state fidelity, response-direction alignment, and response-magnitude calibration, thereby learning both operator values and their local variations. Because the response labels are constructed solely from state outputs at paired inputs, the method requires neither explicit derivative labels, linearized or adjoint PDE solves, nor full Jacobians, and remains applicable to nonlinear solvers without derivative interfaces. Building on this, we additionally propose a selectively executed adaptation mechanism to strengthen local operator representations under finite-difference supervision. Budget-matched and correspondence-perturbed controls isolate the effect of relational supervision from improvements attributable to additional state observations or increased data volume. Designed as a plug-and-play training framework, TangentPDE integrates with diverse neural-operator backbones. Across four PDE systems and 20 task-backbone settings, TangentPDE lowers the three-seed mean state error in 18 settings and the corresponding Rollout@20 error in 18, while intrinsic finite-step response gains are strongest on DiffReact and remain task- and backbone-dependent elsewhere.
  </details>

- **[Atomic MathReasoner: Instance-Level Composition of Atomic Reasoning Skills for Mathematical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24846)**  `arXiv:2611.24846`  
  _Jiashu Li, Tianyu Gao, Qinglong Liu, Jinping Wang, Ye Wei, Zhiqiang Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most existing approaches enhance mathematical reasoning in large language models (LLMs) as a monolithic capability, despite the fact that different mathematical problems require different reasoning skills. Recent work has explored a more fine-grained perspective by decomposing mathematical reasoning into atomic skills and developing specialized modules for individual capabilities. However, how to effectively utilize these atomic abilities remains underexplored. To address this challenge, we propose Atomic MathReasoner (AMR), an instance-level framework for composing atomic reasoning skills. AMR first trains a library of capability-specific LoRA experts and performs gradient-free optimization over held-out validation subsets to discover a diverse set of low-loss composition prototypes. It evaluates the preference of each prototype using the token-level negative log-likelihood of reference solutions, filters ambiguous assignments through a confidence margin, and distills the resulting pseudo-labels into a lightweight router. During inference, the router selects an appropriate composition prototype for each problem, enabling problem-specific utilization of atomic skills without costly online optimization. Experiments on the AtomicMath benchmark across general and mathematics-specialized LLMs demonstrate consistent improvements. On Qwen2.5-Math-Instruct-7B, AMR increases average field accuracy from 64.4% to 68.1% at Level 1 and from 59.3% to 63.7% at Level 2, while improving average logical reasoning performance from 39.4% to 46.8%. These results demonstrate that effectively composing specialized atomic skills provides a promising approach to strengthening mathematical reasoning in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[What Survival Benchmarks Don't Tell You: Impact of Model Selection and Dataset Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24837)**  `arXiv:2611.24837`  
  _Sandra Dening, Emre Kavak, Christian Wachinger, George H. Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the proliferation of survival analysis methods, current benchmarks often operate under narrow conditions: they heavily rely on the concordance index (C-index) for evaluation and model selection, and frequently either exclude deep learning (DL) methods or underrepresent challenging dataset regimes. To address these limitations, we conduct a large-scale, neutral benchmark comparing 10 classical, tree-based, and DL methods across 49 diverse datasets. Crucially, we treat the model selection criterion (optimizing for C-index, Integrated Brier Score (IBS), or Mean Absolute Error (MAE-PO)) as an independent experimental variable. Using Bayesian mixed-effects modeling, we demonstrate that evaluation design heavily dictates conclusions. For complex DL and tree-based architectures, selecting hyperparameters to optimize for IBS instead of C-index yields substantial improvements in probabilistic accuracy and calibration at a negligible cost to discriminative ranking. Furthermore, method performance (C-index, IBS, MAE-PO, and X-CAL) is fundamentally governed by dataset characteristics. While DL models scale exceptionally well with larger sample sizes, they suffer notable performance drops in high-dimensional feature spaces. Additionally, we expose a structural vulnerability where heavy censoring artificially inflates absolute calibration metrics, creating overly conservative tests. Ultimately, our findings challenge the utility of global single-number performance summaries and advocate for survival analysis results that transparently report the evaluation setup. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Bias-Only Training Dynamics in ReLU Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24797)**  `arXiv:2611.24797`  
  _Shreyas Chaudhari, Jose M. F. Moura_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural networks with randomly initialized, fixed weights and trainable biases are universal approximators, yet the dynamics of bias-only training remain largely unexplored. We study these dynamics in one-hidden-layer ReLU networks through activation codes, the binary patterns of active neurons that partition the input space into convex polyhedral regions. We show that the gradients with respect to the biases depend on each input only through its activation code, and hence the training dynamics can be characterized with these codes. In particular, as long as the activation codes remain fixed, training reduces to a linear least-squares problem with the codes as features. Training can then be understood as a sequence of least-squares problems, with transitions occurring when neurons change their activation states. We show that the fraction of switching neurons vanishes as the network widens, and the training dynamics approach those of linear least squares on the initial activation codes in the infinite-width limit. Our experiments support these theoretical findings, showing that while activation switches can noticeably affect training in narrower networks, their relative frequency and the associated changes in the dynamics diminish as the width increases.
  </details>

- **[Reconstruction vs. Prediction Pre-Training of Discrete Video Representations for Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24781)**  `arXiv:2611.24781`  
  _Ron Kosova, Francesco Croce_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised video encoders are commonly trained to either reconstruct pixels or predict latent features, yet it remains unclear how these objectives shape representations for downstream dynamics. We present a capacity-matched study isolating this choice: two identical video encoders with a shared FSQ bottleneck are trained under autoencoding and V-JEPA objectives on a continuous control environment. We introduce a simple asymmetric discretization scheme to apply the same bottleneck to the predictive model. The resulting representations exhibit a clear trade-off: the autoencoder preserves substantially more physical state information and achieves slightly better one-step latent prediction, while the predictive encoder accumulates less error under autoregressive rollout. Structural probes suggest that this advantage is associated with greater robustness to low-level visual perturbations and stronger dependence on temporal context. These results indicate that predictive and reconstructive objectives induce distinct representations of dynamics, trading state fidelity for properties that may benefit long-horizon prediction.
  </details>

- **[Approximate Dynamic Programming with Basis Function Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24737)**  `arXiv:2611.24737`  
  _Mohammadreza Fathi, Chi-Guhn Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents a novel approach for approximating the value function in Markov decision processes (MDPs). Our approach is grounded in the approximate linear programming (ALP) framework, wherein basis functions (BFs) play an important role. Traditionally, BFs have been pre-defined, randomly generated, or restricted to specific structures, which both limits the accuracy that can be achieved and leaves no mechanism for improving a given approximation. We instead learn BFs from the model itself, by repeatedly applying the Bellman operator to the current value estimate. We introduce the Bellman operator basis function (BOBF) learning method, and prove that each learned BF improves the approximation by a quantifiable amount and that the method converges to the optimal value function. Its shrunken counterpart (SBOBF) retains this guarantee while capping the number of BFs, and therefore the computational effort per iteration. For large-scale problems, where the number of constraints is prohibitive, we develop hybrid variants (HBOBF and SHBOBF) that combine constraint sampling with BF learning, and we show that the improvement and finite-termination guarantees are preserved under explicitly stated conditions. Numerical experiments on (continuous) perishable inventory and queueing control problems demonstrate that our BF learning techniques significantly outperform existing methods in the literature.
  </details>

- **[How Generative Models Generate: A Concise Guide to the Generative AI Landscape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24728)**  `arXiv:2611.24728`  
  _Tianhua Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapidly expanding generative AI landscape brings together methods that differ in architecture, learning objective, mathematical representation and sampling procedure. A concise, selectively organised technical guide can provide an accessible starting point for developing a coherent understanding of this increasingly complex landscape. This article takes a generation-centred approach, connecting how models produce new observations with the modelling and learning principles that make generation possible. We develop a two-level perspective: first, distinguishing three recurring generation routes-direct transformation or decoding, sequential conditional construction, and iterative updating or transport; and second, examining the modelling and learning principles underlying these routes. Representative approaches are introduced through their key components, learning objectives and sampling procedures. We then examine their broader connections, including how different architectures and generation mechanisms can overlap or be combined. By bringing together essential technical explanations within a compact conceptual framework, the article aims to help readers develop a working understanding of unfamiliar approaches, recognise connections among models they have encountered, and navigate different approaches within the broader generative AI landscape.
  </details>

- **[Equal Steps, Unequal Curvature: Where Muon's Update Goes Wrong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24644)**  `arXiv:2611.24644`  
  _Biswa Sengupta, Jinhua Wang, Léo Maxime Brunswic_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon updates each hidden weight matrix along the polar factor of its momentum, the steepest-descent direction under the spectral norm. That choice fixes two allocations without consulting the loss: every singular direction of the momentum receives the same step, and in a non-square matrix each neuron's step is set by its leverage score. Whether they are good is a question about curvature, which steepest-descent arguments do not answer; we measure it. Using held-out gradients and Hessian-vector products, we decompose Muon's step by singular-value band and by neuron group and compute each group's second-order optimal multiplier. In a 124M-parameter decoder trained on FineWeb-Edu, the multipliers rise monotonically from the top singular directions to the tail: Muon over-steps the high-curvature top of its spectrum. We prove in Lean when such a diagnostic licenses an intervention at Muon's own update norm. On the measured curvature, the criterion predicts, and held-out losses confirm, that moving step length off the top helps mid-training and early in the cooldown, but not at its end. We then test interventions under per-step norm matching, tuned baselines and paired seeds. Orthogonalizing Q, K and V separately rather than as one fused matrix lowers the loss by 0.0250 nats and accounts for all of the gain of block-wise variants. A matmul-only, rotation-equivariant spectral tilt $\sigma^{-1/4}$ applied in the cooldown lowers the loss of this stronger baseline by a further 0.0120 nats, additively with NorMuon (0.0158 nats together, three seeds; the baseline needs about 4.3% more tokens to match), and by 0.0188 nats at 355M parameters (three seeds). Energy-matched Schatten-1 weight decay adds a further 0.0052 nats. Geometrically principled changes that do not act on the measured miscalibration, namely exact polar factors, steepest-descent input whitening and top-heavy decay, do not help.
  </details>

- **[Choosing the Right Representations: Task-Aware Fusion for Multimodal Materials Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24641)**  `arXiv:2611.24641`  
  _Huu Binh Ta, Alex Morehead, N. Benjamin Erichson, Ferdinando Fioretto, Sandeep Madireddy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal language models for materials science rely on effective mechanisms for incorporating three-dimensional structural representations. MatterChat addresses this challenge using a large bridge module between a pretrained structural encoder and the LLM, together with a separate contrastive alignment stage. We instead ask whether this bridge can adaptively select and combine representations from multiple structural encoders according to the target property. We find that supervised and generative material encoders provide complementary information for property prediction. We found that, for Zatom-1, this information is not concentrated in the final layer. Instead, the most informative representations occur at different depths depending on the target property. This motivates Task-Conditioned Attention Fusion (TCAF), a lightweight attention-based bridge that uses task-conditioned latent queries to combine CHGNet features with intermediate Zatom-1 representations. The bridge is trained directly on downstream prediction tasks, allowing representation selection and material-language alignment to be learned jointly without separate contrastive pretraining. Across classification and regression tasks, TCAF outperforms MatterChat, with up to 47.8% lower RMSE, while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and halving the overall training time. Further experiments incorporating UMA-S demonstrate TCAF's flexibility across encoder combinations, with the three-encoder configuration achieving the highest macro-average classification accuracy of 89.61%.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Effectiveness of Location Encoding Methods for Species Distribution Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24622)**  `arXiv:2611.24622`  
  _Christian Lange, Oisin Mac Aodha_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the spatial distribution of species is a key question in ecology. A variety of solutions, from statistical, traditional machine learning, through to deep learning-based methods have been proposed. These methods take sparse observations for a species of interest as input and aim to generate dense spatial outputs indicating the spatial range of the species. Orthogonal to the choice of statistical model used is the question of what input features are most informative. Thanks to recent developments, the number of options has greatly expanded, with choices spanning different climate variables, learning-based encodings, earth embedding models, or raw transformed geographic coordinates. In this work, we introduce a standardized evaluation framework and quantify the effectiveness of different location encodings in the context of species range estimation, both at the local and global scale.
  </details>

- **[Morpho-Spectral Projection Analysis (MSPA) for Plant Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24599)**  `arXiv:2611.24599`  
  _Lucas Prado Osco, Marcelo Santos da Silva, Filipe Vieira Silva, Liang Zhao, Ana Paula Marques Ramos, Ednaldo Jose Ferreira, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plant reflectance spectroscopy across the visible, near-infrared, and shortwave infrared domain encodes foliar biochemical and anatomical traits into contiguous spectral signatures with locally differentiable structure; however, extreme inter-band collinearity and unfavorable sample-to-feature ratios challenge predictive modeling. Conventional dimensionality reduction methods routinely adopted in spectroscopy, are column-permutation invariant: they treat adjacent wavelengths as unordered tabular columns and discard the sequential differential geometry of absorption features. Here, we propose Morpho-Spectral Projection Analysis (MSPA), a supervised dimensionality reduction operator that projects contiguous spectra into a compact morpho-spectral subspace through three complementary blocks: (i) additive continuum-removed absorption depth, area, and red-edge inflection kinematics; (ii) local multi-order singular eigenmodes (0th, 1st, and 2nd derivatives) partitioned across contiguous radiative sub-windows; and (iii) global Fisher-weighted canonical subspaces regularized via trace-normalized isotropic covariance shrinkage. We validated MSPA across 8,963 spectra spanning five plant systems, coupled with six classical machine learning classifiers and four Tabular Foundation Models (TabPFN v3, TabICL v2, TabDPT v1.2, and TabFM). Despite compressing the 1,580-band spectrum by 96%, MSPA outperformed the full spectrum in 92.0% of all benchmarks and conventional reducers in 90.0% of all model-crop evaluations, raising the global mean macro-F1 from 0.7756 (full spectrum) to 0.8520, accelerating in-context inference by up to 126.5x. Designed as a self-contained spectral projection operator, the MSPA method can be directly integrated into spectroscopy workflows via the open-source code available at GitHub:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Activity Recognition to Shift Detection: Variational Wi-Fi Sensing with Evidential Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24596)**  `arXiv:2611.24596`  
  _Luca Cotti, Marco Cominelli, Francesco Gringoli, Lance M. Kaplan, Mani B. Srivastava, Trevor Bihl, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human motion perturbs wireless channels, making commodity Wi-Fi devices a practical platform for device-free activity recognition. Yet Channel State Information (CSI) also depends strongly on the person, receiver position, recording session, and surrounding environment, so a classifier can continue producing activity labels after its deployment conditions have moved away from those seen during training. We propose Wi-HAR, a multi-antenna architecture in which four Variational Auto-Encoders learn compact distributions from raw Wi-Fi 6 CSI magnitudes and a delayed-fusion classifier combines their means and log-variances. On twelve activities from a public benchmark, Wi-HAR reaches 96.6% median recognition accuracy, compared with 70.8-81.5% for independently optimized single-antenna models, showing that the antennas provide complementary views of the same activity. We then use the same latent backbone to detect deployment shifts without using shifted samples for model selection or calibration. A class-conditional uncertainty score derived from Evidential Deep Learning achieves a macro-average ROC-AUC of 0.897 across eleven datasets that change receiver position, participant, recording session, or room, and detects 82.5% of shifted samples at a realized 5.2% in-distribution rejection rate. Detection performance nevertheless varies substantially across receiver positions, showing that the same physical change does not induce a uniform uncertainty response. The compact representation also supports shallow decision-tree analysis using variables from several antennas.
  </details>

- **[ICICLE: In-Context Inference for Curve-Learning Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24469)**  `arXiv:2611.24469`  
  _Laurits Fredsgaard, Michael Riis Andersen, Mikkel N. Schmidt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Freeze-thaw Bayesian optimization allocates training budget by predicting how partially observed learning curves will continue. LC-PFN first applied prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) to learning-curve extrapolation, and FT-PFN extended this approach to configuration-aware freeze-thaw optimization with a ten-slot hyperparameter encoder. We introduce In-Context Inference for Curve-Learning Extrapolation (ICICLE), an in-context surrogate that accepts a variable number of hyperparameters through a shared set encoder and is trained on a synthetic prior covering typed hyperparameters, heavy-tailed noise, quantization, and divergence. ICICLE has 1.28 million parameters (8.7% of FT-PFN's count) and produces similar mean-regret trajectories to FT-PFN on LCBench, PD1, and TaskSet. On the 86-task Meta-Album benchmark with 33 hyperparameters, ICICLE reaches lower mean normalized regret than Quick-Tune overall and on extended, with no clear difference on mini or micro. It does so without pre-training or meta-training on Meta-Album tasks, whereas Quick-Tune is meta-trained on related Meta-Album tasks. Our release samples synthetic training tasks on the fly and provides a freeze-thaw scheduler for Ray Tune and an ONNX model for inference without JAX or PyTorch.
  </details>

- **[DyNA-Mixer: Decoupling Time and Topology for Scalable Dynamic Node Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24426)**  `arXiv:2611.24426`  
  _Yuanxing Zheng, Liping Wang, Xuemin Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous-time dynamic graphs preserve the ordering, timing, and evolving strength of interactions that static snapshots discard, making dynamic graph mining essential for evolving systems such as recommender systems, trade networks, and transaction platforms. Research in this area has centered on dynamic link prediction, which forecasts only whether a future interaction will occur, whereas many applications also need its intensity. The dynamic node affinity prediction (DNAP) problem generalizes this task to continuous targets and full-candidate ranking, making it more broadly applicable but substantially harder. This raises two coupled difficulties: ranking requires a predictor expressive enough to capture both multi-scale temporal evolution and graph-wide collaborative patterns, yet running such a predictor over every source--destination pair is computationally prohibitive. We propose DyNA-Mixer, which decouples temporal forecasting from collaborative structure modeling. It adopts a multi-scale temporal predictor for pair-specific affinity evolution, constructs a Virtual Collaborative Graph that infers query-time relationships and aggregates trends from related interactions, and fuses the two through a gated residual mechanism. A sparsity-aware evaluation strategy further restricts expensive forecasting to pairs with recent non-zero affinities. Experiments on four Temporal Graph Benchmark datasets for the DNAP problem show that DyNA-Mixer achieves new state-of-the-art NDCG@10 on every dataset, including a 17.8\% relative test improvement over the strongest baseline on tgbn-token, the largest dataset.
  </details>

- **[MINT: Meeting-time Indicators for Truncation in Multi-Step Off-Policy RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24413)**  `arXiv:2611.24413`  
  _Seungyub Han, Taehyun Cho, Dohyeong Kim, Kyungjae Lee, Jungwoo Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many multi-step off-policy temporal-difference methods handle policy mismatch through products of per-step trace coefficients, leading to a trade-off between variance control and long-horizon signal. The maximum trace length consequently becomes a sensitive hyperparameter. We propose MINT (Meeting-time INdicators for Truncation), which replaces these products with a binary stopping indicator: one until coupled behavior and target trajectories first meet, zero thereafter. At the true value function, the indicator's idempotence yields a variance bound governed by meeting-time survival. For practical implementation, a single sampled action provides a model-free surrogate for the stopping rule, requiring no transition model or mixing-time estimate. We derive separate sufficient conditions for contraction of the state-coupled operator and its practical surrogate under fixed-policy evaluation. On MuJoCo and DeepMind Control tasks, MINT outperforms the reported multi-step baselines; the main MuJoCo comparison uses a common cap without per-task cap-length tuning. We also demonstrate the effectiveness of MINT on Atari 100k, RWRL, and OGBench experiments; MINT adapts the backup horizon to reduce target variability relative to long fixed-horizon backups across tasks and dynamics.
  </details>

- **[RSIBench-Infra: Can Agents Optimize Their Own Training and Inference Infrastructure at Production Scale?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24395)**  `arXiv:2611.24395`  
  _Hanlin Wu, Yifei Wang, Zhicheng Cai, Huan-ang Gao, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving the efficiency of training and inference infrastructure is a practical component of recursive self-improvement. Existing benchmarks for agent-driven optimization largely focus on individual software layers such as kernel, leaving unclear whether agents can deliver end-to-end performance gains under production workloads. We introduce RSIBench-Infra, a benchmark of 20 training and inference tasks spanning diverse model families and modalities, with deployments of up to 32 accelerators on NVIDIA GPUs and Ascend NPUs. Agents start from runnable baselines derived from official recipes and can modify the software stack across engine, kernel, and instruction levels. An OCI image-layer submission protocol captures these changes for reproducible deployment, while independent evaluation measures end-to-end performance under task-specific quality constraints. Across all task results, 66.2% exceed $1.10\times$ validated speedup. These results demonstrate that current agents can improve production-scale training and inference performance, but identifying effective optimization strategies and consistently translating local improvements into end-to-end gains remain challenging.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Rank in Recurrent Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24394)**  `arXiv:2611.24394`  
  _Leo Dobson, Anton Ragni_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) are a natural framework for temporal modelling, yet are often dismissed due to their sequential, non-parallelisable computation and difficulty in capturing long-range dependencies, typically attributed to vanishing and exploding gradients. We identify a distinct and under-explored limitation: an information bottleneck induced by rank-constrained hidden state dynamics, which limits the number of independent input directions that can be preserved over time, as reflected in the rank of the input-to-state mapping. We show that this constraint restricts temporal capacity independently of gradient stability. To address this, we introduce structured high-dimensional state dynamics that preserve temporal separability and prevent saturation of the input-to-state Jacobian over time. On controlled sequence tasks, the resulting model exhibits rapid learning despite remaining strictly within a standard RNN formulation. Our results demonstrate that RNN performance is governed by the evolution and preservation of temporal rank, and suggest that mitigating gradient pathologies alone is insufficient without explicitly maintaining temporal capacity.
  </details>

- **[Tell Me How Sure You Are: Communicating Uncertainty in Auxiliary Evidence to Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24351)**  `arXiv:2611.24351`  
  _Soumyajit Chatterjee, Baturalp Buyukates, Abhirup Ghosh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In many queries, the context information provided to generative foundation models, such as LLMs and VLMs, is inherently uncertain, including its representations of the physical world. While conventional prompting discards the associated uncertainty, this paper, for the first time in the literature, systematically demonstrates that communicating such uncertainty in context provides useful additional cues for downstream decisions and yields improvements across a wide range of settings.We formulate the decision-making problem as a zero-shot foundation model-assisted image classification task supported by auxiliary text-based characteristics. We choose this due to the availability of well-defined auxiliary properties, associated uncertainty, and unambiguous task correctness. Using five downstream foundation models across three datasets, we extensively evaluate uncertainty arising from both humans and learned models when observing or predicting auxiliary evidence. Across all settings, we find that communicating uncertainty substantially improves downstream LLM classification performance: macro-F1 improves up to $26.18$ percentage points compared to when the uncertainty information is not communicated.We further observe how the foundation models process such uncertainty information and find that auxiliary uncertainty contains predictive information about the correctness of downstream decisions. Together, these results establish uncertainty as useful contextual information and motivate more systematic consideration of uncertainty communication in future foundation-model inference pipelines.
  </details>

- **[Distribution-Free Uncertainty Quantification for Weather Forecasting: Conformal Prediction Under Spatiotemporal Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24329)**  `arXiv:2611.24329`  
  _Mariya_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal prediction gives finite-sample, distribution-free marginal coverage and has begun to be applied to gridded weather forecasts. Marginal coverage concerns the long-run fraction of covered points at a location, not how coverage behaves jointly across space and time on a given day. I benchmark eight interval-construction procedures (Gaussian, pooled and per-cell split conformal, per-season, adaptive conformal inference and rolling windows) for gridded 2 m temperature forecasts at eight lead times from 6 hours to 10 days, using ERA5 at 5.625 degree resolution, a deliberately modest convolutional forecaster and ECMWF's operational HRES, with calibration in 2017-2018 and testing in 2019-2021. Alongside coverage, width and interval score, I report dependence diagnostics: a spatial overdispersion index, quantiles of daily spatial coverage and miss persistence relative to a shuffled-days null. Per-cell methods reach near-nominal marginal coverage, yet the day-to-day variance of the fraction of cells covered is roughly 3 to 10 times what independent cells would give, and misses cluster in time; both effects grow with lead time for the CNN, also appear for HRES, and are shared by all methods. An oracle that divides each day's normalised errors by that day's mean removes 81 to 92 percent of the excess dispersion for the CNN and 93 to 98 percent for HRES, so days of large errors across much of the grid account for most of it. A day-scale predicted from seasonal terms and already-verified errors lowers the overdispersion index without widening intervals but improves interval score by at most 0.6 percent; on HRES from 12 hours to 3 days it has the lowest overdispersion index while ACI or season-conditional calibration has the best interval score, so no method is best on both. The study covers one variable, one coarse grid, a reanalysis target and two forecasters.
  </details>

- **[Supervised Hebbian Class Networks: Learning Without Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24314)**  `arXiv:2611.24314`  
  _John Loverich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces Supervised Hebbian Class Networks as a method for training multi- layer perceptrons and convolutional networks without gradient calculations or backpropa- gation. Each neuron in a learned layer is assigned a class, while connections follow the usual fully connected or convolutional pattern. The method combines a class-based supervision rule shared across layers with a Lion optimizer adapted to Hebbian activity products. All layers train together throughout the run, and their activities provide class predictions di- rectly, without a separately trained classifier. The experiments test the method on MNIST with a multilayer perceptron and on CIFAR-10 with a convolutional networ
  </details>

- **[Element-Wise Femoral Stress Prediction: A Hybrid Graph Neural Network Surrogate for Finite Element Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24274)**  `arXiv:2611.24274`  
  _Sikha O K, Abdollah Moslemi, Elham Alizadeh, Ludovic Humbert, Miguel A. Gonzalez Ballester, Jerome Noailly_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hip fractures represent a significant clinical challenge because they cause excess disability and mortality, and have a growing incidence in aging populations, as bone fragility increases. Although finite element analysis (FEA) provides biomechanical assessment of femoral strength, its clinical applicability is mainly limited by high computational cost. Conventional machine learning (ML) approaches have been explored as surrogate models for FEA; predictions are fast but fail to capture the spatial and topological structure of finite element meshes. To address these limitations, this study proposes a hybrid Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based framework for femoral stress prediction using graph-structured finite element modeling (FEM) data. The proposed GCN-SAGE-Residual architecture combines graph convolution, neighborhood aggregation, and residual learning to model spatial dependencies and stress propagation across mesh elements. We systematically evaluated 14 models in a primary comparison, spanning classical ML methods and baseline GNN architectures, complemented by a targeted architectural ablation that isolates the contribution of each component of the proposed design. Evaluation results demonstrate that the proposed hybrid architecture achieves the strongest predictive performance among all evaluated models. The proposed hybrid model achieves the best performance with an R^2 score of 0.8022 and mean absolute error of 2.879 MPa, highlighting the importance of incorporating mesh topology in biomechanical prediction tasks. The proposed framework provides a scalable and computationally efficient alternative for FEM-based orthopedic analyses and fracture risk assessment. The code is publicly available on GitHub.
  </details>

- **[Towards Reliable Scaling Laws for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24214)**  `arXiv:2611.24214`  
  _Johannes Hog, Neeratyoy Mallik, Steven Adriaensen, Jan Hendrik Metzen, Tom Viering, Danny Stoll, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling laws are widely used to predict foundation model performance and guide compute allocation. However, tabular foundation models (TFMs) differ from conventional foundation models in that they are pretrained on virtually unlimited synthetic data from configurable distributions, while ultimately being evaluated on real-world datasets. We investigate whether scaling laws for TabPFNv2-style models reliably extrapolate to larger compute budgets and generalize across evaluation settings. Our analysis reveals differences between synthetic and real-world scaling behavior and shows that accurately fitting a scaling law does not necessarily yield reliable prescriptions on unseen datasets. In particular, selecting configurations using too few evaluation datasets leads to systematically over-optimistic performance estimates. We further demonstrate that compute-optimal scaling requires expanding the range of synthetic pretraining dataset sizes alongside model size and training compute. Increasing the maximum numbers of rows and features sampled during pretraining improves performance and produces more predictable scaling trends. These insights guide the training of more efficient TabPFNv2-like models, using the open-source training pipeline of nanoTabPFN.
  </details>

- **[Learning with Unlearning: Synaptic Role Partitioning for Simultaneous Continual Learning and Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24205)**  `arXiv:2611.24205`  
  _Mohammad Ali Vahedifar, Behzad Bozorgtabar, Qi Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual Learning (CL) asks a model to acquire new knowledge without forgetting what it already knows. Machine Unlearning (MU) asks it to destroy specific knowledge without damaging the rest. The challenge is integrating them in the same system; the two objectives pull on the same weights, and an edit made for one harms the other. To address this, we propose Learning with Unlearning (LwU), a unified framework that resolves the conflict where it occurs: at the individual synapse. LwU scores every weight twice, once against the data to keep and once against the data to delete, and partitions the network into four zones with separate update rules. Weights that only require retention are frozen. Weights that only require deletion are cleared and returned to the network as free capacity. Contested weights take a gradient-ascent step projected orthogonally to the retention gradient. The remaining capacity learns the incoming task by a privileged teacher via self-distillation. Deletion is served post hoc, and no retraining is required. Across both CL and MU jobs, LwU outperforms or matches the strongest baseline. Its real-world practicality has been demonstrated on the Physical Artificial Intelligence (PAI) dataset we conducted. Additionally, in the image generation task and in removing dangerous content, it outperformed the closest baseline, as shown in Fig.1.
  </details>

- **[QuantAtlas-OM: Taming Neural Spectral Bias in Long-Horizon Spatiotemporal Ocean Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24124)**  `arXiv:2611.24124`  
  _Hao Wu, Yuan Gao, Tong Wu, Fan Xu, Fan Zhang, Jinhan Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive neural networks exhibit spectral bias in long-horizon spatiotemporal prediction. As rollouts progress, high-frequency details decay, blurring the predicted fields and destabilizing long-range forecasts. Ocean dynamics offer a particularly stringent test because kinetic energy is concentrated in mesoscale eddies and fronts spanning tens of kilometers. This degradation stems from coupling large-scale phase evolution and small-scale energy retention in a shared feature space. Evolution on a coarse grid smooths out high-wavenumber eddies, whereas evolution on a fine grid amplifies small-scale phase errors and triggers rollout instability. We introduce QuantAtlas-OM (QuantAtlas Ocean Model), a scale-spectral decoupling framework that confines deep temporal evolution to a low-wavenumber latent manifold for long-term stability while routing full-resolution eddy and front residuals through an outer bypass to the decoder. Across physical regimes, the evolution core pairs non-local attention with resolution-invariant dilated spatial gating. Across three western boundary currents, QuantAtlas-OM retains $43.7\%$ to $86.5\%$ of mesoscale spectral energy along the $k^{-3}$ cascade at Day $120$, sustains anomaly correlation coefficients of $0.81$ to $0.88$ at Day $60$, and reaches $0.66$ at Day $120$ in the Kuroshio ($2.5\times$ to $7.7\times$ the best baselines). Trained at $0.25^\circ$, QuantAtlas-OM transfers zero-shot to a global $0.125^\circ$ grid ($0.81$ Day-$10$ spatial correlation) and leads global $0.25^\circ$, $23$-level 3D ocean simulations ($120$ days) and coupled ocean-atmosphere forecasts ($60$ days), ranking first on $15$ of $16$ metrics and cutting $60$- to $120$-day salinity RMSE by $13\%$ to $27\%$ across global ocean basins.
  </details>

- **[The 7th International Verification of Neural Networks Competition (VNN-COMP 2026): Summary and Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24114)**  `arXiv:2611.24114`  
  _Tobias Ladner, Konstantin Kaulen, Stanley Bak, Julien Girard-Satabin, Taylor T. Johnson, Edoardo Manino, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This report summarizes the seventh International Verification of Neural Networks Com- petition (VNN-COMP 2026), held as part of the Symposium on AI Verification (SAIV) co-located with the International Conference on Computer-Aided Verification (CAV) at FLoC 2026 in Lisbon, Portugal.
