The goal of the competition is to provide an objective comparison of the state-of-the-art methods in neural network verification, in terms of scal- ability and speed. Along this line, we used standard formats (ONNX for neural networks and VNNLIB for specifications), standard hardware (all tools are run by the organizers on AWS), and tool parameters provided by the tool authors. This report summarizes the rules, benchmarks, participating tools, results, and lessons learned from this competition.
  </details>

- **[UCLIR: Unsupervised Continual Learning using Independent Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24093)**  `arXiv:2611.24093`  
  _Kamel Aouaidjia, Yanchao Duan, Zihui Zhen, Xin Li, Yongfei Lv, Chongsheng Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In continual learning, regularization-based methods aim to mimic neuroplasticity by updating network weights to learn new tasks while constraining changes to parameters that are important for previously learned tasks through penalty terms. However, as the number of tasks increases, preserving knowledge from earlier tasks becomes less effective. This is mainly because updates for new tasks are distributed across the entire network, where parameters are highly interdependent due to the dense connectivity between neurons. Inspired by the Exponential Distance Rule (EDR), which states that the number of neural connections decreases with increasing physical distance, learning is naturally locally correlated while inter-area updates remain less task-dependent. Motivated by this principle, in this paper we demonstrate that applying weight regularization to multiple small and independently learned complementary representations is more effective than regularizing a single large shared representation. By decomposing the input into independently learned components, learning new tasks induces only localized parameter updates within specific representation, which limit interference and improve knowledge retention across tasks. We employ independent autoencoders that learn different parts of the input independently through unsupervised regularization. At inference time, a linear classifier is used for evaluation. The experimental results on a sequence of tasks using 5-Splits MNIST, sequence of 5-datasets, 5-Splits CIFAR-10, 10-Splits Tiny ImageNet shows competitive results with supervised and replay-based methods. Code is available.
  </details>

- **[VESPA: Verifiable Symbolic Policy Adaptation for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24083)**  `arXiv:2611.24083`  
  _Paul König, Sascha Marton, Stefan Lüdtke, Christian Bartelt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe reinforcement learning (RL) aims to prevent agents from taking actions that are dangerous or destructive to their environment. This is especially important for safety-critical domains, such as robotics or autonomous driving. Formal verification can be used to prove agent safety through safety constraints. However, in practice, many policies do violate safety constraints, and thus are verified to be unsafe. Since the goal is to obtain a safe policy, we would need to retrain a new policy from scratch and then verify it again until a candidate happens to pass. Both training and verification are usually not cheap, which makes this process inefficient. We introduce VESPA ($\textbf{VE}$rifiable $\textbf{S}$ymbolic $\textbf{P}$olicy $\textbf{A}$daptation), a framework for targeted fine-tuning of decision tree policies. We show that using counterexamples for policy adaptation can make existing unsafe policies safe, or at least make considerable progress towards safety. Targeted fine-tuning of the existing policy on those counterexamples enables it to learn how to avoid violating safety constraints. We thus use the result of a formal verification as a training signal for fine-tuning. On CartPole, VESPA raises the mean number of verified safe steps from 63.8 to at least 92.7, reaching after two iterations what the same loop without counterexamples reaches after ten. On MountainCarContinuous, VESPA turns an unsafe policy into a safe policy in 53 of 60 runs. We conclude that counterexamples from a verifier are an effective training signal, which can be utilized to systematically improve the safety of policies. Our implementation is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Replay, Don't Resample: Off-Policy Consolidation of Long-Horizon RL Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24059)**  `arXiv:2611.24059`  
  _Zhenrui Yue, Gongxiaohui Chen, Huimin Zeng, Honglei Zhuang, Jiashuo Sun, Zhen Han, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier post-training follows a common recipe: train one expert per domain with reinforcement learning (RL), then consolidate the experts into one model via multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD). Such consolidation is expensive: the student samples in every environment again, an expert scores every sampled token, and the student inherits the drift of the final experts. Yet we argue that the experts' RL runs have already produced what consolidation needs: rollouts with their sampling probabilities and rewards, in the order the experts learned from them. We propose multi-expert replay policy distillation (MRPD), which replays these training logs through the student in the experts' order. At each step, the policy gradient is recomputed on these scored trajectories, and the final experts' distributions are distilled at the logged prefixes. Following the experts' own trajectory reduces the training-inference mismatch and keeps the student within the clip range, so the replay is off-policy in origin but quasi-on-policy throughout consolidation. MRPD samples nothing and calls no environment or reward model: the trainer is the sole process, and consolidating the experts costs only about a seventh of the compute used to train them. On three long-horizon agentic domains (reasoning, search and SQL), MRPD yields the best student model, substantially outperforming online RL baselines, and surpassing MOPD from the same experts at a fraction of MOPD's cost. Our analysis on the same logs shows that the replayed trajectories carry most of the gain, and that both the policy gradient and the distillation term add to it. Most interestingly, the order of the logs matters more than their amount or rewards, suggesting that expert training logs are a curriculum, not a data mixture.
  </details>

- **[Faster to the Wrong Answer: The Price of Local Updates in Federated Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24057)**  `arXiv:2611.24057`  
  _Molly Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local computation can improve the conditioning of federated updates but may also introduce bias, causing their fixed point to differ from the global minimizer. We characterize the minimum worst-case bias achievable under a prescribed conditioning bound. We consider strongly convex quadratic client objectives with Hessian eigenvalues in $[\mu,L]$ and local updates represented by a shared positive spectral filter. Client heterogeneity is measured by the root-mean-square gradient at the global minimizer. For a uniform surrogate condition number bounded by $\chi\in[1,L/\mu]$, we establish the exact worst-case bias per unit heterogeneity, $(L-\mu\chi)/(2\mu L\sqrt{\chi})$, and show that local gradient descent with sufficiently small step sizes and proximal updates attain this bound. Our analysis reduces populations of arbitrary dimension, including noncommuting Hessians, to distributions over scalar curvatures. This reduction provides optimality certificates and worst-case constructions involving at most three scalar clients. We further extend the sharp proximal bias bound to smooth strongly convex objectives beyond quadratics and quantify the error introduced by inexact local solves.
  </details>

- **[From Data to Valves: A Self-Learning, Production-Grade Edge Stack for Sustainable HVAC Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24052)**  `arXiv:2611.24052`  
  _Abbas Raza Ali, Muhammad Hamza Rehman, Xu Li, Muhammad Ajmal Siddiqui_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned HVAC controllers rarely leave the simulator, and those that do are typically trained offline, frozen at deployment and retrained from the cloud. We present a production stack in which three learning components run on one NVIDIA Jetson Orin gateway per building, adapt continuously from the building's own data, and verify their own effect. (a) A compact transformer-encoder forecaster, one per equipment group with learned per-unit embeddings, predicts every air-side unit's next-cycle temperature and learns prequentially, one gradient step per five-minute cycle, behind five independent gates that bound the influence of any corrupt reading. (b) A distributional soft actor-critic agent, one shared policy across unit types, converts the measured state into a chilled-water valve position under a structural safety envelope that no learned component can soften; its second generation learns nightly on the device from recorded transitions alone, with a behaviour anchor and critic calibration terms, is scored by four model-free metrics and promoted by atomic hot-swap. (c) A change-point regression baseline held to ASHRAE Guideline 14 supplies the counterfactual against which savings are certified. On eighteen months of data from a hotel the forecaster converges from random weights within a week and reaches 0.37, 0.39 and 0.11 degree C MAE for air-handling, fresh-air and fan-coil units with no retraining event; on a different hotel the baseline reproduces a 46-day held-out period to within 0.07%. The RL agent has completed its in-simulator campaign and runs in shadow on 104 units; we report measured latency and memory of every service on a shared 15 GB device, the systems lessons of the campaign, and precisely what the evidence does and does not yet establish.
  </details>

- **[PGU-Decoder: Prior-Guided Unified Decoding for Industrial Road-Network Map Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23936)**  `arXiv:2611.23936`  
  _Yuguang Ning, Bingbing Kang, Nan Zhao, Zhixuan Li, Ze Zhao, Dongdong Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Timely road-network map updates are essential to navigation and autonomous driving. Three key tasks are intra-intersection road network generation, merging/diverging detection, and drivable-area detection, covering topology, geometry, and attributes, respectively. Separate pipelines duplicate rule maintenance, quality assurance, and manual verification, increasing operational costs. We propose PGU-Decoder, a production-grade unified decoding framework for semantic prediction across these tasks. A Prior Query Module (PQM) constructs point-level prior queries from sampled locations on road-network geometries. Fixed reference points preserve positional priors throughout decoding, allowing the model to focus on surrounding context. Point-level representations are aggregated into instance-level representations, and a Relational Graph Attention Network (RGAT) captures inter-instance dependencies. Task-specific heads then generate predictions. We validate PGU-Decoder in large-scale automated road-network map updating across more than 360 cities in China, where it consistently outperforms seven baselines. Following deployment at AutoNavi Map, it achieves quality scores above 98% on all three tasks, reduces manual verification costs by 95%, and increases update frequency from weekly to hourly. These results demonstrate a practical and cost-effective industrial solution for road-network map updating.
  </details>

- **[Kernel Ridge Attention: Nonlinear Memory with Selective Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23896)**  `arXiv:2611.23896`  
  _Haowei Chen, Yichen Wang, Congfu Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Kernel Ridge Attention (KRA) is a recurrent sequence mixer that combines learned nonlinear features with covariance-guided memory updates. It stores associations through recursive ridge regression, using correlations among past keys to determine how new observations modify memory. A content-dependent Ornstein--Uhlenbeck transition controls retention and restores uncertainty for future writes. This construction admits an exact temporal-kernel interpretation and maintains an inference state whose size is independent of context length. Associative block maps and explicit adjoints support parallel training while preserving the recursive update. In controlled language-modeling experiments, KRA improves prediction quality over attention and recurrent baselines, strengthens natural associative recall over the evaluated recurrent models, and achieves practical distributed training throughput. These properties provide a principled foundation for nonlinear, adaptive sequence memory.
  </details>

- **[A Rank-One Bypass of Fixed Linear Erasure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23880)**  `arXiv:2611.23880`  
  _Jai Kumar Sharma, Vanshaj Khattar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear concept erasure can make a target unreadable to a specified family of linear classifiers applied to a model's current internal features. In an open-weight release, however, an attacker can modify the layers that produce those features. We study a fixed affine erasure layer whose upstream model is allowed to change. If the layer transmits even one direction visible to an allowed downstream classifier, the attacker can write a target score into that direction and carry it through the layer. We show that this condition is also necessary, and that for a nonconstant linear target score the required change is a rank-one affine update. Across six CIFAR-10 class-pair tasks, the route restores nearly all pre-erasure target performance and can leave a deployed benign linear head's scores unchanged to numerical tolerance. We then price the attack: with eight target labels and 2,000 unlabelled calibration examples, a 10% mean relative feature edit yields 79.8% test accuracy, which is 91.2% of the fitted score's gain over chance; tighter edit limits sharply reduce success. Linear guardedness therefore remains a valid guarantee about the current representation, but it is not by itself a tamper-resistance guarantee for an editable open-weight model.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Cross-Modal Reconstruction: Participant-Level Evaluation of sEMG-Kinematic Coupling in Post-Stroke Gait](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23856)**  `arXiv:2611.23856`  
  _Rafsan Jany, Md Rafid Islam, Mohammad Fahim Islam, Hafiz Abdur Rahman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate cross-modal reconstruction is often treated as evidence of physiological coupling, although it may simply reflect temporal patterns shared across participants. We tested whether frozen bidirectional models captured subject-specific neuromuscular coupling between electromyographic activity and gait kinematics in 82 held-out able-bodied and 43 post-stroke participants. Complete source-modality removal and cross-participant source substitution, along with a cycle-position-age-sex template baseline, were applied. Learned bilateral asymmetry was also compared with transparent waveform and derivative asymmetry for predicting Performance-Oriented Mobility Assessment (POMA) scores in the case of repeated nested cross-validation. Complete source removal increased reconstruction RMSE by 6.90% for electromyography-to-kinematics and 11.38% in the reverse direction. In contrast, cross-participant substitution increased RMSE by only 1.68% and 1.67%, respectively, while the template reduced RMSE by 2.89% and 0.55%. For POMA prediction, walking speed, age, and sex achieved an MAE of 3.693, compared with 4.229 and 4.427 after adding absolute and signed learned asymmetry, respectively. In all of the 20 repeated evaluations, all asymmetry representations performed worse than the covariate baseline. No localized learned feature remained significant after covariate adjustment and false-discovery-rate correction. The model mainly relied on shared gait-phase patterns, with no clear subject-specific coupling or added clinical value. Participant-level controls are therefore needed before reconstruction accuracy is treated as evidence of individualized physiology.
  </details>

- **[Online State-Representation Adaptation under Synthetic Interaction-Cue Remapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23836)**  `arXiv:2611.23836`  
  _Bin Yin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When cues change what they predict, continuing to update a learned mapping may help an agent choose the appropriate action. We tested this in a one-step synthetic task by comparing a logistic state predictor that continued to learn from state labels with the same predictor frozen at transfer. We evaluated ten conditions in six held-out environments, with 1,296 paired runs per condition and 1,296,000 transfer records in total. Late action accuracy was 93.39% with online updates and 64.63% with frozen coefficients, a paired difference of 28.76 [8.73, 53.19] percentage points (95% environment-bootstrap interval). A direct policy reached 95.65% using the same predictions with a different action threshold. A contextual bandit reached 94.23% and earned more cumulative utility than the adaptive predictor. The bandit used the reward of its chosen action, which in this task reveals the true state when combined with that action. Weights based on development history lowered cumulative utility relative to neutral weights (difference -3.45 [-4.48, -2.43]). The adaptive predictor outperformed its frozen counterpart in late accuracy in each of the six test environments. The other comparisons show how the choice of action threshold and the information available in feedback affect what can be attributed to online adaptation.
  </details>

- **[TabBioMed: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Biomedical Tabular Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23779)**  `arXiv:2611.23779`  
  _Pau Mateo-Bernadó, Saivenkata Nagavyjayanthi Polapragada, David Bonet, Pol Arbiol Rakuljic, Laia Mogas Pladevall, Marçal Comajoan Cara, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in machine learning have accelerated biomedical prediction, but evaluation on structured biomedical data remains fragmented, especially for datasets with high dimensionality, large sample size, missingness, and class imbalance. We introduce TabBioMed, a large-scale benchmark of 94 curated public biomedical tabular datasets spanning electronic health records, drug discovery, genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, metabolomics, single-cell omics, and systems biology. TabBioMed unifies these datasets through a standardized, dataset-aware preprocessing and evaluation framework, enabling reproducible comparison across classification, regression, and multi-target tasks. We benchmark 23 models across classical baselines, gradient-boosted trees, neural tabular architectures, and tabular foundation models. Foundation models deliver the strongest family-level predictive performance. However, their gains are regime-dependent: tree-based models and tuned multilayer perceptrons remain highly competitive, often dominating under inference-time or training-cost constraints. Overall, TabBioMed shows that biomedical tabular learning is strongly context-dependent, with optimal model choice depending on data regime, task type, and computational budget. We release TabBioMed with curated datasets and splits, preprocessing code, baselines, and results, providing a reproducible benchmark for practical model selection and future methods development in biomedical machine learning, available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Normal-Only Updates Are Not Enough: Test-Time Adaptation in Reconstruction-Based Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23761)**  `arXiv:2611.23761`  
  _Jianhuan Mao, Mengxiao Zhu, Yue Pei, Lei Li, Haogang Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Normal operating conditions can evolve after deployment, motivating test-time adaptation (TTA) of time-series anomaly detectors using incoming data. Yet the same stream may contain anomalies, so TTA with online updates must decide which test samples should be used for adaptation. This leaves a more basic question: even if updates are restricted to samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, does TTA reliably improve anomaly detection? We study this question with a controlled intervention that replaces the practical update selector with a rule that uses only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, while keeping the rest of the online adaptation procedure unchanged. Across nine benchmark families, four optimizer configurations, and five random seeds, we obtain three main findings: (1) using only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark does not reliably improve detection; (2) practical TTA failures exhibit two distinct responses to this intervention, with 63 of 180 settings recovering a clear detection gain while 90 still show no clear detection gain; and (3) the updates reduce their immediate reconstruction objective across the evaluated settings, yet these reductions do not consistently lead to better anomaly ranking. Both failure patterns persist across alternative practical adaptation variants and modest perturbations of the benchmark labels. Together, these findings show that improving sample selection alone is not a complete solution to reconstruction-based TSAD TTA.
  </details>

- **[Same Rank, Different Needs: Rethinking LoRA Redistribution in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23742)**  `arXiv:2611.23742`  
  _Jinqian Chen, Jihua Zhu, Chang Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated low-rank adaptation (LoRA) enables clients with different resource budgets to collaboratively fine-tune foundation models. To distribute an aggregated update within each client's rank budget, existing methods commonly retain its leading singular directions. However, directions that best reconstruct the update matrix need not best preserve its responses on a particular client's inputs: a lower-energy direction may matter more to that recipient. We formalize this mismatch and show that matrix-optimal truncation can incur arbitrarily larger response error than another readout of the same rank. Motivated by this result, we propose Receiver-Aware Subspace Adaptation (RASA), a plug-in framework for receiver-aware knowledge transfer. RASA uses compact diagonal input second moments to perform uncentered PCA of receiver-weighted update responses. The resulting readout is globally optimal for the rank-constrained receiver objective and preserves at least as much response energy as spectral truncation under the diagonal metric. RASA also introduces spectral-reference aggregation, which removes the personalized initialization offset while retaining the locally learned increment. Experiments on DomainNet-100, NICO++, GLUE, and SuperNI demonstrate RASA's effectiveness across methods under resource, task, and data heterogeneity. In particular, RASA improves DomainNet-100 accuracy by 3.23 percentage points with FlexLoRA and achieves gains of up to 8.33 ROUGE-L points on seen SuperNI tasks and 4.37 on unseen tasks with modest overhead.
  </details>

- **[Toward Out-of-Distribution Generalization in Neural AC Power Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23729)**  `arXiv:2611.23729`  
  _Ojas Sanghi, Anvita Bhagavathula, Alvaro Carbonero, Priya L. Donti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Growing uncertainty in power system operations from renewables integration, extreme weather, and data center loads challenges the reliability of traditional power flow solvers at scale. Machine learning-based proxies are a potentially promising alternative, but the prohibitive cost of generating training data for large grids makes out-of-distribution generalization a key capability for deployment; currently, models trained on small grids lack that capability. In this work, we systematically evaluate how physics-informed losses, multi-grid training, and perturbation-based data augmentation improve the zero-shot transfer of three graph neural network-based power flow models from smaller systems to a larger, unseen 500-bus system. We find that (1) physics-informed losses improve transfer relative to purely supervised objectives; (2) multi-grid training on smaller systems matches the best single grid but does not close the gap to target-grid training; (3) perturbation-based augmentation on edge attributes improves transfer by up to 54.6%; and (4) random line/generator dropout does not consistently improve performance.
  </details>

- **[HypoLoop: Revising Weight-Editing Plans from Scientific Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23718)**  `arXiv:2611.23718`  
  _Mengran Li, Xichen Yan, Bo Li, Chengyang Zhang, Jinfeng Xu, Zheng Lin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific experiments continually reveal prediction errors. Effective updates depend on which parameters change and which errors drive the correction. We introduce HypoLoop, a family of native weight-editing methods that connects these choices to experimental feedback. Fixed, numerically adaptive, and LLM-guided policies produce editing plans; numerical solvers compute weight patches, and internal validation selects the deployed predictor. Successive observations and validation records support revisions to editable locations and fitting rules. Experiments cover 22 scientific tasks and both DeepSeek and Claude. On Burgers, reallocating a matched parameter budget reduces error by 58.80% and 47.72% relative to fixed locations. On DIODE, positive-residual weighting reduces absolute relative error (AbsRel) by 13.35% in internal cross-validation, with the same 33 editable coordinates. Revising the complete plan across rounds lowers final AbsRel by 10.59% relative to keeping the initial plan fixed. SpaceNet7 and Make3D connect accepted edits to improved spatial predictions. These results show how experimental evidence can shape the update problem itself. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Simpler Tiny Recursive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23677)**  `arXiv:2611.23677`  
  _Torben Glass, Zhengxi Zhang, Paul Swoboda_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small models that execute deep looped computational graphs have shown great experimental results on puzzle-like problems like Sudoku, finding shortest paths through mazes, etc. The most well-known one is TRM (tiny recursive model), a simplification of HRM (hierarchical reasoning model). However, the underlying recursion is complicated and involves a high-level and a low-level state, a nested loop recursion, and the network calls with different input and output signatures depending on where we are in the loops. These mechanisms are needed to avoid memorization and overfitting. We propose STRM, a simplified design with a single state and a uniform update. STRM does not sacrifice generalization, improves training speed and final performance. Our key architectural choices that allow our simpler recursion to work are exponential moving updates on the states with varying rates that are jittered during training and readout of the solution on slow-updating channels only. We test STRM on Sudoku-Extreme, PPBench and Maze-Hard with the same data, parameters and compute as TRM: we improve over TRM by $3.8$ points on Sudoku-Extreme and $8.4$ points on PPBench and match it on Maze-Hard. On ARC-AGI-1 and ARC-AGI-2, STRM improves over the published TRM results by $5.4$ and $0.5$ points. We will release our code upon acceptance of the paper.
  </details>

- **[Toaster: Nonlinear Memory from Cascaded Linear Memories and Target Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23648)**  `arXiv:2611.23648`  
  _Zeman Li, Ali Behrouz, Yuan Deng, Praneeth Kacham, Rami Al-Rfou, Meisam Razaviyayn, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent memories let language models process long sequences with a fixed-size state. Linear memories such as DeltaNet and KDA store one matrix and take one delta-rule step per token. The product of a chunk of such steps has an exact WY form, so they train fast. Deep memories such as TTT-MLP and Titans store a small MLP, which can hold maps that no matrix can, but their update has no WY form. Toaster bridges the two by building on a known fact: a gradient step on a linear layer is a delta-rule write. It builds a two-layer nonlinear memory from two linear memories with an activation between them. We use target propagation to give the hidden memory its values by projecting the output error back through the second memory. Given a chunk's cross-layer signals, approximated once per chunk, each layer can then run on an unmodified delta-rule kernel. Our default runs this kernel on the output layer and updates the hidden layer once per chunk. Running it on both layers gives a better model in our ablation. At matched backbones and training-token budgets, Toaster has lower loss than KDA at all five tested sizes (0.2B-1.6B non-embedding parameters) on both C4 and PG19. Its C4 perplexity also matches or slightly improves on the attention baseline at every size. At 1.2B it is also below Titans. In a 3:1 hybrid with global attention, the Toaster hybrid has the lowest perplexity of the four hybrids tested, and it is 0.18 (C4) and 0.44 (PG19) below the attention baseline. At the largest size, Toaster's mean three-shot accuracy on 13 tasks is 51.84%, compared with 50.52% for KDA and 50.04% for Titans. In length extrapolation, Toaster's loss stays below KDA's up to 20K tokens (5x the training length). On the 145M backbone, at a hidden width matched to Titans' memory, Toaster takes at most 1.33x KDA's training-step time and is 2.8-5.5x faster than the tested Titans configurations at 32K context.
  </details>

- **[FairPGT: Partitioned Graph Transformers for Dual-Level Fair Graph Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23631)**  `arXiv:2611.23631`  
  _Siamak Ghodsi, Amjad Seyedi, Sahar Tahmasebi, Eirini Ntoutsi, Sören Auer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clustering on attributed graphs can produce demographically biased clusters, particularly when communities align with protected groups. Graph transformers can amplify this bias through same-group attention, while global attention limits scalability. We propose FairPGT, a partitioned graph transformer for fair graph clustering. FairPGT restricts attention to METIS partitions and connects them through routing tokens, which divides the quadratic cost of attention by the number of partitions. A graph-aware self-training target guides clustering, with a provably bounded deviation from the standard DEC target. Fairness is imposed at two levels: a mutual-information term promotes demographic parity in cluster assignments, while a normalized cross-group attention ratio (nCGAR) guides attention toward group neutrality through self-distillation. We prove that the nCGAR correction moves attention toward neutrality and establish a condition preventing overshooting. Across seven benchmarks, FairPGT achieves the best clustering performance and statistical parity on the three largest graphs (Credit, Pokec-n, Pokec-z), with a statistical-parity gap approximately ten times lower than DMoN, and is Pareto-dominated on only one dataset. On controlled stochastic block models, FairPGT attains 97% of the derived upper bound on NMI for exactly fair clustering when communities and protected groups coincide. These graphs further show that, under strong alignment, assignment-level fairness alone makes the attention group-heterophilous, whereas attention-level fairness alone improves neutrality without ensuring fair clusters. Code for reproducibility is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Interpolators Store and What They Express: An Information-Theoretic Framework for Benign Overfitting and Label-Free Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23630)**  `arXiv:2611.23630`  
  _Nikolaos Nikolaou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Overparameterized learners can fit noisy training labels perfectly and still predict well, which neither classical complexity measures nor information-theoretic generalization bounds explain. We develop an information-theoretic framework that separates two things an interpolating learner does with its sample: storage, the information the trained model retains about its training labels, and expression, the information about the sample that reaches its predictions at fresh inputs. We first characterize the ideal model: for a loss l, the least complex l-sufficient representation is the Bayes act, whose entropy is the minimal sufficient complexity; this generalizes, and corrects, an earlier "lossless maximal compression" view of boosting. We then show that every interpolator stores exactly nH(Y|X) bits of label noise, so storage, and bounds built on it (including CMI bounds), cannot tell interpolators apart. Expression can: excess risk is bounded above and below by the expressed information V, the entropy over training samples of the prediction at an input, plus a modal-bias term, exactly so for binary labels. Benign overfitting is therefore vanishing expression, a picture that covers nearest-neighbour rules, averaging, minimum-norm least squares and double descent, and that links V to classical bias-variance decompositions and to model disagreement. Finally, V gives a model-selection criterion that needs no held-out labels. Among overparameterized learners on 15 noisy datasets, V estimated from two disjoint halves has 60% less regret than holdout validation at the same cost and matches 5-fold cross-validation at a quarter of its cost; at matched cost it also beats selection by conditional mutual information. We characterize when it fails and give remedies. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Agent Verifiers in Counterfactual Analysis: A Pilot Study Benchmarking Agentic Heterogeneous Treatment Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23621)**  `arXiv:2611.23621`  
  _Satiyabooshan Murugaboopathy, Entejar Alam, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents can expand the covariate space of randomized experiments by retrieving fast-moving local context missed by slow-changing survey baseline covariates, helping characterize who benefits most from an intervention. Verifying such covariates is difficult: individual treatment effects are unobserved, retrieval leakage can pull post-treatment information into nominally pretreatment summaries, and encoder leakage can inject post-treatment pretraining knowledge into representations even after temporal filtering of inputs. Guided by a two-channel information bound separating summary provenance from encoder weights, we propose a three-layer benchmark protocol: (i) retrieval admissibility via pre-treatment date filtering; (ii) heterogeneity validation scored rank-weighted average treatment effects (RATEs); and (iii) encoder admissibility checked with chronologically vintaged encoders, future-fact probes, and weight perturbations. Across 11 datasets anchored by Uganda's Youth Opportunities Program, we compare session-isolated retrieval (sandboxed, bounded-budget agent runs per study) against a persistent workspace teammate (xAI's GrokBot) that builds one date-verified corpus per study while blinded to outcomes. The workspace teammate yields higher mean RATE ratios in 6/11 datasets for text alone and 7 for combined features, though paired tests show neither workflow universally dominates. Even on fixed retrieved text, encoder choice significantly shifts heterogeneity signal. Chronologically vintaged probes show no clear evidence of weight leakage, but single-record adapter perturbation analyses show that memorized future facts, once present, persist until injected noise degrades model output. Temporal filtering of retrieval is necessary but insufficient: verifying agent-generated covariates requires jointly examining source provenance, out-of-sample treatment-effect signal, and encoder weights.
  </details>

- **[JEPA as a Transferable Optimizer: Learning and Understanding Black-Box Optimization from Reward-Agnostic Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23608)**  `arXiv:2611.23608`  
  _Mayukh Debnath, Manan Roy Choudhury_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Black-box optimization evaluates candidates through simulations, rollouts, or experiments, often without gradients. Learned optimizers are usually judged by whether they outperform existing methods. We ask a complementary question: what information does a learned optimizer use from its search history? We introduce JEPA-PS (JEPA Policy Search), based on a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture. It is pretrained on random rollouts using parameter vectors and scalar returns. Its latent-prediction objective predicts the next point in an optimization trajectory rather than its reward or gradient, allowing us to examine what was tried and how well it performed. At test time, JEPA-PS reads a short evaluation history and proposes a candidate in one forward pass, without task descriptions, reward shaping, or test-time gradients. We evaluate it on 15 control environments against 12 optimizers, including evolutionary methods, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and an LLM optimizer. JEPA-PS is competitive with tuned classical baselines and outperforms the LLM optimizer by more than 2x on Swimmer at 71x lower per-step cost. It also transfers zero-shot to environments excluded from pretraining. Null-model comparisons and decoder-training ablations indicate that its latent representation carries information beyond simple history statistics; every trained decoder variant performs worse than the default frozen representation. Our main finding is that the history JEPA-PS uses depends on the task. On smooth landscapes it uses the full trajectory, whereas on rugged landscapes the best-seen point carries nearly all the signal. This memory requirement has a weak positive correlation with fitness-distance correlation (FDC; r=0.112, n=7), while an independent reward-ordering sensitivity probe does not corroborate the relationship (r=-0.024, n=7). Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients for Stable Feature Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23600)**  `arXiv:2611.23600`  
  _Rukmangadh Sai Myana, Sumit Kumar Jha, Yanzhao Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients (IG) are widely used to interpret neural networks, yet imperceptible input perturbations can drastically alter their attribution maps while leaving model predictions unchanged. We introduce Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients (ERIG), a path-based attribution method that intrinsically attenuates gradient irregularities along the integration path using maximum-entropy softmax weighting with temperature parameter $\tau$. We quantify the effect of the entropy-regularized weighting on attribution stability by using the Lipschitz properties of the model and its gradients. We decompose the attribution change under an input perturbation into a model-response term, whose bound is $\tau$-independent, and a weight-variation term, whose bound decreases at rate $\mathcal{O}(1/(2\tau))$. Their ratio $R(\tau)$ characterizes the trade-off between gradient information and attribution stability. We further add a new dimension to attribution evaluation by adapting classical noise estimators into two metrics, LINV-C and LINV-P, that quantify attribution noise. Across eleven CNN and Transformer backbones on ImageNet and Oxford-IIIT Pet datasets, our experiments show that, relative to the next-best method, ERIG remains comparable on ranking-based fidelity metrics while improving localization by approximately 24% and attribution stability by approximately 47-fold on average, reaching up to four orders of magnitude improvement over IG, all at negligible additional computational cost.
  </details>

- **[DuoCL: Dual-Path Online Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23598)**  `arXiv:2611.23598`  
  _Jebacyril Arockiaraj, Dhruv Parikh, Rajgopal Kannan, Viktor Prasanna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online continual learning (OCL) requires learning from a data stream whose arrival rate is independent of model training speed. When training cannot keep pace, computationally expensive gradient-based methods update less frequently, limiting adaptation. Analytic methods reduce this overhead, but their frozen backbones cannot adapt representations to changing distributions. We propose DuoCL, a dual-path OCL framework whose fast analytic path accumulates feature statistics from every incoming minibatch while its slow path periodically adapts the backbone. The slow path adjusts its sample count to the available compute budget without interrupting fast-path learning. Feature alignment compensates for drift in the accumulated classifier statistics. Across four datasets and three computational budgets, DuoCL improves online accuracy by an average of 9.67 percentage points over the strongest gradient-based baseline in each setting. DuoCL consistently outperforms analytic learning under distribution shifts, with gains of up to 4.00 percentage points on CIFAR-C, and achieves higher accuracy than prior methods across disjoint class-incremental, domain-incremental, and Si-Blurry settings.
  </details>

- **[FACT: Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23463)**  `arXiv:2611.23463`  
  _Hadi Fares, Mayssa Hajj Hassan, Ali Salman, Ammar Mohanna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates language-model generation by verifying multiple draft tokens in a single target-model pass. However, better draft predictions and longer accepted continuations do not necessarily produce faster generation. To examine this gap, we present FACT (Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training), an experimental framework for studying how drafter training, candidate allocation, and execution conditions jointly affect acceptance and latency. Using a lightweight feature-prediction drafter with a frozen Llama-3-8B-Instruct target under greedy decoding, we first examine how training translates into accepted tokens. Backpropagating through predicted-feature rollouts with greater weight on early steps improves acceptance by 15-24% over uniformly weighted detached training, yet average rollout proxy cross-entropy does not reliably rank drafters by acceptance. Beyond prediction quality, the placement of draft candidates also matters: with a fixed 256-node budget and matched per-depth widths, replacing static allocation with confidence-guided allocation increases speedup over autoregressive decoding from $1.46\times$ to $2.02\times$ on HumanEval and from $1.31\times$ to $1.75\times$ on MT-Bench. This allocation benefit extends to an independently trained drafter, but stronger training does not consistently amplify it, showing that the two improvements do not necessarily reinforce each other. Even these speed gains depend on execution conditions: changing only host-thread settings can nearly eliminate speedup without changing acceptance. Together, these findings show why training and allocation must be evaluated jointly using both acceptance and end-to-end latency under controlled execution conditions.
  </details>

- **[Conformally Calibrated Adaptive Computation in Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23440)**  `arXiv:2611.23440`  
  _Abdul Qadir Ibrahim, Martin Burger, Abel Maviano Jonathan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting less computation in a neural operator need not reduce inference time. We study this gap using conformal calibration to choose Fourier modes or channel width per input subject to a fixed error budget. A triangle-inequality envelope bounds all candidates simultaneously; a direct-score rule handles non-monotone candidate errors and, with an input-independent score, calibrates a static baseline under the same marginal guarantee. Under frozen-model and exchangeability assumptions, both rules bound the probability of issuing a prediction outside the budget and abstain when calibration is infeasible. On Burgers and Darcy flow across three training seeds, mode selection reduces active spectral support without a measurable latency reduction, because full-grid transforms and channel mixing dominate the cost. Narrowing channels does reduce Darcy forward cost, but reference-based selection must first compute the dense prediction. An input-only binary width selector avoids that pass and runs at 0.52-0.95 times dense latency; yet static widths calibrated under the same rule are often as fast or faster, and on a separately generated 1,000-example test set they generally achieve higher coverage, while the selector falls below its 90% target in more than half of the settings. Adaptive computation in neural operators should therefore be judged by achieved coverage and complete runtime against a matched static comparator.
  </details>

- **[Stability Does Not Mirror Plasticity: A Module-Level View of Declarative and Procedural Learning in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23427)**  `arXiv:2611.23427`  
  _Jinjun Peng, Wailiang Zhao, Baishakhi Ray, Zhou Yu, Jufeng Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plasticity and stability in continual learning are typically studied at the entire-model level and in a task-agnostic manner, yet prior work has reported divergent conclusions across settings. Inspired by cognitive distinctions between declarative and procedural memory, we instead study how plasticity and stability vary across transformer modules and task types. In a controlled synthetic environment with clean measurement, we show a double dissociation: query-key (QK) and value-output (VO) projections are the most plastic on procedural tasks, whereas MLPs are the most plastic on declarative memorization. Stability, however, does not simply mirror plasticity: when learning a new procedure, updating QK retains over half of the stored declarative memory, while updating MLPs retains far less and updating VO loses roughly four-fifths. Targeted module reset-and-recovery experiments explain this asymmetry: plasticity tracks which module is best suited to a task's primary computation or storage, whereas stability depends on all ways the existing capability relies on that module, including access to information stored in other modules. Experiments on a realistic model and tasks broadly echo these asymmetric dynamics. Overall, our work shows that plasticity and stability depend on different aspects of a module's functional role, offering a task-dependent, module-level perspective on continual learning.
  </details>

- **[Implementing Binary Transformers with Approximate Memories with Permutation Invariance Enabled Selective (PIES) Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23358)**  `arXiv:2611.23358`  
  _Zhen Gao, Xiao Yin, Kexin Liang, Pedro Reviriego, Shanshan Liu, Fabrizio Lombardi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization is an effective technique to reduce memory storage, computation complexity and power for Transformers, and the binarization of the weights is important for meeting these requirements. Approximate memory can further reduce the power for memory, but it also introduces errors. Current research for binary Transformers focuses on task performance, but the model dependable operation against errors on the memory has not been well studied. In this paper, we first evaluate the dependability of binary Transformers with the Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT) as a case study. A large accuracy loss occurs only at high Bit Error Rates (e.g. BER = 5% or 10%). Then we propose a novel Permutation Invariance Enabled Selective (PIES) scheme to protect the binary weights at very low overhead. This scheme exploits the invariance of Transformers to permutations in the rows and columns to group the critical weights and perform selective protection on those weights. Simulation results show that PIES can recover most of the performance loss caused by bit errors at BERs as high as 10%, at an overhead significantly lower than traditional error correction schemes. These results confirm the feasibility of running binary Transformers using approximate memory with aggressive voltage scaling on resource-limited low power edge devices. The code for PIES is released on github (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[BiMa-ECGSR: reconstructing the 12-lead ECG from reduced lead sets with bidirectional state-space models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23345)**  `arXiv:2611.23345`  
  _Ugo Lomoio, Pierangelo Veltri, Pietro Hiram Guzzi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wearables and monitors record few electrocardiogram (ECG) leads; most diagnostic tools assume 12 leads. BiMa-ECGSR reconstructs the 12-lead ECG from reduced lead sets with a bidirectional Mamba state-space encoder--decoder, as regression (BiMa-SR) or diffusion (DiBiMa-SR). Models were trained on PTB-XL for six input sets (I; II; I+II; I+II+V1; I+II+V2; I+II+V1+V4), see only the measured leads at inference, and were tested on the PTB-XL test fold (2,198 records) and two external cohorts (Georgia, 10,275 records; Chapman-Shaoxing/Ningbo, 43,559). From lead II alone, the pooled median Pearson correlation (PCC) over the 11 predicted leads was 0.914 on PTB-XL, against 0.784 for a least-squares linear filter (lead I: 0.906 against 0.789). As limb leads are exactly derivable from I and II, multi-lead inputs were compared on predicted chest leads: 0.939 against 0.849 for I+II and 0.979 against 0.962 for I+II+V1+V4. BiMa-SR exceeded the linear comparator in all 18 configuration--cohort pairs (by 0.012--0.136), a margin that shrank as chest leads were added. From lead II, regression was more accurate than diffusion (DiBiMa-SR: 0.903 one-step, 0.862 with six DDIM steps). A 12-lead classifier trained only on real ECGs reached a superclass macro AUROC of 0.912 on real PTB-XL ECGs, 0.843 on reconstructions from lead II and 0.908 from I+II+V1+V4; externally, reconstructions from three or four leads scored within 0.004 of real ECGs. BiMa-SR (2.68\,M parameters) takes 45.8 ms per 10-s record on a GPU. Reconstruction makes reduced-lead recordings usable by 12-lead tools; it cannot add information absent from the measured leads.
  </details>

- **[Optimize the Cob, Then the Kernel: Optimizing Beam Search for Generative Recommendation Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23339)**  `arXiv:2611.23339`  
  _Shing Wai Pun, Kai Wu, Pengfei Xia, Hui Zang, Wei Guo, Yong Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative recommendation (GR) inference differs substantially from conventional LLM serving, featuring long input sequences, wide beam search, and only a few fixed output tokens. Existing LLM serving engines such as vLLM process beams as batched independent sequences, causing redundant KV cache accesses over the shared prompt prefix. Furthermore, vLLM executes each beam search generation step as a separate engine invocation, incurring host-side beam processing overhead between decoding steps and substantial runtime KV block management in PagedAttention. Using Tree Attention, we concatenate beams along the sequence dimension to eliminate redundant KV accesses, and further optimize execution through pre-generated partial-attention masks, static KV cache allocation, loop-unrolled generation dispatch, TorchAir graph compilation, automatic operator fusion, and vocabulary-constrained decoding. Without the use of custom kernels, our approach reduces end-to-end latency by up to 25x over vLLM on Ascend NPUs, and achieves generation latency comparable to a SOTA solution using custom kernels, showing that the generation pipeline should be optimized holistically before resorting to custom kernel development.
  </details>

- **[Exploring Parkinson's Diagnosis Using Machine Learning Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23248)**  `arXiv:2611.23248`  
  _Farhaan Qazi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, machine learning (ML) techniques have emerged as promising tools for accurate and timely diagnosis of Parkinson's disease (PD) which is an incurable neurodegenerative disorder. This study explores the integration of ML algorithms and PD diagnosis, highlighting their potential applications in various aspects of the disease. ML methods such as support vector machines, decision trees, multilayer perceptron, k-nearest neighbours, logistic regression and random forest (with and without particle swarm optimisation) were applied on a PD dataset obtained from Leeds Teaching Hospitals NHS Trust containing data from 58 people. In terms of accuracy, the Decision Tree and Logistic Regression performed the best with scores of 72-76% while multilayer perceptron was least effective. All the models were remarkable in classifying positive PD cases with sensitivity scores going up to 97.56%, with SVM classifier at the pinnacle. However, ability to classify healthy controls from PD cases was insignificant throughout the board. Although, all the employed models delivered reasonable results in categorising PD patients in the presented dataset; access to more data, further study and advancement of these ML approaches could lead to enhanced diagnostic outcomes, supplementing to an improved understanding of Parkinson's disease.
  </details>

- **[Budgeted Proper Loss Design for Uncertain Decision Thresholds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23247)**  `arXiv:2611.23247`  
  _Ankit Sharma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A binary probability estimate may be used by several decision makers with different error costs. Proper scoring rules can emphasize selected probability thresholds, but a single average threshold distribution need not give a good worst-case regret guarantee. We study the choice of a proper loss when the threshold distribution belongs to a finite specified family. Among integrable loss weights with a positive pointwise floor and a fixed total mass, we derive the weight that minimizes the sharp, distribution-free multiplier from surrogate regret to threshold-averaged decision regret. The solution clips a rescaled upper envelope of the threshold densities at the prescribed floor; its normalization is found by a one-dimensional root search. We give proofs of sharpness, optimality, and stability under density misspecification, as well as a limitation for atomic threshold laws. An exact histogram implementation and two synthetic experiments across 20 seeds verify the construction. The optimized multiplier improves over uniform and average-density weights, while fitted-model performance does not improve uniformly. The result is a loss-design guarantee under an explicit budget, rather than a general claim of predictive superiority.
  </details>

- **[Response-Relevant Information Is Not Sufficient for Neural Training-State Equivalence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23245)**  `arXiv:2611.23245`  
  _Truong Xuan Khanh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Response-relevant information is not sufficient evidence that two neural networks are in the same training state. We define training-state equivalence operationally: two checkpoints are equivalent, relative to an intervention family and horizon, if they respond alike when training is perturbed and continues. On fresh runs of a one-layer transformer learning modular addition, response to a weight-decay pulse is highly reproducible relative to between-checkpoint differences (rho_state = 0.934 at 500 steps), establishing the target as measurable. Fifteen descriptors of weight spectra, representation geometry and optimiser geometry carry within-run response-relevant information beyond weight norm and loss on a development dataset, predicting held-out response deviation across seed blocks. But their apparent 16.1% advantage at matching checkpoints by future response does not separate from a conditional placebo preserving each descriptor's fitted dependence on the conventional observables, and a calibrated positive control makes simple insensitivity an unlikely explanation. Information a decoder can extract need not be organised in the pairwise geometry that similarity judgments require.
  </details>

- **[Biologically-Inspired Spike Encoding and Optimization for Spiking Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23242)**  `arXiv:2611.23242`  
  _Ayaan Qayyum, Bruce Yi Bu, Zicong Li, Jingfeng Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) offer theoretical energy-efficiency advantages over conventional deep networks, but realizing these advantages on vision tasks requires sparse spike representations that naive rate-coding methods fail to provide. We combine biologically motivated spike-encoding schemes with two training improvements: Rouser adaptive-threshold surrogate gradients and Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature (K-FAC) second-order optimization. The spike encoding schemes experimented with are rate coding, latency coding, DVS-style delta modulation, and Difference-of-Gaussians (DoG) filtering. Experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST demonstrate that biologically inspired encoders yield the largest accuracy gains in capacity-constrained regimes and serve as an effective parameter-efficiency lever. DoG and delta modulation encoders produce significantly sparser spike trains than rate coding. K-FAC yields modest but consistent accuracy improvements over Adam-trained baselines, and Rouser's learnable per-neuron thresholds improve Fashion-MNIST accuracy from 87.37% to 88.13% by enabling homeostatic firing-rate regulation. Biologically grounded pre-processing methods can help close the accuracy gap between SNNs and non-spiking baselines without increasing model size.
  </details>

- **[GridForge: An Open Physics-Informed Digital Twin and Benchmark Suite for UK 11 kV Underground Distribution Cables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23241)**  `arXiv:2611.23241`  
  _Lilliane Linnet Musoke_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The UK distribution-cable monitoring sector is data-rich but framework-poor: each Distribution Network Operator (DNO) collects megabytes of telemetry per asset per day, but there is no public benchmark dataset, no shared reference model, and no commonly agreed evaluation protocol for benchmarking sectoral, data-intensive models for operational decision support; notably for maintenance planning and prioritisation. We address this gap with GridForge, an open-source physics-informed digital twin and benchmark suite for fault diagnosis and prediction, integrating four building blocks: (i) a verified steady state and transient thermal model based on IEC 60287 and IEC 60853; (ii) a calibrated synthetic dataset of 64 cables (covering 320 cable-years) representing four canonical UK 11 kV thermal archetypes and four condition modes; (iii) a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) surrogate that satisfies the algebraic balance defined by IEC 60287 during training and reaches an RMSE of 0.148 °C on an independent held out set against the reference analytical oracle; and (iv) a five-task benchmark suite that includes three reference baseline models (pure physics, gradient-boosted trees, and the PINN). All numerical parameters are sourced from public standards, the code is licensed under the MIT licence, and the trained PINN converges in only two minutes (as run on the computing platform in Section 10.1), enabling independent verification by DNO engineering and research practitioners. At present only the virtual-sensor task (T4) discriminates between the reference baselines; the other tasks tie or are undefined on this test split, so extending the baselines with task-specific logic is a priority future enhancement. For open validation, the dataset, test sets and models are made available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Pareto-Informed Smart "Predict, then Optimize" for Multi-Objective Combinatorial Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23076)**  `arXiv:2611.23076`  
  _Aabhash Dhakal, Tim Lachner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many real-world planning applications, from logistics, routing to resource allocation, give rise to optimization problems that need to balance multiple, conflicting objectives. While machine learning is often used to predict the unknown parameters or costs of these problems, standard models trained on prediction accuracy can lead to suboptimal plans. Decision-Focused Learning (DFL) addresses this by integrating the downstream optimization task into the training objective. However, existing DFL methods for multi-objective problems rely on simple scalarization, which fails to capture the rich trade-offs of the Pareto Frontier. To address this gap, we propose a Pareto-aware extension to single-objective Smart "Predict, then Optimize" (SPO+) for multi-objective combinatorial optimization problems. Our loss function leverages the true Pareto Frontier, forming a target decision that aligns with a sampled preference vector, and minimizes regret against this target. Experiments on multi-objective shortest path and multi-objective knapsack problems show that our approach outperforms traditional two-stage Mean Squared Error (MSE) baselines. It also surpasses scalarization-based SPO+ approaches.
  </details>

- **[Auditing World-Model Explanations in Retail Forecasting: Faithfulness, Accuracy and Price Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20811)**  `arXiv:2611.20811`  
  _Karthik Iyer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting models that read the price and promotion plan explain each forecast as a baseline, what would sell with nothing planned, plus the lift the plan adds, and merchants order stock and plan promotions on that lift. We show that an explanation can pass the checks used to trust it and still be wrong enough to cost money. We define eight checks for any forecaster that states a plan lift, six with a pass rule fixed before any result, and run them on a world model and on LightGBM. In generated store data with known effects, the world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet when merchants plan into high-demand weeks it states 1.33 to 1.40 times the true lift, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world; LightGBM overstates it too (1.26, and 1.16 to 1.35). Both still pick the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to the world model's lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. The world model's stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, an error in kind that only a measured cause finds. Sanity checks for explanations test whether an explanation depends on the model; a decision needs to know whether it is right about the world, and these results show the first does not give the second. For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan on every window, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05). A noisy benchmark of price episodes in Dominick's data is reported as a check of direction only.
  </details>

- **[ReCALM: A Residual-enhanced Crowdsourced Analytic Learning Mechanism for Distributed Continual Service Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19276)**  `arXiv:2611.19276`  
  _Kejia Fan, Yajiang Huang, Jianheng Tang, Jinfeng Xu, Huiping Zhuang, Anfeng Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crowdsourcing has emerged as a promising paradigm for constructing AI services by harnessing the collective capabilities of distributed workers. As consumers' demands continually evolve, service models must acquire new knowledge while retaining previously learned information. However, due to the inherited reliance on gradients for backpropagation, existing approaches typically suffer from catastrophic forgetting during continual model updates on heterogeneous data. Recent studies have introduced analytic learning, a gradient-free paradigm that achieves invariance through recursive closed-form solutions. Nevertheless, existing analytic methods are typically constrained by single-layer linear networks, which tend to suffer from underfitting due to their inability to perform deep representation learning. To address this issue, we propose ReCALM, a Residual-enhanced Crowdsourced Analytic Learning Mechanism that achieves deep nonlinear invariance for distributed continual service construction. Inspired by the success of ResNet, we develop gradient-free residual blocks for ReCALM with closed-form solutions. To implement distributed continual training for ReCALM, we further devise a cross-recursive strategy via least squares. Both theoretical and experimental analyses verify ReCALM's dual superiority in heterogeneity invariance and deep representation. With 20 residual layers, ReCALM improves accuracy by 8.85%-9.83% over the single-layer analytic baseline, while reducing computational costs by over 94% compared with gradient-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[Probabilistic State Space Enumeration in Neural Network Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15062)**  `arXiv:2611.15062`  
  _Victor O. K. Li, Yang Han, Jacqueline C. K. Lam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many methods for pruning neural networks rank weights or groups of weights by importance and retain a single subset. We study how channel groups contribute jointly to the output of multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) in Transformer language models. Inspired by network reliability analysis, probabilistic state space enumeration (PSSE) represents the dense MLP output as the expected importance-corrected masked output over $2^G$ joint states of $G$ channel groups. Summing the most probable states' probability-weighted outputs gives an approximation whose absolute error is bounded by the omitted probability mass times the maximum masked-output norm. NORDER-II adapts ORDER-II to enumerate states in decreasing probability without sorting the full space. Its first $B$ states maximize the probability mass captured at that budget. A weight-entropy scorer fixes each layer's ordering from pretrained weights. We evaluate all 168 MLP layers across six models from the Llama, Gemma, and Qwen families on two datasets with 8, 16, and 32 channel groups. In all 1,008 comparisons, the 95%-mass prefix yields lower mean relative error than uniform sampling at the same state count, with or without bias correction. At all three group counts, it also yields lower median and 99th-percentile relative error than a single top-$k$ mask. For downstream inference, we use the number of distinct channels in each prefix to set pruned MLP widths. With one validation-selected group count per model, PSSE achieves lower perplexity than both top-$k$ pruning baselines in all 36 matched-FLOP settings. Our pruned decoder MLPs achieve mean speedups of $1.11\times$, $1.23\times$, and $1.36\times$ over dense MLPs at 10%, 20%, and 30% target MLP projection FLOP savings, respectively. PSSE offers a principled path toward both probability-mass-controlled approximation and more efficient inference in pretrained language models.
  </details>

- **[MAGE: Towards Generalizable Multi-timescale EEG Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09928)**  `arXiv:2611.09928`  
  _Tuan-Kiet Doan, Van-Chien Nguyen, Trung-Hieu Tran, Quang-Hung Pham, Ngoc-Son Vu, Huy Phan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) data plays an important role in understanding human brain activity, with various major applications. While current approaches, which largely build on EEG foundation models, have demonstrated strong performance across datasets and tasks, they typically rely on a fixed temporal scale. This design choice limits their ability to capture characteristics of neural dynamic behavior, often spanning across multiple timescales, which are naturally captured in EEG recordings. To address this limitation, we propose MAGE (\textbf{Multi-timescale Adaptive Generalization for EEG}), a foundation model that dynamically adapts to capture and integrate information across different timescales. Specifically, MAGE combines two key mechanisms: (i) a novel multi-timescale attention mechanism, capable of learning representations across multiple temporal scales and (ii) a trainable aggregation module, that dynamically identifies and adapts to the most informative timescale of the input signal. Experimental results across seven datasets demonstrate that MAGE consistently outperforms existing EEG foundation models while using substantially fewer parameters. Moreover, our results support the hypothesis that different downstream tasks benefit from distinct temporal scales.
  </details>

- **[Calibrated Uncertainty Quantification on TDC ADMET: What Can Be Resolved Across 22 Datasets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09373)**  `arXiv:2611.09373`  
  _Laurits Fredsgaard, Michael Riis Andersen, Mikkel N. Schmidt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) models often rely on small, heterogeneous datasets, yet their predictions may determine which compounds proceed to experimental testing. Useful evaluations must therefore assess both predictive performance and the quality of reported probabilities or intervals. We ask which common uncertainty-quantification methods perform best on the 22 Therapeutics Data Commons (TDC) ADMET datasets and what this benchmark can resolve. We compare eleven calibrated pipelines using nested five-fold cross-validation across ten chemical partitions and five model seeds. Ensemble Monte Carlo (MC) dropout has the best average rank for TDC performance, Brier score, and 90% interval score. For TDC performance, however, it is not separated from the other ensemble pipelines; for Brier and interval score, LightGBM is also not ruled out as best. LightGBM wins more individual datasets than any other method for TDC performance and Brier score, but performs poorly on several endpoints. Smaller evaluations can identify a different leader and often fail to recover the broader comparison. These results make LightGBM and an ensemble neural model complementary baselines. Comparative claims should be checked across multiple chemical partitions using statistical analyses matched to the question being asked.
  </details>

- **[Systolic Linear Attention: Co-Designing Tile-Grouped Gated Delta Attention for TPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06828)**  `arXiv:2611.06828`  
  _Gagik Amirkhanyan, Nuojin Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gated-delta linear attention layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), Kimi Delta Attention (KDA), and GDN-2 are tuned for GPUs, where small tensor-core tiles and fast vector registers make unpacked heads, per-channel decay rescaling, and per-value-head chunk solves cheap. On Google TPUs, which concentrate compute in wide $256 \times 256$ systolic matrix units backed by a slower vector unit, direct ports leave half of each matrix tile padded (for $d_k = 128$ heads) and repeat decay rescaling and chunk solves across every value head. We present \textbf{Systolic Linear Attention (SLA)}, which reshapes gated delta attention around the matrix unit by widening keys to $256$ and sharing both keys and scalar write gates $\beta_t$ across grouped value heads ($R\times$ fewer chunk solves), grouping decay gates into four $64$-channel tiles, and fusing exact FP32 block-doubling chunk inversion and its backward reuse inside one on-chip kernel. With exact chunk inversion on TPU v7x, $2$-stage \texttt{SLA-R4} (four value heads per key head) runs $2.1\times$--$2.6\times$ faster than open-source MaxText KDA and $1.4\times$--$2.4\times$ faster than open-source Tokamax KDA, while using $49\%$--$61\%$ less temporary high-bandwidth memory (HBM). Across a $2{,}048$-chip TPU v7x pre-training study of sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models from $6.5\text{B}$ to $63.1\text{B}$ nominal parameters, SLA trains $11\%$--$24\%$ faster than MaxText KDA ($4\%$--$21\%$ faster than Tokamax KDA), matches KDA perplexity on three paired seeds at $12.5\text{B}$, and stays within $3\%$ at other scales.
  </details>

- **[Training Autoregressive Language Models on Rounded, Bounded, and Censored Numerical Labels via Exact Likelihood](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02461)**  `arXiv:2611.02461`  
  _Jingquan Yan, Yuwei Miao, Yang Xie, Junzhou Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Numerical labels often specify a set of possible values: a measurement is rounded, a laboratory test reports only a bound, or a program is stopped at a timeout. We introduce boundary marginalization, the first method that trains an autoregressive language model on the exact likelihood of such observations through its existing digit tokens. A fixed-width, order-preserving numeral representation groups consecutive grid values under shared digit prefixes. An interval is a union of such groups, so its exact probability and gradient follow from at most two boundary paths, the successive digit prefixes of its endpoints, each of length logarithmic in the number of grid values. The resulting objective unifies precise, rounded, bounded, and censored supervision and trains digit decisions at the resolution supplied by each observation. The same computation supports exact distributional queries and unbiased gradients for the continuous ranked probability score on exact labels. Experiments show recovery of a conditional distribution from rounded and censored reports alone, improved likelihood on naturally censored molecular measurements, and more accurate code latency distributions on two backbones. Exact interval updates remain tractable as the numerical support grows to one million values.
  </details>

- **[Profile-aware assessment of block-diagonal Hessian approximations in neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01335)**  `arXiv:2611.01335`  
  _Maxim Bolshim, Alexander Kugaevskikh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating block-diagonal curvature models in neural networks involves two questions: how much curvature they omit, and what a specific correction changes at a finite step. We measure the squared off-block fraction of the parameter Hessian against references for group sizes and the observed within-group curvature profile. In CIFAR-100 ResNet-18 and a skip-free counterpart, similar initial discarded fractions accompany different profiles. At saved trained ResNet-18 weights, changing from stage to layer groups moves estimated off-block mass from below to above the profile reference. An approximate assessment of probe uncertainty conditional on fixed weights and inputs resolves this crossing; some skip-free comparisons remain unresolved. Separately, in nine saved ResNet-50/Stanford Cars states from three training seeds, we compare candidates formed from off-group Hessian action, the remaining model residual and their combination. For each state, we construct two full-Hessian-informed maps on separate geometry draws. Within each draw, all three inputs share one map. At unit scale off-group input alone raises evaluated minibatch loss relative to the base preconditioner candidate in 17 of 18 state/draw comparisons, yet the combined candidate has lower loss than the remainder-only candidate in nine of those cases; preferences vary with scale and correction-norm matching. The profiles help assess block models and alternative groupings, while same-state candidate comparisons test a particular correction without equating structural mass with training gain.
  </details>

- **[Your LR Schedule May Not Be Principled: Effective Learning Rate Scheduling in Weight-Normalized Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40362)**  `arXiv:2610.40362`  
  _Qifan Yu, Xinyu Ma, Xingyan Bin, Di He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-normalized (WN) training is emerging as a compelling alternative to conventional weight decay (WD), with recent studies reporting substantial pretraining loss gains. We find that the effective learning rate (ELR) trajectory accounts for most of the loss dynamics difference between WN and WD. Matching blockwise ELR trajectories step by step closely aligns their loss curves. Schedule design should therefore target ELR rather than the nominal learning rate (LR). Building on this principle, we model directional-alignment dynamics under WN and derive $1/\sqrt{t}$ decay as the optimal schedule. For finite-horizon pretraining, we propose AIR (Affine Inverse-square-Root decay) to match prescribed peak and terminal LRs. Experiments on 128B- and 500B-token Mixture-of-Experts pretraining under WN show that AIR outperforms cosine and alternative schedules in loss, downstream performance, and hyperparameter robustness, reducing terminal training loss by 0.01 relative to cosine. We further identify late-stage scheduling as the primary source of this gain through controlled stage interventions, and demonstrate that a shared late schedule progressively attenuates alignment differences left by earlier LRs. Finally, transferring AIR's ELR trajectory to WD reproduces the same improved loss dynamics, demonstrating that these gains come from ELR scheduling itself and extend beyond fixed-norm training.
  </details>

- **[TGTV: Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection in UAV Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38106)**  `arXiv:2610.38106`  
  _Zixuan Wang, Changbo Shi, Ji Liu, Shiping Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection in multivariate fixed-wing unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) telemetry is complicated by operating-condition transitions, asynchronous sensor sampling, and control-system compensation that can obscure fault signatures. Existing temporal detection methods have limited capacity to represent coupled faults across subsystems, track their evolution, and constrain language-model diagnoses with quantitative evidence. This paper proposes TGTV, an interpretable framework for multivariate telemetry anomaly detection and traceable fault analysis that integrates spatiotemporal graph learning with a large language model. A temporal convolutional network and a graph attention network with weighted fused adjacency extract multiscale temporal patterns and spatial dependencies among sensors. A hybrid temporal Transformer and variational autoencoder then evaluates window-level anomalies using prediction errors and reconstruction probabilities. Continuous-time Shapley attribution identifies critical sensors and high-contribution intervals while preserving the temporal continuity of fault evolution. A structured evidence package combines detection results, graph relationships, temporal attribution, sensor attribution, and operating context. An evidence-constrained diagnosis protocol uses this package to generate flight-level conclusions with traceable evidence identifiers. Cross-flight holdout experiments on the public ALFA fixed-wing flight dataset yield a window-level area under the precision-recall curve of 0.976, accuracy of 0.973, recall of 0.902, and F1 score of 0.920. Flight-level fault diagnosis achieves recall of 0.943 and an F1 score of 0.944. These results demonstrate accurate anomaly detection and quantitatively supported fault-tracing cues, providing a basis for interpretable post-flight fault review and analysis.
  </details>
