- **[FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35801)**  `arXiv:2610.35801`  
  _Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang, Chunsheng Zuo, Anqi Liu, Benjamin Van Durme_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($\lambda$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).
  </details>

- **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Stateful Streaming Inference for Multimodal LLMs on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309)**  `arXiv:2610.35309`  
  _Yilong Li, Chengpo Yan, Yijing Zeng, Hanlei Wu, Mingjun Song, Suman Banerjee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming multimodal large language models (MLLMs) must take in new video frames while answering questions about what they have already seen. On an edge SoC, if one engine does both, ingestion and answering compete for its time while the other engines sit idle. To take over a query, another engine must first import or rebuild the accumulated history, because each runtime keeps it as KV state in its own format. \name lets one engine answer from history that another engine built. Each query reads the history as it stood when the query was asked, so one engine can keep ingesting while another answers. The CPU and GPU can also decode answers to different queries simultaneously. Phase-level state continuation lets an engine prefill a query, pass its KV state to another engine for decoding, and return to ingestion. State-Driven Execution Planning (SDEP) chooses where each query is prefilled and decoded, based on the state each engine holds and the work queued on it. Across M4 Max, RK3588, and QCS6490, \name outperforms single-engine execution. On a live M4 Max stream, SDEP cuts p95 time to first token by $76\%$, and every query receives its first token within one second. Under backlog, it raises request throughput by up to $35\%$. For two concurrent video streams on RK3588, SDEP delivers $19.6\%$ higher request throughput than single-engine execution and $27.2\%$ more than running both engines in parallel without shared state. \name delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of the same engines run serially. Every answer matches serial execution token for token.
  </details>

- **[The Effectiveness and Durability of Pretraining Data Filtering in Protein Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26651)**  `arXiv:2610.26651`  
  _Aaron Maiwald, Roderick McNeill, Jaeyoung Lee, Matthew A. Clarke, Lennart Justen, Alejandro Acelas, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretraining filtering is one of the most common safeguards for open-weight language models. Here we study its effectiveness, durability and scalability using the ESM-2 family of protein language models. We find that filtering viral proteins from the pretraining data meaningfully degrades masked language modelling, mutation effect prediction and long-range contact prediction on viral proteins, while largely preserving performance on non-viral proteins. These effects are concentrated among proteins from human-infecting viruses, and grow as we scale models from 8M to 3B parameters. As models increase in size, we find that stringent filtering becomes more effective. However, fine-tuning on sequences from the removed viral families restores the lost performance. Although the cost to recover performance increases with model scale, even for the largest models it costs about 80 USD of H100 time. This work establishes pretraining filtering as moderately effective at selectively degrading performance on viral proteins, but not durable against fine-tuning with modest computational resources and access to relevant data.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning for Large-Scale Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding with Shared Spatial Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18186)**  `arXiv:2610.18186`  
  _Jean Seong Bjorn Choe_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale applications of lifelong multi-agent path finding (LMAPF) call for computationally inexpensive movement decisions under changing traffic conditions and across varied layouts. We propose a navigation policy in which a convolutional network computes a shared spatial field over the map once per timestep and a compact goal-conditioned operator derives agents' movement preferences. The complete policy is trained end-to-end using average-reward reinforcement learning with throughput as the reward, without planner demonstrations or a separate online path planner. For structured and unstructured layouts, we train one policy per class on pools of small maps and deploy each without retraining on larger, unseen layouts and agent populations. Across six layouts with 10,000 agents, our policies achieve throughput comparable to or higher than the strongest evaluated baselines. The proposed method has an average execution time below 10 ms per step, more than an order of magnitude faster than several established planners.
  </details>

- **[One Checkpoint, Many Runs: Recipe Cards for Fine-Tuned Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16825)**  `arXiv:2610.16825`  
  _Jaewon Choi, Jihye Kang, Namhyuk Ahn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuned language models are shared with model cards that describe the training setup in detail, often down to the default random seed, but report the score of a single released checkpoint. Such setups are retrained routinely, by fine-tuning services for each customer, by organizations at each update, and by users on their own data, so the model that is deployed comes from a new run of the setup. Some runs fail, and a failed run can go unnoticed and reach the people the model serves. We call such a setup a model recipe and argue that its documentation should state how often a run of the recipe misses a required performance bar. In a survey of the model cards of 300 fine-tuned language models on Hugging Face, none reports this failure probability, and only three report results from more than one training run. Across 2,124 fine-tuning runs, we find that reading the mean, the median, or a bootstrap interval as an approval often approves recipes whose failure rate exceeds a stated failure tolerance, because these summaries do not measure how often a run fails. Model cards do not report how often the runs of a recipe fail, although whoever runs the training is best placed to count them, while those who rely on a retrained model bear the cost of a failed run. To report this failure probability directly, we propose recipe cards, an addendum to model cards that documents the model recipe behind a checkpoint. A recipe card states the bar and the tolerance and who chose them, reports how many runs are made and how many of them miss the bar, gives an upper bound on the failure probability, and warns when this bound exceeds the tolerance. Recipe cards record who set and who accepted this risk, and let deployers and regulators state acceptance criteria for retrained models.
  </details>

- **[Considering Context: When World Models Need Context Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06651)**  `arXiv:2610.06651`  
  _Oleg Smirnov, Sofiane Ennadir, John Pertoft, Bjartur Hjaltason, Sara Karimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Methods for generalization in model-based reinforcement learning typically assume that an agent cannot recover the latent context governing the environment dynamics from its own experience, and therefore supplies it externally. We formalize and test this assumption with \emph{predictive sufficiency}, which quantifies what access to the context adds to next-step prediction under the visitation distribution an agent induces, and separates that quantity into a history-recoverable part and a residual requiring the true context, both distinct from a finite model's deficit. We classify context-aware algorithms by the predictive risk their conditioning set can target and demonstrate across environments of increasing identification difficulty that the headroom does not follow the MDP class. The same task under different priors leaves predictive headroom in one setting and nothing distinguishable from zero in another, where the agent's behavior implicitly identifies the context and any benefit of such a mechanism cannot be attributed to missing one-step predictive information. Where headroom persists, the learned state exposes it only partially, and adding the true context still lowers the risk. Our contribution is an analytical framework, instantiated by a practical criterion for matching contextual mechanisms to the predictive information available to them, estimated from the ordinary trained agent.
  </details>

- **[Elastic Selective Spectral Hybrids for Train-Once, Export-Many Budgeted Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32486)**  `arXiv:2609.32486`  
  _Dachuan Song, Chuchu Chen, Xuan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying a language model under different computing and latency budgets calls for compact models with different quality-cost trade-offs. Towards this end, elastic spectral state space models provide ordered and temporally decomposed channels that can be truncated, but they are associated with linear time-invariant filters that cannot selectively preserve relevant past information or forget irrelevant information as the context evolves. To address this, we introduce the Elastic Selective Spectral Hybrid (ESSH), which realizes each Hankel spectral channel as an independent recurrent unit using fitted damped rotation modes. It also features an input-dependent decay and write/read gates that make temporal retention and state update input-dependent while preserving channel-wise truncation and a structured recurrence for efficient execution. ESSH combines these selective spectral mixers with sliding-window attention and jointly trains multiple capacities by reducing spectral-channel count and feed-forward width at different rates through a two-rate capacity map with full-model distillation. The resulting models support chunked parallel training and fused recurrent decoding while avoiding computation for discarded channels. At full capacity, ESSH achieves language-modeling quality comparable to similarly sized independently trained models, while smaller exports exhibit a smooth quality-cost trade-off. We validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework using language understanding, retrieval, cross-domain text, and DNA experiments, by assessing quality retention and the trade-off against independently trained and elastic baselines. At the 1.53B model configuration, fused batch-one decoding takes 1.37 ms per token on a B300, providing a 2.14-2.80x speedup over the tested Mamba-2 and Mamba-3 implementations and 3.03x over Transformer++ at matched parameter counts.
  </details>

- **[Perplexity Ranks Activation Quantisers but Understates What They Remove](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23125)**  `arXiv:2609.23125`  
  _Anish Sathyanarayanan, Min Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Four-bit activation quantisation of language models is usually tuned and compared by perplexity. We ask what that misses, and find three things. First, perplexity gets the order of quantisers right but the size of their damage wrong. Across four models it ranks 33 of 34 pairs of settings correctly by closed-book QA loss, yet a 6-16% rise in perplexity comes with a 10-23% drop in QA accuracy, and the lost facts are mostly rare ones. Second, the ranking only holds while the quantisation error acts like random noise. Without rotation, rounding small values to zero deletes part of the signal, and this hurts in-context retrieval far more than noise of the same size would. The crest factor of the quantiser's input (its peak-to-average ratio) helps tell the two cases apart. Third, rotating in blocks, the usual workaround when a layer's width has no ready-made Hadamard matrix, can land a model in between and leave outliers behind; 10 of 42 popular open models have such a width. A full-width Hadamard exists for every such width we found, costs about the same, and on Qwen3-4B halves the closed-book TriviaQA loss (0.112 to 0.060, over three rotation seeds). We recommend reporting a knowledge-heavy task alongside perplexity, and rotating at full width wherever blocks leave an input outlier-heavy.
  </details>

- **[TabBench-Bio: A Living Benchmark for Machine Learning on High-Dimensional Biomedical Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07441)**  `arXiv:2609.07441`  
  _Jules Kreuer, Sofiane Ouaari, Julia Hellmig, Julius Braitinger, Nico Pfeifer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical tables often combine thousands of measured variables with only tens or hundreds of labelled samples, a regime that is poorly represented in general-purpose tabular benchmarks. We introduce TabBench-Bio, a living and interactive benchmark of 43 biomedical datasets spanning multiple domains. Under a shared cross-validation protocol, we compare classical estimators, neural networks, and tabular foundation models across 28 feature-by-sample operating points. At the reference cell of 10,000 features and 100 training samples, RealTabPFN 2.5 has the highest point estimate, closely followed by TabPFN 3 and Logistic Regression. A paired bootstrap over the target pool separates RealTabPFN 2.5 from TabPFN 3 by 87 Elo (95\% interval [43, 129]).Tabular foundation models generally occupy the leading ranks, while the strongest configuration depends on the operating point and biomedical modality. The AutoML framework AutoGluon, using its one-hour "extreme" preset, is configured as a separate resource-intensive reference and reported here at the reference and full cell. Fold-level predictions, run status, and deterministic aggregations make every reported result reproducible and reusable. The benchmark is open to contributions of new biomedical datasets.The interactive leaderboard is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Two Principles for Autoregressive Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02986)**  `arXiv:2609.02986`  
  _Runlin Shi, Bojian Yin, Guoqi Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate the functional differentiation learned by RoPE Transformers to derive principles for autoregressive language modeling. We introduce two intervention-based metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS) measures how removing a frequency contribution changes a head's full attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD) isolates the effect of rotary modulation. In Qwen3 and Llama3.1, these metrics distinguish retrieval from positional modeling, while individual heads can exhibit both functions. A mid-low-frequency Global Positional Band (GPBand) separates their frequency dependencies and tracks the training sequence length in controlled experiments, suggesting that global positional fitting can hinder zero-shot length extrapolation. These findings motivate two principles. (1) Functional Organization: retrieval and positional functions should be assigned separately and mixed within layers with layer-specific allocation; and (2) Positional Dependence: positional modeling should operate only locally, while global access should rely on position-independent retrieval. Matched pretraining from scratch and targeted architectural ablations at 380M and 1.4B parameters support both principles and highlight the importance of mechanism choice in realizing each function. Together, these findings suggest a promising route toward architectural innovation grounded in the functional requirements of autoregressive language modeling.
  </details>

- **[How do features act in an individual prediction? Separating standalone and interaction effects with a case-based taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27821)**  `arXiv:2608.27821`  
  _Sejong Oh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local explanation methods such as SHAP and LIME summarize each feature with a single attribution score, and interaction indices quantify how strongly features interact. Neither tells a user how a feature acts in a given prediction: whether its effect is the same alone and in context, or whether the other features amplify it, make it unnecessary, switch it off or reverse it. We extend case-based feature importance (CBFI), originally a global method, to individual predictions. Evaluating the model in four hybrid states per background sample decomposes the in-context importance of a feature into a standalone and an interaction effect, and a case taxonomy labels each feature of an instance as inactive, standalone, amplified, redundant, gated or reversed. We compare the method with k-SII, TreeSHAP interaction values and the H-statistic on synthetic functions, and study eight real tabular datasets with six model families. On the true functions, the decomposition recovered interactions exactly (correlation 0.99 with the analytic local interaction). On fitted models, pair recovery was slightly below that of dedicated indices (local precision@k 0.86-0.91 versus 0.91-0.95). Interaction values alone could not distinguish substitution from gating, whereas the taxonomy identified both without knowing the partner (accuracy 0.74-0.76); applied to Shapley interaction values, it performed comparably. On real data, 45-57% of feature pairs with nearly identical SHAP values received different labels, and in a medical-insurance model the method showed that the interaction component of smoking changes sign at a body-mass index of 30. Localized CBFI does not detect interactions more accurately than existing indices; what it adds is a decomposition into standalone and interaction effects and a label stating how each feature acts.
  </details>

- **[CommitKV: Commit-Aligned KV Cache Compression for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07855)**  `arXiv:2608.07855`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal agents gather visual evidence through reasoning and tool use, extending their ability to solve tasks beyond the initial input. However, accumulating observations increases KV cache memory and inference costs. Token eviction reduces these costs by retaining useful cached tokens. Existing methods use attention, redundancy, or stage-specific queries to rank tokens, without explicitly comparing the same history across tool interactions. When observations shift attention, they may mistake temporarily reduced influence for lost usefulness and evict information needed later. We propose CommitKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that accounts for this changing evidence use. Commit-aligned measurement compares the deletion effects of the same page before a tool call completes and after its observation returns. Lifecycle-aware compression retires pages with low influence in both windows and temporarily protects pages influential before the call. Joint scoring combines attention importance, key redundancy, and token-level deletion effects to fill the remaining budget. Extensive experiments cover four open-weight multimodal models and eight benchmarks. CommitKV achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared compression methods on all four models. On Qwen3-VL-8B, it achieves 69.85% at a 512-token budget, only 0.91% below FullKV, and exceeds the strongest compression baseline by 8.44% at 256 tokens. Deploying the Qwen3-VL-8B agent with SGLang and vLLM at a 256-token budget reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 54.37%-55.23% and accelerates end-to-end inference by 2.12-2.17$\times$ over FullKV on the matched workload.
  </details>

- **[Graph-of-Thoughts for LLM Causal Discovery with a Fairness-Pathway Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02877)**  `arXiv:2608.02877`  
  _Nitish Nagesh, Elahe Khatibi, Thomas Dean Hughes, Mahdi Bagheri, Pratik Gajane, Amir M. Rahmani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Methods that use large language models (LLMs) for causal discovery reason locally, deciding one variable pair or one node at a time, so they never compare alternative whole graphs. We introduce GoT-CD, a Graph-of-Thoughts method that asks an LLM for several complete candidate graphs from variable metadata alone, ranks them with a deterministic validity score, merges them under a hard union constraint, refines the merge, and projects the result to a DAG. We evaluate on Asia, Child, and an Alzheimer's disease graph with 100 samples and GPT-4o-mini. Over five seeds on Alzheimer's and Child, GoT-CD gives the highest mean F1 score and the lowest mean structural Hamming distance of the LLM methods, with 95% intervals of the difference excluding zero in seven of eight comparisons, and it returns a DAG in every run while using three LLM requests per graph. Replaying the logged runs without individual components attributes most of the gain to generating and merging several complete candidates. A case study on the effect of Sex on MOCA Score in the Alzheimer's graph shows the limits of structural scores: the fairness verdict drawn from the discovered graph disagrees with the reference verdict in twelve of fifteen LLM runs, including all five GoT-CD runs, which mostly drop the edge out of Sex. Graphs meant for a specific downstream analysis should therefore also be checked on the pathways that analysis depends on.
  </details>

- **[Operational Proto-Introspection in Looped Language Models: Reading Correctness Before the Answer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18553)**  `arXiv:2607.18553`  
  _Jan Kirin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can one tell, while a language model reasons, whether its answer will be right? Looped models are a good place to ask: they run the same layers several times per token, leaving one hidden state per loop for a probe to read. I probe Ouro-RLTT, a 2.6B-parameter looped model, for what these states reveal about how its reasoning will end. I call such information operational proto-introspection: operational because it is defined by what a probe can read, and proto because nothing here shows that the model itself uses it.In part, yes. Reading the first 48 tokens of reasoning, from answers that state no answer yet, a probe on the hidden states reaches an AUROC of 0.758 on a synthetic logic task, against 0.515 for token confidence. Question difficulty is a rival explanation: on 1,164 further questions, token confidence plus simple features of the question predict about as well. It is not only difficulty: between two answers to the same question, the probe scores the correct one higher 74% of the time. A fine-tuned language model reading the same tokens does about as well. On GSM8K and on a second checkpoint this within-question test is unresolved.A probe that ranks finished candidate answers is weak at layer 8 on the first loop, much better on the second, and as accurate as the last layers by the third, on four Ouro checkpoints. The direction it reads is largely shared across loops: a probe trained on the last loop reads the first nearly as well as a probe trained there.The signal helps decide what to do with finished outputs. It improves abstention over surface features and picks correct answers among candidates better than chance, though surface features pick about as well. Once several samples are finished, a baseline that knows their agreement leaves the answer-free probe nothing measurable to add. None of my attempts to use it to make the model generate better answers worked.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Lost Updates Is Not Enough to Choose a Rounding Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09800)**  `arXiv:2607.09800`  
  _Zekai Shang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In low-precision training, a weight can round back to its previous value after a small update. We test whether predicting how often this happens helps choose a rounding rule. In Pythia-410M continuations from a common checkpoint, a higher-precision pilot accurately predicted the fraction of matrix weights left unchanged at each of the first 64 updates. Across three schedules with optimizer state retained or reset, nearest rounding gave lower validation loss at 256 updates than stochastic rounding or retaining 32-bit weights. The same ordering held at 512 updates with retained state. Accurately forecasting the unchanged fraction did not identify a benefit from switching policies in these runs.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty-aware damage identification in short-span bridges via physics-informed variational autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05025)**  `arXiv:2607.05025`  
  _Ana Fernandez-Navamuel, A. Javier Omella, Diego Zamora-Sanchez, David Pardo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vibration-based damage identification in civil infrastructure is a challenging, ill-posed inverse problem due to measurement noise, sparse sensor arrays, and modeling errors. While deep learning is powerful for system identification, deterministic approaches lack reliable uncertainty quantification and can yield physically inconsistent results. This work proposes a probabilistic Scientific Machine Learning framework for structural health monitoring: a physics-informed Gaussian copula variational autoencoder. The methodology introduces two main contributions. First, a numerical eigenvalue solver is embedded directly into the variational autoencoder as its decoder, which replaces data-driven surrogates, ensures that every posterior sample satisfies the governing equations of structural dynamics, and reduces the number of trainable parameters. Second, a Gaussian copula describes the posterior distribution of the stiffness factors, capturing cross-correlations between components with fewer parameters than Gaussian mixtures in real time. We validate the method numerically on a short-span bridge supported on elastic bearings, whose response is dominated by vertical bending, accounting for modeling errors, measurement noise, and a finer finite element discretization for testing. The method estimates the posterior distribution of the damage condition, i.e., the stiffness reduction factors of the deck zones and bearings, and these posteriors closely match the exact ones computed with sequential Monte Carlo sampling. The results reveal that the proposed methodology offers a robust and interpretable decision-making tool, able not only to indicate the presence and location of damage, but also to inform on the uncertainty associated with the diagnosis, for instance, when light damage is masked by the effect of noise or when the measured modal properties present limited sensitivity to damage.
  </details>

- **[When Do Foundation Models Pay Off? A Break-Even Analysis of Pretrained Time Series Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04919)**  `arXiv:2607.04919`  
  _Nicholas Tan Jerome, Frank Simon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying a time series foundation model (FM) requires GPU infrastructure and engineering overhead, with no guarantee of improvement over classical forecasting methods. We provide the first systematic break-even analysis of when this investment pays off. Across 30 benchmark datasets, we compare zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned FMs (Chronos-Bolt, Moirai, Lag-Llama) against classical baselines (Naive, ETS, ARIMA, XGBoost) at six training set sizes from 2% to 100% of available data. FMs outperform classical methods at every evaluated training fraction on 16 of 30 datasets. On 6 datasets, classical methods surpass zero-shot FMs with at most 5% of the training data (21--6,324 samples); on the remaining 8, break-even ranges from 24 to 22,177 samples. No classical method dominates: ARIMA, Naive, ETS and XGBoost are each best on 6--11 datasets. One robust rule requires no model training: if n_train < 700 and seasonality is non-negligible, FM zero-shot is the right low-data choice -- below 20% of the history, classical methods either lose to it (4 of these 10 datasets) or cannot be fitted at all (6) -- and it stays best at full history on 6 of them; fine-tuning should be skipped. Contrary to common practice, LoRA fine-tuning rarely helps and degrades short series by up to 52%. We operationalise these findings as a two-step decision framework -- compute dataset length and seasonality strength, run a brief 5--10% pilot only if needed. Cheap dataset features do not predict the break-even category at this benchmark scale, so outside the rule a pilot remains necessary. Code and benchmark:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[FlexMoE: Learning One-for-All Multi-Budget Expert Pruning for Flexible MoE Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27866)**  `arXiv:2606.27866`  
  _Fan Mo, Yuxuan Han, Geng Zhang, Wangbo Zhao, Yang You_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models scale model capacity through sparse expert activation, yet deployment still requires storing and serving all experts. Moreover, deployment budgets can vary substantially across devices, users, and workloads. Existing MoE compression methods typically optimize a separate compressed model for each target budget. We introduce FlexMoE, a post-training one-for-all framework that converts a large pretrained MoE model into a multi-budget family of smaller deployable subnetworks. FlexMoE first ranks expert FFN channels by importance to construct a sliceable weight space shared across budgets, and then learns expert-wise discrete retention actions under gradually increasing cost pressure, allowing a single action-training run to produce reliable pruning masks across budgets. Moreover, only a single mid-budget fine-tune can further recover degraded performance and yields a shared recovered model that transfers to other unseen budgets, avoiding repeated per-budget recovery and separated checkpoint storage. Across tested MoE LLMs with diverse architectures and scales, FlexMoE consistently outperforms strong MoE compression baselines. In particular, on Qwen2-57B-A14B, it retains ~99.8% of base performance while pruning 50% of routed-expert parameters even without recovery fine-tuning. For deployment, pruned subnetworks deliver real memory reduction and end-to-end throughput gains, while our kernel co-design enables runtime budget switching without checkpoint reloading and delivers substantial inference acceleration.
  </details>

- **[SharQ: Bridging Activation Sparsity and FP4 Quantization for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26587)**  `arXiv:2606.26587`  
  _Haoqian Meng, Yilun Luo, Yafei Zhao, Wenyuan Liu, Huaqing Zheng, Xindian Ma, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit floating-point formats and semi-structured sparsity are increasingly supported by modern accelerators, yet combining them for LLM activation compression remains challenging: activations contain input-dependent outliers that dominate block scales in FP4 quantization, and directly applying N:M sparsity masks discards moderate values, coupling sparsification loss with quantization error. We introduce SharQ, a training-free inference method that bridges activation sparsity and FP4 quantization through an online sparse--dense decomposition. For each activation tensor, SharQ generates an input-adaptive N:M mask to extract an outlier-dominated sparse backbone, quantizes it to FP4, and defines a dense residual relative to the quantized sparse backbone rather than the unquantized sparse values. A sparse FP4 GEMM processes the backbone while a dense FP4 GEMM compensates for both mask-induced activation loss and sparse-path quantization error. The two paths share a single FP4 weight payload with path-specific scale views, and a fused preparation kernel absorbs mask generation, residual construction, and layer normalization into one operator. SharQ requires no calibration data, retraining, or model-specific tuning. Evaluated on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-30B-A3B, and Qwen3-VL-8B, SharQ recovers 43--63% of the NVFP4-to-FP16 accuracy gap across language and vision-language tasks, and generalizes across NVFP4, HiF4, and MXFP4 formats. On an RTX 5090, SharQ delivers 2.2--2.4$\times$ latency reduction over FP16 and 1.2--1.4$\times$ throughput improvement over FP8 in language model serving, and up to 1.58$\times$ speedup on Wan2.2-T2V-A14B video generation when combined with SageAttention. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning through Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20937)**  `arXiv:2606.20937`  
  _Nikolaos Tsilivis, Nirmit Joshi, Marko Medvedev, Julia Kempe, Nathan Srebro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study internalization processes, by which neural-network-based systems absorb an explicit computational procedure into their own weights, and how they facilitate learning. We investigate how transformers internalize the simulation of semiautomata by internalizing chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens, which classes of semiautomata are harder to internalize, and expose the flip side of internalization, that is, a progressive degradation of out-of-distribution performance. We then provide the first provable analysis of successful internalization: for the task of learning parities, we show that a simplified one-layer transformer provably first learns the target with explicit CoT supervision and then internalizes the autoregressive generation as CoT tokens are progressively removed, learning to directly compute the parity. This task is computationally hard to learn from data without CoT supervision. Finally, we discuss how learning through internalization relates to the \textit{Positive Distribution Shift} phenomenon recently introduced by~\citet{Med+26}.
  </details>

- **[Rational Sparse Autoencoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14990)**  `arXiv:2606.14990`  
  _Naiyu Yin, Maosen Tang, Alex Townsend, Yue Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are standard tools for mechanistic interpretability, but current SAE families are constrained by fixed encoder nonlinearities such as ReLU, JumpReLU, and TopK. This hard-codes a particular sparsity mechanism into the model and can distort the reconstruction-versus-sparsity trade-off. We introduce the Rational Sparse Autoencoder (RSAE), which replaces the fixed encoder activation with a trainable rational function. The design is motivated by a reconstruction advantage that we prove for RSAE at matched expected sparsity, with guaranteed non-inferiority to ReLU and, under stated assumptions, provable improvements over JumpReLU and TopK. The model is then implemented through a two-stage pipeline: an initialization procedure that copies the pre-trained baseline SAE weights and calibrates the scale parameters along with the rational coefficients; followed by a fine-tuning step under a sparsity-regularized reconstruction objective that holds $\ell_0$ at the teacher's value. Consistent with this theory, on residual-stream activations of three open-weight language models and across all three baseline activation families, the RSAE improves reconstruction fidelity after the fine-tuning step at the same level of sparsity. These gains are consistent across host language models, across baseline activation families, and across the full range of baseline sparsity we tested, while the upgrade itself adds only 20 scalar parameters per autoencoder and one training run takes between about 4 and 25 minutes on a single consumer GPU.
  </details>

- **[FedRAN: Accurate and Efficient Analytic Federated Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11480)**  `arXiv:2606.11480`  
  _Jebacyril Arockiaraj, Dhruv Parikh, Jayashree Adivarahan, Rajgopal Kannan, Viktor Prasanna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated continual learning (FCL) enables distributed clients to learn from evolving data streams while retaining knowledge from previously observed tasks. Existing optimization-based FCL methods often rely on repeated local training, incurring communication overhead and feature drift across clients. Analytic alternatives avoid iterative training, but high-dimensional random features used to improve accuracy require communicating a Gram matrix whose cost grows quadratically with feature dimension. Communication-efficient alternatives reduce this cost by statistically reconstructing the Gram matrix from first-order summaries. We propose FedRAN, a low-rank analytic FCL framework that instead compresses the required second-order structure directly through spectral summaries that are merged across clients and tasks. The retained rank explicitly controls the tradeoff between approximation fidelity and communication, reducing second-order communication from quadratic to linear in feature dimension for a fixed rank. FedRAN further supports label scarcity through prototype-based pseudo-labeling. Across CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and VTAB, FedRAN improves average accuracy by up to 4.8 percentage points over the strongest baseline, uses 30.6-121.8x less per-client communication, and is 190.3x faster than optimization-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting for Alleviating Over-smoothing in Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10592)**  `arXiv:2606.10592`  
  _Xingyu Zhang, Jingyao Wang, Xin Yu, Zeen Song, Jianqi Zhang, Changwen Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting often suffers from over-smoothing, especially when future dynamics are multi-modal. Forecasts may follow the coarse trend of the observed future, but fail to preserve sharp changes, oscillations, turning points, and regime transitions that define plausible dynamic evolution. In this work, we revisit over-smoothing from the perspective of latent dynamical mode compression: under partial observation and single-realization supervision, multiple plausible future modes can be weakened, merged, or averaged during forecasting. Based on this view, we propose Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting (DGF), a mode-preserving forecasting framework that explicitly models multiple mode-conditioned predictive distributions and uncertainty over their selection probabilities. DGF uses a Dirichlet-guided hierarchical sampling mechanism and reward-based optimization to encourage forecasts that are accurate, dynamically consistent, and mode-distinct. Extensive experiments on real-world forecasting benchmarks show that DGF reduces over-smoothing while improving forecasting accuracy, diversity, and dynamical consistency. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Measuring What Matters: Synthetic Benchmarks for Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04326)**  `arXiv:2606.04326`  
  _Julian Skirzynski, Harry Cheon, Shreyas Kadekodi, Meredith Stewart, Berk Ustun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept bottleneck models predict using high-level concepts detected in inputs. Although these models provide a simple way to reap benefits from interpretability, they are hard to train and evaluate because very few real-world datasets include concept labels. In this paper, we develop synthetic benchmarks for concept bottleneck models. With our benchmarks, researchers can generate labeled datasets and control properties that affect performance, such as data modality, the choice of concepts, or annotation quality. They can thus find problems that suit these models and isolate the factors behind their failures. We demonstrate how our benchmarks can be used to evaluate major use cases for concept bottleneck models: decision support, i.e., helping human experts make better decisions; and automation, i.e., handling routine tasks without supervision. Our demonstrations show how the benchmarks can identify new failure modes and attribute them to either the architecture or the data.
  </details>

- **[The Silent Hyperparameter: Quantifying the Impact of Inference Backends on LLM Reproducibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19537)**  `arXiv:2605.19537`  
  _David Pape, Jonathan Evertz, Lea Schönherr_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in LLMs is increasingly measured through standardized benchmarks, where state-of-the-art improvements are often separated by fractions of a percentage point. At the same time, the computational cost of evaluating modern LLMs has driven widespread adoption of specialized inference backends, software systems that execute trained models efficiently at inference time. While critical for scalability, system-level optimizations, such as custom CUDA kernels and reduced-precision arithmetic, can alter token probabilities and introduce non-determinism, possibly cascading into divergent generation. In this work, we first survey the inference landscape, identifying $200$ distinct engines, and analyze $35,000$ ML publications, finding that the specific inference stack is rarely reported despite this widespread diversity. We then present a systematic empirical study of how inference backends affect LLM benchmark results. Holding model weights, decoding parameters, and hardware constant, we evaluate five widely used inference engines, including vLLM, SGLang, and llama$.$cpp, across multiple open-weight models spanning 4B-30B parameters and established benchmarks. We show that the choice of backend alone can shift benchmark scores by up to $16.6$ percentage points and induce high rates of output disagreement. By isolating backend optimizations and tracing the execution pipeline, we find this divergence is driven by system-level optimizations such as prefix caching and CUDA graphs, custom kernels, and engine-specific defaults in logit processing. Our findings identify the inference backend as a previously unreported yet consequential hyperparameter in LLM evaluations and advocate standardized reporting of inference stacks to improve the reproducibility and interpretability of benchmark comparisons.
  </details>

- **[KVBuffer: IO-aware Serving for Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19049)**  `arXiv:2605.19049`  
  _Longwei Zou, Lin Zhong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear attention has recently gained significant attention for long-context inference due to its constant decoding cost with respect to context length. However, existing serving systems typically serve linear attention by recurrently computing and updating a large linear attention state in every decoding step. Since the state is much larger than the per-token key and value, recurrent decoding incurs substantial memory access and becomes inefficient for serving linear attention. In this paper, we propose KVBuffer, an IO-aware serving mechanism for linear attention. By buffering recent keys and values, KVBuffer enables serving systems to compute linear attention outputs in more flexible and memory-efficient ways. For decoding, KVBuffer enables chunkwise computation, which reduces average memory access and decoding latency by deferring state updates and applying them in batch. For speculative decoding, KVBuffer verifies draft tokens in parallel and avoids storing temporary states. For short contexts, KVBuffer computes attention outputs directly from buffered keys and values, without creating or updating the linear attention state. We implement KVBuffer in SGLang for Qwen3.5 and Nemotron-3 series models. Our evaluations show that KVBuffer can speedup batch decoding by up to 1.47x and increase the maximum number of serving requests by 5x for speculative decoding when verifying four draft tokens.
  </details>

- **[What competing constraints reveal about representation learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15995)**  `arXiv:2605.15995`  
  _Gwenolé Quellec_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning latent states from temporal, multimodal, and partially observed data requires decisions about what information representations should retain, discard, and organize. I argue that six complementary properties help clarify these decisions: predictive sufficiency, minimality, temporal coherence, observation compatibility, nuisance invariance, and structural constraints. Drawing on established approaches, I distinguish desired representation properties from the objectives used to encourage them and the diagnostics used to assess them, since neither a training objective nor an evaluation metric guarantees that a property has been achieved. Controlled synthetic experiments examine how training objectives influence predictive performance, nuisance decodability, and latent organization. The results reveal discrepancies between seemingly favorable diagnostics and the information retained in learned representations, as well as latent-coordinate ambiguities under equivalent predictive performance, illustrating how complementary evaluations can improve interpretation and guide design choices. Together, these analyses provide a basis for designing, comparing, and evaluating learned latent states.
  </details>

- **[StreamPhy: Streaming Inference of High-Dimensional Physical Dynamics via State Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07384)**  `arXiv:2605.07384`  
  _Panqi Chen, Yifan Sun, Shikai Fang, Xiao Fu, Lei Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inferring the evolution of high-dimensional and multi-modal (e.g., spatio-temporal) physical fields from irregular sparse measurements in real time is a fundamental challenge in science and engineering. Existing approaches, including diffusion-based generative models and functional tensor methods, typically operate in offline settings, depend on full temporal observations, or incur substantial inference cost. We propose StreamPhy, an end-to-end framework that enables efficient and accurate streaming inference of full-field physical dynamics from incoming irregular sparse measurements. The framework integrates a data-adaptive observation encoder that is robust to arbitrary observation patterns, a structured state-space model that supports memory-efficient online updates across irregular time intervals, and an expressive Functional Tensor Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FT-FiLM) decoder for continuous-field generation. We prove that FT-FiLM is more expressive than the functional Tucker model, admitting a richer function class for handling complex dynamics. Experiments on three representative physical systems under challenging sampling patterns show that StreamPhy consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with at least 48\% improvement in accuracy and up to 20--100X faster inference than diffusion-based methods.
  </details>

- **[TraXion: Rethinking Pre-training Frameworks for Mobility and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06906)**  `arXiv:2605.06906`  
  _Shang-Ling Hsu, Mark Tenzer, Cyrus Shahabi, Khurram Shafique_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human mobility differs from text and from generic time series in three structural ways: visits are tuple-valued events whose meaning depends on the joint distribution over location, time, and activity; users carry persistent signatures across trajectories; and visits are not independent across users, since co-location at shared places is a primary signal. Existing pre-training recipes for mobility import objectives from language modeling, treating trajectories as sentences and visits as tokens, an analogy that fails against each of the three properties above. These properties define a broader class, multi-entity spatiotemporal event streams (MESES), spanning enterprise authentication logs, electronic health records, and other event-stream domains where entities share infrastructure, schedules, or contexts. We make the properties precise as three axioms that any pre-training framework for MESES should satisfy, and introduce TraXion, whose objectives and architecture are jointly designed to meet them. A single TraXion checkpoint per dataset leads task-specific baselines on every location-ranking and detection metric across six public mobility datasets covering anomaly detection, next-POI recommendation, next-visit prediction, and social-link prediction. The same recipe, applied unchanged to enterprise authentication logs and ICU mortality prediction, matches or exceeds prior work on both, showing that event streams from domains as different as mobility, security, and healthcare can be modeled under a single framework.
  </details>

- **[Reading Human Preference from Looped Transformer Internal States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09870)**  `arXiv:2604.09870`  
  _Jan Kirin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Show a frozen language model two replies to the same request, one of which a person preferred. Can a small probe on the model's hidden states tell which? On Ouro-2.6B-Thinking, a model that runs its layers four times per token, and Anthropic's HH-RLHF data, the answer is yes, but only faintly. A linear probe picks the preferred reply 61.5% of the time, a small nonlinear network that scores each reply separately 64.9%, and the evaluator trained for version 1 reaches 64.7% on the test split: clearly better than simple rules such as picking the longer reply, but weak. Whether preference is stored as a comparison between the two replies or as a property of each is still open. A linear probe cannot tell, and a network that reads both replies together has an estimated edge of only 0.7 points (95% CI 0.0 to 1.4) over one that scores each reply separately. These numbers hold under three controls: scoring every pair in both orders, splitting the data by pair, and, for the probes, keeping the end of each conversation, where the two replies differ. Version 1's 95.2% and 84.5% were obtained without the first two and do not hold; version 2's corrected relational-probe accuracy, obtained without the third, was four to five points too low. This version replaces both.
  </details>

- **[The multiplicative Lipschitz bound as an inductive bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28113)**  `arXiv:2603.28113`  
  _Simon Kuang, Yuezhu Xu, S. Sivaranjani, Xinfan Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the problem of training a neural network to minimize a loss function given a Lipschitz constraint. While the most obvious way to estimate the Lipschitz constant of a neural network is to multiply the Lipschitz constants of its layers, this bound, which we term the _trivial bound_, is often considered impractically loose. We show that, on the contrary, it can be more effective than state-of-the-art methods (Sandwich, Almost-Orthogonal Layers, Convex Potential Layers, and SDP-based Lipschitz Layers) for Lipschitz-constrained learning. Our method combines the trivial bound with a novel activation function (the quadrature nonlinearity) to achieve lower cross-entropy over most of the tested classification bounds. For autoencoders constrained to be nonexpansive in a learned norm, it achieves the lowest pixel reconstruction error at every tested bottleneck width, improving on PCA by 12--24%. Our architecture, consisting of concatenated cosine and sine activations, is supported by a semi-rigorous theoretical analysis of Lipschitz bound looseness that may prove valuable in its own right.
  </details>

- **[Shapley meets Rawls: an integrated framework for measuring and explaining unfairness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26476)**  `arXiv:2603.26476`  
  _Fadoua Amri-Jouidel, Emmanuel Kemel, Stéphane Mussard_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explainability and fairness have mainly been considered separately, with recent exceptions trying the explain the sources of unfairness. This paper shows that the Shapley value can be used to both define and explain unfairness, under standard group fairness criteria. This offers an integrated framework to estimate and derive inference on unfairness as-well-as the features that contribute to it. Our framework can also be extended from Shapley values to the family of Efficient-Symmetric-Linear (ESL) values, some of which offer more robust definitions of fairness, and shorter computation times. An illustration is run on the Census Income dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository. Our approach shows that ``Age", ``Number of hours" and ``Marital status" generate gender unfairness, using shorter computation time than traditional Bootstrap tests.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Path-Specific Fairness Across Causal Discovery Algorithms: A Heart Failure Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15926)**  `arXiv:2603.15926`  
  _Nitish Nagesh, Elahe Khatibi, Thomas Hughes, Mahdi Bagheri, Pratik Gajane, Amir M. Rahmani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal discovery is increasingly applied to healthcare data, where the recovered relationships can encode biased pathways that propagate to downstream prediction. Path-specific fairness analysis, through direct, indirect, and spurious effect decompositions along with fairness-utility ratio assessment allows bias quantification and associated prediction cost. However, existing evaluations of causal discovery under fairness constraints have largely used non-clinical datasets and have not separated the contribution of confounders from that of mediators, leaving open how discovery-algorithm choice propagates through path-specific fairness to the utility trade-off in clinical settings. This work addresses that gap by establishing an expert-elicited causal graph benchmark for the heart failure clinical records dataset and tracing discovery algorithms through composite, per-variable fairness decompositions and fairness-utility ratio. ICA-LiNGAM achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance and highest recall, while GES achieves the highest precision. For path-specific effects, there was a 1.2 percentage-point difference in the mediated effect on ejection fraction on overall causal fairness between the original and discovered graphs. This ultimately led to variations in the fairness-utility ratio highlighting need for fine-grained evaluation. These results indicate that discovery-aware, path-specific fairness evaluation is consequential in clinical settings and warrants inclusion in routine auditing of clinical risk models.
  </details>

- **[Counterfactual Feature Regularization for Expansion-Based Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09145)**  `arXiv:2603.09145`  
  _Zhen Zhang, Jielei Chu, Jiangtao Hu, Bin Liu, Jie Wang, Ya Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current expansion-based methods for Class Incremental Learning (CIL) effectively mitigate catastrophic forgetting by freezing old features. However, such task-specific features learned from the new task may collide with the old features. From a causal perspective, spurious feature correlations are the main cause of this collision, manifesting in two scopes: (i) guided by empirical risk minimization (ERM), intra-task spurious correlations cause task-specific features to rely on shortcut features. These non-robust features are vulnerable to interference, inevitably drifting into the feature space of other tasks; (ii) inter-task spurious correlations induce semantic confusion between visually similar classes across tasks. To address this, we propose a Probability of Necessity and Sufficiency (PNS)-based regularization method to guide feature expansion in CIL. Specifically, we first extend the definition of PNS to expansion-based CIL, termed CPNS, which quantifies both the causal completeness of intra-task representations and the separability of inter-task representations. We then introduce a dual-scope counterfactual generator based on twin networks to ensure the measurement of CPNS, which simultaneously generates: (i) intra-task counterfactual features to minimize intra-task PNS risk and ensure causal completeness of task-specific features, and (ii) inter-task interfering features to minimize inter-task PNS risk, ensuring the separability of inter-task representations. Theoretical analyses confirm its reliability. The regularization is a plug-and-play method for expansion-based CIL to mitigate feature collision. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
  </details>

- **[Towards Generalizable Reasoning: Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06475)**  `arXiv:2602.06475`  
  _Jingyao Wang, Xingyu Zhang, Peizheng Guo, Jiahuan Zhou, Zhenghao Zhang, Changwen Zheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at complex tasks with advances in reasoning capabilities. However, existing reward mechanisms remain tightly coupled to final correctness and pay little attention to the underlying reasoning process: trajectories with sound reasoning but wrong answers receive low credit, while lucky guesses with flawed logic may be highly rewarded, affecting reasoning generalization. From a causal perspective, we interpret multi-candidate reasoning for a fixed question as a family of counterfactual experiments. Building on this, we propose Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization to explicitly train LLMs to learn generalizable reasoning patterns. It proposes an episodic causal counterfactual reward that jointly captures (i) robustness, encouraging the answer distribution induced by a reasoning step to remain stable under counterfactual perturbations; and (ii) effectiveness, enforcing sufficient variability so that the learned reasoning strategy can transfer across questions. We then construct token-level advantages from this reward and optimize the policy, encouraging LLMs to favor reasoning patterns that are process-valid and counterfactually robust. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate its advantages.
  </details>

- **[CoreQ: Mismatch-Corrected Successive Rounding for LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05902)**  `arXiv:2602.05902`  
  _Seohyeon Cha, Huancheng Chen, Dongjun Kim, Haoran Zhang, Kevin Chan, Gustavo de Veciana, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) enables efficient deployment of large language models by mapping pretrained weights to low-bit formats without retraining, typically using a small calibration set to minimize a layer-wise calibration objective. However, this sequential procedure induces a mismatch: errors from earlier quantized layers alter the inputs received by later layers, causing the activations to deviate from those of the full-precision model. Recent approaches introduce mismatch-aware calibration objectives to compensate for this effect, but leave open how much of the observed mismatch should shift each layer's calibration target. We show that fully applying the empirical mismatch correction can overfit finite calibration data, while using a fixed correction coefficient ignores variation in mismatch estimates across layers. To address these limitations, we propose CoreQ, a learning-free PTQ framework that uses a geometric decomposition of the mismatch to compute a closed-form, layer-adaptive correction coefficient. This coefficient controls how much of the observed mismatch enters each layer's calibration target, reducing overfitting to limited calibration data without coefficient tuning. CoreQ then applies efficient greedy successive rounding to the resulting triangular least-squares objective. Its bounded beam-search extension, K-CoreQ, trades modest additional compute for improved performance. Across multiple LLM families, scales, bit-widths, and quantization settings, CoreQ and K-CoreQ achieve lower perplexity and higher downstream accuracy than strong PTQ baselines. Code is available at GitHub.
  </details>

- **[Consecutive Preferential Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.05163)**  `arXiv:2511.05163`  
  _Aras Erarslan, Carlos Sevilla Salcedo, Ville Tanskanen, Anni Nisov, Eero Päiväkumpu, Heikki Aisala, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preferential Bayesian optimization allows optimization of objectives that are either expensive or difficult to measure directly, by relying on a minimal number of comparative evaluations done by a human expert. Generating candidate solutions for evaluation is also often expensive, but existing preferential optimization methods do not account for this production cost. We introduce Consecutive Preferential Bayesian Optimization (CPBO) for the consecutive setting, where production cost is reduced by reusing the previous candidate in each comparison. We also account for the perceptual ambiguity of the oracle providing the feedback by incorporating a Just-Noticeable Difference threshold into a probabilistic preference model to capture indifference to small utility differences. We adapt an information-theoretic acquisition strategy to this setting, selecting new configurations that are most informative about the unknown optimum under a preference model accounting for the perceptual ambiguity. We empirically demonstrate a notable increase in accuracy in setups with high production costs or indifference feedback, and demonstrate the practical feasibility of CPBO in a real-world food extrusion task.
  </details>

- **[Hyperparameter Optimization via Budget Allocation in Lipschitz Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10605)**  `arXiv:2510.10605`  
  _MohammadHossein Bateni, Hossein Esfandiari, Samira HosseinGhorban, Alireza Mirrokni, Radin Shahdaei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperparameter optimization and model selection often require allocating a limited evaluation budget across many candidate configurations, where performance improves with additional training or resources. We study this setting when each configuration has an unknown performance curve over budget. Similarity between configurations induces similarity between their curves, capturing geometric structure in the search space. We formalize the resulting decision problem as Unknown Value Probing and propose budget allocation strategies that combine principled exploration of the configuration space with value-informed concentration on regions supported by observed evidence. We prove worst-case approximation guarantees for our approach and show these guarantees are near-optimal by matching corresponding hardness barriers. Extensive experiments on large benchmark suites spanning more than 250 experimental settings demonstrate consistent gains over strong baselines in both anytime behavior and final incumbent performance.
  </details>

- **[Survival of the Fittest Perturbations: Uncertainty-Aware Evolution of Adversarial Distributions for Robust Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02871)**  `arXiv:2411.02871`  
  _Junhao Dong, Zicheng Yu, Xinghua Qu, Z. Jane Wang, Yew-Soon Ong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite deep learning's broad successes across diverse scenarios, its vulnerability to adversarial examples impedes deployment, making adversarial training a leading approach to improve robustness. However, pointwise adversarial training maps each clean sample to a single adversary, overlooking surrounding adversarial variability and the associated shifts in feature statistics. To address these issues, we propose Evolutionary Uncertainty-Aware Distributional Adversarial Training (EvoUAD-AT). Specifically, we design an evolutionary adversarial uncertainty modeling module that maintains an instance-wise search distribution over adversarial feature statistics around an adversarially generated anchor. Candidates are evaluated under the current model, selected by their threat to a benign reference, and used to adapt the distribution; strategy parameters are retained across epochs. To avoid anchoring on misclassified clean samples, we refine the alignment reference and match benign and adversarial distributions. Introspective gradient matching further regularizes input gradients without external supervision. Our analysis characterizes the elite fitness surrogate and gives a conditional certificate for latent adversarial features. Extensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in both clean accuracy and robustness, with thorough analyses confirming the effectiveness of each proposed component.
  </details>

- **[Loss-Controlling Calibration with Asymptotic Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.04378)**  `arXiv:2301.04378`  
  _Di Wang, Yujin Li, Junzhi Shi, Pingping Wang, Shuo Zhuang, Hongyue Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a learning framework for calibrating predictive models to perform loss-controlling prediction, which extends our proposed conformal loss-controlling prediction framework to more general settings. In contrast to existing methods, the predictors built by the proposed approach are not restricted to set-valued predictors, and the loss function can be any measurable function without requiring monotonicity assumptions. The approach is a natural extension of conformal loss-controlling prediction, since it reduces to the latter when the predictors are set-valued with the nesting property and the losses are monotone. The loss-controlling guarantee of the proposed framework is established in the asymptotic setting and is empirically verified in selective regression and high-impact weather forecasting, demonstrating its effectiveness for the domains requiring general loss-controlling prediction.
  </details>

### Multiagent Systems 🌐

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[Generalized Per-Agent Advantage Estimation for Multi-Agent Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02654)**  `arXiv:2603.02654`  
  _Seongmin Kim, Giseung Park, Woojun Kim, Jiwon Jeon, Seungyeol Han, Youngchul Sung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose a novel framework for multi-agent reinforcement learning that enhances sample efficiency and coordination through accurate per-agent advantage estimation. The core of our approach is Generalized Per-Agent Advantage Estimator (GPAE), which employs a per-agent value iteration operator to compute precise per-agent advantages. This operator enables stable off-policy learning by indirectly estimating values via action probabilities, eliminating the need for direct Q-function estimation. To further refine estimation, we introduce a double-truncated importance sampling ratio scheme. This scheme improves credit assignment for off-policy trajectories by balancing sensitivity to the agent's own policy changes with robustness to non-stationarity from other agents. Experiments on benchmarks demonstrate that our approach outperforms existing approaches, excelling in coordination and sample efficiency for complex scenarios.
  </details>

- **[The Alignment Flywheel: A Governance-Centric Hybrid MAS for Architecture-Agnostic Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02259)**  `arXiv:2603.02259`  
  _Elias Malomgré, Pieter Simoens_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems provide mature methodologies for role decomposition, coordination, and normative governance, capabilities that remain essential as increasingly powerful autonomous decision components are embedded within agent-based systems. While learned and generative models substantially expand system capability, their safety behavior is often entangled with training, making it opaque, difficult to audit, and costly to update after deployment. This paper formalizes the Alignment Flywheel as a governance-centric hybrid MAS architecture that decouples decision generation from safety governance. A Proposer, representing any autonomous decision component, generates candidate trajectories, while a Safety Oracle returns raw safety signals through a stable interface. An enforcement layer applies explicit risk policy at runtime, and a governance MAS supervises the Oracle through auditing, uncertainty-driven verification, and versioned refinement. The central engineering principle is patch locality: many newly observed safety failures can be mitigated by updating the governed oracle artifact and its release pipeline rather than retracting or retraining the underlying decision component. The architecture is implementation-agnostic with respect to both the Proposer and the Safety Oracle, and specifies the roles, artifacts, protocols, and release semantics needed for runtime gating, audit intake, signed patching, and staged rollout across distributed deployments. The result is a hybrid MAS engineering framework for integrating highly capable but fallible autonomous systems under explicit, version-controlled, and auditable oversight.
  </details>

- **[Reuse, Don't Recompute: Efficient Large Reasoning Model Inference via Memory Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.12987)**  `arXiv:2511.12987`  
  _Daivik Patel, Shrenik Patel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) achieve strong accuracy through test-time scaling, generating longer chains of thought or sampling multiple solutions, but at steep costs in tokens and latency. We argue that memory is a core ingredient for efficient reasoning: when evidence already exists, models should think less by reusing structured memory instead of recomputing derivations. We present ENGRAM-R, an inference-time memory layer that integrates typed retrieval with compact fact card representations and explicit citation control. On the LoCoMo benchmark, ENGRAM-R reduces input tokens by 85% and reasoning tokens by 75% compared to full context while maintaining high accuracy. On a multi-hop slice of the LongMemEval benchmark, it achieves similar efficiency with substantial accuracy gains. These results show that memory is not only critical for long-horizon correctness but also a practical lever for efficient reasoning under tight compute, memory, and latency budgets.
  </details>

- **[Strategic Concealment of Environment Representations in Competitive Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.05503)**  `arXiv:2509.05503`  
  _Yue Guan, Dipankar Maity, Panagiotis Tsiotras_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper investigates the strategic concealment of environment representations used by players in competitive games. We consider a defense scenario in which one player (the Defender) seeks to infer and exploit the representation used by the other player (the Attacker). The interaction between the two players is modeled as a Bayesian game: the Defender infers the Attacker's representation from its trajectory and places barriers to obstruct the Attacker's path towards its goal, while the Attacker obfuscates its representation type to mislead the Defender. We solve for the Perfect Bayesian Nash Equilibrium via a bilinear program that integrates Bayesian inference, strategic planning, and belief manipulation. Simulations show that purposeful concealment naturally emerges: the Attacker randomizes its trajectory to manipulate the Defender's belief, inducing suboptimal barrier selections and thereby gaining a strategic advantage.
  </details>

### Robotics 🤖

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[ULTRA: Unified Multimodal Control for Autonomous Humanoid Whole-Body Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03279)**  `arXiv:2603.03279`  
  _Xialin He, Sirui Xu, Xinyao Li, Runpei Dong, Liuyu Bian, Yu-Xiong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving autonomous and versatile whole-body loco-manipulation remains a central barrier to making humanoids practically useful. Yet existing approaches are fundamentally constrained: retargeted data are often scarce or low-quality; methods struggle to scale to large skill repertoires; and, most importantly, they rely on tracking predefined motion references rather than generating behavior from perception and high-level task specifications. To address these limitations, we propose ULTRA, a unified framework with two key components. First, we introduce a physics-driven neural retargeting algorithm that translates large-scale motion capture to humanoid embodiments while preserving physical plausibility for contact-rich interactions. Second, we learn a unified multimodal controller that supports both dense references and sparse task specifications, under sensing ranging from accurate motion-capture state to noisy egocentric visual inputs. We distill a universal tracking policy into this controller, compress motor skills into a compact latent space, and apply reinforcement learning finetuning to expand coverage and improve robustness under out-of-distribution scenarios. This enables coordinated whole-body behavior from sparse intent without test-time reference motions. We evaluate ULTRA in simulation and on a real Unitree G1 humanoid. Results show that ULTRA generalizes to autonomous, goal-conditioned whole-body loco-manipulation from egocentric perception, consistently outperforming tracking-only baselines with limited skills.
  </details>

- **[Tether: Autonomous Functional Play with Correspondence-Driven Trajectory Warping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03278)**  `arXiv:2603.03278`  
  _William Liang, Sam Wang, Hung-Ju Wang, Osbert Bastani, Yecheng Jason Ma, Dinesh Jayaraman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ability to conduct and learn from interaction and experience is a central challenge in robotics, offering a scalable alternative to labor-intensive human demonstrations. However, realizing such "play" requires (1) a policy robust to diverse, potentially out-of-distribution environment states, and (2) a procedure that continuously produces useful robot experience. To address these challenges, we introduce Tether, a method for autonomous functional play involving structured, task-directed interactions. First, we design a novel open-loop policy that warps actions from a small set of source demonstrations (<=10) by anchoring them to semantic keypoint correspondences in the target scene. We show that this design is extremely data-efficient and robust even under significant spatial and semantic variations. Second, we deploy this policy for autonomous functional play in the real world via a continuous cycle of task selection, execution, evaluation, and improvement, guided by the visual understanding capabilities of vision-language models. This procedure generates diverse, high-quality datasets with minimal human intervention. In a household-like multi-object setup, our method is the first to perform many hours of autonomous multi-task play in the real world starting from only a handful of demonstrations. This produces a stream of data that consistently improves the performance of closed-loop imitation policies over time, ultimately yielding over 1000 expert-level trajectories and training policies competitive with those learned from human-collected demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[HoMMI: Learning Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation from Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03243)**  `arXiv:2603.03243`  
  _Xiaomeng Xu, Jisang Park, Han Zhang, Eric Cousineau, Aditya Bhat, Jose Barreiros, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Whole-Body Mobile Manipulation Interface (HoMMI), a data collection and policy learning framework that learns whole-body mobile manipulation directly from robot-free human demonstrations. We augment UMI interfaces with egocentric sensing to capture the global context required for mobile manipulation, enabling portable, robot-free, and scalable data collection. However, naively incorporating egocentric sensing introduces a larger human-to-robot embodiment gap in both observation and action spaces, making policy transfer difficult. We explicitly bridge this gap with a cross-embodiment hand-eye policy design, including an embodiment agnostic visual representation; a relaxed head action representation; and a whole-body controller that realizes hand-eye trajectories through coordinated whole-body motion under robot-specific physical constraints. Together, these enable long-horizon mobile manipulation tasks requiring bimanual and whole-body coordination, navigation, and active perception. Results are best viewed on:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ACE-Brain-0: Spatial Intelligence as a Shared Scaffold for Universal Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03198)**  `arXiv:2603.03198`  
  _Ziyang Gong, Zehang Luo, Anke Tang, Zhe Liu, Shi Fu, Zhi Hou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Universal embodied intelligence demands robust generalization across heterogeneous embodiments, such as autonomous driving, robotics, and unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs). However, existing embodied brain in training a unified model over diverse embodiments frequently triggers long-tail data, gradient interference, and catastrophic forgetting, making it notoriously difficult to balance universal generalization with domain-specific proficiency. In this report, we introduce ACE-Brain-0, a generalist foundation brain that unifies spatial reasoning, autonomous driving, and embodied manipulation within a single multimodal large language model~(MLLM). Our key insight is that spatial intelligence serves as a universal scaffold across diverse physical embodiments: although vehicles, robots, and UAVs differ drastically in morphology, they share a common need for modeling 3D mental space, making spatial cognition a natural, domain-agnostic foundation for cross-embodiment transfer. Building on this insight, we propose the Scaffold-Specialize-Reconcile~(SSR) paradigm, which first establishes a shared spatial foundation, then cultivates domain-specialized experts, and finally harmonizes them through data-free model merging. Furthermore, we adopt Group Relative Policy Optimization~(GRPO) to strengthen the model's comprehensive capability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ACE-Brain-0 achieves competitive and even state-of-the-art performance across 24 spatial and embodiment-related benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[From Language to Action: Can LLM-Based Agents Be Used for Embodied Robot Cognition?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03148)**  `arXiv:2603.03148`  
  _Shinas Shaji, Fabian Huppertz, Alex Mitrevski, Sebastian Houben_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In order to flexibly act in an everyday environment, a robotic agent needs a variety of cognitive capabilities that enable it to reason about plans and perform execution recovery. Large language models (LLMs) have been shown to demonstrate emergent cognitive aspects, such as reasoning and language understanding; however, the ability to control embodied robotic agents requires reliably bridging high-level language to low-level functionalities for perception and control.
